273

                     IX
       Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

1 Abstract

 2
 3 Enhanced Weathering (EW) is an emerging Carbon Dioxide Removal (CDR) approach within a
 4 growing portfolio of mitigation strategies, offering the potential for durable CDR alongside
 5 agronomic co-benefits. As interest in CDR increases, EW is transitioning from a primarily
 6 scientific concept toward early-stage implementation, requiring a comprehensive synthesis of the
 7 current evidence base. This systematic review focuses primarily on soil-based EW using silicate
 8 rock feedstocks. Across empirical and modelling studies, area-normalized CDR fluxes have a
 9 median value of 0.84 tonnes of carbon dioxide per hectare per year (tCO2 ha1 yr1) and span
10 several orders of magnitude. The variance in reported fluxes reflects not only context-dependent
11 differences in EW performance, but also the diversity of quantification approaches, which differ
12 by use case and vary in terms of their system boundaries and treatment of loss processes. When
13 scaled globally, evidence from empirical constraints and/or modelling approaches converges on a
14 maximum technical CDR potential of ~0.2­2 GtCO2 yr¹, though some estimates are higher.
15 Significant uncertainty remains regarding the magnitude, persistence, and timing of loss processes
16 after initial CDR has occurred, including secondary phase formation and cation exchange, and in
17 how these losses depend on local soil and climate conditions and deployment strategies.
18 Interactions with soil organic carbon introduce additional uncertainty but also the potential for
19 increasing total CDR. Beyond CDR, EW is consistently associated with improvements in soil
20 properties and crop productivity. Its deployment within managed land systems creates
21 opportunities for synergies with other land-based CDR approaches, enabling combined inorganic
22 and biological carbon sequestration. At the same time, EW raises important socio-economic and
23 governance considerations, including distributional impacts, environmental risks, and conditional
24 public support. Overall, EW represents a context-dependent and multifunctional CDR pathway,
25 with increasing evidence for its potential to contribute to both climate mitigation and multiple
26 Sustainable Development Goals. Its effective and scalable deployment will depend on improved
27 standardisation, robust monitoring and verification, and alignment with economic, environmental,
28 and societal constraints.

                                                                 1
       Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

29 1 Introduction

30 Carbon Dioxide Removal (CDR) methods are increasingly recognised as an essential complement
31 to rapid and sustained emissions reductions to limit global temperature rise in line with the Paris
32 Agreement and comply with a growing number of sub-national, national and international targets
33 and assessments.1­5 Across most mitigation pathways consistent with the Paris agreement, CDR is
34 required within the coming decade to compensate for residual emissions from hard-to-abate
35 sectors, limit or reverse temperature overshoot, and provide insurance against climate system
36 uncertainties.2,4­12
37
38 Existing reviews have provided valuable high-level syntheses of the overall CDR landscape,
39 summarising broad categories of methods, their theoretical potential, and their possible roles in
40 mitigation pathways.2,3,6­8 These assessments have been instrumental in establishing CDR as a
41 core component of climate stabilisation strategies and in enabling comparison across methods at
42 the global scale. Nevertheless, as CDR research and deployment progress there is increasing
43 demand for complementary assessments that examine individual CDR methods in greater depth,
44 with consistent treatment of empirical evidence, modelling assumptions, and methodological
45 choices.
46
47 This systematic review assesses the available evidence on Enhanced Weathering (EW; also called
48 Enhanced Rock Weathering, ERW), a novel land-based geochemical CDR approach that
49 accelerates the natural weathering of rocks through grinding and application to soils, thereby
50 enhancing CO2 uptake and long-term carbon storage in dissolved inorganic carbon (DIC),
51 secondary carbonate minerals, and marine reservoirs.13­26 Enhanced Weathering has attracted
52 increasing attention due to its potential scalability, its compatibility with existing agricultural
53 practices, and the possibility of co-benefits for soil chemistry, nutrient supply, and crop
54 productivity.27,28 At the same time, EW research spans a wide range of disciplines, including
55 geochemistry, agronomy, soil science, hydrology, and life cycle assessment (LCA), resulting in a
56 heterogeneous and fragmented evidence base. Carbonate rock powder additions to soils,
57 commonly referred to as liming, have long been used to manage soil pH and can also generate
58 CDR.29­31 Carbonate-based EW studies are included where available, but because the current EW
59 literature is dominated by silicate feedstocks, the main synthesis focuses on silicate-based EW.
60 Similarly, the co-benefits assessment incorporates new syntheses of silicate rock-flour soil
61 amendments while drawing on existing meta-analyses to utilize evidence from agricultural liming.
62
63 This systematic review of EW is part of a broader ecosystem of coordinated CDR systematic
64 reviews that aims to provide a rigorous, detailed, and methodologically consistent assessment of
65 the state of knowledge across a wide portfolio of CDR approaches and cross-cutting topics. Similar
66 systematic reviews within this ecosystem have been undertaken for other CDR methods, including
67 direct air carbon capture and storage (DACCS),32 establishing a common foundation for synthesis
68 and comparison across approaches. Within this ecosystem of reviews, all contributions follow a
69 harmonised set of definitions, assessment methods, and review designs. This coordination enables
70 cross-method synthesis, supports cumulative learning across the CDR research community, and
71 enhances the utility of the reviews for scientific assessments, policy processes, and practical
72 decision-making related to the future role of CDR.
73

                                                                 2
       Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)
74 This review begins by describing the systematic methods used to identify, screen, and code the
75 EW literature, followed by an overview of the size, growth, and disciplinary composition of the
76 existing evidence base. It then provides a structured synthesis of the fundamental principles of
77 EW, key factors controlling weathering efficiency, transport and long-term carbon storage
78 pathways, as well as the major loss processes, reversals, and limitations that affect net CDR.
79 Building on this process-level understanding, the review evaluates current approaches for
80 monitoring, reporting, and verification (MRV) across solid, aqueous, and gaseous phases,
81 highlighting how different methods capture distinct components of the EW system and providing
82 context for the discussion of observed CDR rates and potentials. We then synthesize reported CDR
83 rates and potentials across spatial scales, examine costs and techno-economic considerations, and
84 assess agronomic, environmental, and socio-economic side effects. The review further situates EW
85 within broader LCAs, interactions with other CDR approaches, policy frameworks, equity and
86 justice considerations, and public perceptions, before concluding with an assessment of upscaling
87 challenges, technology readiness, and the role of EW in integrated assessment models as well as
88 implications for future research and deployment.

                                                                 3
         Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

89 2 Methods

 90 We undertook this systematic review following the general protocol for the Ecosystem of
 91 Systematic Reviews on CDR (ESROC) and report our methods following the Reporting Standards
 92 for Systematic Evidence Syntheses (ROSES) reporting standards.33 The following sections step
 93 through the scope of this review and the methods used to search for EW literature, screen for
 94 relevant studies, and extract data, ending with an overview of the EW literature landscape.
 95
 96

 97 2.1 Scope definition and relation to adjacent mineral-based CDR
 98 pathways

 99 Enhanced Weathering is one of several mineral-based CDR pathways that rely on the reaction of
100 alkaline rock types with acids to neutralise acidity and generate alkalinity, thereby enabling
101 atmospheric CO2 uptake and long-term carbon storage. Closely related approaches include in situ
102 mineralisation, in which CO2 is injected into reactive geological formations and converted into
103 stable carbonate minerals,34,35 reactor-based or ex situ mineral carbonation, where silicate or
104 carbonate materials are processed in engineered systems to accelerate weathering and carbonation
105 reactions,36 ocean alkalinity enhancement (OAE), which increases seawater alkalinity through the
106 addition of finely ground minerals or alkalinity generated via industrial or electrochemical
107 processes,37,38 and EW of mine tailings, which exploits highly reactive waste materials to promote
108 carbonation and alkalinity release within waste stockpiles or ponds.39 While these pathways share
109 a common geochemical foundation--namely the neutralisation of acidity and the release of
110 alkalinity through mineral­acid reactions--they differ substantially in the environmental setting
111 where reactions occur, the controlling processes that determine process efficiency, timescales, and
112 effective monitoring approaches, and their relevance for land management and agricultural
113 systems.
114
115 Within ESROC, these distinctions motivate a clear separation of scope across pathways. This
116 review concentrates specifically on terrestrial EW as implemented through the application of rock
117 powder feedstock to soils, where weathering reactions occur within biologically active,
118 hydrologically complex, and heterogeneous soil environments. Related CDR approaches such as
119 OAE are covered in separate systematic reviews being developed within ESROC. This focus
120 allows for a targeted assessment of EW performance, limitations, monitoring approaches, and side
121 effects under conditions most relevant to agricultural and managed land deployment.
122
123

124 2.2 Literature search

125 We searched for EW literature using the Web of Science and Scopus literature databases, which
126 are two of the largest bibliographic databases for peer-reviewed literature. Our search query was
127 developed and refined as part of the first global CDR literature mapping exercise and includes a
128 range of relevant terms to capture different EW domains in the context of CDR (see SI).28 To
129 ensure relevance to the focus of this review, we limited our search to literature focused on EW in
130 soil-based settings (see sections 2.1 and 2.3). The cut-off publication date for this systematic search
131 was July 2024.
132

                                                                   4
         Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

133 One challenge when reviewing EW literature is the large body of research on weathering and the
134 carbon cycle in the geological past, rather than on human activities to remove CO2. Our search
135 query explicitly excluded specific terms relevant to previous geological eras, though we note this
136 can have unintended consequences if relevant studies mention the geological past as contextual
137 information and are excluded as a result. To ensure key studies were not missed, and to capture
138 recent papers published after our systematic search cut-off date, we also included studies identified
139 using expert knowledge and forward/backward snowballing to supplement our systematic search.
140 The cut-off date for these non-systematic searches was April 2025. Authors of individual text
141 sections were also free to consider studies published after this date where relevant for interpreting
142 recent developments and ensuring that narrative discussions reflected the most up-to-date science.
143 However, data from these later studies were not systematically extracted (see below) and are not
144 included in quantitative assessments or figures presented in this review. In addition, we included
145 non-peer reviewed (e.g., dissertations) and grey literature where we considered the methods to be
146 robust and the results a valuable contribution to the EW evidence base.
147
148 Another challenge relates to the boundaries of our review: we are interested in EW in the context
149 of CDR, but we also want to understand the side effects (both negative and positive) of EW
150 deployment in soils. Relying solely on CDR literature could miss some of these side effects. We
151 therefore also included studies (n = 104) focused on the agricultural use of rock flour, identified
152 via expert knowledge and forward/backward snowballing. Only studies published after 1980 were
153 considered, even though a small number of earlier publications existc (e.g., ref. 40).
154
155

156 2.3 Inclusion and exclusion criteria for the EW literature

157 The objective of this review is to evaluate the efficacy, constraints, CDR potential, costs, and
158 impacts of EW in soil-based settings, with particular emphasis on agricultural systems. We
159 therefore included literature that explicitly examines terrestrial EW in a soil context, encompassing
160 field trials, laboratory experiments with clear relevance for field-based EW, modelling studies,
161 natural analogues discussed within an EW framework, reviews, policy and governance analyses,
162 and socio-economic, equity, or justice-focused work. In each of these cases we only included
163 literature for which EW constituted a substantive part of the analysis: Laboratory studies
164 determining mineral dissolution, weathering, or carbonation rates were included when they
165 explicitly linked their findings to EW deployment, for example by discussing implications for soil-
166 based applications, surface passivation, reaction kinetics, or transport limitations. Similarly,
167 studies assessing downstream impacts in rivers, catchments, or coastal systems were included only
168 when these impacts were explicitly evaluated as part of a terrestrial EW system.
169
170 To maintain relevance to soil-based deployment, we excluded literature focused on reactor-based
171 weathering or carbonation, industrial flue gas treatment, and ex situ mineral carbonation outside
172 of soil environments. Studies on mine tailings or other industrial residues were excluded unless
173 they explicitly assessed or proposed the application of these materials to agricultural soils,
174 recognising that fully concentrated feedstock settings comprised of thick layers of pure rock
175 powder without intermixed soil (i.e., mine tailings) may not be directly informative for weathering
176 dynamics, transport, or monitoring in soils. Similarly, OAE studies were excluded unless they
177 explicitly framed alkalinity generation as originating from terrestrial EW. Papers describing

                                                                   5
         Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

178 natural weathering in soils, watersheds, or streams without an explicit "enhanced" component were
179 excluded unless they were clearly discussed as natural analogues for EW.
180
181 Our review focuses on EW as a CDR strategy, so we identified those studies that explicitly engage
182 with EW in the context of CDR, for example by quantifying CO2 uptake, alkalinity generation, or
183 net removal, or by framing EW as a climate mitigation strategy. Studies that investigated the use
184 of silicate rock flour solely for agronomic purposes--such as soil fertility, nutrient supply, or yield
185 effects--without reference to CDR were not incorporated into the evidence base for quantifying
186 EW CDR but were instead separately labelled as agronomic rock-powder studies and were used
187 to constrain agronomic responses and side effects.
188
189 While the use of carbonates in EW and CDR contexts is beginning to receive increased
190 attention,30,41 the existing literature remains limited. This review includes carbonate-based rock
191 amendment studies where they explicitly frame amendments as EW for CDR. In contrast, the
192 extensive literature on carbonate amendments for agronomic purposes, i.e., agricultural liming, is
193 excluded from the quantitative assessment where it lacks EW framing. Where relevant, we instead
194 draw on existing meta-analyses to contextualize insights from this broader literature on the co-
195 benefits of rock-powder amendments.42,43 Accordingly, this review primarily focuses on silicate
196 feedstocks, which form the core of the EW literature and the main basis for current EW CDR
197 assessments.
198
199 The full list of inclusion and exclusion criteria, including decision rules applied during abstract
200 and full-text screening, are provided in the Supplementary Information (SI) to ensure transparency
201 and reproducibility. We developed these criteria before screening the literature and iteratively
202 adjusted them through regular reviewer meetings.
203
204 We used the review management platform NACSOS2 to screen the titles and abstracts of the
205 records from our literature search using the criteria described above.44 As a first step, we used the
206 automatic classifier that was trained in Lück et al. (2025)28 to identify papers that were most likely
207 to be relevant. This reduced the number of records requiring manual screening from 1,151 to 826.
208 Two expert reviewers then applied the screening criteria to identify relevant records, followed by
209 sorting into pre-defined categories to facilitate further analysis (see section 2.4). In cases where
210 the abstract was not sufficient to decide, reviewers consulted the full text and conferred until
211 agreement could be reached. At the end of this process, we had a total of 241 peer-reviewed and
212 40 non-peer-reviewed records for inclusion in the review (see Figure 1). We have added these to
213 a bibliometric database of EW literature, which is continually updated as new studies are
214 published.27
215

223 2.4 Data extraction

224 Data extraction was guided by a purpose-built codebook designed to capture the breadth and
225 heterogeneity of the EW literature while maintaining internal consistency across multiple
226 contributors. The codebook was developed iteratively prior to data extraction to cover reported
227 CDR estimates, underlying geochemical and agronomic measurements, methodological choices,
228 and a wide range of qualitative and quantitative metadata. Because the data extraction effort was
229 embedded within a broader database and meta-analysis project, the codebook was intentionally
230 designed to be more comprehensive than required for this systematic review alone. As a result,
231 only a subset of the extracted variables is used directly in the synthesis presented here, while the
232 full database, intended to support additional meta-analyses and synthesis efforts, will be made
233 publicly available as an open-source resource upon completion.
234
235 The codebook structures data extraction into hierarchical sections capturing: (i) basic bibliographic
236 and identifying information, including unique experiment identifiers; (ii) study type, methods, and
237 actors involved; (iii) experimental or model conditions, including spatial scale, duration, soil type,
238 climate, hydrology, and vegetation; (iv) detailed pre- and post-amendment measurements of soils,
239 pore waters, effluent waters, exchangeable pools, and biomass, including elemental
240 concentrations, carbonate system parameters, isotopic data, mineralogy, and physical soil
241 properties; (v) feedstock characteristics and application details, such as mineral type, particle size

                                                                   7
         Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

242 distribution, composition, application rate, and co-amendments; (vi) CDR estimates and associated
243 carbon budgets reported at different spatial and temporal scales, together with methodological
244 details on measurement, reporting, and verification (MRV), loss processes considered, and
245 analytical approaches; (vii) quantitative LCA data and cost estimates, including system
246 boundaries, assumptions, and reported cost components; and (viii) qualitative discussion topics,
247 including agronomic effects, environmental and social side effects, policy landscape, and
248 interactions with other CDR approaches.
249
250 Data extraction was carried out by a team of 34 contributors, each of whom applied the shared
251 codebook to extract data from up to 15 publications using a standardised Excel-based data entry
252 template. To ensure data quality and consistency across contributors, the extraction process
253 included two organized rounds of quality assurance and quality control (QA/QC). In the first
254 round, a designated reviewer in a supervisory role manually checked all submitted entries against
255 predefined criteria, focusing on completeness, internal consistency, correct use of categories, unit
256 reporting, and adherence to codebook instructions, providing structured feedback to contributors.
257 In the second round, automated checks implemented in Python were used to identify logical
258 inconsistencies and missing dependencies across database fields, for example ensuring that the
259 presence of data in one category (e.g., reported CDR rates) was accompanied by corresponding
260 information in required related categories (e.g., MRV type, system boundaries, etc.). In both
261 rounds, contributors addressed QA/QC queries through documented responses that explicitly
262 justified data entries and any subsequent revisions, following a structured review­response process
263 that was subsequently verified by the designated reviewer. Additional targeted QA/QC was
264 conducted iteratively during data analysis, when ambiguities or inconsistencies became apparent
265 for specific variables or subsets of studies. The full codebook, including definitions, decision rules,
266 and data entry instructions, is provided in the SI to ensure transparency and reproducibility of the
267 data extraction process
268
269

270 2.5 Literature overview

271 First mentions of the concept of EW can be traced back to the early 1990s.13,46 However, systematic
272 discussion in the context of CDR only began in the late 2000s and early 2010s, making EW a
273 comparatively recent CDR pathway relative to more established land-based options. Based on the
274 peer-reviewed CDR literature identified through the systematic review process, we identify 241
275 EW-focused peer-reviewed studies published between 1995 and 2025 (Figure 2a). We note that
276 this is smaller than some other estimates28 because we applied focused inclusion and exclusion
277 criteria, followed by additional manual screening, to exclude false positives and identify studies
278 most relevant to soil-based EW for CDR. Publication activity remained very limited until the mid-
279 2010s, followed by a marked acceleration after 2017 and particularly rapid growth since 2020.
280 Annual publication counts increased from fewer than 15 studies per year prior to 2021 to 50 studies
281 in 2024, reflecting growing scientific and policy interest in EW as a scalable CDR option. The low
282 count in 2025 reflects the April cut-off date for inclusion.
283
284 Geographically, judging by first author location, EW research is currently dominated by
285 institutions based in the Global North (Figure 2b). First authors are most frequently affiliated with
286 institutions in the United Kingdom (27%), the United States (18%), and Germany (10%), followed
287 by Canada (8%) and China (7%). In contrast, large parts of Africa, South America, and Southeast

                                                                   8
         Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

288 Asia are sparsely represented in terms of first-author affiliations (Figure 2c). At the same time, the
289 geographic distribution of experimental and trial locations reveals a more nuanced pattern (Figure
290 2c). While authorship is concentrated in Europe and North America, many field trials--
291 particularly those investigating silicate rock flour as a fertiliser--are conducted in the Global
292 South, including Brazil, parts of Southeast Asia, and Africa. This divergence highlights a structural
293 imbalance between where EW research is led and where on-the-ground experimentation and
294 agronomic application are taking place.
295
296 The literature on silicate rock flour used as a fertiliser (i.e., without explicit CDR framing) shows
297 a much stronger representation from countries in the Global South (Brazil 19%, China 9%, Egypt
298 5%, Indonesia 5%). Brazil, with a strong focus on using rock flour as an agricultural
299 amendment47,48, accounts for nearly one fifth of fertiliser-focused studies, followed by
300 contributions from the United States, China, the United Kingdom, Australia, Egypt, Indonesia, and
301 several other regions with strong agricultural research programs. These studies form an important
302 complementary evidence base for understanding agronomic responses and potential side effects of
303 silicate amendments, even where CDR is not the primary research objective.
304
305 In terms of research approaches, the EW literature spans a wide range of study types (Figure 2d).
306 Experimental studies conducted under laboratory or mesocosm conditions represent the largest
307 single category (23%), closely followed by modelling studies (21%). Reviews and perspective or
308 opinion pieces together account for over a quarter of the literature, reflecting both the rapid
309 expansion of the field and ongoing efforts to synthesize emerging evidence. Field experiments
310 make up ~10% of studies, underscoring that EW remains at an early stage of real-world testing
311 relative to its proposed scale of deployment. Smaller but important fractions of the literature
312 address LCA, integrated assessment modelling, data analysis, natural analogue studies, surveys,
313 and qualitative research, illustrating the growing diversification of EW research beyond purely
314 geochemical questions.
315
316 With respect to content, the majority of peer-reviewed EW studies focus primarily on quantifying
317 CDR through EW, including assessments of weathering rates, alkalinity generation, and carbon
318 budgets (65%; Figure 2e). Environmental side effects, technological and logistical considerations,
319 and agronomic impacts each account for smaller but significant shares of the literature. Topics
320 such as public perception, governance and policy, equity and ethics, carbon losses and lag times,
321 and resource availability remain comparatively under-represented, although several of these
322 themes have gained traction in recent years. Overall, the literature reveals a field that is expanding
323 rapidly in volume and disciplinary breadth, but remains unevenly distributed across geographies,
324 research methods, and thematic priorities--highlighting both the progress made and the key gaps
325 that motivate a systematic synthesis of the available evidence.
326

     Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

336 2.6 Calculations and data processing

337 Several of the figures presented in this review summarise treatment effects associated with EW
338 interventions across the literature landscape, expressed as observed changes in geochemical or
339 agronomic parameters relative to appropriate reference conditions. Depending on the variable and
340 study design, treatment effects are reported either as absolute differences ()--for example,
341 changes in soil pH--or as logarithmic response ratios, calculated as log10(treatment/control).
342 Absolute differences are used where direct interpretation of magnitude is meaningful. In contrast,
343 log response ratios are widely applied in systematic reviews and meta-analyses because they
344 provide a dimensionless, scale-independent measure of proportional change, are symmetric around
345 zero, and tend to stabilise variance across heterogeneous studies, facilitating comparison across
346 experiments with differing baselines, units, and magnitudes.49,50 A positive
347 log10(treatment/control) value indicates an increase relative to the control, while negative values
348 indicate a decrease. A value of 1 (-1) corresponds to an increase (decrease) by a factor of 10.
349
350 Where data availability permitted, treatment effects were calculated relative to both concurrent
351 control treatments and baseline conditions, defined as measurements at the treatment site prior to
352 EW application (t = 0). Control-based assessment contrasts differences between treated and
353 untreated systems under otherwise similar conditions, whereas baseline-based assessment reflects
354 temporal changes within treated systems relative to pre-treatment measurements; both types of
355 treatment effects are included in the compilation figures.
356

                                                                  11
         Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

357 3 Enhanced Weathering overview

358 This section introduces the fundamental principles underlying EW and the factors that regulate its
359 performance across environmental settings. We begin with overviews of co-benefits and broader
360 system impacts compared to other CDR approaches, the mechanistic basis of EW reactions, and
361 distinctions between inorganic and organic carbon pathways (Section 3.1). We then examine the
362 physicochemical controls on EW efficiency, focusing on how feedstock and mineral properties,
363 grain size, soil chemistry, climate, and hydrology interact to govern weathering rates (Section 3.2).
364
365 Next, we consider the transport processes linking terrestrial weathering to downstream carbon
366 storage, and the implications for long-term CO2 sequestration in EW systems (Section 3.3). We
367 then outline the principal pathways that can reduce net CDR, including both carbon losses and
368 limitations on primary weathering (Section 3.4). The section concludes with an overview of
369 environmental and operational constraints (Section 3.5), followed by a discussion of the modelling
370 approaches used to quantify EW performance and scalability (Section 3.6).
371
372

373 3.1 Basic principles of EW

374 Natural silicate weathering is a fundamental component of the long-term carbon cycle that removes
375 CO2 from the atmosphere through reactions between CO2, water, and silicate minerals. These
376 reactions generate dissolved alkalinity and ultimately transfer carbon from the atmosphere into
377 stable inorganic reservoirs, including dissolved bicarbonate in the ocean and solid carbonate
378 minerals.51,52 As such, this process regulates soil formation, controls the chemical composition of
379 rivers, and governs the transfer of dissolved elements from continents to the oceans, thereby
380 shaping global ocean chemistry and salinity.52,53 Over geological timescales, this process acts as a
381 stabilising climate feedback because weathering rates increase with temperature54­59 and runoff54­
382 57,59, thereby enhancing CO2 consumption under warmer and wetter conditions. This negative
383 feedback mechanism has long been described as a planetary thermostat regulating global
384 temperatures and atmospheric CO2 concentrations.60,61
385
386 Enhanced Weathering seeks to accelerate this naturally slow process by increasing mineral
387 reactivity and optimizing environmental conditions for dissolution.13­26 This is typically achieved
388 by mechanically grinding, crushing, or pulverising (i.e., comminuting) suitable silicate rocks to
389 increase reactive surface area and applying the resulting material to soils using existing
390 infrastructure for fertiliser or lime (calcium carbonate) application. Here, elevated soil CO2
391 concentrations generated by root respiration and microbial activity under often acidic conditions
392 enhance mineral dissolution kinetics.15,19,62,63 Nevertheless, smaller particle size does not
393 universally translate into faster weathering under field conditions because hydrological transport,
394 solution saturation, and soil physical structure can limit reaction progress even when surface area
395 is high;63,64 see Section 3.2 for more details. Moreover, because comminution is energy-intensive,
396 optimal grain size reflects a trade-off between dissolution kinetics and life cycle energy demand
397 rather than a simple preference for the finest material.18,19,23
398
399 Carbon Dioxide Removal through EW occurs through a sequence of coupled geochemical
400 processes, each of which is introduced in more detail in the following text sections. First, CO2

                                                                  12
         Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

401 dissolves in soil water to form carbonic acid, which supplies protons that drive mineral dissolution
402 and release base cations such as Ca2+ and Mg2+ (see Section 3.1.2). This reaction generates DIC
403 primarily in the form of bicarbonate, representing the main pathway for atmospheric CO2
404 consumption during silicate weathering.15,19 Second, these dissolved weathering products are
405 transported in solution through soils and aquatic systems.30,65­67 Hydrological pathways and
406 geochemical processes in soils determine whether alkalinity and cations are retained locally,
407 exchanged on soil surfaces, precipitated as secondary minerals, or exported to rivers and
408 groundwater. The efficiency of this transport step strongly influences the fraction of captured
409 carbon that ultimately contributes to durable CDR rather than being re-released locally.14,19,68
410 Third, dissolved alkalinity exported to the ocean represents a long-lived carbon reservoir because
411 ocean residence times of bicarbonate and carbonate species span millennia.37 Alternatively, cations
412 released during weathering may precipitate as solid carbonates in soils or sediments, forming
413 inorganic carbon pools with variable stability. While such mineral precipitation can represent long-
414 term storage, it also causes some of the carbon initially captured by the weathering reaction to be
415 re-released as CO2.15
416
417 Most EW studies and deployments have focused on croplands and other managed agricultural
418 systems because these environments already possess the infrastructure, machinery, and logistical
419 networks required for distributing finely ground materials at scale, and because mineral
420 amendments can provide agronomic co-benefits that facilitate adoption.15,19,22,69 Agricultural soils
421 therefore represent the primary near-term deployment context in much of the literature, as
422 spreading technologies developed for lime or fertiliser application can be readily adapted for
423 silicate materials. Forest ecosystems have also attracted increasing attention as potential
424 application environments, including earlier work motivated by soil amelioration, nutrient
425 replacement for nutrient removal due to harvest and export, and mitigation of soil
426 acidification.64,70­74 However, implementation in such systems is generally considered more
427 operationally complex and costly due to access constraints and heterogeneous terrain. Marginal or
428 degraded lands have likewise been proposed as candidate sites where mineral amendments could
429 contribute simultaneously to CDR and soil restoration, particularly in contexts where conventional
430 agricultural productivity is limited.75­77 However, with current technologies, deployment in both
431 forested and marginal landscapes will face logistical challenges related to transport, application
432 efficiency, and monitoring, which may constrain feasibility relative to intensively managed
433 agricultural systems.
434
435

436 3.1.1 Co-benefit profile of EW in comparison to other CDR approaches

437 As a geochemical CDR strategy that stores carbon predominantly in stable inorganic reservoirs
438 (section 3.5), EW occupies a distinctive position within the portfolio of proposed CDR
439 approaches.2,6­8,78 In comparison to chemical CDR technologies such as DACCS, EW generally
440 exhibits lower operational energy requirements per unit CO2 removed because its core reactions
441 are thermodynamically spontaneous and are driven by natural gradients rather than requiring
442 energy-intensive separation processes.7,18,19 Consequently, EW deployment does not intrinsically
443 depend on large, dedicated renewable-energy inputs, although access to low-carbon energy can
444 substantially improve life cycle emissions associated with material processing and transport (cf.,
445 Section 8).7,18,19

                                                                  13
         Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

446
447 Relative to most biologically mediated CDR approaches, the storage resulting from silicate
448 weathering is expected to be more durable because it resides primarily in dissolved alkalinity or
449 mineral phases rather than living biomass or SOM pools, which can be rapidly remobilised under
450 changing environmental conditions.7,15 Biological sinks such as forests and soil carbon are
451 vulnerable to disturbances and climatic feedbacks that can reduce long-term storage efficiency--
452 including fire, decomposition, and warming-induced respiration increases79­81--whereas
453 inorganic weathering products are not subject to the same ecological sensitivities. Beyond this
454 high-level comparison, synergies and potential antagonisms between EW and other CDR
455 approaches are explored in detail in Section 9.
456
457 Enhanced Weathering also differs from many bio-based CDR strategies in that it does not require
458 dedicated biomass production and therefore does not inherently compete for biomass associated
459 with those approaches, or for land needed for food production or ecosystem conservation.7,82
460 Instead, silicate feedstock can be applied directly to already managed lands using existing
461 infrastructure (see also Section 12 on technology readiness), particularly agricultural soils, where
462 they can improve soil chemical conditions through pH buffering, supply essential plant nutrients
463 such as Ca, Mg, K, and Si, and enhance soil fertility.15,22,47 These agronomic effects can translate
464 into increased crop productivity, improved nutrient-use efficiency, and greater resilience to soil
465 degradation and acidification.19,20,22 Accordingly, mineral soil amendments of this type have been
466 used in agricultural practice for decades to centuries.40,47 Rather than competing for biomass, EW
467 may therefore enhance biomass production, creating opportunities for synergies with biomass-
468 based CDR approaches (see Section 9).
469
470 Beyond farm-scale benefits, partial substitution or supplementation of conventional fertilisers
471 through mineral amendments may reduce GHG emissions associated with fertiliser manufacture
472 and application. Such additions may also influence soil chemical and microbial processes in ways
473 that suppress non-CO2 GHG emissions such as N2O.22,83 EW can additionally provide broader
474 environmental services, including mitigation of soil acidification, replenishment of weathering-
475 derived nutrients lost through harvest and erosion, and reductions in nutrient runoff via improved
476 nutrient-use efficiency.15,19,22 Collectively, these characteristics position EW as a multifunctional
477 land-management intervention capable of delivering climate mitigation alongside tangible
478 agricultural, environmental, and societal co-benefits that are aligned with multiple Sustainable
479 Development Goals (SDGs),19,84 thereby distinguishing it from CDR approaches that provide CDR
480 as their sole primary outcome. These co-benefits are examined in greater detail in Section 7, where
481 the empirical evidence base and the magnitude of reported effects are assessed.
482
483 Taken together, this combination of durable carbon storage, comparatively modest energy
484 requirements, limited resource competition, and clear agricultural co-benefits gives EW a
485 distinctive profile within diversified CDR portfolios. These attributes suggest that EW may be
486 deployable not only as a climate mitigation measure but also as a land-management strategy with
487 independent agronomic value, which could facilitate implementation and stakeholder acceptance
488 even outside explicit carbon-removal policy frameworks (cf., Sections 7 and 10).
489
490

                                                                  14
     Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

491 3.1.2 Geochemical reactions driving CDR from EW

492 The CDR potential of EW arises from a sequence of aqueous geochemical reactions that govern

493 mineral dissolution, alkalinity generation, and carbon storage; a comprehensive treatment of these
494 reactions can be found elsewhere.15 Briefly, CO2 derived from soil respiration reacts with water to

495 form carbonic acid:

496                      !"! + %!"  %!!""  %# + %!""$

497                                                                                                  1

498

499 In terrestrial environments, CO2 derived from soil respiration is typically present at higher partial

500 pressures than in the atmosphere, further driving this reaction forward. Carbonic acid is a weak
501 acid with a pKa1 of 6.35 in aqueous solution.85 Under most common soil pHs, carbonic acid is

502 partially or completely dissociated, releasing protons and bicarbonate:

503

504                      %!!""  %!""$ + %#  !""!$ + 2%#                                              2

505

506 Protons--from this or other sources--drive mineral dissolution reactions that consume protons

507 and liberate base cations capable of charge-balancing bicarbonate or carbonate anions. These

508 reactions ultimately lead to two principal inorganic carbon storage states discussed in the literature:

509 carbon as dissolved carbonate alkalinity (primarily HCO3 and CO32) and carbon in solid
510 carbonate minerals.14,15,19,24 For example, the dissolution of olivine may lead to alkalinity
511 production due to an increase in HCO3- counterbalanced by cations (i.e., Mg2+):

512  *+!,-"% (/) + 4 !"! + 4 %!"  2 *+!# + 4 %!""$ + %%,-"%

513                                                                                                  3

514

515 Here, CO2 is converted into dissolved bicarbonate, which constitutes the principal pathway for
516 long-term atmospheric CO2 consumption via silicate weathering.15 Similarly, dissolution of

517 wollastonite produces alkalinity but may also be followed by secondary carbonate precipitation:

518

519  !4,-"" (/) + 2 !"! + 3 %!"  !4!# + 2 %!"" + %%,-"% 

                         !4!""(/) + !"2 + %!" + %%,-"%                                               4

520

521 In such cases, precipitation of carbonate minerals can release a fraction of the CO2 initially

522 consumed during dissolution, meaning that alkalinity storage and solid phase carbonate storage
523 differ in their net CDR efficiency depending on reaction pathways and environmental conditions.15

524 The dissolved silica produced during silicate dissolution, represented here as silicic acid (H4SiO4),

525 often does not fully leave the reaction site because it can reprecipitate as secondary phases or
526 coatings on mineral surfaces,63,86 limiting downstream silica export.

527

528 In contrast to silicates, carbonate mineral weathering does not generally produce net long-term

529 CDR when followed by carbonate reprecipitation. Dissolution of calcite, for example,

530

531                      !4!"" + !"! + %!"  !4!# + 2 %!""                                            5

532

533 can temporarily increase alkalinity, but if carbonate subsequently precipitates, CO2 is released

534 again--making carbonate weathering alone a transient rather than permanent atmospheric CO2
535 sink.15 However, if secondary carbonate formation is delayed until after dissolved alkalinity has

                         15
         Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

536 been transported to the ocean on long timescales, this pathway can still contribute effectively to
537 CDR,30,31,87 albeit with a higher likelihood of reduced net uptake in systems where strong-acid
538 inputs drive dissolution without equivalent atmospheric CO2 consumption.
539
540 Beyond its impact on the CDR balance of carbonate weathering, the presence of strong acids (in
541 particular nitric acid derived from fertilizer application and atmospheric deposition of
542 anthropogenic nitric and sulfuric acid) can further modify these EW reactions because protons
543 derived from strong acids can drive mineral dissolution without consuming atmospheric CO2,
544 thereby reducing net CDR efficiency. The thermodynamic and conceptual framework underlying
545 alkalinity generation and acid-base system behaviour is treated in detail elsewhere,85,88­90 and the
546 implications of strong-acid weathering for EW carbon balances are discussed in more detail in
547 Section 3.4.3.
548
549

550 3.1.3 Inorganic vs. organic CDR from EW

551 The reactions described above represent only the inorganic geochemical component of CDR
552 associated with EW. However, mineral amendments interact with biological processes and nutrient
553 cycling, which can modify ecosystem carbon dynamics and thereby influence net carbon balances
554 beyond the purely abiotic weathering signal.70,75,84,91­111 These coupled soil­plant­microbe
555 interactions can regulate both mineral dissolution and carbon storage, indicating that biological
556 responses are an important component of the overall carbon-cycle impact of EW in terrestrial
557 systems.
558
559 Enhanced Weathering has the potential to impact SOM stocks in opposing directions. Increases in
560 soil pH and nutrient availability following mineral additions may stimulate microbial activity and
561 accelerate decomposition of labile organic matter, potentially producing short-term increases in
562 respiration and transient carbon losses or other changes to SOM pools.112­114 Evidence from the
563 literature on liming shows that such responses can occur shortly after amendment, particularly in
564 acidic soils or systems where microbial activity is nutrient-limited 42,43. Conversely, mineral inputs
565 can also enhance stabilisation of organic carbon through increased mineral­organic associations
566 and improved plant productivity that raises organic carbon inputs to soils--processes widely
567 recognised as key controls on long-term soil carbon persistence.75,94 Liming literature indicates
568 that this latter process often dominates in the medium to long term, though there is considerable
569 variability both within and between studies.42,43
570
571 If EW positively alters soil organic carbon (SOC) stocks, the resulting impact on ecosystem carbon
572 balance could equal or exceed the magnitude of inorganic CDR.75,94 Such outcomes would
573 represent an important additional climate benefit and highlight the potential of mineral
574 amendments as tools for improving soil carbon management. At the same time, organic-carbon
575 responses differ fundamentally from inorganic EW removal in durability and accounting
576 characteristics. Soil organic carbon typically turns over on decadal to centennial timescales and
577 remains sensitive to disturbance, land-use change, and climatic variability,81,115 whereas carbon
578 stored through alkalinity generation and subsequent ocean storage can persist for millennia.15,37
579 Because persistence, reversibility risk, and monitoring approaches differ so strongly between
580 organic and inorganic processes and carbon pools, combining resulting carbon impacts into a

                                                                  16
         Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

581 single CDR estimate would obscure their distinct temporal characteristics and could lead to
582 inconsistent or non-comparable crediting across CDR approaches.
583
584 For this reason, CDR during EW in this review refers exclusively to carbon removal arising from
585 inorganic weathering processes. Potential effects of EW on SOM are not disregarded but are
586 instead treated as complementary outcomes whose magnitude, direction, and variability must be
587 quantified empirically and independently. Accordingly, responses of SOC observed across the
588 compiled EW studies are assessed separately in Section 7.4.2.
589
590

591 3.2 Factors affecting EW efficiency

592 The efficiency with which EW generates alkalinity and climate-relevant CDR depends on a suite
593 of interacting physical, chemical, and biological controls that regulate mineral dissolution and the
594 subsequent fate of weathering products. Across environments, dissolution rates translate to CDR
595 potential and are governed jointly by intrinsic mineral properties (e.g., mineralogy, surface area,
596 and application amount) and extrinsic environmental conditions, including soil chemistry,
597 hydrology, and climate (see Figure 3).62 These controls are highly coupled and operate across
598 spatial scales--from mineral surfaces to catchments--often interacting nonlinearly such that
599 weathering responses cannot be predicted from any single factor in isolation.116
600
601 A useful conceptual starting point for interpreting EW is the distinction between supply-limited
602 and weathering-limited behaviour in natural weathering systems. In natural settings with low
603 erosion or mineral replenishment rates, chemical weathering fluxes can be constrained by the
604 supply of fresh reactive minerals to the weathering zone, such that fluxes scale with material supply
605 and cannot exceed it.59 Where fresh mineral surfaces are abundant, by contrast, weathering fluxes
606 are controlled more strongly by reaction kinetics and transport processes, including the delivery of
607 undersaturated water to mineral surfaces and the removal of dissolved products from porewaters.59
608 EW can be understood as an intentional shift along this continuum: by adding finely ground
609 silicates, rock flour applications aim to move systems away from supply limitation toward
610 weathering-limited behaviour, where dissolution rates are governed primarily by kinetics and
611 transport rather than by scarcity of reactive surface area. Once reactive feedstock is present,
612 realised dissolution and CDR may instead be governed by kinetic and transport limitations,
613 including soil hydrology, climate, porewater residence time, saturation state, mineral accessibility,
614 secondary mineral formation, and surface passivation.
615
616 The following subsections synthesize current knowledge on the dominant factors controlling EW
617 efficiency and highlight major sources of uncertainty that complicate prediction across sites and
618 timescales. Separated into primary feedstock characteristics, soil geochemistry, and
619 hydrology/climate, this section provides a high-level overview of how these processes regulate
620 EW outcomes while also identifying key conceptual, methodological, and reporting challenges in
621 the current literature. Emphasis is placed on the coupled roles of feedstock characteristics, grain
622 size and surface area, soil chemical environment, and hydroclimatic conditions, as well as on how
623 their interactions shape both dissolution dynamics and downstream alkalinity export.
624
625

                                                                  17
Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

631

632 3.2.1 Feedstock properties and application

633

634 3.2.1.1 Feedstock composition
635 Feedstock type and composition is a primary determinant of EW performance because mineral
636 composition governs dissolution kinetics, theoretical CDR potential, and the release of both
637 beneficial and potentially harmful elements into soils.15,62 This section reviews how feedstock
638 properties shape outcomes, covering composition and major feedstock classes, mineralogical
639 controls on weathering rates, compositional controls on CDR potential, materials used in existing
640 studies, commercial availability, pollution risks, and key reporting and comparability challenges
641 in the literature.
642
643

                     18
         Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

644 3.2.1.1.1 Mineralogical control on weathering rates

645 Weathering rates of silicate minerals vary systematically as a function of both thermodynamic
646 stability and dissolution kinetics, making mineralogy a primary control on EW performance.117­
647 121 This relationship is classically described by the Goldich stability series, in which minerals that
648 crystallize at high temperature (e.g., olivine, pyroxenes, Ca-plagioclase) are typically least stable
649 at Earth's surface and weather most rapidly, whereas low-temperature minerals such as K-feldspar
650 are more resistant. Consistent with this framework, the major base-cation­bearing minerals that
651 dominate mafic and ultramafic rocks--particularly olivine (forsterite Mg2SiO4­fayalite Fe2SiO4),
652 pyroxenes (e.g., diopside CaMgSi2O6), and plagioclase feldspars (albite, NaAlSi3O8­anorthite,
653 CaAl2Si2O8)--span orders-of-magnitude differences in dissolution rates under comparable
654 conditions.119,120,122,123
655
656 Silicate minerals commonly form solid-solution series in which elemental substitutions produce
657 continuous compositional variation within a fixed crystal structure.124 These compositions are
658 conventionally expressed as proportions of end members--for example, olivine compositions are
659 reported as Fo (percentage forsterite component), while plagioclase compositions are expressed
660 as An (percentage anorthite component), reflecting coupled substitutions that preserve charge
661 balance. Such compositional variability may occur even within single grains (zoning), reflecting
662 evolving magma chemistry during crystallization and leading to grain-scale heterogeneity in
663 reactivity.124 Mineral proportions also vary among rock types: dunite consists of ~90­100%
664 olivine, whereas basalt typically contains mixtures of pyroxene and plagioclase with variable
665 olivine (often zero). These differences translate directly into differences in weathering behaviour
666 and CDR potential among nominally similar lithologies, with measured basalts showing up to six-
667 fold variation in modelled CDR due largely to variations in reactive mineral content such as olivine
668 and augite.125 Additional variability arises from the proportion of amorphous phases (e.g., glass),
669 which can dissolve as fast as or faster than crystalline silicates depending on composition and pH,
670 producing especially rapid early-stage weathering.123
671
672 Accessory minerals may further influence reactivity and environmental behaviour. Mafic and
673 ultramafic rocks commonly contain Fe­Ti oxides, and olivine-rich lithologies such as dunite often
674 contain chromite (FeCr2O4). Chromite is resistant to chemical weathering and tends to persist as a
675 residual mineral that can be concentrated by sedimentary processes, illustrating that not all mineral
676 phases contribute equally to dissolution reactions. Beyond igneous feedstocks, the Ca-silicate
677 mineral wollastonite (CaSiO3), typically formed by contact metamorphism of impure limestones,
678 is also of interest for EW because of its rapid dissolution rate (log rate = -6.97 mol m2 s1),
679 comparable to that of forsterite (log rate = -7.16 mol m2 s1).118,124
680

681 3.2.1.1.2 Feedstock CDR potential
682 The maximum theoretical CDR capacity of a feedstock is determined by its bulk elemental
683 composition, because the availability of reactive base cations governs how much CO2 can
684 potentially be converted into bicarbonate or carbonate during weathering. This potential is
685 commonly estimated using the modified Steinour formulation, which calculates stoichiometric
686 CO2 uptake directly from oxide concentrations (e.g., CaO, MgO, Na2O, K2O) while accounting
687 for species that reduce effective capacity, such as sulfur- or phosphorus-bearing phases.18,37,126
688 Because the equation depends explicitly on bulk chemistry, feedstocks richer in Ca- and Mg-
689 bearing silicates exhibit higher theoretical CDR capacities, whereas materials dominated by less

                                                                  19
     Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

690 reactive or non-alkalinity-generating components yield lower values. For EW, the Steinour-based

691 formulation to estimate a feedstock's CDR potential can be written as:

692

693  !78&'(  =  *)*!  ;<      !4"   +  =  *+"   +    ?4!"   +    A!"   +    ,""   +    D!"1   E  10"  6
                100           *)+*        *,-*       *.+!*       */!*       *0*"       *2!*#

694

695 where oxide concentrations are expressed in weight percent, Mx denotes molar masses, coefficients

696 (, , , , , and ) represent the relative alkalinity contribution of each oxide (, , ,  are 1 for

697 typical surface water pH ranges, while  and  are negative representing reduction in CDR

698 potential), and  is the molar CO2-to-divalent-cation ratio. While idealized silicate weathering

699 implies  = 2, buffering in the carbonate system reduces this to ~1.4­1.7 under typical seawater

700 conditions; a value of 1.5 is commonly adopted as a conservative global average that accounts for
701 thermodynamically driven degassing of CO2 in the oceans.126 The same formulation shows

702 explicitly that sulfur- and phosphorus-bearing phases (e.g., apatite, Ca5(PO4)3(OH)) reduce

703 effective CDR capacity because their dissolution either does not consume CO2 or may generate
704 acidity that releases it.126

705

706 Where feedstocks contain carbonate minerals, additional care is required because carbonate

707 dissolution generates alkalinity with different atmospheric CO2 uptake stoichiometry: dissolution

708 of CaCO3 by carbonic acid produces two equivalents of alkalinity, but only one of the two DIC

709 equivalents is derived from atmospheric CO2, while the other originates from the carbonate

710 mineral lattice. Consequently, carbonate-derived CaO and MgO should not be treated identically

711 to silicate-derived oxide contributions when estimating theoretical CDR capacity. Carbonate-

712 derived Ca and Mg correspond to only half the atmospheric CO2 uptake implied by the same

713 divalent cation release from silicate minerals. Where nominally silicate feedstocks contain trace

714 or minor carbonate phases, this contribution to CaO and MgO concentrations should therefore be

715 quantified separately or corrected for.

716

717 Because this formulation is purely based on rock composition, it defines a theoretical upper bound

718 on CDR set by feedstock chemistry alone under the implicit assumption that all weathering occurs

719 with carbonic acid. Differences in CaO and MgO content explain large contrasts in potential

720 between lithologies (e.g., dunite versus basalt)15,23 and substantial variability among basalts due to
721 differing proportions of reactive minerals.125,127 Mineral dissolution kinetics, together with

722 negative feedbacks in soils, will then determine how quickly a given feedstock CDR potential is

723 realized via weathering.

724

725

726 3.2.1.1.3 Rocks used in EW studies

727 The literature shows a clear preference for mafic and ultramafic silicate rocks in CDR-focused EW

728 (Figure 4), reflecting their favourable balance of reactivity, availability, and chemical composition.
729 Basalt is the single most widely used feedstock (n = 86), largely because it is abundant, widely

730 quarried, and contains reactive Ca- and Mg-bearing minerals that support alkalinity generation and
731 nutrient release.15,128 Nevertheless, basalts vary substantially in weathering rate and CDR potential
732 depending on mineralogy and glass content, meaning that not all basalts perform equally.125,127
733

                                                     20
         Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

734 Olivine-rich ultramafic rocks are often considered among the most effective feedstocks because
735 they combine high theoretical CDR capacity with rapid dissolution kinetics.23,122 However, their
736 geographic availability is more limited and they can contain elevated trace metal concentrations
737 that require additional risk assessment.129,130 Wollastonite represents a highly reactive Ca-silicate
738 alternative with fast dissolution and typically low heavy-metal contents, though deposits are
739 comparatively scarce and some sources may contain asbestos phases that must be screened.131
740
741 Carbonate materials, while abundant, rapidly dissolving, and widely used for soil pH
742 management,29 differ fundamentally from silicates in their atmospheric CO2 uptake stoichiometry.
743 Carbonic-acid dissolution of carbonates generates only half as much atmospheric CO2 uptake per
744 Ca or Mg released to solution as equivalent silicate dissolution, because one carbon equivalent
745 originates from the carbonate mineral lattice. In the presence of strong acids, carbonate dissolution
746 may release carbonate-derived CO2 before longer-term alkalinity effects are realised. Although
747 carbonate materials can be used in EW contexts, they are not a central focus of this review,
748 reflecting both their distinct carbon-accounting considerations and the limited amount of work that
749 has focused explicitly on carbonate-based EW to date.30,31,87
750
751 Industrial waste streams, such as mine tailings or concrete-derived materials, were included only
752 where their application was discussed in the context of agricultural soil amendments.
753 Consequently, the counts reported here do not include the broader literature on ambient weathering
754 or carbonation of mine tailings, waste piles, and similar materials outside agricultural application
755 contexts.
756
757

758
759 Figure 4: Distribution of feedstock types used in Enhanced Weathering (EW) and rock flour studies. Counts show the
760 number of unique studies reporting each feedstock class, separated into (a) EW and (b) agronomic rock flour
761 applications. Mafic rocks--primarily basalt--are the most frequently used materials in EW studies (n = 86), followed
762 by ultramafic rocks such as olivine-rich lithologies (n = 53), whereas fertiliser-focused studies more often employ
763 felsic materials (n = 26) alongside mafic rocks (n = 23). Less commonly reported categories include wollastonite,
764 industrial alkaline wastes, intermediate compositions, and other feedstocks. Counts reflect one entry per feedstock per
765 study.

766
767

                                                                  21
         Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

768 Overall, feedstock choice reflects trade-offs among reactivity, theoretical CDR capacity,
769 availability, agronomic co-benefits, and environmental constraints, and these trade-offs explain
770 the dominance of basaltic materials in EW research (Figure 4). In contrast, agronomic rock flour
771 studies show a broader distribution of feedstock types, including more felsic materials, reflecting
772 goals centred on soil amendment and nutrient supply rather than maximising CDR. Note that this
773 categorisation should be viewed as an approximate representation of the literature: it reflects both
774 inconsistencies and misclassifications in the primary studies themselves (see Section 3.2.1.1.6
775 below),132 as well as the necessary simplifications introduced here when mapping reported
776 feedstocks into broad categories.
777
778

779 3.2.1.1.4 Commercial availability of main feedstock types

780 Assessing commercial availability is essential for evaluating the scalability of EW. Even
781 feedstocks with high theoretical CDR potential are largely irrelevant on large scales if extraction
782 volumes, permitting constraints, infrastructure, or competing markets limit supply. Thus, estimates
783 of technical CDR potential must be interpreted in light of real-world production capacity and
784 constraints on capacity expansion, reserve definitions, and market structure.133
785
786 Determining the availability of suitable rock types is, however, complicated by inconsistent
787 nomenclature and reporting practices. The most accessible production statistics are compiled by
788 the United States Geological Survey (USGS) National Minerals Information Center through the
789 Mineral Commodity Summaries and Minerals Yearbook,134 and by the British Geological Survey
790 (BGS).135 These sources provide annual production figures, and in some cases estimates of
791 resources and reserves, but typically combine lithologies into broad industrial categories. Brazil
792 and other countries also publish national statistics, which are consulted by the USGS and the BGS.
793
794 Care must be taken in interpreting these statistics, particularly regarding the terms `resource',
795 `reserve base', and `reserves', which have strict technical definitions established by the USGS.136
796 A resource refers to a concentration of material for which economic extraction is currently or
797 potentially feasible; the reserve base is the portion meeting defined physical and chemical criteria
798 for mining; and reserves represent the economically extractable part of the reserve base at the time
799 of determination. In investment contexts, reserve estimates are validated under internationally
800 recognised reporting codes (e.g., CRIRSCO, NI-43-101), but such documentation is often not
801 available in the public domain for quarrying operations that supply crushed rock for construction
802 and industrial use. Consequently, data relevant to EW deployments are often inferred from
803 aggregate production statistics rather than from CDR-specific resource assessments.
804
805 For basalt in particular, the distinction between geological abundance and economically accessible
806 supply is critical. Basalt constitutes ~3.5% of the global continental crust,128 but this figure reflects
807 theoretical resource abundance rather than extractable reserves. Reserves are site-specific and
808 constrained by economics and permitting. The concept of permitted reserves--the volume
809 authorised for extraction under regulatory approval137--is especially relevant for deployment
810 planning, because annual production cannot exceed permitted quantities. In the absence of explicit
811 permitted-reserve data, annual production figures provide a conservative lower bound.133 It is also
812 important to recognise that reserve estimates may remain relatively constant over time, as
813 companies continually reclassify proven resources into reserves.138

                                                                  22
     Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

814

815 A further challenge arises from the combination of lithologies within industrial reporting

816 categories. The USGS reports production under "crushed stone," combining limestone and
817 dolomite (~70%), granite (~14%), sandstone (~6%), traprock (~6%), and other lithologies.139

818 Similarly, the BGS combines igneous, metamorphic, and sedimentary rocks within "crushed rock"

819 categories. Consequently, basalt-specific production is rarely reported directly. Nevertheless,

820 disaggregated data from BGS sources can be interpreted to estimate the availability of basic rocks
821 (basalt, dolerite, gabbro, microgabbro) in the UK,133 highlighting both the feasibility and

822 uncertainty of such reconstruction efforts. Commercial estimates of global basalt production

823 address the construction market, including manufactured slabs and basalt fibre, and do not consider

824 EW as a potential demand sector; consequently, projected market sizes do not account for
825 additional demand from CDR deployment.140 The global market value for basalt is reported as 2
826 billion USD,140 which--when converted using the USGS average price for crushed rock (17.5
827 USD t1)--corresponds to an implied production of ~115 Mt yr1. This back-of-the-envelope
828 estimate is likely conservative, as the UK alone produces 14.8 Mt yr1 for construction.133

829
830
