839 Table 1 summarises available production, reserve, and price data for key feedstocks relevant to
840 EW. Crushed stone production in the United States alone exceeds 1.5 billion tonnes annually,139

841 indicating that large-scale material handling infrastructure already exists. In contrast, wollastonite
842 production is ~1­1.2 Mt yr1 globally,141,144 reflecting its specialised industrial use and limiting the

843 scalability of the current product type and market for new uses in EW. Dunite/olivine production
844 is similarly limited, with Norway reporting ~1.4 Mt yr1 and long reserve lifetimes at specific

                                                23
         Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

845 sites.143 The relatively high nominal price of wollastonite compared to crushed rock reflects
846 processing for high-value applications;141 lower-grade material not suitable for fillers may be more
847 appropriate for CDR deployment and is likely not reported in published production (or price)
848 statistics.
849
850 Overall, these data illustrate that basaltic materials benefit from existing large-scale quarrying and
851 distribution systems that support scalability, whereas high-reactivity endmembers such as olivine-
852 rich dunite and wollastonite are more constrained by geographically limited production and
853 specialised markets. Importantly, current statistical reporting frameworks were not designed with
854 CDR in mind, and improved lithology-specific production reporting would significantly enhance
855 assessments of EW scalability. Although these figures clearly show that human society already
856 mobilises Gt-scale rock fluxes annually,139 large-scale EW scenarios would need to operate
857 within--or substantially expand--this existing extraction envelope. This implies that deployment
858 trajectories will be governed as much by mining infrastructure, permitting timelines, and land-use
859 constraints as by mineral reactivity or theoretical CDR capacity. Given how fundamental feedstock
860 availability is for scaling EW, it is striking that such basic data as lithology-specific global
861 production (e.g., basalt output) are rarely reported, a gap that complicates near-term deployment
862 estimates and represents a clear priority for future data collection and resource assessments.
863
864

865 3.2.1.1.5 Pollution concerns
866 Trace elements present in silicate feedstocks--particularly Cr and Ni--are frequently discussed as
867 potential risks for EW because they can accumulate in soils if added repeatedly at large application
868 rates (cf., Section 3.5).129,131 However, bulk concentrations alone are not sufficient to assess
869 environmental hazard, as risk depends strongly on mineralogical host phases, dissolution kinetics,
870 and resulting bioavailability. For example, chromium in mafic and ultramafic rocks is typically
871 present as trivalent Cr(III) in chromite (FeCr2O4), whereas the toxic hexavalent form Cr(VI) is not
872 a primary constituent of such minerals. Chromite is also resistant to weathering and commonly
873 persists as a residual phase in soils derived from mechanically weathered parent materials. Ni
874 behaves differently: it commonly substitutes for Mg in olivine and can therefore be released during
875 dissolution of olivine-rich lithologies, a process reflected in the occurrence of metallophyte plant
876 communities adapted to naturally Ni-rich substrates.148 Both Cr and Ni can function as
877 micronutrients at low concentrations, and Cr deficiency has been reported in human diets,149
878 underscoring that toxicity depends on dose and speciation rather than simple presence.
879
880 Evaluation of potential contamination must also consider natural background concentrations in
881 soils. Reported baseline levels vary widely depending on parent material.150 For example, soils in
882 eastern England average ~68.5 mg kg1 Cr and ~22.5 mg kg1 Ni, whereas soils developed on
883 volcanic or metamorphic rocks in northern Britain can contain substantially higher
884 concentrations.151 By comparison, basalt feedstocks proposed for CDR have been reported to
885 contain roughly 35­350 mg kg1 Cr and 18­195 mg kg1 Ni, values that fall within the lower
886 portion of ranges observed in natural soils.125,150 Moreover, groundwater drawn from basalt
887 aquifers supplies millions of people in parts of Brazil without documented adverse health
888 effects.152 These comparisons highlight that natural geochemical variability is large and must be
889 considered when interpreting feedstock composition.
890

                                                                  24
         Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

891 Nonetheless, trace-element accumulation remains a legitimate concern under sustained application
892 scenarios. Modelling indicates that repeated additions of feedstock could exceed regulatory
893 thresholds for certain elements over decadal timescales depending on feedstock composition and
894 soil properties, particularly where weathering releases metals faster than they are removed by
895 leaching or plant uptake.129 It is also important to recognise that existing regulatory limits for
896 agricultural amendments were largely developed for relatively fast-dissolving materials (e.g.,
897 soluble fertilisers or liming products) and are typically based on short-term contaminant release
898 fluxes.129,131 EW feedstocks, in contrast, often dissolve more slowly and release trace elements
899 over longer time horizons, meaning that both the temporal dynamics of release and cumulative
900 loading need to be explicitly evaluated. In addition, pH increases or secondary phases may
901 temporarily immobilise trace elements, with the potential for later remobilisation if soil conditions
902 change (e.g., acidification following cessation of application), emphasizing the importance of
903 long-term monitoring and risk assessment.
904
905 Beyond trace metals, mineralogical contaminants must also be considered. Asbestiform minerals
906 may occur in some mafic and especially ultramafic rocks and pose inhalation hazards if present,
907 as documented for residues from asbestos mining.153 Screening for such phases is therefore
908 standard practice in industrial mineral supply chains. In contrast, natural radioactivity is generally
909 low in mafic and ultramafic rocks because their concentrations of K, U, and Th are typically much
910 lower than those of felsic lithologies, which are more commonly associated with Rn emissions.154
911
912 Overall, available evidence suggests that potential contamination risks from EW feedstocks will
913 be highly context-dependent. Risk will vary with mineralogy, weathering rate, soil chemistry, and
914 background geochemistry, meaning that it cannot be inferred from composition alone. A balanced
915 interpretation is therefore that some high-reactivity feedstocks may pose elevated trace-element
916 concerns under certain conditions,130 but these risks must be evaluated and managed through
917 appropriate feedstock characterization, site selection, application limits, and monitoring
918 strategies.129,131
919
920

921 3.2.1.1.6 Challenges in the EW literature
922 A persistent weakness in the EW literature is the inadequate and inconsistent characterization of
923 feedstocks,132 particularly mafic rocks described as "basalt," despite mineral composition being a
924 primary control on both theoretical CDR capacity and reaction kinetics.18,125,126 Classification
925 schemes established by the International Union of Geological Sciences (IUGS) provide clear
926 protocols for naming igneous rocks based on bulk chemistry,155,156 yet these standards are not
927 consistently applied. In the literature assessed here, only 33 publications use the terms basalt,
928 dolerite (diabase), or gabbro for feedstocks that are texturally distinct but compositionally related
929 mafic rocks. However, correct naming requires more than nominal similarity: it depends on
930 analytically robust major-element data and adherence to established classification boundaries.
931
932 Evaluation of 56 published bulk chemical analyses from 33 publications in our database reveals
933 substantial deficiencies in reporting and analytical closure. Only 34 analyses provide an explicit
934 analytical total, and of these, just 16 fall within the acceptable 99­101% range (Figure 5a). Exact
935 totals of 100% strongly suggest post-analytical normalisation without reporting that this has been
936 done, precluding assessment of data quality and potentially biasing subsequent calculations of

                                                                  25
     Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

937 weathering potential. When analytical totals are recalculated from reported oxide concentrations,
938 values span 79.4­103.1% (Figure 5c), indicating either analytical problems, incomplete reporting
939 (e.g., omission of volatile components), or mischaracterization of altered materials as pristine
940 igneous rocks. Some of the lowest totals occur in materials described as basalt but known to
941 contain substantial amounts of secondary hydrated minerals such as clinochlore,157,158
942 demonstrating how alteration can confound both classification and CDR potential estimates.
943
944

953
954
955 Loss on ignition (LOI) is particularly critical in this context because it quantifies volatile
956 components, including structurally bound water and CO2 in secondary minerals. The IUGS
957 volcanic rock classification requires LOI values below 2 wt% for reliable normalisation and
958 classification.155 Yet only 32 of 56 analyses report LOI (Figure 5b), and reported values range up
959 to 13.21 wt%, indicating significant alteration in some samples. Among EW studies, 20 analyses
960 report LOI <2 wt%, but many do not, and in several cases LOI is entirely omitted. Elevated LOI
961 values imply that Ca and Mg may be partly hosted in hydrated or carbonated phases rather than

                                  26
         Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

962 primary silicates, necessitating correction before applying stoichiometric approaches such as the
963 Steinour equation. Without such correction, CDR capacity can be systematically overestimated.
964
965 A particularly important case is the presence of calcite or other carbonate minerals in nominally
966 basaltic feedstocks. Even trace carbonate phases can dissolve much faster than primary basaltic
967 silicates and may dominate early Ca release in experiments or field deployments. Without explicit
968 carbonate quantification, rapid initial Ca mobilisation could therefore be misinterpreted as fast
969 basalt weathering, biasing estimates of silicate dissolution rates and theoretical CDR potential (cf.,
970 also section 3.2.1.1.1). Carbonate contents should therefore be quantified separately, for example
971 through LOI, X-ray diffraction, or selective dissolution approaches, before applying stoichiometric
972 CDR calculations to basaltic feedstocks.
973
974

975
976 Figure 6: Total alkali­silica (TAS) diagram showing the compositions of samples reported as basalt with loss on
977 ignition (LOI) < 2 wt%. Major-element data are plotted as Na2O + K2O versus SiO2 (wt%), and volcanic rock-type
978 fields and boundaries follow the combined Le Maitre classification scheme as implemented in the pyrolite Python
979 plotting template. Points denote individual analyses, and field labels indicate standard TAS rock names.
980
981
982 When analyses with LOI <2 wt% are normalised and plotted on a Total Alkali­Silica (TAS)
983 diagram, most samples fall within basalt or basanite fields (Figure 5, Figure 6), indicating that
984 classification is generally straightforward and appears to be often correct where adequate data
985 exist. However, only 23 samples meet the minimum requirements for such classification and are
986 plotted in Figure 6. For the remaining majority, available data preclude rigorous application of
987 IUGS criteria, and the rocks cannot be named based on the information provided by the authors.
988 Even if one considers SiO2 content alone, as shown in Figure 5d, reported compositions cluster

                                                                  27
      Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

 989  across basaltic and basanitic ranges, with roughly equal numbers corresponding to basalt and
 990  basanite. Despite this, only a small fraction of studies explicitly identify their materials as basanite
 991  (e.g., ref. 159), suggesting that formal classification boundaries are rarely considered. This
 992  inconsistency hampers comparability across studies and obscures potentially important differences
 993  in alkalinity content and weathering behaviour.
 994
 995  These issues extend beyond terminology. Recent critiques have highlighted that EW experiments
 996  frequently rely on a narrow set of commercially available materials, often selected for convenience
 997  and lacking sufficient petrographic characterisation.132 Mineralogical information is commonly
 998  limited to bulk oxide chemistry or qualitative phase identification, whereas dissolution rates vary
 999  by orders of magnitude among minerals within the same compositional group.123,160 Without
1000  detailed mineralogical and microtextural characterization, including identification of secondary
1001  phases and alteration features, experimental results cannot be reliably generalized or used to
1002  constrain reactive transport models (RTMs).161
1003
1004  Similar concerns apply to ultramafic feedstocks described as dunite. By definition, dunite should
1005  contain 90% olivine and minimal volatile content; however, reported LOI values as high as 6.48
1006  wt% indicate substantial serpentinization or other secondary alteration.162 From a petrological
1007  perspective, such materials cannot be considered pristine dunite, and their weathering behaviour
1008  will differ accordingly. Where artificial or industrial by-products are used, strict petrological
1009  nomenclature is less relevant, but rigorous chemical and mineralogical characterization remains
1010  essential to support quantitative modelling of CDR.
1011
1012  Taken together, Figure 5 and Figure 6 demonstrate that more than half of the published
1013  compositional datasets lack sufficient information for reliable classification under established
1014  IUGS standards. The required analytical approaches--complete major-element analyses, reporting
1015  of analytical totals and LOI, and formal classification using TAS criteria--are long established
1016  and straightforward. Without their consistent application, subsequent modelling, upscaling, and
1017  MRV efforts rest on uncertain geochemical foundations. Greater integration of petrological
1018  expertise into EW research is therefore essential to ensure that feedstocks are accurately described
1019  and that estimates of CDR potential are robust and comparable across studies. Beyond
1020  characterization, limited reporting on feedstock production pathways, accessible reserves,
1021  expansion constraints, permitting requirements, and realistic development timelines represents an
1022  additional potential bottleneck for assessing EW scalability.
1023
1024  3.2.1.2 Feedstock application amount
      Feedstock application amount in EW sets the inventory of reactive mineral (and thus potential
1025  reactive surface area) available to dissolve and generate alkalinity, release base cations, and shift
      soil chemistry--often increasing shifts in pH, cation exchange capacity (CEC), nutrient
1026  availability, and CDR yields as dose increases.75,163­167 However, these responses are frequently
1027  non-linear: (i) agronomic and soil physical benefits can show optima or diminishing returns (e.g.,
1028  lower yields at very high doses; plateauing CEC/base cation responses beyond ~10­25 t ha-
1029  1),164,168­170 (ii) dissolution per unit applied can decline at higher doses--consistent with negative
1030  feedbacks in soils (e.g., saturation effects and/or secondary phase formation reducing weathering
1031  efficiency),75,161,171,172 and (iii) higher doses can impair soil physical/hydrological function in some
1032
1033
1034

      28
      Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

1035  contexts (e.g., pore clogging, reduced gas transport), while showing mixed or minimal effects in
1036  others.173 Application rate also affects detectability and transfer of the weathering signal beyond
1037  soils (e.g., alkalinity transfer) and can influence whether field/mesocosm studies observe a
1038  measurable chemical weathering signal at all.174,175
1039
1040  Additionally, dose interacts with material and environment: experiments explicitly comparing low
1041  vs. high application rates (or multiple doses) show rate-dependent changes in weathering/nutrient
1042  uptake, soil inorganic carbon (SIC) accumulation, and sometimes phytotoxicity or contaminant
1043  mobilisation risks,170,176­180 while models similarly suggest application rate can independently
1044  change both total CDR and CDR efficiency (CDR per mass of feedstock applied).75 Finally,
1045  application amount also determines the total load of contaminants (e.g., heavy metals) introduced
1046  to soils, such that high cumulative inputs can approach or exceed regulatory thresholds and
1047  increase risks of ecological or agronomic harm.129,131
1048
      3.2.1.2.1 Implementation and challenges in primary literature
1049
      Across the compiled literature (98 unique experimental studies; 234 unique application-rate
1050  datapoints restricted to field and laboratory work; Figure 7), two distinct practice patterns emerge
1051  depending on whether studies are framed as EW or as rock flour used for agronomic fertilisation.
1052  In EW-focused studies (44 studies; 79 datapoints), application amounts are generally high and
1053  often substantially exceed typical agronomic fertilisation rates. For EW, the median application
1054  amount is 74 t ha1 (Interquartile Range, IQR, 20­153 t ha1; total range 0.05­125,000 t ha1).
1055  Field studies tend to apply lower rates than laboratory experiments: field medians are 20 t ha1
1056  (IQR ~6­100 t ha1; total range 1.24­125,000 t ha1), whereas laboratory studies cluster around
1057  100 t ha1 (IQR 50­200 t ha1; total range 0.05­500 t ha1). In other words, EW laboratory
1058  experiments typically test substantially higher loadings than field trials, and the upper end of the
1059  EW distribution includes very large, sometimes extreme experimental doses. In contrast, studies
1060  treating rock flour primarily as a fertiliser or soil amendment (54 studies; 155 datapoints) apply
1061  markedly lower amounts: the median is 5 t ha1 (IQR ~1­20 t ha1; total range 0.001­300 t ha1).
1062  Field-based fertiliser studies use the lowest rates, with a median of 2 t ha1 (IQR ~0.2­5 t ha1;
1063  total range 0.001­50 t ha1), while laboratory fertiliser experiments apply higher amounts (median
1064  10 t ha1, IQR ~3­28 t ha1; total range 0.01­300 t ha1). In both cases, these remain far below
1065  typical loadings in EW laboratory studies.
1066
1067  A recurring challenge in the literature is the inconsistent and sometimes incomplete reporting of
1068  application amounts, which complicates cross-study comparison and synthesis. In laboratory and
1069  mesocosm experiments in particular, feedstock additions are often reported only as mass per
1070  treatment (e.g., grams per pot) without sufficient information on surface area, soil depth, bulk
1071  density, or experimental footprint, making it difficult to derive area-normalised application rates
1072  (t ha1). Some studies report applied rock volumes without providing material densities, preventing
1073  conversion to mass-based metrics, while others describe repeated applications without clearly
1074  specifying frequency, duration, or cumulative totals. These reporting gaps hinder quantitative
1075  comparison and meta-analysis. To improve comparability and reproducibility, studies should
1076  always report (i) area-normalised application amounts (t ha1) alongside mass per treatment, (ii)
1077  material density, (iii) soil depth and experimental dimensions, and (iv) clearly stated application
1078  frequency and cumulative total applied mass over the full study period. Consistent reporting of
1079

      29
      Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

1080  these parameters would substantially strengthen synthesis efforts and the interpretation of dose­
1081  response relationships in EW research.
1082
1083

1084  Figure 7: Distribution of total (cumulative) feedstock application amounts reported in field and laboratory studies,
1085  separated by study framing: Enhanced Weathering (EW) vs. agronomic fertiliser use. Each occurrence represents one
1086  unique value per unique study, where some were calculated from given application amounts and experimental
1087  dimensions. Enhanced Weathering studies (blue tones) generally apply substantially higher amounts than fertiliser-
1088  focused studies (green tones). In both research domains, laboratory experiments tend to use higher application rates
1089  than field trials, with medians of ~100 vs. 20 t ha1 for EW and ~10 vs. 2 t ha1 for fertiliser studies. These patterns
1090  highlight systematic differences in experimental design and objectives between CDR-focused and agronomic research
1091  traditions.
1092
      3.2.1.3 Grain size and surface area
1093  One of the most important factors influencing EW outcomes--and a defining feature
1094  distinguishing it from natural weathering--is the fine particle size of applied rock materials.
      Particle (or grain) size and the associated reactive surface area exert a fundamental control on
1095  mineral dissolution and therefore represent key parameters in efforts to accelerate mineral
      reactivity and CDR. Finer particles generally have higher specific surface area (SSA, in m2 g-1),
1096  promoting fluid­solid interaction and faster dissolution kinetics, a relationship widely documented
1097  across laboratory experiments, natural weathering systems, and theoretical rate formulations.181­
1098  183 Thus, grain size is primarily a proxy for SSA in EW studies. Because of this mechanistic link,
1099  surface area is commonly used to normalise dissolution rates and evaluate the reactivity of
1100  candidate feedstocks in EW studies (e.g., refs. 125,184,185).
1101
1102  Surface area can be determined either experimentally or by geometric estimation. The most
1103  commonly reported experimental measure is SSA determined using the Brunauer­Emmett­Teller
1104  (BET) method, which derives SSA from gas adsorption isotherms and accounts for both external
1105  particle surfaces and internal features such as surface roughness, pores and microfractures.186­188
1106  N2 is most frequently used as the adsorbate due to its availability and suitability for a wide range
1107
1108
1109
1110
1111

      30
      Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

1112  of solids, although alternative gases such as krypton or argon may be employed depending on
1113  surface characteristics.189­191 BET measurements provide a comprehensive representation of
1114  surface complexity, but results may vary substantially due to differences in adsorbate gas, sample
1115  preparation, analytical conditions and inter-laboratory procedures.188,192,193
1116
1117  In contrast, geometric surface area (GSA) is calculated from particle size data assuming idealized
1118  particle shapes, typically spherical or cubic, and smooth surfaces.194­196 Particle size information
1119  is commonly derived from laser diffraction or sieving techniques and may be reported in diverse
1120  formats, including mean or median diameters, minimum-maximum ranges or percentile-based size
1121  distributions. This heterogeneity in reported grain size metrics complicates direct comparison
1122  across studies and requires pragmatic assumptions to derive representative particle diameters.197­
1123  199 Representative diameters can be estimated using geometric means of size bounds or percentile
1124  bins following established particle technology guidelines,200­202 allowing GSA to be calculated
1125  consistently across datasets when particle density and shape are assumed or known.
1126
1127  While GSA offers a reproducible and transparent approach that is less sensitive to analytical
1128  variability and sample ageing,193,203 it systematically underestimates total SSA because it neglects
1129  surface roughness, porosity and microfracturing.181,194 Consequently, GSA values are typically one
1130  to two orders of magnitude lower than BET-derived SSA, a discrepancy widely reported in
1131  dissolution studies (e.g., refs. 183,184,188,204). Conversely, BET measurements may overestimate the
1132  truly reactive surface by including mineralogically non-reactive surface area (i.e., co-mingled
1133  phases not susceptible to acidic dissolution) and micropores not accessible to fluids.193,194,205
1134  Neither approach is universally optimal, and the choice of surface area normalisation depends on
1135  data availability, study objectives, and the balance between physical realism and methodological
1136  consistency.
1137
1138  The divergence between BET and GSA surface areas is often conceptualized using a surface
1139  roughness factor, defined as the ratio between measured BET surface area and geometrically
1140  estimated surface area.125,206,207 This provides a first-order indication of the extent to which real
1141  mineral surfaces depart from idealized particle geometries. In practice, however, studies report
1142  grain size and surface area using a wide range of metrics, measurement approaches, and derived
1143  quantities that are not directly comparable and may reflect fundamentally different physical
1144  properties of the material. This heterogeneity--in both what is reported and what those quantities
1145  represent mechanistically--poses a substantial challenge for synthesizing EW datasets and
1146  interpreting cross-study trends. The implications of this variability for evaluating reported CDR
1147  rates are examined in Section 5.1.2.
1148
1149  Importantly, neither particle size nor measured surface area alone uniquely determines dissolution
1150  behaviour. Experimental and modelling studies show that the sensitivity of weathering rates to
1151  grain size depends strongly on environmental context, including solution chemistry, pCO2,
1152  hydrologic conditions, and secondary mineral formation.65,184,208 This reflects the fact that reactive
1153  surface area interacts with transport processes and evolving porewater chemistry, such that grain
1154  size acts as a first-order control on intrinsic reactivity but does not independently predict realised
1155  weathering fluxes across natural systems.
1156

      31
      Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

1157  Finally, because specific surface area is fundamentally determined by the degree of comminution,
1158  particle size also links mineral reactivity to energetic and logistical constraints. Producing finer
1159  materials generally requires greater comminution energy, increasing costs and potentially reducing
1160  net CDR under some system boundaries.23,209,210 As a result, deployment strategies must balance
1161  the kinetic advantages of fine particles against the practical considerations of processing intensity
1162  and scalability. The implications of grain size for observed CDR rates across experimental, field,
1163  and modelling studies are examined in detail in Section 5.1.2.
1164
1165  3.2.1.3.1 Implementation and challenges in primary literature
      Across the literature compiled here, primary studies implement EW using a broad range of particle
1166  sizes and reporting conventions (Figure 8). Mean and median diameters in laboratory and
      modelling studies typically fall in the tens of micrometres, while higher percentiles (p80, p90, p97)
1167  often extend into the hundreds of micrometres and, in some fertiliser-derived field datasets, into
1168  the millimetre range. Surface area reporting shows similarly wide variability (Figure 9). Brunauer­
1169  Emmett­Teller SSA most commonly cluster between ~1 and 15 m2 g1 but span several orders of
1170  magnitude, while GSA values are reported far less frequently and are typically one to two orders
1171  of magnitude lower. Laboratory and field studies dominate BET reporting, whereas GSA is most
1172  often used in modelling contexts, reflecting the practicality of deriving GSA from assumed particle
1173  diameters.
1174
1175  We also note that particle-size distributions are reported using an exceptionally wide range of
1176  metrics across the EW literature, substantially complicating cross-study comparison. Across the
1177  compiled studies, grain size is described using >80 distinct measures or reporting conventions
1178  spanning several fundamentally different representation types. These include (i) continuous
1179  distribution descriptors, such as mean diameter and percentile diameters (e.g., p10­p97),
1180  represented by 8 distinct metrics; (ii) cumulative mass fractions, reporting the proportion of
1181  material finer or coarser than specified size thresholds, represented by 20 distinct cutoff
1182  definitions; and (iii) discrete size-class mass fractions, where material is partitioned into explicit
1183  size intervals (e.g., sieve or laser-diffraction classes), comprising 43 distinct bin definitions and
1184  representing the most heterogeneous reporting category. Additional reporting modes include
1185  aggregated textural classes (sand, silt, clay, and gravel), size-range or extrema descriptors
1186  (minimum and maximum grain sizes and reported size ranges), and hybrid or method-specific
1187  composite classes that reflect analytical or application-driven grouping choices rather than
1188  standardized particle-size conventions.
1189
1190  This diversity in grain-size reporting makes systematic comparison across studies challenging,
1191  particularly because particle size is ultimately used as a proxy for reactive surface area, rather than
1192  a direct controlling variable. Compounding this issue, many studies do not report measured BET
1193  surface area or even calculated GSA, limiting the ability to directly relate reported grain size to
1194  weathering kinetics or CDR outcomes. Together, these factors highlight the need for more
1195  standardised grain-size reporting, alongside more routine measurement or reporting of surface
1196  area, to improve comparability, interpretability, and mechanistic understanding across EW
1197  experiments and models.
1198
1199
1200
1201
1202

      32
      Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

1205  separate EW vs fertiliser studies and are further stratified by primary study type (field, laboratory, and modelling).

      3.2.2 Soil chemistry
1214
1215  The efficiency of EW in soils is strongly regulated by environmental conditions that govern
      mineral dissolution kinetics and CDR efficiency. As extensively reviewed elsewhere,62 pH,
1216  temperature, and saturation state of the soil solution exert first-order controls on both weathering
      rates and the fraction of weathering that results in direct CDR.62,211 Their combined effects define
1217  a constrained set of conditions under which EW can be most effective.
1218
1219  Soil pH is arguably the most critical geochemical variable controlling EW outcomes. Silicate
1220  mineral dissolution rates typically increase under acidic conditions (pH <5.5) and tend to reach a
1221  minimum near circumneutral pH, with some minerals also exhibiting increased dissolution under
1222  highly alkaline conditions (pH >9; Figure 10).123,212 However, highly alkaline soils are rare in
1223  agricultural systems,213 making acidic to circumneutral conditions most relevant for EW.
1224  Importantly, rapid silicate dissolution at low pH does not necessarily translate into direct CO2
1225  sequestration, because additional alkalinity--expressed in its explicit conservative form as a cation
1226  charge surplus89--will not necessarily directly translate into more DIC.214 This concept, known as
1227  alkalinisation carbon-capture efficiency,214 reflects the extent to which added alkalinity increases
1228  the storage of DIC, and depends on the partitioning of DIC between dissolved CO2 (including
1229  H2CO3), HCO3- and CO32-. In soils, this relationship also depends on buffering capacity: where
1230  added alkalinity is consumed by exchange reactions, proton buffering, or dissolution/precipitation
1231  reactions without substantially increasing pH, near-term conversion of weathering-derived
1232  alkalinity into bicarbonate may remain limited; where buffering capacity is lower or becomes
1233  exhausted, weathering-induced pH increases can shift DIC speciation toward bicarbonate and
1234  enhance CO2 uptake.215
1235
1236
1237
1238
1239

      34
      Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

1240  At very acidic pH conditions (pH <5), inorganic carbon exists almost exclusively as dissolved
1241
1242  CO2. Under such strongly acidic conditions, silicate dissolution may initially neutralise acidity
1243
1244  without directly producing substantial bicarbonate, such that near-term CO2 capture per unit
1245  alkalinity generated is low,161 as seen for instance in an Australian trial on acidic tropical soil.216
1246  Conversely, at circumneutral pH, inorganic carbon exists mainly in the form of HCO3-. The same
1247
1248  silicate weathering reaction--generating the same amount of alkalinity--would then convert a
1249  much larger fraction of added alkalinity into bicarbonate ions:214
1250
1251  !4,-"" + %!" + 2 %#  !4!# + %%,-"%             7
1252
1253  !4,-"" + %!" + 2 !"!  !4!# + %%,-"% + 2 %!""$  8
1254
1255  Within this framework, these contrasting tendencies suggest the existence of an optimal pH range
1256  in which moderately acidic conditions combine sufficiently high weathering rates with sufficient
      buffering capacity and carbonate-system conditions to enable substantial CO2 sequestration.

1257  Figure 10: Mineral dissolution kinetics and global cropland soil distribution as a function of pH. Mineral dissolution
1258  rate laws (refs. 119,217) for selected silicate and accessory minerals are shown as a function of solution pH, illustrating
1259
1260  the strong sensitivity of weathering kinetics to soil pH. The shaded curve (right axis) shows the distribution density of
1261  global cropland soil pH derived from gridded global soil datasets.218,219 The area under the distribution between any
1262
      two pH values corresponds to the fraction of cropland soils within that range.

      35
      Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

1263  However, in highly acidic soils where alkalinisation carbon-capture efficiency is close to zero, EW
1264  could still contribute to CDR downstream from the field and potentially much later in time. This
1265  can be conceptualized as a lag time in CDR realisation: weathering in the soil decreases acid export
1266  and releases cations, but atmospheric CO2 uptake is only realised once exported weathering
1267  products encounter higher-pH conditions in rivers or the ocean and carbonate equilibria re-adjust.
1268  As with bicarbonate and cation export more generally, retention of these weathering products
1269  within the field through plant uptake, incorporation into secondary minerals, or adsorption
1270  reactions on SOM, clays, and/or oxides would limit or prevent this downstream CDR.68,92,220
1271
1272  Temperature exerts a fundamental kinetic control on silicate dissolution, with rates generally
1273  increasing exponentially according to Arrhenius-type behaviour when water and mineral supply
1274  are not limiting (see also Section 3.2.3).58,221 Laboratory and soil experiments demonstrate
1275  substantially lower dissolution rates at low temperatures, with differences of up to several orders
1276  of magnitude between <10 °C and >20 °C conditions.222,223 In soils, this kinetic enhancement is
1277  coupled to indirect biological effects: warming increases soil respiration and CO2 production,
1278  potentially enhancing weathering via higher pCO2 and organic acid exudation, but may
1279  simultaneously stimulate organic carbon losses that complicate net carbon accounting.100,224
1280
1281  The saturation state of the soil solution further constrains EW efficiency. As silicate minerals
1282  dissolve, reaction products accumulate in solution, progressively reducing dissolution rates as
1283  thermodynamic equilibrium is approached. Many natural soil solutions are already near saturation
1284  with respect to primary silicate minerals, helping to explain why field weathering rates are often
1285  orders of magnitude lower than laboratory-derived rates.225 Soil texture and structure play a central
1286  role in regulating this process. Poorly drained, compacted, or clay-rich soils restrict solute flushing
1287  and promote saturation,226 while excessively sandy soils limit water retention and mineral contact
1288  time.227 Optimal EW performance might therefore be found in intermediate soil textures that
1289  ensure both adequate water availability for dissolution and sufficient permeability to export solutes
1290  and maintain far-from-equilibrium conditions.
1291
1292  Taken together, these factors underscore that EW efficiency is highly site-specific and will be
1293  contingent upon the alignment of multiple environmental and geochemical parameters. In addition
1294  to regional climatic drivers such as temperature and precipitation, local soil properties--including
1295  pH, texture, structure, and hydrological regime--critically influence weathering dynamics and
1296  CDR potential. Maximizing EW efficiency will therefore require careful site selection and
1297  integrated consideration of climate, soil chemistry, and hydrology, rather than uniform application
1298  across landscapes.
1299
1300  3.2.2.1 Implementation and challenges in the literature
1301  Across the compiled EW literature, baseline pH conditions span predominantly acidic to mildly
      acidic soils, with medians in the range of ~4.8­5.5 depending on extractant (Figure 11). Soil pH
1302  measured in water clusters around moderately acidic values (median  5.5), while CaCl2-based
      measurements are systematically lower (median  4.8), reflecting differences in ionic strength and
1303  exchange equilibria inherent to the extractant chemistry. Porewater and effluent measurements
1304
1305
1306
1307

      36
      Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

1308  exhibit broader variability (median  5.4) with values extending into near-neutral and mildly
1309  alkaline conditions.
1310
1311  These distributions indicate that most EW experiments to date have been conducted in acidic to
1312  weakly acidic agricultural soils, often within the pH range where silicate dissolution kinetics are
1313  enhanced and agronomic lime recommendations are common. This focus is consistent with two
1314  practical considerations. First, acidic soils are widespread in intensively managed croplands,29,228­
1315  230 particularly in humid tropical regions as well as regions with high legacy acidity inputs. Second,
1316  from a geochemical perspective, lower pH conditions increase proton-promoted dissolution of
1317  silicate minerals119,120--but also risk adverse impacts on immediately realised CDR due to strong
1318  acid effects (see also Section 3.4.3).214,231
1319
1320  The comparatively higher porewater and effluent pH data further suggest that EW deployment
1321  frequently occurs in systems where solution chemistry is responsive to mineral inputs and soil
1322  buffering processes. This pattern is consistent with model­experiment comparisons showing that
1323  aqueous pH can increase rapidly following basalt addition, even where bulk soil pH responds more
1324  gradually due to exchange buffering.232 In this context, porewater pH may more directly reflect
1325  short-term reaction progress and alkalinity export potential, whereas bulk soil pH integrates
1326  exchange reactions and longer-term shifts in soil acidity status.
1327
1328  Methodological heterogeneity remains a central limitation. Soil pH depends strongly on extractant
1329  type, molarity, and soil-to-solution ratio, and different measurement definitions (e.g., water pH,
1330  CaCl2 pH, buffer pH) can yield systematically different values for the same soil.232 Because
1331  agronomic liming recommendations are often based on buffer pH rather than water pH, translating
1332  reported baseline values into amendment requirements requires careful attention to measurement
1333  protocol. In the EW literature, such methodological details are not always reported consistently,
1334  complicating cross-study comparison and synthesis. A further source of ambiguity is that soil pH
1335  and aqueous pH values are distinct, even though they are sometimes used interchangeably. For
1336  example, some studies do not clearly distinguish between changes in bulk soil pH and changes in
1337  porewater or effluent pH, nor do they consistently separate soil matrix measurements from
1338  drainage chemistry. Conflating these variables--which reflect distinct biogeochemical domains--
1339  can obscure mechanistic interpretation, particularly when assessing EW-induced alkalinisation,
1340  nutrient availability, or trace metal mobility.
1341
1342  Overall, the baseline pH distributions compiled here indicate that EW research has primarily
1343  targeted acidic agricultural soils where traditional agronomic benefits (acidity remediation) and
1344  enhanced dissolution kinetics are expected. However, this concentration of studies in acidic
1345  systems also limits the empirical basis for extrapolation to neutral or alkaline soils, where
1346  weathering kinetics, buffering capacity, and biogeochemical feedbacks may differ substantially.
1347  The implications of EW-induced pH modification for soil chemistry, nutrient cycling, and long-
1348  term CDR efficiency are examined further in Section 7.
1349
1350

      37
      Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

1352  as fertiliser studies. (a) Soil pH measured in H2O extracts. (b) Soil pH measured in CaCl2 extracts. (c) Pore water
1353  and effluent pH values, combining measurements from soil solution and drainage waters (stream, river, as well as
1354  column experiment effluent). Stacked histograms are further disaggregated by primary study type (field, laboratory,
1355  model). Each occurrence represents one unique value per unique study.
