      Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

1359  3.2.3 Hydroclimatic control on EW

1360  Hydroclimate is a fundamental regulator of CDR through EW, as it controls the availability of
1361  water and temperature--two key factors strongly influencing weathering reaction
1362  kinetics.23,172,233,234
1363
1364  In humid regions, elevated soil moisture increases the wetted surface area of mineral amendments,
1365  enhancing their exposure to weathering agents.235 Elevated soil moisture also promotes soil
1366  drainage236 and the efflux of weathering products.237 Drainage, and lower residence times, is key
1367  to maintain non-equilibrium chemical conditions that sustain higher weathering rates,238,239
1368  compared to low-drainage conditions that favour saturation of soil solution.240 High temperatures
1369  complement moisture availability by accelerating mineral dissolution kinetics.56,221 At regional
1370  scales, the co-occurrence of warm and wet conditions maximizes both reaction rates and solute
1371  export, particularly where temperature and precipitation seasonality are in phase.236 Rainfall and
1372  temperature also regulate ecosystem photosynthesis and respiration, with higher values generally
1373  increasing pCO2 and the release of acidifying root exudates,241,242 further enhancing weathering
1374  rates.
1375
1376  Consequently, warm and humid regions--especially the tropics--consistently emerge as hotspots
1377  of modelled and experimental CDR potential.19,24,243­245 Laboratory experiments frequently
1378  demonstrate higher weathering rates under wetter and warmer conditions.237,246 Substantial CDR
1379  potential has also been reported for example in Costa Rica.244 A global RTM confirms the high
1380  efficiency of warm and humid regions (cf., Section 5.2.3), which often overlap with economically
1381  developing regions.19,24 However, global warming is expected to have a minor effect on CDR
1382  rates24--with even potential negative impacts (at least for carbonates) in some tropical regions.87
1383
1384  Despite these insights, quantifying the hydroclimatic impacts on soil chemistry remains
1385  challenging. Laboratory conditions often struggle to replicate the natural hydroclimatic
1386  variability.180 In the field, CDR rates are influenced by complex and difficult to disentangle
1387  interactions with soil type and biogeochemical conditions:247,248 moisture and drainage are also
1388  controlled by soil texture,97,105 land management and use of other amendments,249 as well as the
1389  hydrologic transport of weathering products through the vadose zone, aquifers, and riverine
1390  systems.66 Moreover, the efficiency of CDR depends on water chemistry and climate214 and may
1391  be constrained by silicicacid saturation or ecological impacts associated with elevated riverine pH
1392  and alkalinity.17 Ultimately, site selection for EW must balance favourable hydroclimatic
1393  conditions with proximity to mineral resources and associated transport requirements.133,250,251 In
1394  drier regions, irrigation is unlikely to fully offset rainfall deficits. In fact, while irrigation can
1395  supplement moisture, modern irrigation methods aim to maximize efficiency and minimize
1396  drainage--contrary to the high-moisture, high-flow conditions required for optimal EW.
1397
1398  Lastly, the temporal structure of water inputs is also critical,66,172,252 as hydrological forcing in soils
1399  is inherently intermittent. For example, a given total water input delivered during a single event
1400  may have very different geochemical consequences than the same amount distributed across
1401  multiple smaller events, due to differences in infiltration dynamics, residence time, and soil
1402  solution chemistry. Reporting time-resolved hydrological data (e.g., event frequency, intensity, or
1403  time-series precipitation records) alongside cumulative water inputs would therefore improve
1404  interpretation of weathering responses. Such reporting would enable more robust meta-analysis,

      39
      Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

1405  reduce ambiguity in interpreting hydrological controls on weathering, and strengthen the empirical
1406  basis for linking climate and water availability to realised CDR from EW.
1407
      3.2.3.1 Implementation and limitations in the literature
1408  Even though climatic conditions strongly influence mineral dissolution kinetics and the transport
      of weathering products, these parameters are reported with varying levels of detail and consistency
1409  across the compiled literature.
1410
1411  Reported average temperatures span a broad range across the compiled studies (Figure 12). Values
1412  extend from ~3­35°C, with a median near 20°C, reflecting the diversity of climatic and
1413  experimental settings represented in the literature. Field experiments generally occur in temperate
1414  to warm climates, with median temperatures of roughly 11°C for EW field studies and 16°C for
1415  fertiliser field studies, while laboratory and modelling studies commonly assume or simulate
1416  warmer conditions (medians ~22­24°C). This wide distribution indicates that EW experiments are
1417  conducted under a broad range of thermal environments, though temperature itself is typically not
1418  a controlled variable in field studies and is therefore often reported only as contextual site
1419  information.
1420
1421
1422
1423

1424  Figure 12: Average temperature distribution from Enhanced Weathering (EW) and rock flour as fertiliser studies.
1425  Occurrences are further stratified by primary study type (field, laboratory, model). Each occurrence represents one
1426  unique value per unique study.
1427
      Despite the critical control of water availability and hydrological flux on EW, the reporting of
1428  water inputs in the literature is uneven and fragmented (Figure 13). Few studies report the full set
1429  of water balance components required to constrain hydrological conditions. While precipitation is
1430  relatively well-documented (Figure 13a), the reporting of other hydrological fluxes is much less
1431  consistent. Irrigation (and precipitation + irrigation) is reported in fewer studies (Figure 13b,c),
1432  but this likely stems from the fact that not all studies add irrigation water. Additional variables that
1433  would help characterize the broader site water balance--such as evapotranspiration (Figure 13d)
1434
1435
1436

      40
      Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

1437  and infiltration (Figure 13e)--are rarely reported. As a result, only a limited subset of studies
1438  provides sufficient information to reconstruct a simplified water balance.
1439
1440

1441  Figure 13: Distribution of water inputs reported in Enhanced Weathering (EW) and rock flour as fertiliser studies:
1442  (a) precipitation, (b) irrigation, (c) precipitation + irrigation, (d) evapotranspiration, and (e) infiltration. All values
1443  were harmonised to mm yr1. Data are stratified by study type (field, laboratory, model) and by study focus (EW vs.
1444  rock flour fertiliser studies). Where studies reported total water fluxes over an experimental period, these totals were
1445  converted to annualized fluxes by dividing by the reported study duration. Each occurrence represents one unique
1446  value per unique study.
1447

      41
      Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

1448  Where precipitation is reported, values span a wide climatic range, with a median near 1000 mm
1449  yr1 and an IQR of roughly 600­1470 mm yr1 (Figure 13a), consistent with typical temperate and
1450  humid agricultural environments. Irrigation rates exhibit substantially greater variability (Figure
1451  13b), with median values near 800 mm yr1 (IQR 128­2000 mm yr1) but extreme values can reach
1452  17000-24000 mm yr1 in some laboratory studies. Similarly high values also occur for combined
1453  water inputs (Figure 13c) and infiltration (Figure 13e). While these extreme fluxes (unrealistic in
1454  field conditions) have been used in controlled laboratory or column experiments to eliminate water
1455  and thermodynamic limitations and provide strong signals for estimating potential rates on
1456  laboratory timescales, EW efficiency in field conditions is often influenced by hydrological
1457  constraints, making it difficult to extrapolate these results to more realistic hydrologic regimes.
1458  Some EW field studies exhibit extremely low water inputs which may significantly limit the
1459  amount of weathering one would expect to occur.
1460
1461  Closely related to this issue, water input and moisture conditions are reported using a wide array
1462  of metrics across EW studies, substantially complicating synthesis and comparison. Reported
1463  measures include total water volumes applied per treatment, irrigation rates expressed per plot or
1464  experiment, and qualitative descriptions of watering intended to maintain a target soil moisture. In
1465  many cases, incomplete metadata--such as missing experimental dimensions or soil water balance
1466  information--make it difficult to convert these values into a common, comparable metric. This
1467  problem is particularly pronounced in studies that aim to maintain constant soil moisture, which
1468  frequently report the moisture target but not the actual water input required to achieve it. Reporting
1469  water depth-equivalent water flux (or simply precipitation depth, e.g., mm or mm yr1) would
1470  substantially improve cross-study comparability by providing a scale-independent representation
1471  of hydrological forcing. These inconsistencies mirror challenges identified for grain-size reporting
1472  and highlight a broader need for methodological standardization.
1473
1474  3.3 Transport and long-term storage of CDR from EW
1475
1476  For EW to deliver durable CDR, weathering products must also be transported through soils,
      groundwater, rivers, and ultimately into long-lived carbon reservoirs. This section examines how
1477  hydrological and biogeochemical transport processes govern the movement of alkalinity, cations,
      and DIC from land to downstream environments, and how these pathways influence the timing,
1478  location, and durability of storage. We first consider transport through soils and rivers and then
1479  discuss the principal long-term storage reservoirs for EW-derived carbon, including ocean
1480  alkalinity and carbonate formation in soils.
1481
1482  It is important to note that the same transport pathways and storage domains considered here are
1483  also where losses, reversals, and delays in CDR can occur. While this section introduces these
1484  processes in the context of carbon transport and storage at a system level, Section 3.4 examines
1485  them explicitly as loss mechanisms, providing a more detailed, process-level assessment.
1486
1487
1488
1489
1490
1491

      42
      Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

1492  3.3.1 Transport of weathering signals in soils

1493  The products of EW are mobilised within soils through a combination of hydrological transport
1494  and biogeochemical processing. As weathering-derived solutes move through porewaters, they are
1495  not transported conservatively but interact with mineral surfaces, organic matter, and soil exchange
1496  complexes--undergoing processes such as adsorption, ion exchange, secondary mineral
1497  formation, and biological uptake--that can modify their composition, timing, and eventual export
1498  to aquatic systems (cf., Sections 3.4.1 ­ 3.4.6). Together, these processes determine how, when,
1499  and in what form the weathering signal is transmitted from soils to downstream environments.
1500
1501  Precipitation, irrigation, or snowmelt that interacts with feedstocks and carries dissolution products
1502  can move to streams along various surface and subsurface pathways. Shallow flow paths activate
1503  during rainfall events as the regional water table rises or as a perched water table forms and
1504  saturates permeable near-surface soils.253­257 While ephemeral, these shallow flow paths may
1505  contribute substantially to streamflow, especially during events with high antecedent water
1506  storage, and can transmit dissolved solutes rapidly to the stream. During dry periods, deeper and
1507  slower-moving groundwater represents the main contributor to flow in perennial streams.258,259
1508  Depending on climate and watershed characteristics, baseflow may provide from one-third to more
1509  than one-half of total annual streamflow.260,261
1510
1511  Because various pathways deliver water to the stream and these precipitation-runoff pathways
1512  originate across the watershed at various distances from the stream, the transit times of catchment
1513  waters that convey solutes--including the EW products--will be distributed. The nature of the
1514  transit-time distribution is influenced by topography, watershed size, prevailing geology,
1515  precipitation characteristics, human modifications (e.g., tile drains), and various other factors.
1516  Although a priori determination is not yet possible, transit-time distributions can be estimated
1517  from analysis of natural tracers in precipitation and streamflow and from simulations with
1518  watershed hydrologic models. Findings from this research reveal that mean water transit times on
1519  the order of years are not uncommon and that the distribution in travel times may span from days
1520  to years within small watersheds and from weeks to millennia in larger watersheds 262­266.
1521
1522  Compared to water transit times, those of EW-derived solutes may be somewhat longer due to
1523  storage of solutes, for example due to sorption to cation exchange sites, conversion to secondary
1524  phases, or plant uptake (cf., Sections 3.4.1­3.4.5). Considering the EW-relevant geochemical
1525  processes together with precipitation-runoff processes, it is reasonable to expect that EW solutes
1526  could appear in surface waters soon after application of a mineral amendment, but a substantial
1527  fraction of alkalinity generated from initial weathering reactions will be stored within the terrestrial
1528  reservoir for extended time periods and released slowly--over a period of years--to surrounding
1529  streams and rivers (e.g., ref. 68; Section 3.4.6).
1530
1531  To date, there are exceedingly few EW field trials suitable for sharpening these expectations.70,243
1532  Extrapolations from soil-column and mesocosm experiments have limited utility for constraining
1533  how EW products are exported from watersheds because these experiments poorly represent
1534  complex watershed flow paths shaped by geologic structure, topography, and natural precipitation
1535  regimes. In contrast, widespread liming across the Mississippi River basin provides an inadvertent
1536  large-scale analogue, demonstrating that weathering products from CO2-capturing soil
1537  amendments can be exported to surface waters.267­269 A >100-year record from the terminus of the

      43
      Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

1538  Mississippi River suggests efficient transport of liming-derived weathering products to the river
1539  network, albeit with a substantial time lag after carbonate rock application.270 Watershed-scale
1540  RTMs capable of simulating these hydrologic characteristics are being advanced and may be
1541  suitable for exploring the dynamics of EW solute delivery to streams and rivers.271­273 Building
1542  confidence in these model-based assessments will require evaluation against streamwater
1543  chemistry measurements from EW field trials.
1544
1545  3.3.2 Transport of weathering signals in rivers

1546  Once EW products are exported from soils into the aquatic continuum, rivers do not merely
      function as passive conduits but operate as active biogeochemical reactors--transforming and
1547  redistributing weathering through coupled physical, chemical, and biological processes.274
1548
1549  On a global scale, recent modelling suggests that riverine carrying capacity is generally not a major
1550  limiting factor for EW deployment.275 However, this conclusion is sensitive to the assumed
1551  saturation state threshold for carbonate precipitation, which releases CO2 and reduces alkalinity
1552  loads. This precipitation threshold remains poorly constrained in river systems and is rarely
1553  evaluated empirically in EW studies.65,276,277 If conservative thermodynamic thresholds are
1554  applied--such as assuming precipitation occurs immediately upon reaching saturation--rivers can
1555  potentially become a bottleneck for durable CDR, significantly reducing the efficiency of carbon
1556  transfer to the oceans.277 At the regional scale, carbon transport limitations arising from carbonate
1557  precipitation also warrant attention. Recent catchment-scale assessments in the UK arable
1558  croplands indicate that carbonate precipitation could reduce the CDR potential of EW by 16­27%
1559  depending on EW deployment amounts.65 However, for both global and regional studies, it is
1560  critical to distinguish between temporary and net carbon loss from carbon precipitation;
1561  precipitated carbonates may redissolve once in undersaturated deep waters, surface bedload or
1562  coastal marine sediments, effectively neutralising the net carbon loss.31
1563
1564  Even in the absence of carbonate precipitation, shifts in the carbonate chemistry itself from
1565  thermodynamic equilibria can compromise net CDR efficiency through carbon degassing (cf.,
1566  Section 3.4.7). The addition of alkalinity alters the aquatic carbonate system, potentially increasing
1567  the partial pressure of CO2 and driving "carbon leakage" via degassing to the atmosphere. In a
1568  recent study that used a dynamic river network model to track downstream carbon loss in rivers
1569  following EW in North America, most analysed river reaches exhibited low leakage rates (<5%),
1570  but specific hydrological regimes experienced leakage exceeding 15% of initially captured
1571  carbon.66 This high variability complicates the quantification of net carbon credits and underscores
1572  the necessity for high-resolution, region-specific analyses rather than relying on continental or
1573  global averages.
1574
1575  Beyond inorganic chemistry, EW products may induce complex indirect effects on riverine
1576  biogeochemical cycles. Increases in pH and alkalinity have been argued to accelerate the
1577  decomposition of dissolved organic carbon, potentially releasing additional CO2 and offsetting the
1578  intended sequestration benefits.111 On the other hand, alterations in river chemistry can also
1579  influence primary production; stimulated photosynthesis may temporarily increase uptake of
1580  DIC,67 yet the long-term fate of this biomass remains a significant uncertainty. These biological
1581
1582

      44
      Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

1583  fluxes are further complicated by exchange with the hyporheic zone, where intense respiration
1584  within the stream corridor contributes substantially to riverine CO2 evasion.278 The effects of EW
1585  on hyporheic zone processes remain largely unexplored. Other downstream processes, including
1586  reverse weathering and the fate of alkalinity as rivers enter the coastal ocean, also remain largely
1587  unconstrained in the context of EW.279
1588
1589  Despite these complexities, rivers represent a potentially critical leverage point for MRV of CDR
1590  from EW.280 By spatially integrating geochemical signals across watersheds, river monitoring
1591  offers a potentially cost-effective and scalable method for verifying net carbon capture. However,
1592  a primary challenge for this approach is the "signal-to-noise" issue: detecting the specific alkalinity
1593  signal from EW against the high background variability of natural river dynamics remains a key
1594  hurdle for confident attribution. River network models coupled with field sampling as the use of
1595  control catchments could help address this question and test the feasibility of this river aggregation
1596  approach.281
1597
      3.3.3 Carbon storage from EW
1598
      For an effective climate intervention, it is imperative that the captured and converted CO2 remains
1599  sequestered from the atmosphere such that our climate goals are met. Alcalde et al., (2018)282
1600  postulate that storage on the order of 10,000 years (a loss rate of 0.01% yr-1) is sufficient for these
1601
1602  ends. This framing implicitly assumes a net-zero emissions context, in which storage must persist
1603  until natural carbon cycle processes restore equilibrium.283 However, under net-negative emissions
1604
1605  scenarios, shorter storage durations may still provide climate benefits by reducing peak
1606  atmospheric CO2 concentrations and delaying warming, even if storage is not strictly
1607  permanent.5,284,285
1608
1609  There are two long-lived products of EW that form the storage repositories for atmospheric CO2,
1610  namely bicarbonate/carbonate ions and solid carbonate minerals. In all processes, CO2 will be
1611  initially transformed into bicarbonate ions dissolved in soil porewaters. These can be transported
1612  through run-off or shallow groundwater to rivers and eventually the ocean, where they may remain
1613  for thousands of years slowly being transformed into solid carbonate minerals (Section 3.3.1).
1614  Alternatively, providing sufficiently over-saturated solutions, solid carbonate minerals may form
1615  within soils or drainage waters enroute to the ocean (Section 3.3.2). These may constitute a
1616  transient carbon pool, dissolving back into bicarbonate ions if drainage waters become under-
1617  saturated. While both storage mechanisms are durable, the precipitation of solid carbonate
1618  minerals reduces the efficiency of EW by nearly half due to the re-release of CO2 during the
1619  precipitation reaction:
1620
1621  !4!# + 2 %!""  !4!""(/) + !"! + %!"  9
1622
      3.3.3.1 Storage in soils and along transport pathways
1623
      Globally, the upper metre of soils contains an estimated ~2,500­2,700 GtCO2-eq as carbonate
1624  minerals, with estimates for the upper 2 m being substantially larger.286,287 Paedogenic
1625  carbonates--those formed in soil--are particularly abundant in arid and semi-arid regions where
1626  leaching is limited. These carbonates may form from the dissolution and precipitation of
1627

      45
      Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

1628  carbonate-rich bedrock and thus may not represent a net increase in carbon storage. Alternatively,
1629  paedogenic carbonates may form in soils atop lithologies that lack carbonates, either via silicate
1630  weathering or via external inputs, especially aeolian deposition.288­293 Similarly, paedogenic
1631  carbonates may be a storage reservoir for carbon dioxide removed by EW.294 Hence, changes in
1632  SIC pools have also been used to quantify CDR from EW (cf., Section 4.1.2).
1633
1634  Paedogenic carbonates can be long-lived within soils,295 with > 250 million years recorded for
1635  some materials.296,297 At the same time, more typical residence times for paedogenic carbonates
1636  are on the order of 103­104 years.233,298 However, these estimates largely derive from arid and
1637  semi-arid environments where limited leaching and persistently alkaline conditions favour
1638  carbonate stability and may not be representative of many agricultural systems targeted for EW.
1639  In more humid or actively managed soils, or where EW application ceases and soil conditions
1640  revert toward more acidic states, paedogenic carbonates may be susceptible to dissolution and
1641  remobilisation, reducing their long-term stability as a carbon store while increasing the overall
1642  efficiency of carbon removal through EW. Dissolution and reprecipitation of paedogenic
1643  carbonates is thought to be important within their formation,298 which is a feature of some EW
1644  RTM simulations (Figure 14; ref. 20). Paedogenic carbonates are also reported in soils heavily
1645  modified with artificial alkaline minerals277 and predicted using geochemical modelling in EW
1646  field trials using returned crushed concrete.299
1647
1648

1649  Figure 14: Accumulation of paedogenic carbonate using an Enhanced Weathering (EW) reactive transport model
1650  (RTM) that simulates basalt dissolution in U.K. soils under winter barley. Adapted from Kantzas et al., (2022)20
1651  Extended Figure 7. Simulations were undertaken for Cragmill basalt mineralogy with p80 particle diameter of 100
1652  m, applied to a winter barley crop rotation field in the UK midlands. The plot shows seasonally driven
1653  dissolution/precipitation of paedogenic carbonate with net accumulation over decades.
1654

1655
1656

      46
      Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

1657  As rivers outgas CO2 (cf., Section 3.4.7), many become saturated with respect to calcium carbonate
1658  minerals, which could result in their precipitation in the water course--often termed `tufa' for non-
1659  geothermal or `travertine' for geothermally produced carbonate minerals.300 This impacts the
1660  natural cation budget of rivers301 and could possibly constrain the scale of bicarbonate flux from
1661  EW to 3 to >20 GtCO2 yr-1 (Section 3.3.2).30,275 If carbonate were to precipitate within drainage
1662  waters, this storage will be transient if saturation states are not sustained and the solid carbonate
1663  remained submerged in river water. Nevertheless, natural tufas and travertines can be long-lived,
1664  with the oldest known records exceeding 100,000 years.302
1665
1666  Taken together, these observations indicate that carbonate precipitation can constitute a stable
1667  carbon storage pathway under specific environmental conditions, particularly in arid and alkaline
1668  soils. However, this stability is strongly context-dependent and may not extend to many managed
1669  or humid systems targeted for EW deployment. Importantly, the limited durability of paedogenic
1670  carbonates compared to oceanic bicarbonate storage does not necessarily represent a net loss
1671  pathway. Redissolution of SIC is equivalent to carbonate weathering, with the same potential
1672  impact on CDR.31 Where dissolution proceeds via carbonic acid--or where acid neutralisation
1673  suppresses CO2 outgassing along the soil­river continuum--this process can partially or fully
1674  recover the initial CDR penalty associated with carbonate formation.
1675
1676  3.3.3.2 Storage in the oceans
      Ocean inorganic carbon is the largest reservoir of carbon at the Earth's surface--equivalent to
1677  150,000 GtCO2--with overall residence times on the order of 100,000 years.37,90 Inorganic carbon
      in the ocean is predominately (99%) bicarbonate or carbonate ions,303 and additional bicarbonate
1678  flux from EW offers the possibility of scalable durable storage. However, >95% of the DIC that is
1679  introduced into to the ocean from EW would remain within the upper 1 km of the water column.
1680  The majority of this would reside within the upper 500 m, or somewhat deeper if the alkalinity is
1681  introduced in bottom water formation regions.304 The mixed surface ocean (<200 m) contains
1682  ~3,400 GtCO2-eq of DIC with a residence time of <1,000 years.37,305 While representing a scalable
1683  and durable reservoir of carbon, surface ocean values are substantially smaller/shorter than the
1684  potential of the total ocean.
1685
1686  Alkalinity removal from the surface ocean through carbonate mineral precipitation is controlled
1687  by calcifying organisms, which collectively remove ~7 GtCO2-eq per year, exported through
1688  sinking particles to deeper waters (ref. 306, and references therein). Alkalinity is also removed
1689  through secondary silicate mineral formation (`reverse weathering') but this makes a relatively
1690  small contribution to the modern ocean.307 Assessing natural chemical gradients in the surface
1691  ocean, Lehmann and Bach (2025)308 suggest that under `moderate' (<50 GtCO2-eq yr-1) increases
1692  in OAE the loss from microorganism calcification is <3% of the added alkalinity.
1693
1694  There is no evidence of significant alkalinity loss in month-long mesocosm experiments,309 at least
1695  within the resolution of conventional routine measurement techniques for total alkalinity (TA; 0.1­
1696  0.5%).310 Spontaneous carbonate precipitation in seawater requires substantial carbonate
1697  supersaturation, and riverine inputs to the ocean are generally not expected to exceed these critical
1698  conditions.311 It therefore appears unlikely that land-based EW would commonly trigger
1699  spontaneous carbonate precipitation in the ocean. Thus, CDR from EW is primarily stored as
1700
1701
1702

      47
      Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

1703  dissolved bicarbonate. Taken together, these constraints indicate that oceanic losses should be
1704  limited (~10­20%; Section 3.4.7) and that most EW-derived carbon will be retained as DIC.37,279
1705  In practice, storage can be considered durable on climatically relevant timescales once carbon is
1706  transferred into the ocean bicarbonate pool.
1707
1708  3.4 Loss processes, reversals, and limitations

1709  The CDR potential of EW is not determined by primary mineral dissolution alone. A range of
      coupled geochemical, biological, and hydrological processes can reduce, delay, or in some cases
1710  reverse realised CDR by retaining weathering products in soils, re-releasing CO2 in soils, slowing
1711  further dissolution, or promoting losses during downstream transport. In this section, we examine
1712  the main processes that constrain net EW performance, including secondary mineral formation,
1713  cation exchange processes, the presence and interaction with strong acids, biomass uptake, surface
1714  passivation, lag times between weathering and CDR occurrence, and CO2 degassing along the
1715  soil­river­ocean continuum.
1716
1717  3.4.1 Formation of secondary phases
1718
1719  During chemical weathering, surface waters are undersaturated with regard to primary silicate
      minerals (olivine, pyroxene, feldspar, etc.), which therefore dissolve. This can drive these waters
1720  to supersaturation with regard to secondary minerals, which include carbonates, (oxy)hydroxides,
      zeolites and clay minerals. The subsequent precipitation of these secondary minerals (known as
1721  neoformation of authigenic minerals) can act to keep waters undersaturated with regard to primary
1722  minerals, supporting continued dissolution (e.g., refs. 312­314). In flow-through soil and watershed
1723  systems, continued primary mineral dissolution can be sustained by solute export and/or by
1724  secondary mineral precipitation in situ or downstream; secondary mineral formation is therefore
1725  not required at every reaction site, but remains a common and important component of weathering
1726  systems. If infiltrating water is derived from rainwater, which is initially strongly undersaturated
1727  with respect to most secondary minerals, it will take longer for porewaters to reach supersaturation
1728  than systems supplied by irrigation groundwater, which may already contain higher dissolved
1729  solute concentrations. Secondary minerals (with the potential exception of carbonates) have low
1730  chemical activity and are therefore difficult to redissolve.
1731
1732  The formation of secondary minerals has several implications for both EW and natural weathering
1733  reactions, and the sequestration of atmospheric CO2:
1734
1735      1. The formation of soil carbonates affects CDR similarly to marine carbonate precipitation:
1736           as discussed above, carbonate precipitation removes alkalinity from solution and releases
1737           approximately half of the previously weathering-derived DIC as CO2, although this CO2 is
1738           not necessarily emitted to the atmosphere. Approximately ~2,500­2,700 GtCO2-eq are
1739           stored in the top m of soil in this way, with millennial-scale turnover.312,315 Changing
1740           rainfall conditions or fertiliser use could redissolve this carbonate, reforming the alkalinity
1741           in the surface waters.
1742
1743
1744

      48
      Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

1745      2. The coating of feedstock silicate grains with secondary minerals will cover the reactive
1746           silicates with unreactive silicate material, thus significantly slowing their dissolution
1747           (known as "surface passivation" or "soil shielding") (e.g., refs. 19,172,316).
1748
1749      3. Over repeated years of amendment, it is possible that sufficient clay minerals form to
1750           change the drainage patterns of fields, due to alteration of permeability and porosity. This
1751           can also change weathering processes via alteration of the hydraulic conductivity of
1752           soils.313
1753
1754      4. Increased amounts of clay minerals will change the CEC of the soil, with implications for
1755           monitoring EW (e.g., ref. 220) and fertility of the soil, which could be affected positively or
1756           negatively (depending on local conditions).
1757
1758      5. Secondary minerals retain cations that would otherwise be used to charge-balance
1759           alkalinity. In other words, the formation of secondary minerals--especially clays--hinders
1760           the efficiency of EW, such that less CDR is realised per unit of feedstock applied 317,318.
1761           This is a slightly different process from "reverse weathering", which occurs in marine
1762           settings when clay formation re-releases stored CO2 (e.g., ref. 319), and the effects of reverse
1763           weathering in continental settings remains largely unknown.320
1764
1765      6. Secondary minerals in the form of oxides (particularly iron-rich oxides and oxyhydroxides,
1766           known as the "rusty carbon sink") and clays can help preserve organic carbon during burial
1767           both in soils and in the marine realm.101,321­324 Hence, secondary mineral formation that
1768           hinders inorganic carbon sequestration may enhance organic carbon sequestration. This
1769           behaviour is largely unconstrained in EW systems but may be more significant than
1770           inorganic carbon sequestration.
1771
1772  One of the key issues in measuring secondary mineral formation is that the most important phases,
1773  especially clays and oxides, will often have no fixed chemical formulae but instead act as a
1774  "sponge" for available cations.124 This means that there are no definitive diagnostic elements, and
1775  in any case each has a different mobility during weathering,312 making secondary mineral
1776  formation difficult to extrapolate across settings. Further, using detection methods such as X-ray
1777  diffraction (XRD) only detects the presence of secondary minerals, rather than their neoformation
1778  due to feedstock weathering. Novel "non-traditional" metal isotopes may be able to resolve this
1779  problem (e.g., refs. 325,326; Section 4.4). In field settings, however, newly formed secondary phases
1780  are likely to represent small changes relative to pre-existing soil mineral pools, making them
1781  difficult to resolve analytically against natural background variability.
1782
1783  The amount of secondary mineral neoformation occurring during EW is largely unconstrained
1784  (e.g., ref. 325). Natural basaltic weathering studies indicate that substantial fractions of Ca and Mg
1785  can be removed from solution by multiple processes, including cation exchange and secondary
1786  mineral formation, with clay formation representing an important sink in some settings.327 Clay
1787  formation relative to primary mineral dissolution during basalt weathering has been reported
1788  across climatic gradients,220,328­331 and weathering rates, and hence potential secondary mineral
1789  formation rates, generally increase at higher temperatures, although temperature also affects
1790  secondary mineral solubility and cation exchange equilibria.57,184,222,223,332­334 These observations
      demonstrate that secondary mineral formation is a potentially important loss pathway.
      Nevertheless, natural basaltic weathering terrains are imperfect analogues for agricultural EW
      systems, where basalt is typically added as a relatively small fraction of the soil mass.335

      49
      Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

1791  Conditions that can promote secondary mineral formation include long water-rock contact
1792  times327,336,337 and pH ranges favouring oxyhydroxides (<6), clay minerals (>6), or carbonates
1793  (>10).333 Laboratory and groundwater studies further show that secondary phases can form over
1794  weeks to months under favourable conditions, including substantial Mg uptake into clays in
1795  basaltic groundwater and reprecipitation of dissolved basalt as clay in highly reactive laboratory
1796  systems.338. However, similar to natural analogues from basaltic catchments, these systems may
1797  overstate the extent or rate of secondary phase formation in field deployments, where feedstock
1798  additions are commonly only on the order of a few percent of soil mass,335 hydrological flow-
1799  through can limit saturation buildup. Constraining the extent of these processes in real-world EW
1800  deployments--and identifying deployment strategies that minimise secondary clay formation
1801  through site, feedstock, and application-rate selection--remains a central uncertainty in assessing
1802  EW efficacy and a major focus of current research.
1803
1804  3.4.2 Cation exchange processes

1805  Cation exchange processes exert a first-order control on whether base cations released during EW
      contribute to alkalinity export and realised CDR or are retained within soils. Upon silicate
1806  dissolution, cations such as Ca2+ and Mg2+ released to soil solution may be reversibly sorbed onto
1807  negatively charged mineral and organic exchange sites,339­342 displacing exchangeable acidity (H+
1808  and Al3+) as well as native cations. This process alters both the magnitude and composition of the
1809  exported solute flux relative to the initial dissolution stoichiometry and can suppress or delay net
1810  CDR through charge-balancing reactions that regenerate CO2 if there is a net increase in BS.68
1811
1812  From a charge-balance perspective, net uptake of base cations by the soil exchange complex must
1813  be balanced by release of acidity. Where this acidity reacts with bicarbonate produced during
1814  silicate weathering, CO2 is regenerated according to Eq. 1, effectively offsetting part of the initially
1815  generated alkalinity and CDR. This concept (see also Figure 15a) has been demonstrated in model
1816  simulations showing that transient cation retention at exchange sites can drive rapid conversion of
1817  bicarbonate to CO2 until base saturation (BS) increases and exchangeable acidity is depleted.68
1818  Experimental and modelling studies consistently identify this exchangeable acidity pool as a
1819  dominant control on EW efficiency in acidic soils.68,172,216
1820
1821  Field and laboratory evidence indicates that the magnitude of losses associated with CEC can be
1822  substantial, particularly in highly weathered soils with large reserves of surface-bound acidity. For
1823  example, in a five-year tropical field experiment, 98% of the alkalinity lost to secondary processes
1824  was attributable to acids stronger than carbonic acid, with surface-bound protons constituting the
1825  dominant acidity source 216. Despite sustained basalt dissolution and increases in soil pH and
1826  exchangeable Mg2+, bicarbonate export in deep drainage remained minimal, demonstrating how
1827  large CEC-associated acidity pools can in some cases temporarily suppress realised CDR. Shorter-
1828  term experiments provide complementary evidence that cation exchange modifies not only the
1829  magnitude but also the composition of exported cation pools. For example, Mg and Li isotope
1830  tracers in olivine-amended soil columns reveal that a substantial fraction of weathering-derived
1831  Mg2+ is transiently retained on exchange sites, leading to isotopic signatures in pore waters that
1832  differ from those expected based on mineral dissolution alone 326. These results demonstrate that
1833
1834

      50
      Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

1835  CEC processes can decouple composition of cations released via weathering from solute export
1836  fluxes, complicating inference of CDR from cation or isotope measurements alone.

                                                                        51
      Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

1858  Experimental studies further show that Ca2+ and Mg2+ can remain sequestered on exchange sites
1859  over agronomically relevant timescales, delaying alkalinity and DIC export even when initial
1860  silicate dissolution is rapid (e.g., refs. 237,343).
1861
1862  An additional complexity is that CEC is not necessarily a fixed soil property. In variable-charge
1863  soils and organic-rich horizons, exchange capacity can increase with pH as deprotonation of
1864  mineral and organic surfaces generates additional negative charge.215 EW-induced alkalinisation
1865  may therefore expand the effective exchange pool, allowing a larger fraction of newly released
1866  Ca2+ and Mg2+ to be retained on exchange sites. This creates a potential negative feedback on
1867  cation export: greater weathering can drive larger pH and CEC responses, increasing cation
1868  retention and thereby amplifying CEC-associated delays or losses in realised CDR.
1869
1870  Cation exchange capacities and related properties vary widely across soil types, horizons, and
1871  management regimes, leading to strong spatial heterogeneity in EW outcomes. Highly weathered
1872  tropical soils typically exhibit large pools of reserve acidity relative to exchangeable base cations,
1873  increasing the potential for CEC-mediated neutralisation of alkalinity.215,216 In contrast, soils with
1874  low CEC or high initial BS are more likely to translate cation release into rapid alkalinity export
1875  and realised CDR.68 Where bicarbonate generated near the surface is neutralised at depth by acidity
1876  released from exchange sites, CO2 may be emitted to soil gas or aquatic systems rather than
1877  transported to long-term marine reservoirs.
1878
1879  By controlling both the timing and magnitude of alkalinity export, CEC processes directly
1880  influence downstream CDR along the soil­river­ocean continuum. These processes provide a
1881  mechanistic basis for soil CO2 leakage and lagged CDR frequently observed in EW studies and
1882  underscore the need to explicitly account for cation exchange dynamics when estimating net CDR,
1883  designing MRV frameworks, and interpreting field-trial data.
1884
1885  3.4.3 Strong-acid weathering

1886  Carbon Dioxide Removal via EW is often quantified by linking mineral dissolution or base-cation
      release to stoichiometric uptake of CO2 through carbonic-acid weathering. However, silicate
1887  minerals can dissolve via reactions that do not involve carbonic acid. Strong acids such as nitric
1888  or sulfuric acid can drive mineral weathering while producing no local CO2 uptake, despite
1889  releasing cations that may otherwise be interpreted as evidence of CDR.14,15,30,62,68,214,216,231,313,344
1890  In addition, strong acids can react with bicarbonate produced during carbonic-acid weathering.
1891  Protonation of bicarbonate shifts DIC back toward dissolved or gaseous CO2, effectively reversing
1892  previously realised CDR. Hence, when cation fluxes are used as a proxy for CDR without
1893  explicitly accounting for strong-acid inputs, these processes can lead to systematic overestimation
1894  of net CDR at the field scale.325 However, as outlined above, strong-acid-driven dissolution may
1895  still contribute to CDR at a larger system scale if it reduces acid export from soils and the resulting
1896  weathering products are transported to more alkaline downstream waters.345 In this case, CDR is
1897  not realised locally at the point of weathering, but may occur later as riverine or marine carbonate
1898  equilibria re-adjust,346 with the timing and efficiency depending on local hydrology, solute
1899  retention, and mixing conditions.
1900
1901
1902

      52
      Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

1903  3.4.3.1 Sources of acidity and the role of soil initial conditions
      Strong acidity influencing EW systems arises from both ongoing inputs and legacy processes. In
1904  agricultural settings, nitric acid generated during fertiliser nitrification represents a major source
      of strong acidity, particularly where nitrogen inputs exceed crop uptake or nitrogen use efficiency
1905  (NUE) is low.228,347,348 Sulfuric acid inputs can originate from atmospheric deposition or sulfur-
1906  containing fertilisers and can also contribute substantially to mineral dissolution without associated
1907  carbonic-acid consumption.349­351 Some feedstocks, particularly when waste-derived, may also
1908  contain sulfur-bearing phases that produce acidity upon oxidation.179
1909
1910  In addition to active acid inputs, many soils contain substantial pools of accumulated acidity stored
1911  on exchangeable sites. Acidic soils with low BS and high cation-exchange capacity contain large
1912  proton pools, delaying alkalinity export and suppressing measurable CO2 uptake via the release of
1913  acidity from exchangeable sites (cf., Section 3.4.2). In such systems, EW feedstocks may initially
1914  neutralise legacy acidity before contributing to net carbonic-acid weathering, making realised
1915  CDR strongly dependent on initial soil pH, BS, cation-exchange capacity, and land-use
1916  history.68,172,216 Over time, depending on land-use history and ongoing acid inputs, many
1917  agricultural soils may reacidify as exchangeable cations equilibrate with soil waters. This can
1918  remobilise cations that were initially retained on sorption sites, contributing to delayed alkalinity
1919  export and CDR rather than permanent loss.
1920
1921  3.4.3.2 Approaches to assessing strong-acid impacts
1922  Several complementary approaches are used to assess the impact of strong-acid weathering on
1923  EW-derived CDR. One method estimates fertiliser-derived acidity under the assumption of
1924  complete nitrification, sometimes combined with assumptions about NUE, and attributes this
      acidity to mineral dissolution that does not contribute to CDR.352 Related approaches infer strong-
1925  acid contributions from measured nitrate and sulphate fluxes in soils, leachates, or catchment
      waters, allowing acid-driven weathering to be quantified directly from observed anion
1926  exports.243,353
1927
1928  Other approaches infer the presence and magnitude of strong-acid weathering from internal
1929  geochemical constraints rather than from external acid inputs. Deviations between observed soil
1930  pH and pH predicted from soil pCO2 provide an estimate of the fraction of acidity--and associated
1931  mineral weathering--not attributable to carbonic acid.231 At the solution or catchment scale,
1932  charge-balance approaches--building on earlier work assessing the CO2 impacts of liming354--
1933  compare the sum of major base cations with bicarbonate alkalinity; any excess positive charge is
1934  attributed to strong-acid anions or to bicarbonate that has been consumed through reaction with
1935  strong acids, thereby quantifying lost alkalinity and associated CDR loss.243 Changing
1936  geochemical conditions downstream (e.g., the loss of nitrate via denitrification) will also shift the
1937  charge balance of a system, increasing or decreasing the total amount of bicarbonate in solution
1938  and shifting strong acid impacts, albeit over variable and uncertain timescales.
1939
1940
1941
1942
1943
1944
1945
1946

      53
      Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

1947  3.4.3.3 Magnitude of strong-acid weathering losses
      The magnitude of CDR losses via strong-acid weathering reported in experimental and modelling
1948  studies varies widely and is strongly controlled by soil acidity, fertiliser inputs, and system
1949  boundaries. In neutral or weakly acidified systems, strong-acid corrections are often small. For
1950  example, large-scale lysimeter experiments following basalt application on agricultural fields
1951  show similarly modest losses, on the order of 0.1­1.8% of potential CDR.353 Similarly, inclusion
1952  of strong-acid corrections typically results in only minor downward adjustments to net CDR
1953  estimates in field trials using concrete.299
1954
1955  In contrast, substantially larger impacts are observed in acidic systems or where strong-acid inputs
1956  or soil acidity pools dominate weathering reactions. For example, controlled experiments
1957  combining silicate amendments with acidifying fertilisers show large increases in cation release
1958  without corresponding increases in inorganic carbon, demonstrating that cation-based proxies can
1959  substantially overestimate CDR under these conditions.237 In acidic tropical soils in Australia, most
1960  mineral dissolution has been attributed to strong acids and exchangeable acidity rather than
1961  carbonic acid, with weathering dominated by surface-bound protons associated with exchangeable
1962  acidity (~45.9%), followed by nitric acid (~19.3%) and organic acids (~16.4%), while carbonic
1963  acid contributes only a minor fraction (~2.0%) of total dissolution; as a result, substantial mineral
1964  weathering can occur without commensurate CO2 uptake, and apparent CDR may remain low until
1965  accumulated soil acidity is neutralised and BS increases.216
1966
1967  Overall, the literature indicates that strong-acid weathering losses are often small in neutral soils
1968  but can be large in acidic or heavily fertilised systems, especially prior to exhaustion of legacy
1969  acidity pools. However, the importance of strong-acid weathering depends on the spatial and
1970  temporal boundary used for CDR accounting. Although strong acids can substantially reduce
1971  initial or near-field alkalinisation efficiency, downstream re-equilibration under less acidic
1972  conditions may partly recover bicarbonate alkalinity, for example through acidity neutralisation,
1973  nitrate removal, or transport into waters with greater buffering capacity. This distinction is relevant
1974  for full-system alkalinity approaches,345 but the magnitude, timing, and verifiability of any
1975  downstream recovery remain uncertain and may be difficult to align with project-scale MRV or
1976  crediting (cf., Section 4).
1977
1978  3.4.4 Biomass uptake
1979
      A portion of the base cations released through EW may be taken up by plants. While this can be a
1980  co-benefit of EW to farmers via increasing crop yields (Section 7.3.1), it negatively affects realised
      CDR. To maintain charge balance, uptake of base cations by plants is accompanied by a release of
1981  protons,355 resulting in a direct loss of realised CDR. The limited number of studies that have
1982  estimated the proportion of base cation plant uptake relative to soil retention indicate that this is a
1983  minor loss term. A field experiment on maize/soy and miscanthus cropping systems reported that
1984  1.1 and 4.5% of base cations were taken up by maize and miscanthus, respectively, with most of
1985  the remainder having leached to deeper subsurface soils.353 Similarly, a mesocosm experiment
1986  lasting for 101 days found that plant base cation charge equivalents were about two orders of
1987  magnitude lower that those retained in the soil.356
1988
1989
1990
1991

      54
      Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

1992  In addition to the direct loss of realised CDR due to base cation plant uptake, there may be an
1993  indirect gain in CDR through stimulating ecosystem productivity and storing more CO2 as organic
1994  carbon in vegetation or soils. This indirect CDR clearly dominates over the direct loss of realised
1995  CDR and may even be larger than the CDR directly resulting from EW. A global modelling study
1996  in which basalt was applied on sparsely populated, vegetated regions indicated that this
1997  productivity effect accounts for 32­80% of the realised CDR.75 A modelling study conducted by
1998  70 on a wollastonite-amended forested watershed impacted by acid rain found that the CDR by
1999  increased wood production was 1­2 orders of magnitude higher than that from mineral weathering.
2000  However, organic and inorganic CDR pathways differ fundamentally in durability, reversibility,
2001  and monitoring requirements: enhanced organic carbon storage is generally much less durable than
2002  inorganic CDR from EW,2 and decomposition of additional biomass can re-release both carbon
2003  and retained base cations. It is therefore important that CDR estimates do not conflate organic and
2004  inorganic effects into a single undifferentiated value. In this review, we assess only the inorganic
2005  CDR effects associated with EW, which are linked to longer storage timescales and higher
2006  durability.
2007
2008  The fate of biomass determines the net impact of plant base cation uptake on realised CDR through
2009  EW and the timescale at which this is relevant.220 Annual biomass turnover results in soil litter
2010  input, directly returning base cations to the soil when decomposed. Upon harvesting, crop residues
2011  added to the soil as well as root biomass can also result in quick biomass decomposition and cation
2012  release into the soil. Indirectly, most base cations removed through harvesting may travel through
2013  the food chain and be released again via animal and human faeces and detritus. However, the
2014  amount, timescale, and location of return is uncertain and will depend, for example, on whether
2015  cations are retained in wastewater treatment plants and where wastewater is released. However,
2016  one may assume that a portion of these base cations will re-enter ecosystems and, at that point,
2017  contribute to the realised CDR.
2018
2019  3.4.5 Surface passivation and reduction in rates

2020  Researchers have shown that surface area-normalised weathering rates of minerals are slower in
      the field than in the laboratory, at least partly because such rates slow with the duration of
2021  weathering. For example, based on published EW data, rates of basalt weathering decrease by
2022  factors of 10­100 over 10 years.357 Only a few very fast- or slow-dissolving minerals such as
2023  calcite, wollastonite and quartz have been reported to react at comparable rates in the field and
2024  laboratory.71,358,359 The simplest explanation for the lab-field discrepancy is that the processes that
2025  limit the rate in larger systems do not occur in smaller systems nor in experiments 357. Factors that
2026  decrease rates in field systems that are of interest to EW (i.e., over 1­100 y) can be roughly
2027  categorised as those that (i) change the surface of the feedstock; (ii) accumulate the weathering
2028  products without release to pore fluids; (iii) are hydrologic in nature; or (iv) are related to structural
2029  changes and system-level feedbacks.
2030
2031  Most practitioners predict the rate of CO2 uptake into pore fluids based on the mass or surface area
2032  of feedstock added at time zero. However, rates may decrease over time due to changes to
2033  feedstock surfaces such armouring of surfaces by organics or inorganic precipitates or
2034  sorbates.360,361 Similarly, the surface can be restructured by episodic wetting and drying362 and the
2035
2036

      55
      Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

2037  number of reactive sites on a mineral decrease as natural fluids approach chemical equilibrium.363
2038  All of these processes slow dissolution by removing, occluding, or deactivating reactive sites.
2039
2040  Some factors related to the accumulation of weathering products without release to pore fluids are
2041  also related to mineral surfaces, but here we emphasize processes that decrease the rate as
2042  measured in leachate. When aqueous cations are not released to solution but instead accumulate
2043  on the soil exchangeable pool, in biota, or in precipitates, the rate of release of cations and
2044  production of alkalinity in the aqueous phase appears slow even if initial weathering may not. Such
2045  cation uptake can also be accompanied by proton release which may further decrease the uptake
2046  of atmospheric CO2. Once the ion exchange pool saturates with respect to the amendment-released
2047  cations, or biota stop growing, further release of cations from EW and associated CDR equivalent
2048  to the true rate of amendment dissolution can be realised. Where secondary minerals precipitate,
2049  however, the mass of precipitate can increase and limit CDR indefinitely. Particularly, if carbonate
2050  minerals precipitate, then CO2 is sequestered in the solid phase: this represents a net sink for
2051  atmospheric CO2 if the amendment is a silicate, whereas the system is neither a sink nor a source
2052  for atmospheric CO2 if the amendment is a carbonate.
2053
2054  Furthermore, the mass or surface area of the amendment may not always be the appropriate scaling
2055  factor for extrapolating rates to the field. Hydrologic effects can cause rates to be limited by the
2056  transport of products or reactants instead of surface reaction. In fully water-saturated soils, for
2057  example, transport of CO2 may limit dissolution. Such factors do not affect the soil homogeneously
2058  because soils are not mineralogically, biogeochemically, nor physically homogeneous.364 The
2059  wide variety of pore sizes in soils mean that macropores can channelize water, leaving the rest of
2060  the surface area coated only in a stagnant, chemically equilibrated fluid film. In addition to such
2061  spatial heterogeneity that can slow bulk rates, temporal heterogeneity also limits rates: wetting and
2062  drying exposes amendments to fresh, flowing water only for short periods of time.
2063
2064  Lastly, effects related to structural changes and system-level feedbacks in the soil--driven by
2065  coupling among transport and chemical reactions--can also reduce rates of EW in field systems.
2066  Such rate-inhibited coupling between chemical reactions and concentration gradients is often
2067  driven by biota.365 In a macro-example, bioaccumulation of weathering products in plants
2068  eventually stops when plants die and decompose, releasing elements back to porewaters near the
2069  soil surface, where high concentrations can slow reaction rates.366,367 The result of such spatial
2070  structuring of soil composition is that bulk reaction rates slow.
2071
2072  If all the rates in every pore could be modelled accurately for appropriate averaging across the
2073  entire porous medium of the soil for all points in time, the bulk-observed rates could be predicted
2074  in EW--but this is well beyond model capability. Ultimately, accurate rate prediction is difficult
2075  for any given natural system, and most practitioners have resorted to tuning the "hydrologically
2076  accessible reactive surface area" as a way to predict rates in natural systems.
2077
2078  3.4.6 Lag times

2079  Time delays between EW deployment and the emergence of measurable alkalinity export or
      climate-relevant CDR are widely observed across laboratory, field, and modelling studies. These
2080
2081

      56
      Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

2082  lag times arise not only from limits on mineral dissolution, but also from constraints on the
2083  realisation of CDR after weathering has occurred (e.g., soil chemical buffering and secondary
2084  phase formation), such that alkalinity generation does not necessarily translate into immediate
2085  downstream alkalinity export and associated CDR.68,172 In particular, a RTM-based study identifies
2086  temporary storage of weathering-derived Ca2+ and Mg2+ on soil exchange sites as a dominant
2087  mechanism generating lagged CDR, because exchange reactions can decouple mineral dissolution
2088  from immediate alkalinity export and CO2 uptake (cf., Section 3.4.2; Figure 15c). These
2089  simulations demonstrate that alkalinity generation and realised CDR can be fully decoupled in
2090  time, even when feedstock dissolution is effectively instantaneous.68
2091
2092  Lag times can be further extended by temporary retention of alkalinity in secondary solid phases,
2093  including carbonate precipitates and secondary silicates, and by re-equilibration of DIC under
2094  conditions where soil or porewaters approach saturation. Such phases can sequester weathering-
2095  derived cations and alkalinity for extended periods, suppressing export and, potentially, realised
2096  CDR until renewed undersaturation, episodic acid inputs, or hydrological flushing drive their
2097  dissolution. Depending on environmental conditions, these processes may represent either
2098  permanent losses or reversible, time-dependent storage of alkalinity.68,172
2099
2100  Model results indicate that lag times between alkalinity input and realised CDR can range from
2101  less than one year to many decades, depending primarily on soil CEC, initial BS, target soil pH,
2102  and hydrological throughput.68 In large parts of the U.S. Corn Belt and Great Plains, less than 50%
2103  of CDR potential is realised within the first decade after EW deployment, with multi-decadal
2104  timescales required to approach steady state, whereas soils with lower CEC and/or higher initial
2105  BS--particularly in parts of the southeastern United States--exhibit substantially shorter lags.68
2106  Consistent with these results, shorter duration laboratory and field studies frequently observe rapid
2107  silicate dissolution and increases in exchangeable Ca2+ or Mg2+ without corresponding short-term
2108  increases in alkalinity or DIC export.184,222,223,237,326,343
2109
2110  Long-term catchment-scale evidence for lagged alkalinity export is also provided by the
2111  wollastonite addition experiments at the Hubbard Brook Experimental Forest.70­74,368 Wollastonite
2112  application led to rapid initial mineral dissolution, increases in soil pH, and large increases in
2113  exchangeable Ca2+ and BS in surface soils, yet the appearance of wollastonite-derived Ca in stream
2114  waters was delayed by several years--demonstrating substantial interim storage within soils and
2115  vegetation.70,71,369,370 Mass-balance studies show that even 6­12 years after application, only ~2­
2116  5% of the feedstock Ca was exported in stream waters, while most Ca remained either undissolved
2117  (54%) or retained on exchange sites (44%) particularly in the organic horizon, consistent with
2118  strong CEC buffering in an acid-depleted system.72,74 Elevated Ca:Si ratios (>2:1) in soil solutions
2119  further indicated non-congruent dissolution and/or post-dissolution immobilisation of Si via
2120  secondary mineral formation, implying that both cation exchange and secondary phase processes
2121  contribute to delayed solute export.71,74 Even after Ca export increased, bicarbonate-based CDR
2122  remained suppressed for more than a decade because a large fraction of weathering-derived
2123  alkalinity was neutralised by strong acids associated with legacy sulphate deposition and ongoing
2124  nitrogen cycling.70 As sulphate deposition and exchangeable acidity declined over time, the
2125  fraction of Ca2+ export charge-balanced by bicarbonate increased, producing a delayed but
2126  sustained increase in catchment-scale inorganic CDR.70 While Hubbard Brook demonstrates how
2127  lag times can emerge from interacting processes such as cation exchange, biological uptake,

      57
      Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

2128  secondary phase formation, and strong-acid weathering, it represents an intentionally Ca-limited,
2129  strongly acidified forest system in which tree uptake constituted a major Ca sink and the
2130  wollastonite amendment itself was originally applied to alleviate stand-level Ca deficiency.72,74
2131  Consequently, the observed delays likely approximate an upper bound on lag magnitude relative
2132  to many agricultural soils with lower biological Ca demand that are less impacted by historical
2133  anthropogenic acidity.
2134
2135  Across systems, lag times are expected to be longest in environments characterized by low pH,
2136  high CEC, and large reserves of exchangeable acidity, as well as in settings where secondary
2137  phases are near saturation and hydrological flushing is limited. Seasonal variability in infiltration
2138  and redox conditions can further modulate lag behaviour by controlling the timing of cation
2139  remobilisation and alkalinity export.68,172,252 These lag effects pose a fundamental challenge for
2140  MRV frameworks that assume a near-term correspondence between feedstock dissolution and
2141  climate-relevant CDR. Indeed, many short-term experiments capture systems in the early, lag-
2142  dominated phase where dissolution, cation release, and bicarbonate export are strongly decoupled,
2143  making long-term inference difficult. In the long term, translating such observations into robust
2144  CDR estimates may therefore require process-based models that explicitly represent cation
2145  exchange, secondary phase dynamics, strong-acid weathering, and hydrological transport.68,252,371
2146  At present, attribution of delayed CDR based on models alone is not MRV-ready and requires
2147  rigorous validation against long-term field datasets which are largely lacking.68 In the interim,
2148  conservative crediting approaches must either discount delayed CDR ex ante or rely on ex post
2149  verification of alkalinity export along the soil­river continuum, explicitly accounting for lag
2150  effects.
2151
2152  An important contextual point is that delayed net CDR is not unique to EW but is a general
2153  characteristic of many CDR approaches, which often exhibit "payback periods" before net climate
2154  benefits emerge.372 For example, LCA of biochar systems show that pyrolysis can generate an
2155  initial pulse of emissions that would otherwise have been released more gradually during natural
2156  biomass decay, such that net removal depends on both feedstock counterfactuals (i.e., what would
2157  have happened with the biomass in the absence of biochar production) and time horizon.373,374
2158  Similarly, chemical CDR systems such as DACCS can be net-emitting during early operational
2159  phases when accounting for construction, sorbent production, and energy supply, with net removal
2160  achieved only after sufficient operating time under low-carbon energy inputs.32,375 These examples
2161  underscore that temporal asymmetry between deployment and realised climate benefit is a
2162  pervasive feature of CDR, reinforcing the need for accounting frameworks that explicitly track
2163  time-dependent net fluxes rather than assuming instantaneous removal.
2164
2165  One proposed approach for managing such temporal mismatches in EW crediting is to pair
2166  delayed, durable CO2 removal with near-term mitigation of short-lived climate pollutants,
2167  particularly methane.376 Because methane reductions generate rapid climate benefits while EW-
2168  derived CDR may be realised over longer timescales, such "stacking" could help bridge radiative-
2169  forcing gaps during lag-dominated phases of EW deployment. This approach would require careful
2170  accounting to avoid double counting and to ensure that methane reductions and durable CDR are
2171  tracked as distinct climate benefits.
2172
2173

      58
      Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

2174  3.4.7 Degassing in rivers and oceans

2175  Waters passing through soils become enriched in CO2, such that their concentrations could exceed
2176  thousands of parts per million by volume (ppmv). A soil water in equilibrium with 104 ppmv CO2
2177  gas will contain ~340 µmol kg-1 of aqueous CO2 as expressed by Henry's law (at 25°C; ref. 377):

2182  drives CO2 degassing (i.e., evasion).
2183

2223  an average value of ~15-20% cumulative loss (both rivers and ocean) is more appropriate for long-

2229  This may be the reason why lower losses are returned from EW simulations coupled to an ocean
2230  biogeochemistry model.22,279 Similarly to rivers, secondary precipitation of minerals in the surface
2231  ocean may result in further evasion of CO2 (Section 3.4.1).
