      3.5 Overview of ecological and environmental risks of EW
2238
2239  Enhanced Weathering combines elements of a long-established agricultural practice with features
      that are novel at scale. However, EW pursued for CDR differs in important respects, including the
2240
2241
2242

      60
      Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

2243  broader range of feedstocks employed, deployment on soils that are not necessarily too acidic for
2244  optimal agronomic performance, and the intention to operate at spatial and temporal scales far
2245  beyond conventional soil amendment practices, all of which introduce additional environmental
2246  and ecological uncertainties. A comprehensive evaluation of environmental risks and
2247  corresponding mitigation strategies is available in prior syntheses (e.g., ref. 131). The following
2248  section provides a high-level overview of key considerations relevant to deployment, while a more
2249  detailed empirical assessment of how frequently these risks have been observed in field trials is
2250  presented in Section 7.
2251
      3.5.1 Environmental risks
2252
      Many feedstocks proposed for EW contain trace contaminants, including potentially toxic
2253  metals.69,129,150,248,382 Even when present at low concentrations in feedstock, repeated applications
2254  could lead to accumulation in soils to levels considered hazardous for crop health or food
2255  safety.129,131,383­385 Scenario analyses based on measured feedstock composition and typical
2256  application rates therefore emphasize the importance of careful feedstock screening and, where
2257  necessary, limiting application frequency or duration to prevent exceedance of regulatory
2258  thresholds.69,129
2259
2260  At the same time, total concentrations alone do not determine ecological risk. Mineral amendments
2261  commonly raise soil pH, which can enhance sorption of certain metals to mineral surfaces and
2262  thereby reduce their bioavailability to plants and soil organisms.109,386 Consequently, both
2263  accumulation and speciation should be monitored131 and, where possible, modelled as part of EW
2264  assessment frameworks. If elevated metal concentrations arise, mitigation options include
2265  phytoremediation approaches,150 adjustments to application strategies,129 and stricter feedstock
2266  selection criteria.248
2267
2268  Potential downstream transport must also be considered. Fine mineral particles are susceptible to
2269  erosion and may be mobilised into surface waters, where associated metals could contribute to
2270  ecotoxicological risks.162,180,387 However, export rates are expected to be site-specific and often
2271  limited, and geochemical changes induced by mineral additions--such as elevated pH or
2272  adsorption to reactive mineral phases--may in some cases decrease metal bioavailability in aquatic
2273  environments.388
2274
2275  Alteration of aqueous chemistry is more likely to occur through shifts in alkalinity and pH,
2276  processes broadly analogous to those produced by conventional liming. Enhanced Weathering is
2277  therefore expected to increase alkalinity export from treated systems,17,65­67 with predominantly
2278  beneficial effects in historically acidified catchments.131,179 Under such conditions, mineral
2279  additions may contribute to partial chemical restoration of acidified waters, although site selection
2280  criteria optimized for CDR could extend these interventions to regions where liming has not
2281  traditionally been applied (e.g., ref. 389).
2282
2283  Airborne particulates represent an additional potential exposure pathway; fine silicate dust
2284  generated during comminution and field application poses recognised inhalation hazards.131,383,387
2285  Certain lithologies may also contain asbestos-forming or naturally radioactive minerals,
2286  underscoring the need for strict exclusion criteria during feedstock sourcing.131,383,390 Practices,
2287

      61
      Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

2288  protocols, and equipment for safely amending agricultural land with fine particulates exist as a
2289  result of longstanding agricultural liming practice, but additional care should be taken with fine
2290  silicate dust. Beyond site-level effects, life cycle emissions associated with extraction, processing,
2291  and transport--such as NO released during fuel combustion--must be incorporated into
2292  environmental assessments for large-scale deployment.385
2293
2294  3.5.2 Strategies for Mitigation, Monitoring, and Safeguards

2295  Prior to deployment, careful site selection, feedstock screening, and baseline characterization can
      substantially reduce environmental risks.131 Site selection should prioritize locations with the
2296  lowest inherent vulnerability and should include establishment of baseline conditions for key
2297  indicators, including soil chemistry and function, aquatic ecosystem status, airborne particulate
2298  concentrations, and contaminant levels relevant to food safety and human health. Feedstock
2299  screening should exclude certain mineral sources entirely--for example those containing asbestos
2300  or radionuclides131,383,390--while documenting concentrations of other constituents such as heavy
2301  metals to enable adaptive management through adjusted application strategies thresholds.129,131 In
2302  addition, predefined safeguard thresholds for environmental indicators should be established based
2303  on existing regulatory standards for air and water quality, occupational exposure, and contaminant
2304  concentrations in soils, crops, and ecosystems.129,131
2305
2306  During deployment, risks to air and water quality can be reduced through established
2307  environmental-management practices. Covering mineral stockpiles during storage and transport,
2308  maintaining setback distances from waterways and residential areas, avoiding unnecessarily fine
2309  particle-size distributions where compatible with CDR performance, and implementing
2310  engineering dust controls such as moisture conditioning, enclosed handling systems, dust
2311  collectors at crushing or grinding facilities, and dust suppression on haul roads can substantially
2312  limit particulate emissions.20,131,383,387,391­394 Personal protective equipment and operational
2313  constraints--such as enclosed vehicle cabs, reduced vehicle speeds during spreading, and avoiding
2314  application during dry or high-wind conditions--further reduce inhalation exposure to respirable
2315  mineral dust, including crystalline silica, which is associated with well-documented occupational
2316  health risks.131,395­397 High-frequency or continuous environmental monitoring allows for adaptive
2317  management should thresholds be exceeded, compensating for unknown variables such as the
2318  actual bioavailability or leachability of heavy metal contaminants.131 Monitoring can also target
2319  key areas of concern, such as fisheries for water quality or neighbouring schools for air quality.
2320
2321  Environmental risks can also be reduced through careful avoidance of high-risk deployment
2322  contexts. Sites with elevated background metal concentrations, whether from historical
2323  agrochemical use or geogenic sources, may be unsuitable because additional inputs could increase
2324  the likelihood of exceeding ecological or food-safety thresholds.131 Sensitive ecosystems and
2325  downstream environments should likewise be excluded, especially those characterized by highly
2326  acidic soils such as wetlands or bogs that host specialized microbial communities with limited
2327  tolerance to geochemical disturbance,398 as well as habitats dominated by dust-sensitive organisms
2328  including lichens, liverworts, and sphagnum.399 Certain soil and climatic conditions can further
2329  elevate risk: poorly drained or fine-textured soils may favour secondary mineral accumulation that
2330  degrades soil structure and hydrologic functioning,131 while arid or windy environments can
2331
2332

      62
      Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

2333  enhance dust generation and persistence that increases ecological and occupational exposure
2334  hazards. Incorporating these environmental constraints into site-selection frameworks therefore
2335  represents a primary preventative strategy for minimizing unintended impacts.131
2336
2337  Should exceedance of predefined environmental thresholds occur, mitigation measures should be
2338  supported through contingency funds held in reserve by project developers. Potential responses
2339  include targeted phytoremediation using hyperaccumulating plants to remove trace metals,150 soil-
2340  management interventions such as organic amendments--including biochar400,401--to enhance
2341  sorption capacity and stabilise contaminants, or tillage and related practices to maintain soil
2342  structure and water-holding capacity.400 Establishing financial and operational provisions for such
2343  responses in advance can help ensure that corrective actions are implemented rapidly and
2344  effectively.
2345
2346  Beyond project-level monitoring, evaluation of cumulative or spatially distributed impacts
2347  requires coordinated data sharing and governance across projects and jurisdictions. Because many
2348  environmental effects propagate downstream, protecting freshwater and marine ecosystems will
2349  often depend primarily on managing upstream inputs. Accordingly, response strategies for
2350  downstream impacts should be incorporated into project design from the outset, with monitoring
2351  frameworks structured to detect watershed-scale changes and enable coordinated mitigation where
2352  necessary.
2353
2354  3.6 Modelling approaches in EW research

2355  Modelling has played a central role in EW research from its early conceptualization to more recent
      assessments of deployment potential, costs, and co-benefits. Across the literature, models are used
2356  for several partially overlapping purposes:
2357
2358      · simulating mineral dissolution kinetics and CO2 consumption under idealized or site-
2359           specific conditions for individual deployments;
2360
2361      · representing soil, hydrological, and catchment-scale transport and transformation of
2362           weathering products;
2363
2364      · embedding EW into regional-to-global carbon cycle and Earth system frameworks;
2365      · estimating life cycle emissions, economic costs, and system-level trade-offs for at-scale
2366
2367           deployment; and
2368      · estimating the contribution of EW to climate change mitigation and emissions/CDR
2369
2370           pathways scenarios.
2371
2372  The diversity of modelling approaches reflects both the multi-scale nature of EW processes and
2373  the current scarcity of long-term, spatially resolved empirical datasets against which models can
2374  be comprehensively validated.

      63
      Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

2375  3.6.1 Simulating weathering reactions and CO2 uptake

2376  3.6.1.1 Reactive transport and geochemical process models
      Many EW modelling studies employ RTMs or closely related soil geochemical frameworks to
2377  represent mineral dissolution, aqueous speciation, formation of secondary phases, and acid­base
2378  reactions in soils and porewaters, particularly when inferring large-scale processes and CDR
2379  potentials.19,20,24,68,402 These models typically resolve mineral kinetics explicitly, often using rate
2380  laws derived from laboratory experiments, and track the evolution of soil and solution chemistry
2381  as a function of time, depth, and hydrological fluxes.
2382
2383  Common implementations include one-dimensional vertical RTMs based on existing chemical
2384  models403 such as PHREEQC108,125,404 and TOUGHREACT,97 as well as more EW-tailored models
2385  such as SCEPTER,24,68,110,232 and the LC3M models19,20,22 amongst others. An alternative
2386  approach, SMEW, employs zero-dimensional models that focus exclusively on the upper soil
2387  layers prioritizing the temporal dynamics of ecohydrological fluxes at the expense of explicit
2388  vertical process representation.252,371,405 All together, these approaches allow detailed interrogation
2389  of how pH, temperature, fluid residence time, and mineral surface area jointly control dissolution
2390  rates and alkalinity generation. Their principal strength lies in mechanistic transparency: individual
2391  processes (e.g., kinetic inhibition, secondary mineral precipitation, or cation exchange buffering)
2392  can be isolated and tested. Importantly, however, RTMs do not all include the same processes or
2393  represent them in the same way, such that the specific mechanisms that can be interrogated depend
2394  on model structure and parameterization. As a result, RTMs are often data-intensive and sensitive
2395  to parameters that are frequently poorly constrained, including reactive surface area, saturation
2396  thresholds for secondary phase formation, effective flow paths, vertical variability in soil
2397  physicochemical properties, and the treatment of biological feedbacks.406,407
2398
2399  3.6.1.2 Shrinking-core and particle-scale models
      Most of the RTMs introduced above implement or include options for a shrinking-core­type
2400  formulation to represent the progressive dissolution of primary mineral grains, explicitly tracking
      particle size as dissolution proceeds. In addition to their use within RTMs, shrinking-core
2401  approaches are also applied as stand-alone models that do not explicitly simulate soil geochemical
2402  evolution. In such cases, grain size (or a grain-size distribution) is coupled to assumed area-
2403  normalised weathering rate laws to generate dissolution or weathering-rate trajectories under
2404  prescribed solution conditions.185,408­412 These models are computationally efficient and well-
2405  suited for sensitivity analyses of grain size distributions and application rates. Their simplicity,
2406  however, comes at the cost of limited environmental realism. Most implementations assume
2407  uniform exposure to solution--neglecting aggregation and evolving saturation state increases that
2408  limit weathering rates--and therefore tend to represent upper-bound dissolution scenarios rather
2409  than field-realistic behaviour.
2410
2411  3.6.2 From soils to rivers to oceans: transport and fate of weathering products
2412
2413  To link in-field weathering to downstream CDR, EW models increasingly extend beyond soils to
2414  include catchment-scale river transport and ocean biogeochemistry. River network and mass-
      balance models are used to estimate the capacity of fluvial systems to export alkalinity and DIC as
2415

2416
2417
2418

      64
      Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

2419  well as to assess CDR losses along riverine transport.17,22,30,65,275,379,413 These approaches highlight
2420  the importance of residence times, gas exchange, and carbonate precipitation in regulating net
2421  CDR efficiency. In parallel, some foundational assessments constrain storage efficiency in the
2422  oceans using thermodynamic equilibrium carbonate chemistry--placing first-order bounds on
2423  persistence and CO2 outgassing without explicitly resolving kinetics or hydrologic routing
2424  upstream of delivery to the coastal ocean.18,37,126
2425
2426  At larger scales, ocean biogeochemical and Earth system models such as cGENIE, BIOGEM, and
2427  BICYCLE have been used to explore the long-term fate of alkalinity additions and their feedbacks
2428  on ocean chemistry and atmospheric CO2.17,20,22,24,279,413­415 Although these models have been
2429  indispensable for assessing large-scale durability and leakage risks, they operate at spatial and
2430  temporal resolutions far removed from individual EW projects. Many of the modelling tools
2431  developed for terrestrial weathering, nutrient cycling, and carbon­climate feedbacks in other
2432  contexts are, in principle, applicable to EW.403 However, EW-specific parameterizations remain
2433  sparse, and cross-model consistency in parameterization, scenario testing, and interoperability
2434  remains a significant challenge.
2435
2436  3.6.3 Integrated assessment and life cycle models

2437  Beyond process-based simulations, EW is represented in integrated assessment models (IAMs)
      such as GCAM, EPPA, and REMIND-MAgPIE, where it appears as a stylized CDR option with
2438  prescribed costs, efficiencies, and deployment constraints (see Section 11, e.g. refs. 23,78,416­419).
2439  These models are essential for exploring system-level interactions with energy, land use, and
2440  climate policy but necessarily rely on simplified representations of weathering processes.
2441  Similarly, LCA and optimization models (e.g., refs. 250,420­430) are used to quantify upstream
2442  emissions, energy requirements, and trade-offs associated with mining, comminution, transport,
2443  and application (see Section 8). While critical for bounding net CDR, these models typically treat
2444  weathering efficiency as an external input rather than an emergent property.
2445
2446  3.6.4 Model requirements and challenges
2447
2448  Across all model classes, a recurring limitation is the lack of long-term, spatially resolved
      validation datasets that capture the coupled dissolution, transport, and downstream fate of carbon
2449  and alkalinity. Many models are calibrated using laboratory kinetics or short-term field trials, then
      extrapolated across years to decades and from plot to national, continental, or global scales.
2450  Discrepancies between model predictions and empirical observations have been reported at
2451  multiple scales,252 underscoring the need for coordinated field measurements designed explicitly
2452  for model testing and validation. Key data gaps include effective reactive surface area (and surface
2453  area evolution) under field conditions, hydrological residence times relevant for alkalinity export,
2454  rates and saturation state thresholds of secondary phase formation and dissolution, mineral
2455  distribution heterogeneity in feedstocks, and biologically mediated feedbacks.252 Without
2456  harmonised datasets and a coordinated effort to transparently evaluate parametric and structural
2457  uncertainty across models, intercomparison between models remains challenging and apparent
2458  agreement may reflect shared assumptions rather than independent validation.
2459
2460
2461
2462

      65
      Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

2463  The diversity of EW models has not yet been matched by systematic model intercomparison
2464  efforts. Studies using different RTMs, shrinking-core formulations, or Earth system models often
2465  produce divergent estimates even under nominally similar scenarios. Harmonisation of inputs,
2466  boundary conditions, and reporting conventions would enable clearer attribution of differences to
2467  model structure versus parameter choice. Hence, despite their value, existing EW models are not
2468  currently suitable as stand-alone tools for MRV.68,431,432 Most models are insufficiently constrained
2469  by observations, lack robust uncertainty quantification at project scales, and cannot independently
2470  verify that modelled CO2 uptake corresponds to durable CDR. In practice, models are best viewed
2471  as complements to empirical monitoring, helping to interpret measurements, estimate
2472  unobservable and downstream losses fluxes, and test consistency across system boundaries. Until
2473  models can be routinely validated against long-term field and catchment-scale datasets, MRV
2474  frameworks that rely solely on modelled outcomes risk overstating confidence and
2475  underrepresenting uncertainty. This limitation is not unique to EW but is particularly acute given
2476  the complex and lagged nature of EW-driven CDR.
2477
2478  3.6.5 Experimental and model disparities
2479
      Estimating CDR from EW requires consistent quantification of three coupled elements: the amount
2480  of mineral that dissolves, the subsequent fate of weathering products within the soil­water system,
      and the interactions between weathering products and the soil­water­air carbon system.
2481  Accurately representing this sequence of processes in models remains challenging due to the
2482  diversity of soil chemical, biological, and hydrological processes that regulate weathering kinetics
2483  (many of which remain poorly constrained quantitatively), as well as the partitioning of released
2484  base cations among dissolved, exchangeable, biological, and secondary-mineral pools. Site-
2485  specific disparities between observations and their representation in models for a given component
2486  are therefore common and propagate directly into uncertainty in EW projections. Within this
2487  framework, the largest source of uncertainty is typically associated with weathering rates, for
2488  which observed-to-expected deviations span several orders of magnitude.172,357
2489
2490  Discrepancies between modelled and observed weathering rates represent a long-standing
2491  challenge in geochemistry.119 Most mineral-specific weathering rate formulations account for
2492  climate effects through temperature dependence, soil acidity via pH, and saturation state with rate
2493  constants derived from laboratory experiments performed under abiotic, well-mixed, and far-from
2494  equilibrium conditions.123 When extrapolated to field conditions, these formulations tend to
2495  overestimate dissolution rates across a wide range of minerals, environmental settings,
2496  experimental scales, and lithologies. Evidence for this mismatch in the EW context is reported in
2497  natural weathering analogues,102 laboratory-scale soil experiments,162,184 and field applications,405
2498  such that agreement between observed and modelled weathering kinetics is the exception rather
2499  than the rule.
2500
2501  Recent model­experiment comparisons further illustrate this variability.252 Using the
2502  ecohydrological­biogeochemical model SMEW,252 some experimental EW datasets could only be
2503  reproduced by reducing modelled weathering rates by up to two orders of magnitude,98,162,177
2504  whereas only one required no adjustment.108 Subsequent comparisons with field data confirm this
2505
2506
2507

      66
      Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

2508  range, with inferred deviations spanning from order unity353,371 to reductions of approximately two
2509  orders of magnitude.405 While other model­data comparisons, such as those based on the STELLA
2510  model,433 indicate good agreement with extrapolated experimental results as well as with a field
2511  dataset,353 these field estimates have been questioned due to large measurement uncertainty--
2512  suggesting that inferred CDR rates may not be statistically distinguishable from zero.434,435
2513
2514  Despite advances in RTMs, including increased emphasis on hydrological controls,238,436,437
2515  persistent discrepancies between modelled and observed weathering rates are generally attributed
2516  to rate-limiting processes in heterogeneous, multiphase soils that are not captured by agitated-flask
2517  experiments or standard dissolution formulations. These include diffusive limitations at mineral­
2518  water interfaces, incomplete or transient mineral­water contact, and surface passivation by
2519  secondary mineral or organic coatings.172,438 Available evidence suggests that, when combined,
2520  these inhibitory processes often outweigh potential biotically driven enhancements of weathering
2521  rates in soils.439,440 In addition, the spatial and temporal scales of analysis play a critical role: as
2522  integration scales increase, effective weathering fluxes tend to decrease.357 Collectively, these
2523  findings indicate that weathering rates are strongly regulated by site-specific rock­soil
2524  interactions, limiting the generalizability of model-based estimates and motivating caution when
2525  estimating EW CDR potential without observational validation.161,352
2526
2527  Beyond uncertainties in weathering kinetics, additional uncertainty arises from the fate of
2528  weathering products--specifically base cations released during dissolution--and their
2529  contribution to CDR. This distinction underlies the difference between potential and effective CDR
2530  in soils. Measurements that quantify weathered material directly (e.g., soil-based
2531  approaches),352,441 as well as model-based estimates of weathering fluxes,19,20,23 provide upper-
2532  bound (potential) CDR estimates under the assumption that released cations fully equilibrate with
2533  the water­air system, yielding bicarbonate formation at near-stoichiometric efficiency (base
2534  cation:bicarbonate charge ratios of 1:1 in soils or ~0.8 in the surface ocean).37,214
2535
2536  Experimental evidence and soil biogeochemical observations indicate, however, that a substantial
2537  fraction of weathering-derived cations can be retained within soils through cation exchange, plant
2538  uptake, or secondary mineral precipitation, thereby limiting the fraction available for realising
2539  CDR and downward transport.108,162,172,184 Bridging potential and effective CDR therefore requires
2540  either coupled measurements, such as leaching-water-based estimates for effective removal442
2541  combined with soil-based estimates for potential removal, or observational constraints integrated
2542  with process-based models. In this context, available evidence suggests that uncertainty associated
2543  with the partitioning of weathering products in soils, when represented explicitly, is smaller than
2544  the uncertainty associated with weathering rates themselves.110,252 Additionally, recent studies
2545  indicate that EW applications alter SOC through biotic pathways,224 even dominating the overall
2546  carbon balance in some settings. Accurately assessing total CDR therefore requires observational
2547  constraints and emerging coupled representations of inorganic and organic carbon dynamics.92,371

      67
      Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

2548  4 Monitoring, verification, and reporting

2549  Monitoring, verification, and reporting are central to establishing EW as a credible, scalable, and
2550  durable CDR strategy. Demonstrating net CDR from EW at the field scale under real-world
2551  agronomic and environmental conditions may draw from a variety of methodologies. Field-scale
2552  MRV must resolve the fate of weathering-derived carbon across solid, aqueous, and gaseous pools
2553  while accounting for spatial heterogeneity in soils, hydrology, and management practices. Many
2554  of these approaches325,383 and related quantitative measurements192 have been reviewed in detail
2555  elsewhere. In this section, we provide a brief overview and develop a synthesis comparing different
2556  MRV approaches. We structure MRV around three primary domains: (i) the solid phase, including
2557  soil mass balance approaches, the formation of SIC, and changes in CEC and BS; (ii) the aqueous
2558  phase, encompassing alterations in alkalinity and DIC fluxes, shifts in cation export, and the use
2559  of electrical conductivity (EC) and resin devices; and (iii) the gas phase, addressing potential CO2
2560  and other GHG exchanges. We then evaluate isotopic approaches that can be utilized in MRV,
2561  including emerging isotopic tracer techniques. Finally, we evaluate spatial and temporal
2562  mismatches among these approaches and their implications for uncertainty and attribution.
2563
2564  4.1 Solid phase

2565  Quantifying CDR in the solid phase is central to field-scale MRV of EW, as soils represent both
      the primary reaction environment and a potential medium for carbon storage. Solid phase
2566  measurements aim to capture mineral dissolution, secondary mineral formation, and the
2567  stabilisation of weathering-derived carbon within soil pools. This includes: (i) soil mass balance
2568  approaches to constrain elemental gains and losses associated with weathering; (ii) the formation
2569  and accumulation of SIC as paedogenic carbonates; and (iii) shifts in soil chemical properties such
2570  as cation concentrations adsorbed to exchangeable sites (usually CEC and BS based), which reflect
2571  changes in exchangeable base cations and soil buffering status. Together, these metrics provide
2572  complementary lines of evidence for tracking weathering progress, attributing carbon
2573  sequestration pathways, and evaluating the permanence and co-benefits of EW within managed
2574  field systems.
2575
2576  4.1.1 Soil Mass Balance
2577
2578  Soil mass balance approaches to MRV in EW are used to estimate the extent of chemical
      weathering of rock feedstocks that have been applied to soils.231,243,280,335,343,352,435,439,441,443­445
2579  These approaches involve measuring the elemental composition of the solid or mineral phase of a
      soil-feedstock mixture after a period of time, during which weathering is likely to have occurred,
2580  and calculating the loss of mobile metal cations (Ca2+, Mg2+, Na+, K+) from this phase relative to
2581  the initial soil-feedstock mixture. This loss can be used to determine a potential amount of CDR
2582  from weathering if the assumption is made that these cations charge-balance (bi)carbonate
2583  alkalinity in solution.
2584
2585  The initial amount of feedstock relative to soil at individual sampling locations analysed for
2586  element loss can be estimated based on sampling immediately after spreading feedstock or can be
2587
2588
2589
2590

      68
      Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

2591  assumed based on known application and mixing. However, this is unlikely to be uniform in the
2592  field because of spatial heterogeneity in both feedstock addition and background soil composition
2593  and is susceptible to errors given bioturbation and soil erosion. Instead, the concentration of
2594  immobile--or detrital--elements that are enriched in the feedstock relative to soils (e.g., Zr, Ti,
2595  Ni, Cr) can be used as tracers for feedstock addition.335,352,353,434,441,444,446 This approach builds on
2596  the well-established use in geochemical studies of natural weathering of mass transfer coefficients
2597  that normalise cations to immobile elements in order to compare a starting mass of parent material
2598  with an equivalent mass of soil, e.g. ref. 447
2599
2600  The benefit of using soil mass balance as an MRV tool is that weathering signals are time-
2601  integrated, unlike gas- or aqueous phase approaches. However, there are several considerations
2602  when interpreting these results for the purposes of MRV of CDR. First, depending on the treatment
2603  of samples (e.g., not washing with a leaching agent such as ammonium acetate to remove soluble
2604  cations held at soil exchange sites) and the method of bulk soil measurement (e.g., X-ray
2605  fluorescence (XRF); aqua regia digest or hydrofluoric acid digest followed by inductively coupled
2606  plasma mass spectrometry (ICP-MS) or inductively coupled plasma optical emissions
2607  spectroscopy (ICP-OES)), cations already released through weathering held at exchange sites may
2608  be identified as still being present within the primary solid phase, or elements may not be measured
2609  at all due to the recalcitrance of the mineral phases they are held within. This can cause under- or
2610  overestimation of feedstock weathering, respectively. For example, total digestion of Zr-bearing
2611  phases often requires HF digestion at high temperatures and pressures.
2612
2613  Second, the signal-to-noise ratio for changes in elemental composition of soils in response to
2614  mineral feedstock addition and weathering can be low depending on the differences in composition
2615  between soil and feedstock endmembers, their relative amounts in the post-amendment mixture,
2616  and underlying heterogeneity in soil and feedstock composition.335,352,435,441,446 In order to resolve
2617  EW using soil mass balance approaches, it is necessary to demonstrate that signal-to-noise for
2618  specific soils and feedstocks used in deployments can be overcome by appropriate choices in
2619  analytical technique,352 sampling strategy, and data aggregation,280,441,446 else values derived using
2620  this method will be unreliable. In general, a signal will be most easily resolvable the greater the
2621  amount of feedstock applied, the greater the extent of weathering, the more enriched a feedstock
2622  is in cations and immobile elements of interest relative to the soil, the lower the analytical
2623  uncertainty and soil heterogeneity, and the greater the number of samples and area over which the
2624  calculation is performed.280,335,441
2625
2626  Finally, the calculation for mobile element loss by solid phase mass balance is agnostic to the
2627  chemistry of weathering reactions, or the fate of weathered cations. This means that the
2628  contribution to weathering from acids other than carbonic acid, or non-acid chelating agents, is not
2629  known and must be constrained with additional measurements in order to relate cation release to
2630  potential CDR (see e.g., Section 3.4.3). This also applies to (bio)geochemical processes
2631  downstream of the site of weathering--such as ex situ clay or carbonate formation, degassing of
2632  CO2 due to pH shifts, and incorporation into biomass--which may impact the realisation of CDR.
2633
2634

      69
      Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

2635  4.1.2 SIC formation

2636  Soil inorganic carbon includes carbon present in mineral form, primarily as carbonate minerals,
2637  rather than as organic matter. This includes both inherited lithogenic carbonates and secondary or
2638  paedogenic carbonates formed within soils. Where new carbonates form from Ca2+ or Mg2+ and
2639  bicarbonate generated by silicate weathering, they can represent a net carbon sink because
2640  atmospheric or soil CO2 has been converted into a solid carbonate phase. However, carbonate
2641  precipitation is less efficient than storage as dissolved alkalinity because it removes cations and
2642  alkalinity from porewaters and releases a fraction of the previously weathering-derived DIC as
2643  CO2 during precipitation. Consequently, changes in SIC stocks can be used to quantify the solid-
2644  carbonate storage component of EW-derived CDR where the carbonate can be attributed to
2645  silicate-weathering products.103,108,112,170,178,234,237,246,384,404,448­451 However, SIC accumulation does
2646  not capture dissolved bicarbonate export and should not be added to, or subtracted from, soil mass-
2647  balance or aqueous-flux estimates without a clearly defined accounting framework that avoids
2648  double counting. Soil inorganic carbon formation, redissolution, and downstream carbonate
2649  precipitation must therefore be accounted for explicitly in a full CDR budget.
2650
2651  Carbonate formation depends on feedstock characteristics, soil porewater chemistry, and
2652  hydrological conditions. It is favoured where Ca2+ and Mg2+ concentrations, DIC/soil pCO2, and
2653  pH drive porewaters toward carbonate supersaturation, particularly under warmer and drier
2654  conditions that concentrate solutes via evaporation.452 Under wetter conditions, cations and
2655  bicarbonate are more likely to be transported to deeper soils or groundwater, creating spatial and
2656  seasonal variability in carbonate formation and complicating sampling programmes. The presence
2657  of paedogenic carbonate before EW application is therefore a useful indicator that carbonate
2658  formation may be favoured at a deployment site. Local precipitation, typically as calcite, may also
2659  occur in soil microenvironments such as mineral­water interfaces, even where bulk porewaters
2660  remain undersaturated; however, such carbonates may be transient unless saturation is sustained.
2661
2662  Paedogenic carbonate formation may be favoured when using fast-weathering Ca-rich feedstocks,
2663  as these rapidly increase alkalinity and supply the Ca needed for calcite formation. For example,
2664  in a wollastonite EW trial in rice paddies in Northeast China, Wang et al. (2024)453 measured SIC
2665  increases in near-surface soils (0­20 cm) corresponding to a solid-carbonate CDR of 1.20 ± 0.03
2666  tCO2 ha-1. Similarly, Khalidy et al. (2025)103 used a wollastonite-rich feedstock in Southwestern
2667  Ontario, Canada, to track carbonate formation to depths of up to 1 m and reported SIC-derived
2668  CDR rates of 0.55 tCO2 ha-1 month-1, with evidence for greater SIC accumulation in deeper layers.
2669  Because SIC often accumulates deeper in soil profiles (e.g., >1 m; ref. 100), carbonate-based MRV
2670  may require deeper sampling than conventional near-surface approaches to avoid underestimating
2671  this storage pool.
2672
2673  Soil inorganic carbon is measured using a variety of techniques, including calcimetry, combustion,
2674  thermogravimetric analysis, and coulometry;192,454,455 analyses that could be routinely performed
2675  by commercial laboratories. Although SIC measurement is simple and accurate, even small
2676  increases will correspond to significant CDR; however, these changes may not be discernible from
2677  background SIC, particularly in calcareous soils. The measurement of SIC for CDR quantification
2678  will be further complicated if aglime is applied in conjunction with EW amendments, which often
2679  contain carbonates,125 and their addition could be misinterpreted as CDR.161
2680

      70
      Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

2681  4.1.3 Changes in Soil Exchangeable Base Cations

2682  Changes in soil exchangeable base cation concentrations represent one pool of weathering
      products generated during EW that can be used to infer minimum bounds on primary mineral
2683  weathering rates.91,108,177,216,231,237,244,352,443 Increases in exchangeable Ca2+, Mg2+, K+, and Na+
2684  relative to a control or baseline reflect weathering-derived cations retained in the surface soil at
2685  the time of sampling, prior to export to deeper soil layers or incorporation into secondary mineral
2686  phases. The exchangeable cation pool is quantified using standardised extraction methods, most
2687  commonly employing ammonium acetate to displace base cations from exchange sites for analysis
2688  in solution.456 Changes in this pool are determined by comparing measurements from treated and
2689  control soils, or by repeated measurements of the same soil taken before silicate application and
2690  after a period during which weathering has occurred.
2691
2692  Where losses of weathering products from the system can be confidently excluded, such as in
2693  short-term soil incubations177 or pot experiments without drainage,171,457,458 changes in soil
2694  exchangeable base cations can independently serve as a useful proxy for weathering--although
2695  plant uptake and secondary mineral precipitation must still be considered where relevant. In
2696  settings where leaching from the surface soil occurs, including field applications216,243,444,459 and
2697  flow-through laboratory experiments,91,92,98,108,237,343,352,439,460 differences in soil exchangeable
2698  base cations must be paired with approaches that quantify cation export from the surface soil in
2699  order to fully assess total cation release from feedstocks. These include soil mass-balance
2700  methods335,352,441 and direct monitoring of cation fluxes in leachate or drainage water. In these
2701  contexts, exchangeable cation measurements ensure that retained weathering products are included
2702  in EW estimates, avoiding underestimation that may result by only considering cations exported
2703  to deeper layers.343
2704
2705  4.2 Aqueous phase
2706
2707  The aqueous phase also provides a critical pathway for tracking carbon mobilisation and export
      under EW, as dissolved products of mineral dissolution are transported through soil porewaters,
2708  drainage networks, and receiving waters. Field-scale monitoring of the aqueous domain focuses
      on quantifying changes in alkalinity and DIC fluxes, which directly reflect CO2 consumption
2709  during weathering and its stabilisation in bicarbonate form. Complementary measurements of
2710  cation fluxes (e.g., Ca2+, Mg2+, K+, Na+) provide stoichiometric constraints on mineral dissolution
2711  rates and help distinguish weathering-derived signals from background variability. Electrical
2712  conductivity measurements can serve as a rapid, high-frequency proxy for changes in total
2713  dissolved ion concentrations, helping to resolve temporal variability and identify weathering-
2714  related solute pulses. In addition, ion-exchange resin devices act as passive sorbents for dissolved
2715  solutes moving through soil porewater, offering a time-integrated approach to quantifying
2716  cumulative solute release in soils and shallow subsurface environments.
2717
2718  These approaches share the requirement for independent constraints on water fluxes, which can be
2719  challenging given the episodic nature of soil and downstream water movement following
2720  precipitation events. Depending on the measurements used, particularly alkalinity versus cation
2721
2722
2723
2724

      71
      Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

2725  fluxes, aqueous-phase approaches can capture different subsets of upstream losses, although losses
2726  beyond the MRV system boundary may still occur (cf., Section 4.5). Nevertheless, they have the
2727  potential to establish the aqueous mass balance needed to attribute CDR, assess transport and
2728  durability downstream from initial weathering reactions, and reconcile field observations with
2729  solid- and gas phase measurements.
2730
2731  4.2.1 Changes in alkalinity/DIC fluxes

2732  The production of HCO3- during EW in soils serves as the primary indicator for CDR effectiveness.
      Consequently, increases in DIC and TA are frequently monitored in soil pore water, leachate from
2733  soil cores, and streams and rivers, as indirect proxies for HCO3- levels derived from
2734  EW.30,63,65,91,102,174,216,237,343,384,404,437,443,461­464
2735
2736  Dissolved inorganic carbon in the aqueous phase includes dissolved CO2, HCO3-, and carbonate
2737  CO32- ions, with HCO3- as the dominant dissolved carbon species in fresh water with pH between
2738  ~6.3 and 10.3. Dissolved inorganic carbon is often measured by converting all the inorganic carbon
2739  species into CO2 through acidification (e.g., carbon analyser).237 Streams and rivers are often
2740  oversaturated with CO2 due to terrestrial and aquatic ecosystem respiration, with temporal
2741  fluctuations which may bias the measured DIC.442 In addition, the carbonate equilibrium system
2742  in water is dynamic, and can be complicated by the potential for CO2 loss through carbonate
2743  precipitation and re-equilibration if samples are not properly stored and handled.442
2744
2745  Alkalinity represents the acid-neutralising capacity (ANC) of aqueous systems,465 with HCO3-, and
2746  CO32-, and OH- as the primary relevant anions in the environment. The measurement of TA is
2747  typically performed through titrating the water to a pH endpoint using a strong acid (e.g., manually
2748  or with an alkalinity titrator) and is frequently reported as equivalents per mass or volume (eq kg-
2749  1 or eq L-1), mg L-1, or ppm of CaCO3 equivalent.192 However, several limitations identified in
2750  recent literature demonstrate that alkalinity is not always a perfect surrogate for bicarbonate.220
2751  Non-carbonate anions--such as organic acids--can contribute to ANC, potentially leading to an
2752  overestimation of CDR if additionality compared to control conditions is not strictly
2753  apportioned.192 In addition, if a sample is not properly stored, carbonate precipitation may cause
2754  underestimation of the alkalinity level in the water.
2755
2756  The need for high-frequency sampling to capture temporal variability of DIC or alkalinity in the
2757  aqueous phase makes these methods resource-intensive.442 Several studies investigated the use of
2758  EC as a cheaper proxy for alkalinity (cf., Section 4.2.3).466,467 While these studies show a strong
2759  relationship between EC and ANC in controlled mesocosm settings, field lysimeter deployments
2760  suggest that low-cost soil EC sensors are not sufficiently reliable as standalone quantitative proxies
2761  for alkalinity concentrations and may have high failure rates; as with all aqueous-phase
2762  approaches, quantifying alkalinity export further requires water-flux constraints.466
2763
2764
2765
2766

      72
      Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

2767  4.2.2 Changes in cation flux

2768  Base cations (Ca2+, Mg2+, Na+, K+) released from silicates during EW can be mobilised in soil pore
2769  water and subsequently exit the weathering environment and transport into drainage water (e.g.,
2770  as leachate measured via soil core or column experiments), groundwater, streams or rivers.65,325
2771  Monitoring concentrations and fluxes of these ions in the aqueous phase has been widely applied
2772  in laboratory and field studies to estimate EW and associated CDR
2773  rates.65,91,108,158,162,179,184,223,237,243,246,343,352,384,468,469
2774
2775  For every mole equivalent of cation released by silicates through carbonic acid weathering, one
2776  mole of CO2 is removed and transformed into one mole of HCO3- (whereas the ratio is 2:1 for
2777  carbonates). By summing up the total cation concentrations in the aqueous phase within or
2778  exported from the weathering environment, EW and CDR rates can be calculated. These
2779  concentrations can be measured via ICP-OES or Ion chromatography (IC).
2780
2781  To identify the source of the weathering products, stable and radiogenic isotopes are sometimes
2782  integrated into cation analysis (see also Section 4.4). Strontium isotopes (87Sr/86Sr) have been
2783  applied to distinguish between cations derived from the feedstock and those from native soil and
2784  minerals. Peters et al. (2004)71 utilized Ca/Sr ratios and 87Sr/86Sr isotopes to trace the dissolution
2785  of wollastonite in a watershed-scale experiment. Similarly, magnesium (26Mg/24Mg) and lithium
2786  (7Li/6Li) isotopes provide insights into weathering process and can further track the formation of
2787  secondary minerals.326 These isotopes can be measured via multi-collector ICP-MS or thermal
2788  ionization mass spectrometry (TIMS) for high-precision isotopic ratios.
2789
2790  The aqueous cation-based method for estimating EW and associated CDR rates has several
2791  limitations that can compromise its accuracy. Firstly, this approach cannot distinguish whether
2792  weathering is driven by carbonic acid (which consumes atmospheric CO2) or by strong acids (e.g.,
2793  nitric acid from fertilisers) which release cations without providing a CDR benefit--potentially
2794  leading to significant overestimates.237,344 Furthermore, a substantial portion of released cations is
2795  not immediately transported but is instead retained within the soil exchangeable pool or
2796  incorporated into secondary clays, causing a lag that leads to underestimation of actual feedstock
2797  dissolution rates.91,223,343 Cation sorption and uptake via secondary precipitation makes
2798  measurement particularly challenging, as standard extractions may fail to fully account for
2799  exchangeable cations. Finally, depending on sampling location within the watershed, these
2800  methods cannot capture potential downstream cation precipitation as carbonate minerals in
2801  drainage waters, a process that can release part of the captured CO2 back to the
2802  atmosphere.192,246,442
2803
2804  4.2.3 Electrical conductivity

2805  Electrical conductivity reflects the integrated concentration of dissolved ions in solution and
      therefore provides a first-order proxy for the accumulation of weathering products in porewaters
2806  and drainage waters.442,467 Because EC can be measured continuously with in situ sensors, it offers
2807  a potentially scalable complement to laboratory-based alkalinity measurements for monitoring
2808  EW-induced solute release. Under conditions where alkalinity and major cations covary during
2809  silicate dissolution, systematic relationships between EC and TA can emerge (cf., Section 4.2.1).442
2810
2811

      73
      Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

2812  In soils with low CEC, combined measurements of bulk EC and volumetric water content can
2813  further constrain carbonate alkalinity additions, although organic matter content, clay mineralogy,
2814  and pH-dependent exchange reactions strongly influence the EC­alkalinity relationship.467 As a
2815  result, EC-based approaches require site-specific calibration and explicit treatment of ionic
2816  composition before being used to infer bicarbonate concentrations through geochemical speciation
2817  modelling (e.g., using PHREEQC or The Geochemist's Work Bench) and are likely to be most
2818  applicable in systems with relatively simple and stable solute chemistries.
2819
2820  4.2.4 Resin devices

2821  Ion-exchange resin devices (e.g., resin cartridges/capsules, or self-integrating accumulators) act as
      passive sorbents for dissolved solutes moving through soil porewater, yielding time-integrated
2822  estimates of weathering product export (e.g., Ca2+, Mg2+, K+, Na+, and relevant anions) over weeks
2823  to months. The integrative nature of this measurement is quite useful, as it has the potential to
2824  eliminate the labour intensity associated with high-frequency porewater sampling and the
2825  corresponding uncertainties of aqueous sample storage, processing, and analysis. Resins also act
2826  passively, allowing for the capture of a more representative pool of soil water, as compared to
2827  tension lysimeters or rhizons. Resins have been used to quantify leaching in agricultural soils,
2828  demonstrating that they can capture seasonal cumulative signals, however, substantial within-field
2829  variability requires high replication and careful installation to preserve natural flow paths above
2830  the collector.470,471
2831
2832  A core limitation is hydrologic sensitivity: capture depends on solute delivery to the exchange
2833  surface, so dry conditions can suppress adsorption by limiting transport, while preferential flow
2834  can bypass the device if placement/geometry do not intercept dominant pathways. However,
2835  design and installation (i.e., high replication, large size, deep installation) can significantly reduce
2836  flow-field artifacts. Current designs explicitly target cumulative flux measurement (e.g., resin-
2837  column "passive flux meter" variants) to reduce flow-field artifacts. Nevertheless, these systems
2838  still benefit from co-located hydrologic techniques (water balance, lysimeters, or modelling) to
2839  convert accumulated mass to area-normalised fluxes when possible.472
2840
2841  For EW applications, resin approaches are conceptually aligned with other soil-integrating MRV
2842  methods but inherit similar challenges: strong spatial and temporal heterogeneity in dissolution
2843  and drainage, uncertainty in representativeness of sampled flow, and potential disturbance effects
2844  during installation. The only truly comparable empirical method is embedded zero-tension
2845  lysimeters, which come with their own host of issues (e.g., water availability, soil column
2846  disturbance, heterogeneity, preferential flow from installation, etc.). Evidence from ion-exchange
2847  membrane/probe deployments show that measured supply rates can be highly placement-
2848  dependent, reinforcing the need for standardised depth targeting, adequate replication, and site-
2849  specific calibration.473 Finally, resin chemistry and extraction protocols can introduce bias (e.g.,
2850  as dissolved ion sinks, resins can drive local mineral undersaturation and weathering). Given the
2851  remaining uncertainties in applicability of this technique with different soil conditions at this time,
2852  EW deployments utilizing resins will benefit from controls, paired porewater sampling where
2853  feasible, and validation experiments under relevant soil moisture and texture regimes.474
2854
2855
2856

      74
      Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

2857  4.3 Gas phase

2858  Gas phase MRV involves the measurement of the rate of GHG emissions to the atmosphere from
      soil. Greenhouse gases are transferred between ecosystems and the atmosphere as a result of biotic
2859  and abiotic processes in soil and plants. Gas flux monitoring enables the detection of changes
2860  induced by EW.91,175,240,353 This is complicated by the complex spatiotemporal variation in gas
2861  exchange driven by the physical, biological, and chemical heterogeneity of soils at microscopic to
2862  ecosystem scales. This variability can make the separation of an EW signal from natural variation
2863  challenging. As a result, there is no established gas phase MRV methodology specific to EW,
2864  though secondary validation through gaseous monitoring has been previously approved by
2865  registries.
2866
2867  Quantifying gas fluxes offers valuable context of the ecosystem impacts of EW. CO2 fluxes allow
2868  us to characterise the impact of EW on organic and inorganic carbon cycling. Organic carbon
2869  cycling is the major contributor to soil CO2 efflux, making it difficult to rely on gas phase
2870  measurements to quantify inorganic processes.325 However, the fluxes are useful for tracking
2871  whole ecosystem dynamics and monitoring the fate of SOC.91,353,475 Another compelling reason
2872  for the use of gas phase measurements is that, unlike other MRV approaches, they can target non-
2873  CO2 GHG emissions, particularly nitrous oxide (N2O) and methane (CH4).83,106,157,476 Even small
2874  emissions of these highly potent GHGs could offset any benefits due to CDR. Therefore, the
2875  utilization of gas flux measurements remains a compelling method for inclusion in MRV packages.
2876
2877  Existing studies in the context of EW have largely used static, portable, or dynamic chambers that
2878  are installed on the soil in a field or in mesocosms.83,177,353,477,478 Chamber measurements can be
2879  used for mechanistic studies and quantifying GHG budgets alike. Chambers can be opaque to
2880  exclude photosynthesis or transparent and are most insightful when coupled. In a static chamber
2881  approach, gas samples are manually extracted from the chamber over a short closure time and
2882  analysed on a gas chromatograph or using spectroscopy-based gas analysers (e.g., Picarro, Licor,
2883  Aeris, Gasmet). However, this discrete sampling approach has largely been replaced by static
2884  chambers coupled with gas analysers for high resolution in situ measurements over a chamber
2885  closure period. This approach benefits from the comparatively low cost and straightforward
2886  deployment inherent to static chambers but offers a more reliable flux measurement with real-time
2887  verification. There has been work done to resolve the inorganic weathering signal using in-soil
2888  CO2 sensors as a secondary validation technique479 but the context in which it is used and the
2889  applicability of the techniques across deployment contexts remain debated.480,481
2890
2891  Automatic, dynamic chambers that can be deployed long-term and run continuously have the
2892  potential to better account for temporal trends. Automatic in situ measurements are spatially
2893  limited due to the required proximity to a gas analyser and are associated with a significant cost.
2894  Micrometeorological methods such as eddy covariance can also capture field-scale gas dynamics
2895  at high frequency and produce suitable datasets for calculating GHG budgets.353 This method
2896  requires a large, unobstructed measurement area on the hectare scale, free from farm infrastructure
2897  and other barriers, so that GHG fluxes can be quantified without undue influence from microscale
2898  wind dynamics. Eddy covariance measurements require significant technical and analytical skills
2899  in addition to having high installation and maintenance costs.
2900
2901

      75
      Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

2902  Recently, there have been promising developments for gas phase MRV in the context of EW. Low-
2903  cost sensors, remote sensing, and open-access analysis pipelines combined with increasing
2904  knowledge about the influence of EW on processes in soils may increase momentum around gas
2905  phase MRV approaches. In addition, isotopic signatures of GHGs can now be measured in situ.
2906  As the isotopic signatures of CO2 differ between organic and inorganic sources, these
2907  measurements could provide new mechanistic insights. While gas phase MRV does not currently
2908  play a great role in EW research, there is scope for increased application.
2909
2910  4.4 Isotopic approaches
2911
      Isotopic methods involve the measurement of stable (e.g., 13C, 15N, 18O) and radioactive (e.g., 14C)
2912  isotopes as tracers, both of which are analysed using mass spectrometry, to quantify precise
      measurements of carbon sequestration. While most MRV strategies quantify elemental stocks and
2913  fluxes, isotope ratios encode additional information because they respond to both source mixing
2914  and process-driven fractionation.482 Radiogenic isotope systems are primarily used for source
2915  attribution, since instrumental correction removes mass-dependent fractionation and leaves only
2916  source-controlled variability. In contrast, stable isotope systems preserve both source and process
2917  signals and are therefore powerful tools for identifying mineral transformations, secondary phase
2918  formation, and biogeochemical cycling (Table 2). Isotopic methods are typically more costly and
2919  analytically demanding than concentration-based measurements and thus are unlikely to form the
2920  backbone of routine MRV for EW. Nonetheless, they provide constraints on sources, pathways,
2921  and transformation processes that cannot be inferred from bulk chemistry alone and are therefore
2922  particularly valuable for mechanistic understanding and model calibration. In this sense, isotopes
2923  complement rather than replace conventional MRV approaches by adding information on the
2924  mechanisms that drive elemental fluxes, in addition to quantifying their magnitude.
2925
2926  4.4.1 Stable carbon and oxygen isotopes
2927
2928  Stable carbon and oxygen isotopic ratios (13C, 18O) provide valuable constraints on the origin
2929  and transformation of carbon in soil waters and carbonate phases. If isotopic endmembers are well
      characterized, these systems can be used to apportion contributions from atmospheric CO2
2930  processed through respiration, carbonate amendments (e.g., agricultural lime), and lithogenic
      carbon.483 Typical soil-respired CO2 is strongly depleted in 13C, allowing mixing models to
2931  distinguish biologically derived carbon from carbonate-derived inputs.484 In the context of EW,
2932  13C and 18O have been applied across a range of experimental and observational settings--
2933  including agricultural, engineered, urban soils, and natural analogues--to investigate carbon
2934  sources, sinks, and carbonate dynamics associated with reactive mineral amendments.277,450,485­490
2935
2936  4.4.2 Radiocarbon
2937
2938  Radiocarbon (14C) provides a more direct fingerprint of atmospheric carbon incorporation into EW
2939  products, albeit at substantially higher analytical cost due to sample preparation and accelerator
2940
2941

2942

2943
2944

      76
      Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

2945  mass spectrometry requirements. Carbonates precipitated from modern atmospheric CO2 inherit a
2946  characteristic 14C signature that decays predictably over time, enabling the residence age of newly
2947  formed carbonates to be constrained.482 In contrast, geologic carbon sources such as bedrock
2948  carbonate or shale contain effectively "dead" carbon with no detectable 14C, facilitating separation
2949
2950  of newly captured carbon from background pools. When coupled with total carbon inventories,
2951  radiocarbon data can be used to estimate rates of atmospheric CDR by EW,277,490 although
2952
2953  interpretation requires caution, as isotopic exchange between dissolved and solid carbon pools and
2954  kinetic fractionation during precipitation can bias apparent ages or mixing proportions.490
2955

      4.4.3 Radiogenic strontium

      Radiogenic strontium isotopes (87Sr/86Sr) are widely used tracers of weathering sources and
      fluxes.491 Because 87Sr is produced by radioactive decay of 87Rb, silicate minerals exhibit isotope

      ratios that depend on lithology and age, whereas carbonates typically reflect contemporaneous
      seawater values and are less radiogenic.482,491 Strontium behaves similarly to calcium during

      dissolution and transport, allowing Sr isotopes in porewaters and streams to fingerprint the relative
      contributions of silicate and carbonate weathering to cation fluxes. In the context of EW, 87Sr/86Sr

      has been applied across agricultural, mesocosm, and field-trial settings, and in multiproxy

      approaches, to support interpretation of amendment-derived weathering signals and associated
      carbon accounting.108,243,277,443 A key caveat is that even small carbonate fractions in silicate

      feedstocks can disproportionately influence dissolved Sr isotope signatures due to rapid carbonate
      dissolution, especially early in weathering.500,501

                                                          77
      Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

2971  4.4.4 Lithium

2972  Stable lithium isotopes (7Li) are increasingly used to investigate secondary mineral formation in
      soils and weathering environments. Unlike radiogenic systems, Li isotopes are strongly
2973  fractionated by low-temperature processes, making them particularly sensitive to mineral­solution
2974  interactions rather than simply source.482,492 Lithium isotopes are especially responsive to clay
2975  formation and other secondary silicate phases, as Li is preferentially incorporated into newly
2976  forming clays, leading to characteristic fractionation between dissolved and solid phases.336,493 In
2977  the context of EW, this isotope system offers a means to probe whether applied silicates dissolve
2978  congruently or are rapidly transformed into secondary phases that may affect alkalinity generation
2979  and CDR efficiency. Enhanced Weathering-relevant applications remain limited but emerging,
2980  including the use of Li-Mg isotope signatures to constrain silicate weathering and secondary
2981  mineral formation in soil experiments.326
2982
2983  4.4.5 Emerging stable isotope tracers
2984
2985  A growing suite of non-traditional stable isotopes--including Mg, Ca, Si, and stable Sr--offer
      opportunities to resolve EW pathways beyond simple source attribution. Mg and Ca isotopes are
2986  sensitive to fractionation during carbonate precipitation, dissolution, and ion exchange, enabling
      constraints on cation redistribution and secondary carbonate formation during weathering.301,494,495
2987  Silicon isotopes fractionate during secondary silicate formation and biogenic uptake, making them
2988  useful tracers of congruent versus incongruent silicate weathering and Si cycling.497 Stable Sr
2989  isotopes complement radiogenic Sr by resolving secondary mineral processes and adsorption-
2990  desorption dynamics not captured by source-controlled ratios alone.498,499 Although most
2991  foundational work on these systems has focused on natural weathering environments and
2992  fractionation factors, EW-specific applications are emerging. These include the use of Mg isotopes
2993  to investigate biologically mediated olivine dissolution,438 combined Li-Mg isotope signatures to
2994  constrain silicate dissolution pathways in soil experiments,326 and Si isotopes to track soil
2995  processes and cation exchange in EW mesocosms.496
2996
2997  4.5 Comparison of MRV approaches
2998
2999  The MRV approaches outlined above differ fundamentally in their spatial and temporal specificity
3000  as well as system boundaries (Table 3, Figure 17), the CDR loss processes they directly quantify
3001  (Table 4), and exhibit method-specific nuances related to analytes, sampling strategies, and
      analytical protocols.325,383 As a result, divergence between MRV estimates obtained using different
3002  approaches at the same site does not necessarily indicate errors or flaws in the underlying
      protocols. Instead, such divergence often reflects the fact that different MRV approaches quantify
3003  distinct subsets of processes and operate under different spatial and temporal assumptions.
3004  Understanding these differences is therefore essential for interpreting, comparing, and integrating
3005  MRV estimates of CDR from EW.
3006
3007
3008
3009
3010
3011
3012

      78
      Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

3013  A key distinction between MRV approaches lies in their degree of spatial integration.325 Some
3014  measured variables are inherently location-specific, such as soil compositions or pore-water
3015  chemistry, and thus require explicit consideration of spatial heterogeneity when extrapolating from
3016  point measurements (often multiple) to larger spatial units such as fields. In contrast, other
3017  variables integrate signals over much larger areas by virtue of the system being sampled. For
3018  example, stream-water chemistry integrates the sum of biogeochemical processes occurring across
3019  an upstream catchment, such that a single measurement reflects spatially integrated signals at that
3020  scale. Similar contrasts exist for gas phase measurements, where in-soil sensors or chamber-based
3021  approaches reflect highly localised fluxes, while eddy covariance towers integrate fluxes over a
3022  much larger footprint.
3023

      Monitoring, reporting, and verification approaches also differ strongly in their temporal
      integration.325 Some measurements represent instantaneous or short-duration snapshots, such as
      gas fluxes or stream-water concentrations at a given time, whereas others integrate over much
      longer periods. Soil-based approaches, including soil mass balance and changes in exchangeable
      cations, typically integrate temporally over weeks to years, whereas water- and gas phase
      approaches often require high-frequency sampling to capture episodic fluxes associated with
      rainfall events and floods during which much of the export flux may occur as well as seasonal

                                                             79
      Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

3036  hydrologic dynamics. The lack of temporal integration in some approaches can, in principle, be
3037  compensated for through high-frequency or continuous sampling, while the lack of spatial
3038  integration can potentially be mitigated through aggregation across large numbers of sampling
3039  locations.280
3040
3041  Relating measurements with different spatial and temporal specificity within a single cohesive
3042  MRV framework therefore requires interpolation or extrapolation, informed by an understanding
3043  of the spatial and temporal heterogeneity of the system and chosen MRV approaches. The scales
3044  at which such heterogeneity occurs vary between measured variables and depend both on sampling
3045  design--such as the footprint of a flux chamber relative to an eddy covariance tower or the volume
3046  of soil or water collected for analysis--and on the physical, chemical, and biological properties of
3047  the medium being analysed. For example, soil elemental composition commonly co-varies with
3048  mineralogy and texture,215 which are themselves governed by soil-forming processes. Similarly,
3049  water- and gas phase variables often exhibit predictable temporal structure in response to seasonal
3050  changes in temperature, moisture, and hydrology, as well as to discrete events such as storms. In
3051  general, soil-based approaches tend to integrate temporally, while water- and gas-based
3052  approaches may integrate spatially, depending on measurement design (Table 3; Table 2; ref. 325).
3053  Robust long-term CDR estimates therefore require either explicit integration across missing
3054  dimensions or sufficient sampling intensity to compensate for their absence.

3061  Direct comparison of MRV approaches is further complicated by the fact that different
3062  biogeochemical processes influence the variables measured by each method within its system
3063

3066  approach has a characteristic profile of CDR loss processes that it captures within its system
3067  boundary, as well as processes that fall outside that boundary. For example, soil mass balance
3068  approaches cannot directly account for weathering driven by strong acids, whereas measurements
3069  of alkalinity in soil pore water can be used to quantify this contribution. As a result, internally
3070  consistent MRV estimates derived from different approaches may nonetheless reflect different
3071  subsets of the overall CDR budget.
