      Ga s                Yes        Yes       Yes       Yes       Yes Yes            Yes    Yes/No

      * Depends on chemical treatment of soils and location of loss process.

      ** Degassing directly no, via alkalinity charge balance yes, carbonate formation yes.

      An additional and closely related consideration is that any MRV approach is only sensitive to
      processes occurring within its defined spatial system boundary and up to the time of measurement.
      Spatial limitations are often intuitive; for instance, measurements of carbonate precipitation in
      topsoils do not account for carbonate formation occurring downstream in rivers. However,
      temporal limitations are equally important. Many processes affecting CDR from EW operate on

                                                         81
      Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

3086  different characteristic timescales and can introduce substantial lag times between rock application
3087  and detectable signals. These include variability in chemical weathering rates driven by
3088  environmental conditions and negative feedbacks on weathering (e.g., refs. 172,357; cf., Section 3.4),
3089  retention and storage of weathered cations on soil exchange sites prior to export,68 hydrologic
3090  flushing of weathering products during transient rainfall events or seasonal changes in water
3091  storage,502,503 and the loss of bicarbonate alkalinity in rivers and the surface ocean.66,279
3092
3093  Consequently, MRV approaches are primarily informative when their spatial and temporal system
3094  boundaries align with the scales over which measurable responses to EW can reasonably be
3095  expected. Comparisons of CDR estimates derived from different MRV approaches that operate
3096  over mismatched spatial extents or temporal windows are therefore of limited interpretability.161
3097  Rather than indicating methodological failure, such discrepancies often reflect differences in the
3098  timing, location, and processes captured by each approach. Taken together, these considerations
3099  underscore the need to view MRV approaches for EW as complementary, with robust CDR
3100  quantification requiring careful alignment of system boundaries, explicit consideration of spatial
3101  and temporal heterogeneity, and, where possible, integration of multiple lines of evidence.
3102
3103  4.6 Implementation of MRV practices in the Voluntary Carbon Market

3104  4.6.1 Types of VCM MRV documents

3105  Because EW appears capable of producing durable and measurable CDR at large scales, an array
      of private, public, and nonprofit stakeholders has emerged with the goal of integrating the latest
3106  scientific research into MRV methodologies and guidance documents for the Voluntary Carbon
3107  Market (VCM). Both types of MRV documents undergo private and public consultations for
3108  experts to review and comment. However, these documents are not peer-reviewed in the same
3109  manner as academic journal articles, and the governance during their construction is not always
3110  transparent (e.g., refs. 345,431,432,504­506).
3111
3112  Monitoring, reporting, and verification methodologies (or protocols) are most often produced by
3113  carbon registries--organizations responsible for certifying that CDR has taken place and that a
3114  fungible carbon credit can be generated. These documents are binding, and a supplier of CDR
3115  (such as an EW company) cannot claim a creditable climate impact without meeting the
3116  methodology's criteria or receiving an exception from the registry. Private carbon registries have
3117  been the first to produce MRV methodologies for EW,431,432,504,506 while nonprofit carbon registries
3118  currently do not have full publicly available methodologies.507,508 Existing methodologies undergo
3119  minor and major updates (on monthly to yearly cadences), and it is possible that additional
3120  methodologies by new and/or existing registries will be produced in the near future.
3121
3122  In addition to registry methodologies, non-binding guidance, scoping, and synthesis documents
3123  have been produced by nonprofits, consulting firms, and jurisdictional governments. The goals of
3124  these are to systematically discuss current MRV practices, uncertainties, and opportunities, as well
3125  as to steer focus towards open MRV questions and opportunities for research and policy
3126  development (e.g., refs. 345,505,509). While these expansive documents often substantially inform
3127  registry methodologies, they are not binding and do not currently appear to be updated on the same
3128  cadence. An exception to this update cadence is the Ithaka Institute (2022) registry
3129
3130

      82
      Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

3131  methodology.506 Due to its age and the rapidly advancing state of EW research, most of the
3132  summary below is focused on "recent" MRV documents which have been published since 2024
3133  and thus integrate the most up-to-date methods.
3134
3135  4.6.2 Structure and Content of VCM MRV documents

3136  Monitoring, reporting, and verification documents follow a consistent method of calculating net
      CDR from an EW deployment by subtracting life cycle emissions and other carbon losses from
3137  the gross carbon drawdown that results from EW. Here, we briefly summarise the accepted MRV
3138  approaches from various VCM documents for quantifying gross carbon drawdown and carbon
3139  losses, but we do not discuss life cycle emissions. The focus is primarily on "required" MRV
3140  approaches, however MRV documents often "recommend" or "suggest" specific methods--these
3141  are noted when relevant.
3142
3143  Recent registry methodologies have adopted the framework from Mills et al. (2024)505 of
3144  separating carbon quantification into a near field zone component (NFZ; the depth where initial
3145  CDR takes place and must be monitored empirically) and a far field zone component (FFZ; the
3146  spatial area beyond the NFZ where only carbon losses can be incorporated into net CDR
3147  calculations). Some methodologies require a primary MRV approach to be used as the main
3148  quantification method as well as a secondary MRV approach to be used for validation
3149  purposes.431,432 For instance, when solid phase measurements are the primary MRV approach, a
3150  aqueous phase MRV approach must serve as secondary validation (and vice versa).
3151
3152  4.6.3 Solid phase MRV Approaches
3153
3154  Solid phase MRV approaches are accepted as the primary431,432 or majority504 quantification
      approach in all recent methodologies. Some recent methodologies allow for tracking the
3155  weathering of feedstock by measuring base cations either with or without the incorporation of an
      immobile trace elements432,504 All recent methodologies require constraining NFZ losses by at least
3156  considering biomass uptake of cations, cation sorption onto soil particles, non-carbonic acid
3157  weathering, secondary carbonate formation, and secondary silicate formation.
3158
3159  For biomass uptake of base cations, all recent methodologies require that base cations must be
3160  measured in harvested and/or new growth biomass and converted into a total loss of CDR potential
3161  over a specified time period and deployment area. Cation sorption onto soil particles can be
3162  determined by measuring the product of CEC and BS over a specified time period for a
3163  deployment. This method is required in Rainbow (2025)504, however in other recent methodologies
3164  it is only required after baseline sampling if alkalinity flux has not been constrained over the entire
3165  depth of the NFZ.
3166
3167  All recent methodologies provide the option of constraining non-carbonic acid weathering via
3168  direct measurement of anions in the aqueous phase, and some432,504 allow for the use of a
3169  conservative proxy correction factor instead by following the methods in Dietzen and Rosing
3170  (2023).231 Furthermore, some allow for estimation of non-carbonic acid weathering potential based
3171  on fertiliser application rates,431,504 and Puro.earth (2025)432 accepts indirect measurements derived
3172  by subtracting the sum of "bicarbonate + carbonate concentration" from cation concentrations.
3173
3174
3175
3176

      83
      Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

3177  Rainbow (2025)504 also requires constraining pH-dependent speciation and acid buffering in
3178  addition to non-carbonic acid weathering.
3179
3180  Because recent methodologies primarily focus on the ocean as the long-term carbon sink for EW,
3181  secondary carbonate formation is mostly presented as a potential loss of CDR potential. Therefore,
3182  none allow for net new carbonate formation as a sole (or even primary) measure of CDR. In
3183  quantifying new carbonate formation as a loss term, as long as carbonates are retained during soil
3184  sample processing, directly measuring SIC is only required after baseline sampling in recent
3185  methodologies if alkalinity flux has not been constrained over the entire depth of the NFZ.
3186  However, some recent methodologies do allow for secondary carbonates to be counted as newly
3187  stored carbon432,504 if SIC can be demonstrated to be a direct result of EW and can be shown to be
3188  stable over long periods. In contrast, empirically constraining secondary silicate formation as a
3189  loss term is not required in recent methodologies, since: i) an operationally viable method for early-
3190  stage commercial deployments is not available,505 and ii) it is inherently corrected for if samples
3191  are taken over the entire depth of the NFZ.
3192
3193  4.6.4 Aqueous-phase MRV Approaches

3194  Like the solid phase approaches, aqueous phase MRV approaches are accepted across all recent
      methodologies as the primary 431,432 or majority 504 quantification approach. All recent
3195  methodologies allow for constraining (at the base of the NFZ): i) the aqueous carbonate system,
3196  and/or ii) major dissolved ion concentrations. If measuring the aqueous carbonate system, two of
3197  the six parameters are always required to be empirically measured, with three parameters
3198  recommended in Rainbow (2025)504 and four specific parameters (pH, TA, DIC, pCO2) highly
3199  recommended in Isometric (2025). Puro.earth (2025)432 presents two MRV options built around
3200  constraining either: i) TA while monitoring at least pH, or ii) measuring DIC alongside at least pH
3201  and temperature. If measuring major dissolved ion concentrations, the cation proxies used for CDR
3202  quantification (e.g., Ca2+, Mg2+, K+, Na+, etc.) must be justified and empirically measured, and
3203  non-carbonic acid weathering in the NFZ must be corrected for.
3204
3205  Methodologies state a preference for soil water to be sampled using porewater collection devices
3206  such as rhizons and/or lysimeters, however some allow for measurements on well-constrained
3207  catchment waters 431,504 or porewater extracted from soil cores.432 Some methodologies allow for
3208  the use of ion exchange resins as a secondary validation approach431,432 or on a case-by-case basis
3209  pending validation against other measurements.504 However, resin-based measurements are not
3210  currently accepted as a primary or sole quantification approach. In recent MRV methodologies,
3211  registries note that they will continue to revisit ion exchange resins as the field develops.
3212
3213  4.6.5 Gas phase MRV Approaches
3214
3215  Gas phase measurements are not required, nor accepted as a primary form of CDR quantification
      in recent methodologies, although this does not preclude their potential use as a secondary
3216  validation method. Monitoring fluxes of CO2 as well as additional GHGs such as CH4 and N2O is
3217  sometimes recommended via the use of gas flux chambers, eddy covariance towers, or in situ
3218  sensors (e.g., refs. 431,505). The collection of high-resolution CO2 gas concentration data alongside
3219  solid- and aqueous phase approaches has also been encouraged to strengthen cross-validation of
3220
3221
3222

      84
      Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

3223  these methods.505 The interaction between EW and soil GHG species concentrations is also
3224  identified as a key research and development area for commercial deployments and academic
3225  studies.505
3226
3227  4.6.6 Additionality and counterfactual baselines for EW in the VCM

3228  A core requirement for crediting of EW in the VCM--and carbon accounting in general--is that
      credited CDR must be additional to what would have occurred under a plausible counterfactual
3229  scenario, i.e., the carbon drawdown that would have occurred in the absence of a deliberate EW
3230  intervention.510 This ensures that actual climate mitigation takes place, and in practice requires
3231  demonstrating that net CDR resulting from EW exceeds the carbon uptake and fluxes associated
3232  with baseline (or business-as-usual) land management, feedstock handling, and natural
3233  weathering.
3234
3235  A major challenge for demonstrating additionality of EW arises from its strong overlap with
3236  existing agricultural practices, particularly liming and the use of silicate rock flour for soil
3237  conditioning, fertilisation, or pest control in some settings (e.g., refs. 29,47,511­514). In situations
3238  where EW feedstock is substituted for conventional lime or other alkaline amendments, a portion
3239  of the observed alkalinity generation and associated CDR in following years may be due to these
3240  earlier practices rather than EW.29,345 As a result, only the incremental carbon removal relative to
3241  displaced practices can be considered additional. Recent methodological assessments emphasise
3242  that baseline farming activity--including feedstock type, application rate, and application
3243  frequency--must therefore be explicitly characterised and reported via documents such as "Site
3244  Characterization Reports" rather than implicitly assumed to be zero (e.g., refs. 345,504).
3245
3246  Additionality considerations also extend upstream to feedstock sourcing and handling. Quarry
3247  fines, industrial by-products, and demolition wastes proposed as EW feedstocks may already
3248  undergo passive weathering during storage, transport, or disposal. Moisture exposure, particle size
3249  distribution, and storage duration can enable some degree of baseline alkalinity generation and
3250  CDR prior to any deliberate EW deployment. Although these baseline fluxes remain poorly
3251  constrained empirically, they may be non-negligible in certain contexts, implying that conservative
3252  baselining assumptions or partial discounting of feedstock fractions may be required to avoid over-
3253  crediting (e.g., ref. 345). Registry protocols incorporate these processes into net CDR calculation
3254  guidelines as "counterfactual weathering"431,432 or "baseline feedstock CDR".504
3255
3256  Pre-existing in-field weathering and hydrological alkalinity fluxes further complicate
3257  counterfactual definitions, particularly for aqueous phase MRV approaches. Riverine and soil-
3258  water chemistry integrates both anthropogenic and natural signals, such that attributing observed
3259  changes in alkalinity or DIC to EW requires distinguishing project-induced fluxes from pre-
3260  existing activity and natural variability. Failure to adequately characterise these baselines and
3261  monitor variability--especially in regions with high inherent silicate or carbonate weathering
3262  rates--creates a risk of crediting CDR that would have occurred irrespective of the project.345
3263
3264  Taken together, these factors indicate that additionality for EW cannot be demonstrated through a
3265  single generic test but instead requires project-specific counterfactual baselines that integrate land
3266
3267

      85
      Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

3268  management history, amendment substitution effects, feedstock counterfactual uses, and natural
3269  geochemical fluxes. Current VCM methodologies address these challenges through combinations
3270  of practice-based eligibility criteria, financial additionality screens, quantitative baselining
3271  approaches, and monitoring of control plots (e.g.,ref. 431,432,504). However, substantial uncertainty
3272  remains. Improving empirical constraints on baseline liming-related carbon fluxes, passive
3273  feedstock weathering, and natural alkalinity export is therefore a key priority for strengthening
3274  MRV robustness and safeguarding the environmental integrity of EW credits as deployment scales.

      86
      Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

3275 5 Carbon Dioxide Removal: fluxes, potentials, and the

3276 VCM

3277 Building on the mechanistic understanding of EW and emerging MRV frameworks, this section

3278  assesses its quantitative contribution to climate mitigation. We distinguish between CDR fluxes,
3279  defined as area- and time-normalized removal (e.g., tCO2 ha1 yr1); CDR rates, referring to total
3280
      removal per unit time for a defined system (e.g., annual removal at national or global scales); and

3281 CDR potentials, which integrate assumptions over both space and time (e.g., cumulative global

3282 removal by 2100). We first synthesize CDR fluxes reported across the literature, then evaluate

3283 how these translate into regional and global rates and potentials. Finally, we situate these estimates

3284 within the evolving VCM, distinguishing between credit sales and realized deliveries to assess the

3285 role of markets in enabling and scaling EW deployment.

3286

3287

3288 5.1 CDR fluxes

3289 In this section, we focus on CDR fluxes expressed per unit land area and time. This framing reflects

3290 how EW is most commonly implemented and quantified at the field scale: silicate materials are

3291 applied over a defined land area, and weathering-derived carbon or alkalinity fluxes are

3292 subsequently inferred from soil, pore-water, runoff, or catchment-scale measurements integrated

3293 over time. As a result, per-area and time removal fluxes emerge naturally from experimental

3294 designs and are often used to compare findings with the literature as well as reviews of EW
3295 efficacy.161 Importantly, area-normalised fluxes also serve as key inputs for scaling exercises, and

3296 some regional to global CDR estimates are derived by extrapolating such fluxes across assumed
3297 land areas and deployment scenarios.245

3298

3299

3300 5.1.1 CDR fluxes across the literature

3301  To enable comparison across studies, reported CDR estimates were harmonised to a common unit
3302  basis of tCO2 ha1 yr1 using the spatial scales, durations, and system boundaries described in the
3303
      original publications. Where studies reported CDR per unit time or deployment area only, fluxes

3304 were converted based on the reported experimental or modelling conditions. In a limited number

3305 of cases, additional assumptions--such as soil mixing depth or bulk density--were required where

3306 these parameters were not explicitly reported; given the ~6-order-of-magnitude spread in reported

3307 EW fluxes (Figure 18), such assumptions are unlikely to affect the identification of dominant

3308 patterns. The compiled dataset includes both potential CDR estimates derived from weathering

3309 rates and realized CDR estimates that account for loss processes. Where studies reported both,

3310 only the realized CDR after losses was retained; where only one estimate was available, that value

3311 was used. As a result, the dataset combines estimates derived under different system boundaries,

3312 MRV approaches, and assumptions regarding included loss processes (see discussion below).

3313

3314 Across the compiled literature, the dataset includes 511 unique CDR flux datapoints derived from
3315 61 independent studies that report, or could be converted to, units of tCO2 ha1 yr1. 466 of these

3316 datapoints have CDR fluxes larger than 0. These datapoints represent distinct combinations of

3317 study type, experimental or modelling context, feedstock characteristics, application conditions,

3318 and MRV approach and are shown in Figure 18. Together, this compilation captures the current

3319 quantitative landscape of EW CDR flux estimates across experimental scales, methodological

                                             87
      Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

3320  approaches, and deployment contexts. The median CDR flux across datapoints contributing to
3321  Figure 18 is 0.84 tCO2 ha1 yr1. Out of all experimental studies (field and laboratory studies),
3322  ~84% of all reported fluxes are above 0, with ~34% being below 0.1 tCO2 ha1 yr1, ~25% being
3323  between 0.1-1 tCO2 ha1 yr1, and ~33% being between 1-10 tCO2 ha1 yr1, though many of the
3324  higher estimates do not necessarily reflect realistic field conditions (see discussion below). This
3325  range aligns closely with earlier non-peer-reviewed synthesis efforts515 as well as previous
3326  reviews.161 Despite this wide range, central tendencies differ only moderately between modelling
3327  and experimental studies: median CDR fluxes are 1.10 tCO2 ha1 yr1 for modelling studies and
3328  0.55 tCO2 ha1 yr1 for experimental studies, while arithmetic means are 3.12 and 3.77 tCO2 ha1
3329  yr1, respectively, reflecting the influence of rare high-end values. These apparent differences are
3330  not statistically significant when comparing modelled and experimental fluxes either on raw values
3331  or on log-transformed positive values.
3332
3333  When placed in the context of broader discussions of EW deployment potential, the fluxes
3334  compiled here fall within--but generally toward the lower end of--the ranges often assumed to
3335  be feasible in large-scale assessments. For example, global projections of ~0.35­0.76 GtCO2 yr1
3336  by 2050 and ~0.7­1.1 GtCO2 yr1 by 2100 have been derived by scaling a baseline sequestration
3337  flux of 1.5 tCO2 ha1 yr1 according to local temperature and soil water surplus, with some tropical
3338  hotspots reaching ~4 tCO2 ha1 yr1.245 Similarly, cumulative removals of ~64 GtCO2 from
3339  agricultural hotspots and ~215 GtCO2 when extrapolated to all croplands over the period 2006­
3340  2080 have been simulated using a soil reactive transport framework.24 These projections
3341  correspond to modelled CDR fluxes of ~1.2­1.6 tCO2 ha1 yr1 across favourable cropland regions
3342  in Africa, Thailand, eastern China, and India, around ~1 tCO2 ha1 yr1 across major agricultural
3343  regions of North America, South America, and Europe/Russia, and <0.6 tCO2 ha1 yr1 in arid
3344  regions such as Australia.24 The empirical and modelling fluxes compiled here overlap with these
3345  assumed magnitudes but tend to cluster toward lower values and exhibit substantial variability
3346  across study designs and environmental settings. Moreover, not all compiled fluxes fully account
3347  for loss processes or transient early weathering pulses (see below), which may further widen the
3348  gap between reported plot-scale fluxes and the sustained fluxes assumed in large-scale EW
3349  simulations (see Section 5.2).
3350
3351  The highest experimental CDR fluxes appear to cluster in studies where conditions are expected
3352  to favour unusually high realized CDR fluxes, for example by using high feedstock application
3353  inputs. The two field-scale outliers are engineered/artificial soil systems using Ca-rich
3354  construction-derived materials or engineered substrates, with CDR inferred from inorganic
3355  carbonate accumulation.448,450 The laboratory data exceeding 10 tCO2 ha1 yr1 are likewise
3356  dominated by short-duration pot or column experiments using highly reactive feedstocks,
3357  relatively high amendment loadings, humid or intensively irrigated conditions, elevated CO2 or
3358  otherwise optimized reaction environments, that do not (fully) take into account loss processes
3359  beyond the system boundary, and/or use chemical reagents to mimic chemical
3360  weathering.63,177,237,244,246,449,469 Thus, the >10 tCO2 ha1 yr1 values should be interpreted primarily
3361  as high-end estimates from favourable or engineered settings, rather than as representative central
3362  estimates for field-scale EW deployment.
3363
3364

      88
      Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

3365  Figure 18: Distribution and scale dependence of Enhanced Weathering (EW) Carbon Dioxide Removal (CDR) fluxes:
3366  Overview of harmonised EW CDR fluxes expressed in tCO2 ha1 yr1 across laboratory, field, and model studies.
3367  Positive CDR estimates are shown on the logarithmic axis after filtering out within-study duplicates, while zero and
3368  negative CDR estimates are grouped in the "none" bar.
3369
      5.1.2 Key limitations of CDR flux synthesis and literature estimates
3370
3371  Enhanced Weathering is governed by a highly coupled set of physical, chemical, and biological
      processes (see Section 3.2), such that observed CDR fluxes reflect the interaction of primary
3372  mineral dissolution kinetics, soil hydrology and chemistry, biological cycling, feedstock
3373  properties, application strategies, and the MRV approaches used to quantify removal (Section 4).
3374  Consequently, variability in reported EW fluxes reflects the combined influence of multiple
3375  interacting parameters that differ across studies and deployment contexts, such that attributing
3376  CDR outcomes to any single variable--such as feedstock type, application rate, MRV approach,
3377  or study design--necessarily oversimplifies the system. When data from many sites and
3378  experiments are compared simultaneously, the scatter introduced by unaligned deployment details
3379  and process regimes can obscure underlying parameter dependencies, such that genuine trends or
3380  causal relationships may not be apparent in cross-study comparisons broken down by a single
3381  parameter, even when they exist. In contrast, controlled laboratory or field experiments that isolate
3382  individual variables while holding others constant can often resolve such dependencies more
3383  clearly, even if their results are not directly transferable to larger spatial or temporal scales as well
3384  as conditions outside of those explored within the experiment.
3385
3386  The flux compilation presented above (Figure 18) carries several important limitations. Most
3387  notably, it aggregates estimates derived from fundamentally different MRV approaches and
3388  modelling frameworks. As discussed in Section 4, these approaches differ in the extent to which
3389
3390
3391

      89
      Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

3392  reported fluxes represent potential CDR associated with initial weathering versus net CDR after
3393  accounting for post-weathering losses (or only a subset thereof). Consequently, the compiled
3394  fluxes combine fundamentally different definitions of CDR, reflecting divergent assumptions
3395  about system boundaries and timescales: weathering-derived CDR may be realized progressively
3396  over time, whereas changes in export fluxes can reflect more immediate CDR signals. These
3397  conceptual differences can translate into order-of-magnitude variation in reported CDR fluxes
3398  across both modelling68,437 and experimental studies.216,343,445
3399
3400  Another key limitation of both the existing literature and the synthesis presented here is the
3401  extreme heterogeneity in temporal and spatial scales over which EW CDR fluxes are derived.
3402  Large spatial extents and long durations are almost exclusively captured by models (Figure 19),
3403  whereas experimental studies are largely confined to small spatial scales and short durations.
3404  While this reflects the essential role of models in exploring scales not accessible to current
3405  experiments (see also Section 3.6), it also introduces fundamental challenges for comparability
3406  and interpretation across approaches.
3407
3408

3409  Figure 19: Distribution of study duration and spatial scale with experimental studies grouped. (a) Distribution of
3410  study durations (years) by primary study type (field, laboratory, and model studies). (b) Distribution of spatial scales
3411  (hectares), where field and laboratory studies are combined into a single experimental category and compared against
3412  model studies. Values reflect the subset of studies for which duration and spatial extent was extracted from the
3413  literature.
3414
      Extrapolating short experiments to annual removal fluxes, unless accounted for through
3415  comprehensive geochemical modelling,252,371 assumes linear scaling of weathering fluxes over
3416  time, an assumption that can overestimate long-term CDR fluxes when applied to short
3417  experiments, particularly where early-time dissolution kinetics 116 driven by fresh mineral surfaces
3418  and availability of fine particles161,172 dominate observed fluxes. As highlighted in recent non-peer
3419  reviewed synthesis work,515 such linear extrapolation is widespread in the primary literature and
3420  reflects a structural challenge rather than an analytical choice unique to this review. Accordingly,
3421  where studies reported CDR per unit area without explicit annualization, our harmonisation
3422
3423
3424

      90
      Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

3425  procedure likewise converts these values to tCO2 ha1 yr1 based on the reported study duration,
3426  inheriting the same assumptions and limitations as the underlying literature.
3427
3428  A similar limitation applies to spatial scaling. Experimental systems are typically conducted at
3429  small spatial extents (Figure 19b) under well-mixed and highly controlled conditions, whereas
3430  field systems integrate across heterogeneous flow paths, mineral surface accessibility, and
3431  transport regimes that evolve with scale. As a result, apparent weathering rates tend to decrease
3432  with increasing spatial extent, reflecting the growing influence of transport limitation, hydrologic
3433  variability, and system heterogeneity, such that direct extrapolation of fluxes from plot- or reactor-
3434  scale studies to field or regional scales may likewise overestimate sustained CDR if these scale-
3435  dependent constraints are not explicitly represented.116
3436
3437  A further limitation is that reported CDR fluxes are not always accompanied by sufficient
3438  information on application rate, cumulative applied mass, or the elemental basis of the CDR
3439  calculation. This is important because identical soil, climate, and feedstock conditions can yield
3440  very different area-normalised CDR fluxes depending on the amount of material applied.
3441  Moreover, many studies report elemental release, alkalinity generation, or partial cation-based
3442  estimates rather than complete CDR fluxes, and some calculations consider only Ca and Mg rather
3443  than all relevant alkalinity-generating major cations. As a result, apparent differences in reported
3444  CDR fluxes may partly reflect differences in dose, reporting conventions, and calculation
3445  boundaries rather than true differences in EW performance.
3446
3447  Fully disentangling the drivers of EW CDR across the literature will ultimately require a dedicated
3448  meta-analysis employing approaches such as mixed-effects modelling, Bayesian inference
3449  frameworks, or machine-learning-based pattern recognition to account for hierarchical structure,
3450  collinearity, and context dependence across studies. However, implementing these methods lies
3451  beyond the scope of the present review and is further constrained by inconsistent reporting
3452  practices and incomplete metadata across studies (see section 3.2).
3453
3454  5.2 Global and regional CDR rates and potentials

3455  This section examines EW potential across three linked dimensions. It first clarifies how different
3456  types of potential--particularly technical, economic, and sustainable potential1--can be
3457  understood in the context of EW, and why many existing estimates do not fit cleanly into these
3458  categories. It then reviews the range of currently available global and regional rate and potential
3459  estimates, highlighting how these depend on model structure, process representation, and
3460  deployment assumptions. Finally, it moves beyond climate-centric optimization to highlight that
3461  EW performance is governed by multiple interacting parameters and objectives, such that no single
3462  globally optimal deployment environment is likely to exist and system-specific strategies may can
3463  successfully optimize for various outcomes.
3464
3465  5.2.1 Types of potentials in the context of EW
3466
      Estimates of the potential scale of CDR are commonly categorized using standard assessment
3467  terminology developed in climate-mitigation research. Three categories are typically
3468  distinguished: technical, economic, and sustainable potential.1 Technical potential refers to the
3469
3470

      91
      Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

3471  quantity of CDR achievable under biophysical, geochemical, and technological constraints while
3472  assuming deployment is unconstrained by economic, policy, or social considerations. Economic
3473  potential represents the portion of technical potential that remains once costs, infrastructure,
3474  market responses, and system-wide trade-offs are considered. Sustainable potential further restricts
3475  deployment by incorporating environmental safeguards and broader governance or societal
3476  constraints. In the EW literature, an additional category sometimes reported is the CDR potential
3477  implied by available feedstock resources, which quantifies the theoretical CO2 uptake associated
3478  with the total mass of suitable rock assuming complete dissolution by carbonic acid.18,133,410 These
3479  estimates provide an upper-bound resource perspective by assuming complete dissolution with
3480  carbonic acid18,37,126 but do not represent deployable removal because they abstract from kinetic
3481  limitations, hydrologic transport, and environmental feedbacks that determine whether dissolution
3482  products translate into durable carbon storage.325
3483
3484  For EW, the distinction between these potential categories reflects the wide range of processes
3485  controlling whether applied rock ultimately results in net CDR. Estimates of technical potential
3486  must account for a number of processes to realistically reflect the CDR potential of EW--such as
3487  mineral weathering kinetics, saturation feedbacks in soil porewaters, formation of secondary
3488  minerals, cation exchange reactions, hydrologic export of alkalinity, and downstream
3489  losses.19,68,172,325 Upstream emissions associated with mining, grinding, transport, and spreading
3490  can also substantially reduce net removal and must therefore be considered in technical potentials.
3491  Economic potential further incorporates constraints related to energy costs, supply chains,
3492  infrastructure, and monitoring and verification requirements, while sustainable potential
3493  introduces environmental and governance limits such as soil and water quality thresholds,
3494  ecosystem impacts, and social acceptance. The relationships between these categories and the
3495  processes influencing them are summarised in Figure 20.
3496
3497  In practice, however, no existing EW assessments can be fully classified into any of these potential
3498  categories based on the criteria defined above and in Figure 20. This limitation primarily reflects
3499  the evolving understanding of EW system dynamics rather than shortcomings of earlier studies at
3500  the time of conception. Several processes now recognised as key controls on realized CDR--such
3501  as cation exchange reactions, secondary phase formation, and other soil-chemical feedbacks--
3502  have only recently been highlighted through field observations and modelling
3503  studies.19,68,172,216,325,343,445 At the same time, empirical datasets required to constrain many of these
3504  processes remain limited, meaning that incorporating them into models can introduce substantial
3505  additional uncertainty--one of the main motivations behind the database effort behind this
3506  systematic review. Modelling EW across heterogeneous soils, climates, and hydrologic systems is
3507  therefore inherently complex, and the progression of modelling approaches reflects successive
3508  efforts to incorporate these controls as understanding and data availability improve.
3509
3510  Consequently, reported EW scale estimates must be interpreted in light of the processes
3511  represented in each analysis, the assumptions and system boundaries of each study, and the
3512  empirical data they are based on. The literature spans a wide range of modelling approaches that
3513  capture different subsets of processes. Some assessments rely on simplified or empirically
3514  constrained formulations that extrapolate from limited geochemical observations, enabling
3515  extensive sensitivity analysis while requiring relatively few input parameters. Such studies that
3516

3518  Enhanced Weathering (EW).
3519

                                                                    93
      Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

3520  e.g. prescribe mineral dissolution rates without accounting for feedbacks such as porewater
3521  saturation, secondary mineral formation, hydrologic export, or other loss pathways can still
3522  provide useful bounding or sensitivity insights but should be interpreted as theoretical or
3523  exploratory estimates rather than deployable technical potentials. Others employ more
3524  mechanistically explicit frameworks, such as RTMs, that simulate mineral dissolution kinetics,
3525  soil chemistry, hydrologic transport, and downstream carbon storage pathways (see Section
3526  3.5).19,20,22,24,68,97,108,110,125,232,252,371,404,405 These models provide critical insights into system
3527  behaviour and are becoming increasingly important, but they also require numerous input
3528  parameters that are often poorly constrained empirically and can drive order-of-magnitude
3529  differences in predicted outcomes.97,172,252,371,437,516 They are also computationally intensive, which
3530  can limit systematic exploration of parameter uncertainty. Simpler models therefore remain
3531  valuable because they enable broader testing of assumptions and parameter space, even though
3532  they may omit processes influencing net CDR.
3533
3534  These differences are particularly relevant for scenario-based assessments, including those
3535  embedded in IAMs discussed in Section 11. Such frameworks explore EW deployment within
3536  mitigation portfolios optimized under economic and policy constraints but currently differ
3537  substantially in how explicitly they represent weathering processes and associated geochemical
3538  limitations. Their implied CDR magnitudes therefore depend strongly on underlying assumptions
3539  about weathering kinetics, environmental feedbacks, and loss processes. The following section
3540  reviews some of the most widely cited EW CDR potential estimates while interpreting them in
3541  light of the processes and assumptions represented in each study.
3542
      5.2.2 Global rates and potentials
3543
      In this section we first examine three global EW CDR potential estimates that have been
3544  particularly influential in shaping the literature over the past decade. These studies differ
3545  substantially in their modelling approaches, assumptions, system boundaries, and the extent to
3546  which key soil and transport processes are explicitly represented. Understanding these differences
3547  is essential for interpreting reported removal magnitudes and for placing individual estimates
3548  within the broader range of projected EW potentials. The underlying methodological choices and
3549  process representations of these studies--interpreted in relation to the potential definitions and
3550  criteria outlined above--are summarised in Table 5. Together, these analyses represent successive
3551  methodological steps toward quantifying the large-scale CDR potential of EW. We then discuss
3552  additional global estimates, including both simplified modelling approaches and empirically
3553  constrained assessments, some of which have emerged more recently.
3554
3555  One of the earliest global-scale assessments provided a landmark quantitative framework that
3556  helped structure subsequent research by integrating weathering kinetics, particle size, deployment
3557  extent, and cost considerations within a single analysis.23 This work demonstrated the strong
3558  sensitivity of projected removal to grain size and reaction rates and reported global cropland-scale
3559  capacities of up to ~95 GtCO2 yr-1 for dunite and ~4.9 GtCO2 yr-1 for basalt under favourable
3560  assumptions.23 As summarised in Table 5, these estimates are based on prescribed dissolution
3561  kinetics and do not include negative feedbacks on primary weathering rates or subsequent loss
3562  processes (cf., Section 3.4). Because these processes influence the translation of mineral
3563

      as realizable technical potential, even though the study itself remains a seminal and influential

      benchmark. This interpretation is further supported when expressed as implied area-normalized
      fluxes: global average CDR fluxes derived from Strefler et al. (2018)23 correspond to ~120 tCO2
      ha1 yr1 for olivine and ~6.2 tCO2 ha1 yr1 for basalt across croplands. These values are higher

      than most of the data compiled in Section 5.1, and it seems unlikely that such fluxes could be

      achieved globally on average, reinforcing that such estimates represent upper-bound potentials

      under idealized assumptions rather than field-realistic technical potentials. Earlier conceptual

      modelling work similarly suggested that EW of olivine could sequester on the order of ~3.7 GtCO2
      yr-1 under favourable conditions, although these estimates relied on simplified representations of
      weathering environments and deployment logistics.17

      More recent global assessments employ RTM frameworks that simulate the coupled evolution of
      mineral dissolution, porewater chemistry, and solute transport in soils, allowing chemical

                                                         96
      Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

3592  feedbacks and loss pathways to influence projected removal.19,24 These approaches explicitly
3593  represent processes that can regulate weathering efficiency, including feedbacks associated with
3594  porewater saturation and the partitioning of weathering products among carbon reservoirs. At the
3595  same time, projected removal remains sensitive to model structure, parameterization, and
3596  deployment assumptions, and therefore depends strongly on which processes are represented and
3597  how they are constrained empirically. As summarised in Table 5, the studies differ substantially
3598  in the processes represented, parameterization strategies, and underlying assumptions regarding
3599  feedstock deployment. One modelling framework chooses CDR rates emergent from the model
3600  solution space and then optimizes the spatial extent of rock application to minimize land footprint
3601  and cost. Under these assumptions, national-scale simulations suggest that applying basalt to
3602  roughly 10­57% of cropland areas--depending on country and global CDR targets--could
3603  contribute on the order of 0.5­2 GtCO2 yr-1, corresponding to cumulative removals of
3604  approximately 25­100 GtCO2 over multi-decadal timescales.19 Similarly, simulations using a fixed
3605  application rate of 10 t ha-1 yr-1 yield cumulative totals of ~64 GtCO2 from agricultural hotspots
3606  (>70% ag-extent) and ~215 GtCO2 when extrapolated to all croplands over the simulation period
3607  of 2006-2080.24
3608
3609  These frameworks19,24 represent important advances in the explicit representation of soil
3610  geochemical processes and are among the most substantiated modelling approaches currently
3611  available for evaluating EW performance at scale. Nevertheless, the resulting estimates remain
3612  contingent on assumptions regarding system boundaries and deployment conditions--including
3613  life cycle emissions, sustained amendment inputs, feedstock availability, and mining constraints
3614  (Table 5)--as well as on the specific processes represented in the models. Taken together, these
3615  considerations indicate that projected removals should be interpreted in the context of model
3616  assumptions and may not represent definitive bounds on achievable technical potential. Rather,
3617  these studies reflect successive methodological advances toward increasingly comprehensive
3618  representations of soil weathering systems and provide a critical quantitative foundation for
3619  assessing the large-scale feasibility of EW.
3620
3621  Other global assessments use simplified, parameterized weathering formulations rather than fully
3622  coupled soil reactive-transport frameworks, allowing rapid sensitivity analysis but providing a less
3623  complete representation of system behaviour. For example, a global cropland model that expresses
3624  dissolution as a function of soil chemistry, temperature, and mineral type (wollastonite, forsterite,
3625  and lizardite; assuming 1 t ha-1 yr-1 application) produces highly variable outcomes across
3626  environmental settings, with cumulative sequestration spanning a wide range but reaching on the
3627  order of several tens of Gt by 2100 under favourable assumptions.412 Similarly, another recent
3628  global assessment estimates EW removal by scaling a baseline sequestration flux (~1.5 tCO2 ha-1
3629  yr-1 under moderate silicate application; higher than median fluxes compiled here--Section 5.1)
3630  according to local temperature and soil water surplus, reflecting climatic controls on weathering
3631  kinetics.245 Applying this approach at ~5 km resolution produces projected removals of ~0.35­
3632  0.76 GtCO2 yr-1 by 2050 and ~0.7­1.1 GtCO2 yr-1 by 2100, with strong geographic variability
3633  driven by climatic suitability and projected adoption patterns.
3634
3635  At the lower end of global estimates, one early empirically-constrained assessment compiled
3636  observed bicarbonate fluxes from mineral applications and natural weathering environments to
3637  derive a first-order removal potential of ~0.24 GtCO2 yr-1, grounding its calculation in measured

      97
      Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

3638  solute export rather than prescribed reaction kinetics and thereby inherently integrating many
3639  processes that can diminish net CDR.14 Similarly, a later global analysis employed a simplified
3640  parameterization linking weathering rates to climatic and geochemical variables but calibrated
3641  these relationships against experimentally observed dissolution behaviour in EW experiments,
3642  yielding upper-bound estimates of ~0.22 ± 0.16 GtCO2 yr-1 and ~17 ± 13 Gt cumulative removal
3643  by 2100.517 Reflecting sparse experimental data particularly for the earlier study, both approaches
3644  rely on observational datasets that remain minimally available at the global scale, which limits
3645  both their ability to resolve environmental heterogeneity and the accuracy of the empirical
3646  grounding. As empirical datasets increasingly expand through published field trial results,
3647  approaches that scale removal from measured fluxes rather than assumed kinetics will become
3648  increasingly feasible for constraining global potentials over the coming years.
3649
3650  Sustainable potentials for EW have not yet been robustly quantified as none of the global-scale
3651  models discussed above contain feedstock application thresholds based on environmental
3652  considerations and assume high and/or continued rock application (cf., Table 5). Such deployment
3653  scenarios are unlikely to be achievable when considering both environmental129,131 and agronomic
3654  limitations such as rock build up in soils or pH overshoot.19,518,519 For example, simulations of
3655  basalt application at ~40 t ha-1 yr-1 indicate that concentrations of elements such as Cu and Ni could
3656  exceed national soil quality limits within years to decades depending on rock composition and
3657  jurisdiction, with limits for dunite being exceeded much quicker.129 Even lower application rates
3658  may eventually lead to regulatory thresholds being reached, implying that deployment ceilings
3659  could be set not by geochemical potential but by environmental constraints.129 It should be noted
3660  that these thresholds were originally developed for comparatively fast-reacting soil amendments
3661  rather than slowly dissolving silicates, and therefore may ultimately require reassessment.129,131
3662  However, even under revised standards, continued applications would still progressively
3663  accumulate trace metals in soils that may remain largely immobile for centuries due to slow
3664  weathering kinetics but could ultimately become bioavailable or soluble if soil conditions change.
3665  Collectively, these considerations indicate that existing large-scale potentials should not be
3666  interpreted as sustainable potentials, but rather as theoretical upper bounds pending integrated
3667  agronomic, geochemical, and regulatory evaluation.129,131
3668
3669  Taken together, current literature indicates that EW could contribute meaningfully to CDR at large
3670  scales, but that projected magnitudes remain highly contingent on model structure, deployment
3671  assumptions, and the extent to which key soil and transport processes are represented. Across the
3672  studies discussed here, empirically constrained flux-based estimates suggest lower-bound global
3673  technical potentials on the order of ~0.22­0.24 GtCO2 yr-1,14,517 while climate-based modulation
3674  of empirically constrained area normalized CDR fluxes project ~0.35­0.76 GtCO2 yr-1 by mid-
3675  century and ~0.7­1.1 GtCO2 yr-1 by 2100.245 More process-explicit reactive-transport simulations
3676  indicate that sustained large-scale deployment could plausibly reach ~0.5­2 GtCO2 yr-1 under
3677  favourable assumptions,19 with cumulative removals of tens to hundreds of Gt over multi-decadal
3678  timescales, including global projections of ~64­215 GtCO2.24 Despite substantial differences in
3679  modelling approaches, parameterization strategies, and the extent to which loss pathways and
3680  environmental constraints are represented, most current assessments converge on a broadly similar
3681  order-of-magnitude range of roughly ~0.2­2 GtCO2 yr-1 for large-scale CDR potential.520 While
3682  uncertainties in currently poorly constrained input parameters could in principle shift future
3683  estimates upward, the more likely outcome is that these ranges represent the upper bound of

      98
      Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

3684  achievable technical potential, as existing assessments do not yet incorporate all CDR losses and
3685  constraints required to fully characterize deployable technical potentials (cf., Figure 20 and Table
3686  5).1
3687
3688  Hence, interpreting these values requires careful distinction between what is chemically possible,
3689  what is physically achievable, and what is environmentally permissible, as they reflect different
3690  system boundaries and evidentiary bases. As modelling frameworks, observational datasets, and
3691  process understanding continue to improve, convergence among these estimate classes is likely,
3692  enabling more robust quantification of realizable and sustainable potentials. Until such
3693  harmonisation is achieved, global CDR projections for EW are best understood as scenario-
3694  dependent ranges rather than single best estimates, with their primary value lying in identifying
3695  governing controls, research priorities, and deployment constraints. A model intercomparison
3696  project could provide a much better view on key differences between models and likely a more
3697  accurate look at global EW potential.
3698
3699  5.2.3 Regional rates and potentials

3700  This section breaks down the global-scale estimates discussed above into their regional
3701  components. Numerous additional regional assessments of EW potential exist, including those
3702  derived from LCA frameworks and IAMs. These studies are discussed elsewhere in this review
3703  (cf., Sections 8 and 11). However, many derive their geochemical removal estimates from earlier
3704  process-based studies or apply simplified weathering parameterizations.19,23 To avoid duplication
3705  and maintain conceptual consistency, we therefore focus here on how the studies discussed above
3706  resolve spatial variability within their modelling frameworks. We also exclude analyses that
3707  extrapolate national or continental potentials from single experimental sites.
3708
3709  Early global modelling already embedded strong climatic controls into projections of EW potential
3710  by parameterizing silicate dissolution as a function of grain size and laboratory-derived mineral
3711  weathering kinetics, with reaction rates scaled by temperature and soil chemistry 23. This
3712  parameterization leads to substantially higher modelled removal efficiencies in warm climates and
3713  consequently concentrates much of the global potential in tropical and monsoon-influenced
3714  agricultural regions. Under these assumptions, India, Brazil, Southeast Asia, and China together
3715  account for roughly 75% of the modelled global CDR potential.23
3716
3717  Similarly, as a direct consequence of model parametrization, subsequent spatially explicit RTM-
3718  based assessments highlight strong climatic controls on the geographic distribution of EW
3719  potential.19,24 A global techno-economic cropland deployment analysis evaluating removal targets
3720  of 0.5­2 GtCO2 yr-1 finds that the largest national contributions arise in countries combining
3721  extensive cropland area with favourable weathering conditions, particularly China, India, the
3722  United States, and Brazil.19 Under scenarios reaching these global targets, national contributions
3723  range from roughly 0.13­0.53 GtCO2 yr-1 for China, 0.11­0.42 GtCO2 yr-1 for the United States,
3724  and 0.15­0.49 GtCO2 yr-1 for India, with Brazil contributing approximately 0.04­0.17 GtCO2 yr-1
3725  depending on the assumed deployment fraction.19 Consistent with the climatic controls on
3726  weathering kinetics, regions with warm and seasonally wet climates--including Brazil and
3727  Indonesia19--show comparatively high removal potential per unit cropland area, with tropical and
3728  monsoon regions showing the highest uptake rates.24 Modelled CDR fluxes reach approximately
3729

      99
      Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

3730  1.2­1.6 tCO2 ha-1 yr-1 in cropland regions of Africa, Thailand, eastern China, and India, compared
3731  with roughly ~1 tCO2 ha-1 yr-1 across major agricultural regions of North America, South America,
3732
3733  and Europe/Russia, while arid regions such as Australia show substantially lower rates below ~0.6
3734  tCO2 ha-1 yr-1.24 These spatial differences translate into strongly uneven regional contributions,
3735
3736  with Asia dominating modelled cumulative removal followed by Africa, Europe, South America,
3737
3738  and North America, reflecting particularly strong removal across tropical and monsoon-influenced
      croplands (Figure 21).245

3739  Figure 21: Cumulative Carbon Dioxide Removal (CDR) potential from Enhanced Weathering (EW) for 2030­2060
3740  under a human­nature coupled scenario, expressed as tCO2 km2. Values represent land-area-normalized CDR,
3741  integrating both cropland-area-specific EW efficiency and the fraction of cropland within each grid cell. As such, the
3742  mapped patterns reflect the combined influence of biophysical weathering potential and cropland availability, rather
3743  than per-cropland-area removal rates alone. The human­nature coupled scenario accounts for feedbacks between
3744  climate change and societal response, in which EW adoption accelerates as warming crosses key thresholds, reflecting
3745  increased policy action, risk perception, and deployment effort under escalating climate stress. Figure based on data
3746  in Tu et al. (2026).245
3747
      Scenario analyses (reflecting different policy choices) that incorporate technology diffusion
3748  dynamics further suggest that regional deployment patterns depend not only on geochemical
3749  potential but also on socioeconomic adoption trajectories.245 In this global framework, spatially
3750  explicit CDR fluxes are first estimated by modulating nominal sequestration fluxes (1.5 tCO2 ha-1
3751  yr-1) with temperature and soil water surplus, resulting in systematically higher potential
3752  weathering rates in warm and humid regions. When these biophysical estimates are combined with
3753  technology diffusion-based adoption trajectories derived from historical agricultural technologies,
3754  early deployment is concentrated in high-income regions such as North America and Europe,
3755  which lead adoption during the 2030­2040 period. However, as diffusion progresses, deployment
3756  expands rapidly in regions with large cropland areas and favourable climatic conditions. By mid-
3757  century, countries in South and East Asia and parts of Latin America dominate modelled removal,
3758  with South Asia projected to achieve the largest cumulative sequestration (~14 GtCO2 by 2100),
3759  followed by East Asia & Pacific (~13 GtCO2) and Latin America (~9 GtCO2). In contrast, Europe
3760
3761
3762

      100
      Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

3763  and North America contribute smaller cumulative totals (~3 GtCO2 each) despite earlier adoption
3764  because of comparatively lower per-area weathering rates in temperate climates. These shifts are
3765  also reflected across income groups: high-income countries account for roughly 39­57% of global
3766  EW removal by 2040 but decline to <12% by 2100, while low- and lower-middle-income countries
3767  increase their share from ~20­29% in 2040 to nearly 60% by the end of the century as adoption
3768  accelerates in climatically favourable regions.
3769
3770  Taken together, these studies consistently indicate that regional variability in EW potential arises
3771  from the interaction of climatic controls on mineral dissolution, the spatial distribution of cropland
3772  available for deployment, and socioeconomic factors governing adoption. Across modelling
3773  frameworks, the highest geochemical efficiencies occur in warm and humid environments across
3774  tropical and monsoon-influenced regions, while the largest absolute contributions arise in
3775  countries combining favourable climates with extensive agricultural land area, including China,
3776  India, the United States, and Brazil. Importantly, these spatial patterns are not only empirical
3777  outcomes but also reflect how weathering is parameterized within many global models. Most
3778  approaches explicitly scale dissolution rates with climatic variables--commonly using Arrhenius-
3779  type temperature dependencies and hydrological constraints on reaction progress--such that
3780  higher modelled removal rates in warm and seasonally wet regions are partly embedded in the
3781  representation of weathering kinetics itself 19,23,24,245.
3782
3783  5.2.4 Multi-parameter optimization: identifying system-specific "sweet spots"

3784  As demonstrated above, many current global assessments implicitly or explicitly optimize EW
3785  deployment through climatic suitability. In most modelling frameworks, higher temperatures and
3786  excess moisture accelerate silicate dissolution kinetics, leading to the conclusion that warm and
3787  humid regions provide the most favourable conditions for maximizing weathering rates.19,24,245,521
3788  While this framing is useful for identifying broad deployment envelopes, it represents only one
3789  dimension of a much larger optimization problem.
3790
3791  Enhanced Weathering outcomes are influenced by a wide range of interacting environmental,
3792  geochemical, and agronomic controls (Section 3.2)--these are illustrated in the inner ring of Figure
3793  22. They include soil properties such as pCO2,15,63,97 CEC,68 BS,68,216 SOM,42,43 as well as reactive
3794  soil mineral pools and soil acidity/pH 62,68,216; climatic drivers such as temperature and water
3795  inputs;62 and feedstock characteristics including mineral reactivity and dissolution rates,62,119,120
3796  contaminant concentrations.129,130
3797
3798  At the same time, absolute CDR (and initial CDR before losses, for which weathering rate is often
3799  used as a proxy) represents only one of several potential optimization outcomes. Others include
3800  minimizing environmental risks such as trace metal mobilisation131,150 or non-CO2 GHG
3801  emissions,22,83,522 maximizing agronomic co-benefits including soil fertility and crop
3802  productivity,22,47,523 minimizing losses of weathering products to secondary mineral formation or
3803  strong-acid weathering pathways,172,216,343,445,460 and enhancing SOC storage or broader soil health
3804  outcomes.94 These outcome variables illustrated in the outer ring of Figure 22 represent different
3805  objectives that deployment strategies may prioritize depending on environmental context, policy
3806  frameworks, and stakeholder priorities.
3807
3808

      101
      Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

3809
