3812
3813  Figure 22: Conceptual overview of interacting parameters and outcomes in Enhanced Weathering (EW) optimization.
3814  The inner ring illustrates key parameters that can influence EW performance, including soil properties (e.g., pH,
3815  cation exchange capacity (CEC), base saturation (BS), soil organic carbon (SOC)), climatic drivers (temperature and
3816  water input), feedstock characteristics (reactivity, Carbon Dioxide Removal (CDR) potential, contaminant
3817  concentrations), and system properties such as existing reactive minerals or acidity pools. The outer ring highlights
3818  example outcomes that deployment strategies may seek to optimize, expressed as desirable system states (e.g., low
3819  environmental risk, high agronomic co-benefits, minimal CDR losses, or increased SOC storage). Arrows indicate
3820  generalized positive or negative correlations or causal relationships between parameters and outcomes. These
3821  relationships are illustrative rather than deterministic: many represent typical tendencies observed across studies,
3822  but exceptions exist and individual systems may deviate from the indicated directionality (for example, some high-
3823  CDR feedstocks may exhibit low contaminant concentrations even though generally there is a positive correlation
3824  between both parameters130). The figure therefore emphasizes the strong interdependence among environmental
3825  conditions, feedstock properties, and system responses, illustrating that optimizing EW often requires navigating
      trade-offs across multiple interacting factors.
3826
      Several examples illustrate the complexity of this multi-parameter space. For example, soil
3827
      chemical properties such as CEC and BS influence whether newly released cations are retained on
3828  exchange sites and for how long.68 Such factors can substantially delay alkalinity formation,
      extending lag times between mineral dissolution and realized CDR from years to decades.68,343,445
3829  Soil pH represents another critical control, influencing mineral dissolution rates,119,120 nutrient
      availability,215 and trace metal mobility,524 while also regulating the balance between carbonic-
3830

3831

3832

3833

                                                                      102
      Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

3834  acid and strong-acid weathering pathways.214,231 The saturation state of porewaters with respect to
3835  secondary mineral phases can determine how rapidly weathering products are incorporated into
3836  newly formed minerals,172 thereby reducing the fraction of dissolved material contributing to net
3837  CO2 sequestration both via providing a sink for cations (incorporation into clays and carbonates)
3838  as well as limiting weathering rates of primary minerals by forming protective coatings.172
3839
3840  Changes in many of these parameters often also influence other parameters directly or through
3841  shared environmental controls. For instance, feedstocks with higher intrinsic weathering reactivity
3842  or theoretical CDR potential may also contain elevated concentrations of trace metals,129,130
3843  meaning that selecting materials to maximize weathering efficiency can simultaneously increase
3844  environmental risk and regulatory constraints. Similarly, soil chemical properties such as pH,
3845  CEC, and BS are tightly coupled through ion exchange and buffering processes in soil porewaters,
3846  linking mineral dissolution, nutrient availability, and alkalinity retention.215 Climatic factors such
3847  as temperature and water availability further exert first-order controls on a range of soil properties,
3848  including soil pH, mineral weathering rates, and the availability of reactive mineral pools.15,215 As
3849  a result, optimizing one parameter--for example through site selection or feedstock choice--may
3850  simultaneously alter multiple other system properties, improving some outcomes while
3851  constraining others.
3852
3853  Hence these parameters and outcomes are not independent. Many co-vary across landscapes or
3854  exert causal influences on one another, and they simultaneously affect multiple outcome variables.
3855  Most global assessments do not explicitly represent many of these interactions or focus on a single
3856  objective--maximizing CDR--without accounting for potential trade-offs with other outcomes.
3857  The resulting network of correlations and causal relationships is illustrated schematically in Figure
3858  22. The complexity of this network is itself the central point: EW performance emerges from a
3859  densely interconnected system of interacting parameters and outcomes, few of which can be
3860  adjusted in isolation. Because many of these relationships involve trade-offs, deployment
3861  strategies that optimize one outcome may simultaneously compromise another.
3862
3863  Returning to the climate-based optimization implicit in many modelling approaches (Sections
3864  5.2.2 & 5.2.3), the environmental conditions that maximize modelled weathering rates primarily
3865  reflect optimization of reaction kinetics. However, these conditions do not necessarily maximize
3866  system capacity to retain and export weathering products, or even realizable CDR once losses are
3867  considered. Tropical and monsoon-influenced regions often exhibit high temperatures and
3868  abundant moisture, conditions that accelerate mineral dissolution and therefore favour rapid initial
3869  weathering. However, many soils in these regions are also highly weathered, strongly acidic, and
3870  nutrient depleted, typically characterized by low BS and limited exchangeable cation pools. While
3871  these properties can create opportunities for substantial agronomic co-benefits through pH
3872  buffering and nutrient supply, they can also increase early losses of potential CDR. In strongly
3873  acidic systems, a substantial fraction of the alkalinity generated by mineral dissolution may
3874  initially be consumed by neutralizing pre-existing acidity in soils and exchange complexes before
3875  net carbon sequestration occurs, effectively creating a transient sink for weathering products.68,216
3876  Both modelling studies214 and field observations216 suggest that overcoming soil acidity can
3877  represent a significant initial demand for alkalinity in such environments that could obviate CDR
3878  from EW in some tropical settings216--a process not considered in all global and regional model
3879  estimates.

      103
      Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

3880  Additional trade-offs in the optimization-via-climate framing may arise through interactions with
3881  SOM, as mineral amendments can influence microbial activity and carbon cycling processes
3882  affecting both soil carbon storage and non-CO2 GHGs.42,43 Evidence from the agricultural liming
3883  literature indicates that SOC responses to pH changes depend strongly on initial soil conditions
3884  and are often non-linear.525 In moderately acidic, SOC-poor soils, liming can increase SOC
3885  through enhanced plant productivity and stabilisation, whereas in strongly acidic or SOC- and
3886  nitrogen-rich systems responses may be neutral or negative due to stimulated microbial
3887  decomposition.42 In some cases, these effects can be substantial: increases in soil pH may enhance
3888  decomposition of existing organic carbon, as demonstrated for tropical peat soils where EW can
3889  stimulate CO2 emissions and carbon mobilisation that partially or fully offset the expected CO2
3890  uptake.111 More generally, these responses are not unidirectional: increases in pH and nutrient
3891  availability can initially enhance decomposition and CO2 release,112,114 while longer-term changes
3892  in soil structure and mineralogy may promote stabilisation via aggregation and mineral-associated
3893  organic matter (MAOM) formation.92,526,527 As a result, EW can lead to both gains and losses of
3894  SOC, as well as redistribution among pools with contrasting turnover times, with outcomes varying
3895  across soils, feedstocks, and management contexts, including interactions with organic
3896  amendments that may modify SOC accumulation relative to rock amendments alone.101,113,439,528
3897  Consequently, environmental conditions that favour rapid mineral weathering do not necessarily
3898  maximize net climate benefits once SOC dynamics are considered, and in some cases may partially
3899  offset inorganic CDR on relevant timescales.94
3900
3901  Taken together, these interactions suggest that EW deployment is better understood as a continuum
3902  of more- and less-favourable conditions rather than a single optimal setting. Most existing global
3903  assessments have focused on estimating aggregate CDR potential, often emphasizing climatic
3904  controls on weathering rates, rather than identifying optimal deployment environments under
3905  interacting trade-offs. As a result, key factors such as agronomic benefits, environmental risks,
3906  GHG feedbacks, and losses and lag times in CDR are often underrepresented, despite their
3907  importance for determining realizable outcomes. This complexity increases further when multiple
3908  outcomes are considered simultaneously, as improvements in one dimension may constrain others.
3909
3910  Within this framework, deployment environments can be viewed along gradients reflecting trade-
3911  offs between reaction kinetics and system capacity, and thus between immediate and long-term
3912  CDR. Warm, humid tropical systems may offer high long-term CDR capacity due to rapid
3913  kinetics,19,24,245 but can also exhibit substantial initial alkalinity losses, soil carbon responses, and
3914  delayed net CDR as acidity is neutralized.111,216 In contrast, systems with higher BS or lower
3915  acidity may enable more immediate CDR, albeit with slower intrinsic weathering rates. These
3916  patterns highlight that optimal deployment is not defined by climate alone, but by system context
3917  and time horizon, including management practices that influence acid inputs, alkalinity export, and
3918  nutrient cycling. Accordingly, the central challenge is not identifying a single "best" environment
3919  but aligning site conditions and management with desired outcomes across timescales.
3920
3921  This perspective implies that multiple deployment pathways may be viable depending on local
3922  conditions and management objectives. For example, regions with relatively high soil pH and BS
3923  may experience smaller initial alkalinity losses to exchange reactions,68 allowing efficient CDR
3924  even in cooler climates provided that weathering products can be exported from soils. In such
3925

      104
      Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

3926  contexts, rapidly dissolving carbonate feedstocks may sustain high alkalinity fluxes,30,31,516
3927  overcoming climatic constraints on weathering rates due to multiple orders of magnitude faster
3928  weathering kinetics.119 In some cases, sequential strategies may also be advantageous--for
3929  instance, using reactive (e.g., via grain size or mineralogy) silicate amendments initially to
3930  neutralize existing soil acidity and build exchangeable base cation pools, followed by carbonate
3931  amendments that maintain alkalinity export once the soil acidity has been buffered.
3932
3933  Conversely, in strongly weathered tropical soils where initial alkalinity losses may be larger but
3934  agronomic benefits substantial, in addition to optimizing for CDR,19,23,24,245 deployment strategies
3935  may prioritize soil fertility improvement and pH buffering, with CDR emerging as a co-benefit of
3936  agronomic management; particularly over long time horizons once initial soil acidity is buffered.
3937  Because many tropical regions have contributed relatively little to historical GHG emissions yet
3938  face disproportionate climate risks,1,5 prioritizing agronomic co-benefits in these settings may also
3939  align with principles of climate justice (see Section 9) and strengthen progress towards multiple
3940  SDGs84 in addition to insuring benefit-driven stakeholder buy in. In this context, EW may function
3941  not only as a CDR strategy but also as a mechanism for coupling climate mitigation with equitable
3942  and development-oriented land management.
3943
3944  Recognising this diversity of possible pathways shifts the central question from identifying a single
3945  "best" location for EW to determining how deployment strategies should be matched to system
3946  boundary conditions and desired outcomes. Achieving this will require expanded empirical
3947  datasets and modelling frameworks capable of representing interacting soil processes,
3948  management interventions, and multiple optimization objectives simultaneously. As observational
3949  constraints and conceptual frameworks improve, future assessments may move beyond climate-
3950  centric deployment narratives toward more flexible multi-parameter strategies that capture the full
3951  complexity and opportunity of EW in managed landscapes.
3952
3953  5.3 Voluntary Carbon Market activity

3954  A substantial portion of recent EW deployment has occurred through VCM purchasing, creating a
3955  growing record of project activity that can complement the academic literature. Publicly available
3956  order datasets (e.g., CDR.fyi) provide useful basis for tracking market participation and the
3957  evolution of EW crediting over time (Figure 23).
3958
3959  5.3.1 VCM market signals: cumulative activity and delivery status
3960
      Analysis of publicly available CDR.fyi order data indicates a rapid increase in EW crediting
3961  activity over the past several years (Figure 23a). Cumulative EW credits rise strongly through time,
3962  with particularly pronounced growth since late 2024, reflecting increased participation and
3963  purchasing commitments within the VCM, totalling ~0.8 Mt of CDR credits sold in early 2026.
3964  Despite this growth, EW has remained a relatively small component of overall CDR activity to
3965  date, contributing on the order of ~1­2% of cumulative credits sold since 2022 (Figure 23b, status
3966  by announcement not delivery date). This contribution is concentrated among a limited number of
3967  buyers--primarily Microsoft and Frontier Fund (an advanced market commitment funded by a
3968  consortium of corporations in the tech and banking sectors). This indicates that current EW market
3969
3970

      105
      Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

3971  activity is sensitive to purchasing decisions by a small subset of actors, a feature that has been
3972  noted more broadly for durable CDR markets.529
3973
3974  A pronounced distinction between credits sold and credits delivered or retired is also evident.
3975  Credit sales primarily indicate procurement commitments and willingness to pay, whereas delivery
3976  and retirement reflect realized CDR and therefore provide a more direct indicator of climate
3977  relevance. This divergence is illustrated in Figure 23b, which shows the fraction of cumulative
3978  EW credits that have been delivered or retired relative to total credits recorded by a given date
3979  based on CDR.fyi data. As expected, delivery fractions are higher for earlier vintages, reflecting
3980  the additional time available for project execution and verification. Approximately 20­40% of EW
3981  credits contracted prior to mid-2023 are recorded as delivered or retired, whereas delivery fractions
3982  for more recent cumulative sales are currently below 1%. Such discrepancies between sales and
3983  delivery are characteristic of emerging CDR pathways, where project development timelines,
3984  weathering kinetics, and evolving MRV frameworks introduce substantial temporal lags between
3985  procurement and verified outcomes.
3986
3987

3988  Figure 23: Enhanced Weathering (EW) credit deployment and delivery status in the Voluntary Carbon Market (VCM).
3989  (a) Cumulative EW credits recorded in the CDR.fyi orders database, aggregated across all project statuses
3990  (contracted, partial, delivered, and retired). Because project records may transition from "contracted" to "partial",
3991  to "delivered" or "retired" over time, all statuses are treated as representing credits that were sold with the
3992  announcement data reflecting the initial contracting time. (b) Two complementary indicators of EW market
3993  penetration and progress, shown on a logarithmic y-axis: the share of EW credits as a percentage of all CDR credits
3994  sold across all methods, and the fraction of EW credits that have been delivered or retired relative to total EW credits
3995  sold. The x-axis is based on announcement dates; the delivered or retired fraction therefore indicates the share of EW
3996  credits announced by a given date that have subsequently reached delivered or retired status, irrespective of delivery
3997  or retirement date.
3998
      5.3.2 Implications of VCM activity for evidence generation and learning
3999
      Activity supported through the VCM has the potential to contribute meaningfully to evidence
4000  generation for EW. Available information indicates that some VCM-backed deployments are
      occurring at scales that are orders of magnitude larger than published academic field trials in terms
4001  of treated land area and material throughput (e.g., Figure 23a). Nevertheless, direct quantitative

4002

4003
4004

4005

      106
      Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

4006  comparison remains challenging, however, due to, in most cases, incomplete public disclosure,
4007  heterogeneous reporting practices, and differences in study objectives and timelines. Academic
4008  field trials are typically reported years after deployment, whereas VCM contracting often occurs
4009  prior to material application, limiting quantitative comparability between the two bodies of
4010  evidence.
4011
4012  In principle, large-scale VCM-driven deployments could provide empirical constraints on aspects
4013  of EW that are difficult to assess in small or short-duration studies, including operational logistics,
4014  agronomic integration, spatial and temporal variability in performance, and cost drivers. Realizing
4015  this learning potential depends critically on whether deployment and monitoring data are retained
4016  in formats that enable synthesis and secondary analysis.530 Robust evaluation of CDR claims and
4017  associated cost estimates requires broad access to underlying data, particularly while durable CDR
4018  pathways remain at early stages of field implementation. Accordingly, transparency mechanisms
4019  that extend beyond narrowly permissioned or project-specific access are important for independent
4020  evaluation, comparison across approaches, and informed governance.
4021
4022  Data-sharing platforms currently being used in for some EW offtakes may provide a structured
4023  mechanism for research access to EW datasets under defined conditions, including proposal
4024  review, time-limited access, and engagement with individual data providers. Such approaches
4025  could facilitate academic use while addressing legitimate concerns related to proprietary
4026  information and data misuse. At the same time, the need to apply separately for individual datasets
4027  and to coordinate with multiple data licensors can complicate cross-project synthesis, while
4028  restrictions on redistribution and use for site selection will limit the extent to which pooled data
4029  can be used to identify broadly favourable deployment conditions--one of the key motivations for
4030  aggregating empirical evidence. In addition, the notion of non-profit or NGO bodies policing
4031  broader access to data raises clear governance concerns that will likely need to be addressed in
4032  order to engender public trust. Overall, there has been little progress toward meeting repeated calls
4033  for cost transparency, even though such information is essential for assessing the feasibility of EW
4034  at scale and is rarely obtainable from academic field trials alone.
4035
4036  Some registries 431 have begun making portions of the datasets underlying EW crediting claims
4037  publicly accessible, representing an initial step toward empirical accountability. In this respect,
4038  emerging EW crediting frameworks--which typically require quantitative, measurement-based
4039  evidence for credit issuance--stand in marked contrast to SOC crediting, currently the largest
4040  category of agricultural carbon credits. Most SOC crediting approaches rely on measurement­
4041  model hybrids rather than direct observation and operate without standardised mechanisms for
4042  public data disclosure.531­535 However, transparency provisions for early EW crediting remain
4043  limited relative to broader recommendations from the negative-emissions and carbon-market
4044  literature calling for open, interoperable datasets that enable independent validation and cross-
4045  project synthesis.7,505,530,536
4046
4047  Taken together, these considerations suggest that the VCM may function as an important early
4048  deployment and learning environment for EW, but only if accompanied by sufficiently transparent
4049  and interoperable data practices.530 While the VCM alone is unlikely to support EW deployment
4050  at the multi-gigatonne scale implied by long-term mitigation pathways,537 it may nonetheless
4051  contribute to methodological development, MRV refinement, and empirical understanding during

      107
      Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

4052  a period when public funding for large-scale field research and monitoring remains constrained.
4053  In this sense, VCM activity could represent one component of a broader portfolio supporting EW
4054  development, with its primary near-term value lying in learning-by-deployment, contingent on
4055  transparent, accessible, and sufficiently complete data sharing, while longer-term scaling is likely
4056  to depend on complementary policy, procurement, and regulatory frameworks beyond purely
4057  voluntary demand (see also Section 13).

      108
      Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

4058  6 Costs per tonne

4059  Enhanced Weathering has attracted attention as a potentially scalable CDR option because it
4060  leverages mature supply chains in mining, comminution, and agriculture. Costs vary widely across
4061  deployment scenarios, depending on feedstock type and reactivity, whether materials are newly
4062  mined or available as waste streams, grinding requirements, transport distances, application
4063  logistics, MRV intensity, and the credited CO2 removal per unit mass applied. As expected,
4064  published cost estimates differ by more than an order of magnitude, reflecting both genuine
4065  variation in deployment configurations, feedstock types, CDR potential, and geospatial scopes. All
4066  cost estimates reported here are harmonised to 2024 US dollar values using inflation adjustments
4067  and, where required, currency conversions.
4068

4071  removed. Each point denotes a study's central estimate (median across that study's extracted datapoints). Horizontal
4072  whiskers indicate the study-level minimum­maximum across extracted datapoints using reported bounds where
4073  available. For datapoints that report only a cost range (min­max) but no best estimate, the central value is computed

      Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

4097  USD 137­276 per tCO2 and a 10th­90th percentile range of USD 85­310 per tCO2, with study
4098  medians ranging from USD 73­1,015 per tCO2. At a scenario level (e.g., unique feedstock or
4099  geography simulations with multiple possible per study; Figure 25a), harmonised central estimates
4100  span USD 34­1,015 per tCO2, with a right-skewed distribution characterized by a median of USD
4101  193 per tCO2 and an IQR of USD 127­264 per tCO2 (10th­90th percentile approximately USD
4102  74­335 per tCO2). These numbers indicate that while favourable cases exist, the literature supports
4103  a central tendency of roughly a few-hundred USD per tCO2. This spread also reflects that studies
4104  evaluate materially different deployment configurations and often embed different assumptions
4105  about processing intensity, logistics, and credited CO2 uptake per unit mass of material applied.
4106
4107  6.1 Cost breakdown by feedstock and geography

4108  Enhanced Weathering cost estimates vary across commonly documented study context variables.
      Nevertheless, feedstock type (Figure 25b) shows substantial overlap among category envelopes,
4109  indicating that feedstock labels often act as proxies for bundled assumptions about comminution
4110  requirements, transport distances, and credited CO2 removal per ton rather than functioning as
4111  independent cost drivers. Nonetheless, feedstock-level medians are systematically lower for
4112  ultramafic rocks compared to mafic feedstocks. Consistent with this, systematic tendencies are
4113  apparent in case studies: studies using ultramafic materials or alkaline wastes frequently report
4114  lower CDR costs, consistent with estimates that dunite can achieve removal costs near ~60 USD
4115  tCO21 compared with ~200 USD tCO21 for basalt under comparable transport assumptions,23 and
4116  with analyses noting that EW costs depend strongly on grinding, transport, and application
4117  logistics.19 Early conceptual estimates similarly suggested that olivine-based applications could
4118  achieve very low costs where materials substitute for agricultural inputs.16
4119
4120  Consistent with such feedstock-level differences, the relationship between cost and reported CDR
4121  potential (Figure 25c) shows that higher feedstocks with higher CDR potentials (as a result of
4122  higher cation content, and often higher weathering rates; see Section 3) coincide with lower
4123  calculated costs per ton of CO2, reflecting the arithmetic effect of distributing similar operational
4124  expenditures across larger credited removals. However, wide vertical spreads at comparable
4125  potentials indicate that supply-chain assumptions, MRV requirements, and accounting choices
4126  remain major determinants of reported costs. For example, MRV expenditures alone can represent
4127  a substantial fraction of total EW costs, particularly in early deployment phases.542 While regional
4128  cost ranges overlap, all included Global South locations fall toward the lower end of estimates
4129  with median values of <250 USD (Figure 25d), likely reflecting lower labour and associated
4130  operational costs as well as potentially more favourable weathering conditions in tropical
4131  climates.19,23,24
4132
4133  6.2 Cost breakdown by cost component
4134
4135  The cost breakdown by expenditure types clarifies the internal structure of EW economics. The
      CAPEX­OPEX (capital expenditure ­ operating expenditure) decomposition shows that studies
4136  assign very different shares to capital versus recurring expenditures, but variable OPEX clearly
      dominates across most datapoints (Figure 26a). Median contributions are 61.6% for variable
4137
4138
4139
4140

      111
      Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

4141  OPEX, 36.6% for fixed OPEX, and only 6.1% for CAPEX, and variable OPEX is the largest
4142  component in 9 of 13 cases. Substantial within-study spreads (median  55 percentage points)
4143  further indicate that cost structure is highly scenario-dependent even within individual analyses.
4144
4145

4146  Figure 26: Carbon Dioxide Removal (CDR) cost breakdown. (a) Enhanced Weathering (EW) CDR cost breakdown
4147  by CAPEX (capital expenditure), fixed OPEX (operating expenditure), and variable OPEX. (b) EW CDR cost
4148  breakdown by mining, grinding, transportation, and spreading. Enhanced Weathering CDR cost breakdown by
4149  CAPEX, fixed OPEX, and variable OPEX (in 2024 USD).
4150
4151  The stage-resolved breakdown likewise shows that the dominant cost contributor shifts across
      datapoints (Figure 26b), with grinding, mining, and transportation each frequently emerging as the
4152  largest component, with the exception in Taylor et al. (2015)414, where spreading is the biggest
4153  driver, as they assumed expensive air-spreading (Figure 4b). Median shares are broadly
      comparable (20­23% for grinding, mining, and transport), but variability is large (SD  23­27%),
4154  demonstrating that EW economics are governed by physical system configuration (feedstock type,
4155  particle size after grinding, transportation mode, and transportation distance) rather than a single
      universally dominant step. For example, the share of grinding costs varies significantly in Strefler
4156  et al. (2018)23 among different datapoints due to a range of particle sizes (2 µm, 10 µm, 20 µm,
4157

4158
4159

4160
4161

      112
      Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

4162  and 50 µm) assumed, indicating that lower particle sizes after grinding contribute to higher
4163  grinding costs (Figure 4b). This is consistent with multiple studies identifying grinding, transport,
4164  and field operations as principal cost drivers.19,180 Quantitative estimates likewise show that
4165  individual process steps such as grinding can contribute several dollars per tonne of rock
4166  processed,543 and that transport may constitute the largest single cost component in some
4167  scenarios.428,544 Including the co-benefits of EW may reduce deployment costs,106,544 and using
4168  local mining wastes as feedstock is likely to reduce the costs associated with mining and transport,
4169  while the required purification step (froth flotation) introduces a high cost that may equal or
4170  surpass these savings.545
4171
4172  Finally, MRV is increasingly treated as an explicit cost component rather than an afterthought. A
4173  recent report has collected the MRV costs for different CDRs using two datasets: Grantham
4174  Research Institute (GRI) and Frontier.542 According to this report, the average MRV costs for EW
4175  have increased from USD 21 tCO2-1 in 2022 to USD 71 tCO2-1 in 2024 based on the Frontier
4176  dataset, while the average MRV cost was estimated at USD 15 tCO2-1 in 2024 based on GRI's
4177  dataset.542 The large gap in EW MRV cost estimates between the two datasets underscores that
4178  EW MRV is not yet standardised and is highly sensitive to project maturity, scale, and accounting
4179  boundaries. Therefore, MRV costs should be treated as a major uncertainty driver in EW cost
4180  estimates. Nevertheless, some efforts have been made to reduce the uncertainties in MRV cost
4181  estimation. Cascade Climate has released the EW MRV Cost Estimator (EMCE), explicitly
4182  structuring EW project costs into two primary direct buckets, i.e., Rock/Hauling/Spreading (RHS)
4183  and MRV, and compiling more than 1,000 EW-specific measurement cost estimates from
4184  published and sourced quotes to support stacked cost estimation for present-day deployments.546
4185  Together, these sources suggest that MRV can be a noticeable share of total costs in early EW
4186  projects. Over time, MRV costs will depend on how much projects can move from measurement-
4187  heavy monitoring to more model-enabled quantification. Measurement, reporting, and verification
4188  costs will also depend on how strict the crediting rules are and how much uncertainty those rules
4189  allow.
4190
4191  6.3 Price of EW CDR credits on the VCM

4192  Additional information on the economic viability of EW can be drawn from prices observed on
      the VCM. However, it should be noted that prices are not equivalent to costs: costs reflect the
4193  expenditures required to generate and deliver one tonne of credited CDR, whereas prices reflect
4194  what buyers are willing to pay and may be higher or lower than underlying costs depending on
4195  developer margins, market strategy, subsidies, or temporary loss-making sales.
4196
4197  CDR.fyi's durable CDR market tracking reports that the weighted average price per tonne sold
4198  across durable methods fell from USD 490 (2023) to USD 320 (2024),547 alongside rapid growth
4199  in purchased volumes. As shown in Figure 27, prices span a wide range: a small number of
4200  purchases occur at very high levels (up to approximately USD 1,600 tCO21), whereas most
4201  transactions in 2024­2025 cluster in the mid-hundreds (~ USD 250­450 tCO21). Larger purchases
4202  tend to fall within this central band rather than at extreme price levels, suggesting that scaling is
4203  occurring primarily at more typical transaction prices despite occasional high-price deals.
4204
4205

      113
      Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

4206  Although average prices declined from 2023 to 2024, minimum prices have risen over time,
4207  indicating tightening lower bounds as the market matures.
4208
4209

4210  Figure 27: Price of Enhanced Weathering (EW) Carbon Dioxide Removal (CDR) credit sales over time as reported
4211  in the CDR.fyi database. All prices converted to 2024 US dollars.
4212
      A separate CDR.fyi pricing survey highlights a persistent affordability gap for EW. Suppliers
4213  report that roughly USD 349 tCO21 is required for profitability, whereas buyers characterized
4214  USD 271 tCO21 as "expensive" in 2025, with a similar gap projected for 2030.548 This pattern is
4215  consistent with the academic literature: favourable configurations can yield costs in the low
4216  hundreds,23,541 yet, as discussed above, published estimates span a very wide range depending on
4217  assumptions and system boundaries and several analyses indicate that deployment becomes
4218  economically viable only under sufficiently high carbon prices.23,178,549
4219
4220
4221

      114
      Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

4222  7 Side effects, co-benefits, and trade-offs

4223  EW on agricultural land has the potential to generate a multitude of side effects beyond
4224  CDR.38,84,279,384,404,422,523,550­553 These side effects may be positive, representing co-benefits that
4225  strengthen the case for deployment (e.g., improved soil quality or yields), or negative, representing
4226  trade-offs that may constrain scalability, acceptance, or net climate benefit (e.g., emissions of non-
4227  CO2 greenhouse gases (GHGs) or environmental impacts associated with mining and spreading).
4228  Understanding the balance, consistency, and context of these effects is therefore central to
4229  evaluating EW as a viable climate mitigation strategy.
4230
4231  The side effects discussed in the literature span multiple domains, which we group here into four
4232  broad themes: i) social, policy and climate justice, ii) environment and health, ii) agronomic
4233  impacts, and iv) CDR and climate. These themes reflect both biogeochemical processes operating
4234  at field to catchment scales and broader societal considerations that shape the feasibility and
4235  desirability of EW deployment. Together, they capture the diversity of impacts that have been
4236  assessed to date and provide a structured framework for synthesizing heterogeneous evidence.
4237
4238  To robustly assess agronomic co-benefits of silicate rock powder additions, we deliberately include
4239  not only EW-focused studies but also agronomic studies in which silicate rock powders are applied
4240  as soil amendments or fertilizers. This substantially expands the evidence base for agronomic
4241  outcomes and reveals overwhelming support for positive agronomic effects, particularly with
4242  respect to crop yields, soil quality, and resilience. While these studies are not always framed
4243  explicitly in terms of CDR, they provide critical insight into mechanisms, magnitudes, and
4244  variability of agronomic responses that are directly relevant for EW deployment.
4245
4246  Figure 28 synthesizes how frequently different side effects are discussed across the literature and
4247  whether they are reported as positive or negative. Importantly, a given side effect may be reported
4248  as both positive and negative within the same paper. This occurs either because effects are context
4249  dependent (e.g., varying across space, time, or experimental conditions), because different
4250  indicators yield opposing responses, or because a side effect was explicitly assessed but no net
4251  effect was detected, with positive and negative contributions balancing each other. We classify
4252  such cases as neutral (mixed) rather than forcing a binary interpretation. In the figure, horizontal
4253  bars show the balance of positive and negative mentions, while the y-axis labels additionally report
4254  the total number of papers mentioning each side effect and the number classified as neutral (i.e.
4255  discussed as both positive and negative within at least one study).
4256
4257  Across themes, several clear patterns emerge. Firstly, social, policy and climate justice side effects
4258  are discussed less frequently overall than biophysical impacts. Topics such as public perception,
4259  human health, energy demand, and climate justice appear across the literature but are characterized
4260  by fewer empirical assessments and necessarily a greater reliance on conceptual discussion,
4261  qualitative analysis, or broader systems framing. As a result, these dimensions remain less well
4262  constrained than agronomic or geochemical side effects and are addressed in more detail in the
4263  following sub-sections, where we discuss their implications for governance, monitoring, and
4264  responsible deployment.
4265

      115
          Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)
4266

4267  Figure 28: Positive, negative, and neutral or mixed mentions of side effects associated with Enhanced Weathering
4268  (EW) and silicate rock powder applications across the literature. Horizontal bars show the number of studies
4269  reporting each side effect exclusively as positive (right) or exclusively as negative (left), colour-coded by study type.
4270  Studies that discuss the same side effect as both positive and negative are classified as neutral or mixed and are not
4271  included in the positive or negative bars; instead, the number of neutral or mixed mentions is indicated by the size of
4272  the circle in the box on the right. Neutral or mixed classifications therefore reflect context-dependent or contrasting
4273  outcomes within a study, rather than the absence of an effect. Side effects are grouped into four themes: CDR &
4274  climate, agronomic impacts, environment & health, and social, policy & climate justice.
4275
      The environment and health theme shows a more heterogeneous and, in some cases, negative-
4276  leaning profile. However, this pattern is driven primarily by reviews and syntheses, whereas
4277  experimental studies more often report positive effects or, in some cases, no detectable negative
4278  impacts. Heavy metal content in soils, air quality impacts associated with dust generation, water
4279  quality, and metal accumulation in biota are among the most frequently discussed topics in this
4280  category. Notably, the overall negative trend for heavy metals is driven primarily by reviews,
4281  perspective papers, and risk-oriented assessments that highlight potential contamination pathways,
4282  rather than by experimental field or pot studies demonstrating systematic increases in soil or plant
4283  metal concentrations following silicate rock powder application. In contrast, several biological
4284  indicators within this theme--particularly impacts on terrestrial plants and soil biota--are more
4285
4286
4287

      116
      Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

4288  commonly reported as positive, reflecting improvements in plant performance and microbial or
4289  faunal activity under certain conditions. Together, these patterns suggest that while environmental
4290  risks are an important focus of the literature, consistent with overall positive agronomic impacts,
4291  empirical evidence also points to beneficial biological responses, emphasizing the need for
4292  context-specific evaluation rather than uniform assumptions of harm.
4293
4294  In contrast, agronomic impacts are the most frequently discussed side effects overall and show an
4295  overwhelming dominance of positive-only mentions. Agronomic performance, soil quality, and
4296  resilience are consistently reported as improved following silicate rock powder additions.
4297  Importantly, this support is not driven solely by modelling studies, conceptual analyses, or
4298  narrative reviews, but is substantiated by a large body of experimental field and laboratory studies
4299  that directly measure crop responses and soil properties. Negative-only assessments within this
4300  theme are rare, and neutral classifications primarily reflect context-dependent outcomes rather than
4301  systematic trade-offs, indicating broad and empirically grounded support for agronomic co-
4302  benefits.
4303
4304  Finally, within the CDR and climate theme, SIC stocks are the most discussed outcome and are
4305  predominantly reported as positive. This pattern is consistent with the intended climate mitigation
4306  function of EW and reflects both mechanistic understanding and observational evidence linking
4307  silicate dissolution, alkalinity generation, and carbon stabilisation. Soil organic carbon and GHG
4308  effects are more mixed and not as well studied as inorganic carbon stocks. Overall, while some
4309  neutral classifications occur in this category, often related to variability in rates, lag times, or
4310  methodological uncertainty, negative-only assessments are comparatively uncommon, suggesting
4311  general alignment between theoretical expectations and reported outcomes.
4312
4313  Together, these patterns indicate that while agronomic and climate-related co-benefits of EW are
4314  relatively widely supported, particularly by experimental evidence, environmental, health, and
4315  societal impacts remain more uncertain and context dependent. In the following sections, we
4316  therefore examine several of the most consequential side effects in greater depth, focusing on
4317  mechanisms, sources of variability, and implications for monitoring, reporting, and responsible
4318  deployment. These sections generally follow the themes described in Figure 28, though there are
4319  some different areas of emphasis. For example, section 7.1 focuses on the social effects, with some
4320  discussion of human health impacts, but also a significant discussion of economic and policy
4321  impacts. The environment & health section (7.2) addresses impacts on biodiversity and organisms,
4322  water quality, air quality, and soil heavy metal concentrations and toxicity. The agronomic effects
4323  section (7.3) emphasizes findings related to yields, where much research has focused, as well as
4324  exploring the relationship of EW to NUE, soil pH, BS, CEC, and soil physical properties. Finally,
4325  section 7.4 addresses facets of CDR and climate effects not addressed elsewhere, including impacts
4326  on soil inorganic and organic carbon and on non-CO2 GHGs.
4327
4328  7.1 Social, policy and climate justice effects

4329  Though comparatively understudied, the balance of social (see Figure 28), political, and
4330  environmental justice effects--as well as broader impacts on ecosystems, economies, and human
4331  health--is central to assessing the real-world viability of EW. Beyond technical performance and
4332  geochemical potential, EW deployment will be shaped by how benefits and risks are distributed
4333

      117
      Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

4334  across regions, sectors, and communities, and by the extent to which governance frameworks can
4335  ensure equitable and sustainable outcomes. These dimensions are particularly salient given the
4336  strong coupling of EW to land use, extractive industries, and agricultural systems, all of which are
4337  embedded in complex socio-political contexts.
4338
4339  The following subsections synthesize emerging evidence on employment, economic impacts,
4340  resilience, sustainable development linkages, human health, and policy and legal considerations.
4341  Several closely related issues such as public perception, stakeholder engagement, and broader
4342  climate justice considerations are addressed in greater detail in Section 10, with policy frameworks
4343  and implementation pathways explored further in Section 13.
4344
4345  7.1.1 Employment

4346  There has been minimal discussion about the prospective benefits for employment and job creation
4347  in the literature. Where discussed, this is considered for the broad set of geochemical CDR
4348  technologies,554 the potential to create jobs in mining communities22,541,555 and strong connections
4349  between EW and the agricultural and forestry sectors.385 To our knowledge, only Beerling et al.
4350  (2025)22 has attempted to provide an estimate for job creation, suggesting that demand for up to 2
4351  Gt of basalt could require 60,000 to 80,000 more workers to be employed in the U.S.
4352
4353  7.1.2 Economic, social, and climate resilience
4354
      Research has also identified and examined other vectors through which EW can improve resilience
4355  of socio-economic systems, including improved resistance to pests, diseases, and drought,69,385 and
4356  more broadly difficult weather and planting conditions.118 Overall, such resilience is understood
4357  to foster sustainability and food security,69,180,385 including among smallholder farmers and in
4358  tropic regions.173,243 Such opportunities may be challenged by the high economic costs from
4359  mining, grinding, and transportation of sourced rock and potential environmental risks, including
4360  from the release of heavy metals180--though this could possibly be mitigated by greater coupling
4361  with low-cost, efficient quarrying production, where available.100 Seen through the lens of net-zero
4362  transitions, EW (and CDR technologies in general) is also envisioned to reduce potential disruption
4363  to the electricity and industry sectors, particularly for regions and countries more exposed to and
4364  dependent on fossil fuels.416,419
4365
4366  7.1.3 Further economic impacts
4367
4368  Economic impacts principally focused on potential benefits for farmers, notably how EW could
      result in a yield increase523,556,557(cf., Section 7.3.1) or decreasing fertiliser costs125,180,385,444 and
4369  other production inputs.558 For instance, Haque et al. (2024)556 calculate for their field trial with
4370  smallholder farmers in Kenya a potential revenue increase of USD 326 per hectare. There were
4371  also more forward-looking discussions of the carbon credits that could be generated for those
4372  farmers or regions supplying CDR, if and when global trading of emissions permits is allowed 78.
4373  Conversely, Oppon et al. (2023)559 raise concerns about unequal distribution of economic benefits
4374  of EW from developing to developed countries, if the former are increasingly used for siting and
4375  exporting silicate rocks to the latter.
4376
4377
4378
4379

      118
      Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

4380  7.1.4 Sustainable development goals

4381  The development and deployment of EW are linked to the pursuit of a variety of SDGs. Most
4382  frequently, positive synergies with climate action (SDG13) and zero hunger (SDG2) are noted, by
4383  removing carbon dioxide and enhancing crop yields.38,117,387,560­562 Potential increases in food
4384  production and lower need for traditional fertilizers are further connected to responsible
4385  consumption and production (SDG12), life below water (SDG14), life on land (SDG15) as well
4386  as no poverty (SDG1) and decent work and economic growth (SDG8).84,387,560,563,564 Using a
4387  systematic mapping of the relationship between EW and the SDGs, Honegger et al. (2021)560
4388  however emphasize that its deployment could have adverse consequences from the need for more
4389  mining--i.e. on good health and well-being (SDG3), affordable and clean energy (SDG7), and life
4390  on land (SDG15)--and impacts on ecosystems and the environment--i.e. clean water and
4391  sanitation (SDG6) and life below water (SDG14) (see also ref. 565). Thus, the relationship between
4392  EW and the SDGs will ultimately depend on the extent to which it is done in a sustainable
4393  fashion.563,566
4394
4395  7.1.5 Impacts on human health
4396
      Impacts on human health principally relate to harm from heavy metal toxicity on agricultural lands
4397  or leaching into water sources129,179,251,385,567,568 and inhalation risks of dust and particles along the
4398  supply chain (cf., Sections 3.5 and 7.2).22,131,209,248,426,568 Besides risks pertaining from heavy metal
4399  exposure (see also 129,131), the majority of the adverse impacts to human health were tied to
4400  transportation and comminution processes.209,250,426,427 As a result, Feng and Hicks (2023)250
4401  examine how varying rock types, application rates, transportation distance, and geographic
4402  application area could help to mitigate the environmental and health impacts. Regarding inhalation
4403  risks, the need for precautionary approaches and the availability of safety measures to mitigate
4404  such risks are also highlighted.22,131,568
4405
4406  7.1.6 Policy acceptance
4407
4408  Limited attention to public perceptions and acceptability of EW is reflected and exacerbated when
      it comes to perceptions of and support for relevant policies. On a general level, the need for policies
4409  to be perceived by the public as just and equitable is noted.554 One global, large-scale survey
4410  exercise in thirty countries has revealed that support is generally highest for two types of CDR
4411  policies: national-level support and funding for public and private R&D and public information
4412  and engagement campaigns.569 In contrast, there was the least support for independent national
4413  restrictions on use of CDR as well as for a global-level market for trading carbon credits and
4414  offsets. Respondents in the Global South were also much more supportive of a range of CDR-
4415  related policies--separate analysis of the dataset also found broadly higher support among
4416  indigenous and minority-group respondents.570 Specific to EW, a companion set of focus-group
4417  exercises in 22 countries identified a preference for top-down, institution-led decision-making
4418  approach.571,572 The greater understanding that EW decisions are better left to experts or regulatory
4419  authorities distinguished it from ecosystem-based approaches such as soil carbon sequestration as
4420  well as DACCS and Bioenergy with Carbon Capture and Storage (BECCS)--consultation with
4421  affected publics was perceived to be more crucial for the latter two. Indeed, a specific need for
4422  governments to provide coordination between sectors such as land-use and extractive industries as
4423
4424
4425

      119
      Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

4426  well as across a variety of levels and regions and to conduct assessment on behalf of publics was
4427  a common discussion point in focus groups--though EW received relatively less attention than
4428  some of the other methods.571 At the same time, questions about trust and the involvement of big
4429  business actors were frequent,573,574 with members of the public tending to place much more trust
4430  in experts.572 Taking a company perspective, some studies have discussed the importance of
4431  supportive policy frameworks and regulatory approaches for promoting EW adoption,575 along
4432  with modelling how government policies influence company decisions about how to transport EW
4433  materials.576
4434
4435  7.1.7 Policy response

4436  Discussions of effective or appropriate policy frameworks for EW remain a nascent topic in the
4437  literature. One of the most frequent sub-topics is the lack of long-term policy certainty and the
4438  divergence of frameworks (and protocols) between jurisdictions, stressing how this presents
4439  barriers to scaling-up EW.2,552,575,577,578 Thus, Mercer et al. (2024)577 recommend that policies be
4440  developed which are specific to each CDR method along with policy frameworks that are
4441  internationally aligned yet flexible to regional specificities. An example of a type of regulation
4442  which can be more generally employed is that for heavy metals in rock powder, already in force
4443  in countries like Brazil and Germany.129 Transparent policies and greater financial support for
4444  R&D to reduce costs of MRV have been proposed,577 while one survey exercise also found broad
4445  public support for national-level funding for R&D on CDR in general.569
4446
4447  Meanwhile, the need for region- or state-specific frameworks was supported with reference to
4448  regional and geographic differences in sequestration capacity and other resources.579 Publics in
4449  one broad set of focus groups similarly noted the need for national governments to take the lead--
4450  and coordinate with one another--around decisions on how to employ their own advantages and
4451  develop a portfolio of CDR approaches.571 Additional recommendations, and outstanding subjects
4452  of discussion, include how to integrate EW into GHG inventory methodology (e.g., ref. 509), the
4453  extent that such activities are aligned with international mechanisms like Nationally Determined
4454  Contributions, notably for countries in the Global South,580 and whether stocks of inorganic and
4455  organic carbon should be managed separately for carbon crediting purposes.100
4456
4457  7.1.8 Legal dimensions
4458
      Discussions of legal and governance issues are notable more for the tendency to highlight that such
4459  issues are under-represented or that such regulations are broadly absent.38,129,131,509,581 Specific
4460  issues that are highlighted include the regulation of mine tailings 582 and risks of infringing heavy-
4461  metal regulations;129 how legal barriers might impact the scaling-up of EW deployment;554 and
4462  issues of responsibility, liability, and institutional accountability and how these signal the need for
4463  a dedicated EW legal framework.129,581 The importance of international-level coordination as well
4464  as global frameworks and monitoring standards is also underscored, both for successful
4465  implementation of EW583 and to avoid exacerbating climate justice risks and outsourcing pollution
4466  and damages (e.g. from mining) to the Global South.131,565,580
4467
4468
4469
4470

      120
      Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

4471  7.2 Environment and health effects
4472
4473  Beyond its CDR potential, EW can influence environmental quality and biological systems across
4474  the full deployment chain, from rock extraction and comminution to land application, weathering,
4475  and downstream transport of dissolved and particulate products. These effects can be beneficial,
4476  for example through soil improvement, acid neutralization, or enhanced nutrient supply, but may
4477  also introduce risks related to biodiversity, water and air quality, and trace metal release. The
4478  balance of these outcomes depends strongly on feedstock properties, application rates, local
4479  environmental conditions, and the spatial and temporal scale considered. The following sections
4480  therefore assess current evidence on the environmental and health implications of EW across
4481  terrestrial and aquatic systems.
4482
      7.2.1 Impacts on biodiversity and organisms
4483
4484  Impacts of EW on biodiversity and organisms may start at the source: concerns have been raised
4485  about biodiversity losses resulting from the upscaling of mining activities that will be necessary
4486  for large-scale EW,541,580 potentially contributing to deforestation in tropical regions.385 As shown
4487  in Figure 28, organism-related outcomes differ from the broader environment and health theme,
4488  which is overall more negative-leaning due to risk-focused categories such as heavy metal toxicity,
4489  air quality, water quality, heavy metal accumulation in biota, and land demand. In contrast,
4490  terrestrial plant impacts and soil biota are more frequently reported as positive than negative, while
4491  biodiversity and aquatic organism responses are more evenly split or often classified as mixed.
4492  This highlights that biological responses to EW are strongly context-dependent and should not be
4493  inferred directly from broader environmental risk categories.
4494
4495  7.2.1.1 Vegetation
4496  Out of all biota, the impact of EW on vegetation has been most extensively studied. While the
4497  impacts of EW on yield and food quality are discussed elsewhere in this review, there are effects
4498  on plant health and biodiversity that go beyond these. For example, in a greenhouse experiment,
4499  applying rock dust either alone or in combination with a commercial organic fertiliser significantly
4500  reduced the severity of tomato bacterial wilt compared to the organic fertiliser alone584 Another
4501  study showed that herbaceous species richness increased following rock dust application in a
4502  nutrient-depleted, acidified forest soils.514 Both field observations of soils developed on artificial
4503  silicates95 and pot experiments with freshly collected tephra585 indicate quick succession,
4504  consistent with a fertilising effect of these materials. At the same time, concerns have been raised
4505  about negative impacts on biodiversity in forest edges due to rock dust spread on adjacent
4506  farmlands, especially for species adapted to low-pH soils.385 Finally, a change in the rate and type
4507  of nutrients that is released by rock dusts compared to conventional fertiliser on grasslands can
4508  increase forage quality and thus cattle health, mostly due to an increase in Mg and/or Ca. This can
4509  be the case either when Mg-bearing rocks are applied586 or when the K source is biotite, K-feldspar
4510  and/or nepheline instead of KCl salts.587
4511
4512  7.2.1.2 Microbes
4513  In terrestrial systems, studies have mostly investigated the impact of EW on either the microbial
4514  community or on earthworms. In general, given that soil pH is one of the most important factors
4515
4516

      121
      Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

4517  determining the microbial community, increases in soil pH resulting from rock dust application
4518  are expected to affect microbial abundance and activity.94,131,180 In a long-term liming experiment
4519  with a pH range spanning from 4.0 to 8.3, relative fungal abundance did not correlate with pH,
4520  while fungal diversity was only weakly related with pH. In contrast, relative bacterial abundance
4521  and diversity strongly increased with increasing pH.588 This suggests that rock amendments may
4522  more strongly stimulate bacterial communities, particularly in acidic soils.131 Indeed, microbial
4523  biomass carbon was significantly higher following wollastonite application in two acidic soils,
4524  while no significant difference compared to the control was observed in the alkaline soil.114
4525  Microbial growth may be additionally enhanced by rock dust application due to enhanced nutrient
4526  supply and mobilisation, stronger heavy metal immobilisation, and increased plant growth which
4527  stimulate root exudate formation.589 Following basaltic rock powder amendment in temperate
4528  vineyard soils, basal respiration, a widely-used indicator for microbial activity in soils,590 had
4529  increased by 50% compared to non-amended soil one-month post-application.475 Finally, in a
4530  sandy soil Ca provided by the addition of fine dolerite was found to stimulate microbially induced
4531  calcite precipitation more than adding Ca as calcium chloride, further contributing to inorganic C
4532  sequestration.489
4533
4534  Besides overall abundance and activity, also microbial community composition may shift as a
4535  result of rock powder application. Studies in both a tropical rubber plantation487 and a pot
4536  experiment591 showed little changes in soil microbial diversity, but did observe changes in the
4537  microbial community. While in Wang et al. (2025)487 a richer and more interconnected microbial
4538  network was observed following two years after wollastonite amendment, driving changes in soil
4539  multifunctionality, in the experiments of Reis et al. (2024)591 the specificity of the bacterial
4540  community to the different rock powders applied was found to increase with time over the course
4541  of the 8-month experiment. In a two-year pot experiment using two nutrient-poor agricultural
4542  topsoils, Ramezanian et al. (2015)592 observed a minor shift in the soil microbial community
4543  composition following rock dust application, which they attributed to Ca and K released by the
4544  rock dust. In contrast, Campbell (2009)593 observed few significant changes in soil microbial
4545  communities in a three-year field trial.
4546
4547  Soil enzyme activities have mostly been shown to increase as a result of rock powder application.
4548  Following wollastonite applications in a tropical rubber plantation, statistically significant
4549  increases in root phosphomonoesterase, an enzyme released by plants to acquire P from organic
4550  molecules, were observed after two years.594 In a pot experiment growing tomato plants for 30
4551  days in an acidic yellow-brown soil, the combined application of rock dust and a commercial
4552  organic fertiliser significantly increased the activities of alkaline phosphatase, urease, catalase and
4553  sucrase compared to the control, while the latter two were also enhanced when only rock dust was
4554  applied.584 In a greenhouse pot experiment of 120 days growing rice in slag-based silicate fertiliser,
4555  Das et al. (2019)595 observed significant increases in both hydrolytic and oxidase enzyme activities.
4556
4557  Despite fungi having a wider pH optimum than bacteria, increased pH may still positively affect
4558  both fungal growth and diversity, as has been suggested for arbuscular mycorrhiza.596 Indeed, the
4559  few experimental studies investigating fungi did observed significant changes, although results
4560  differ between studies. In the same greenhouse pot experiment as described above, Das et al.
4561  (2019)595 found significant increases in both fungal species richness and diversity following slag-
4562  based silicate fertiliser, with further analyses revealing that the relative abundance of saprotrophic

      122
      Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

4563  fungi (fungi that decompose organic matter), but not symbiotrophic (e.g. mycorrhiza) and
4564  pathotrophic fungi, had significantly increased. This is in contrast to the results of Bi et al.
4565  (2024)594 who observed significant increases in mycorrhizal colonization in the same field
4566  experiment on a tropical rubber plantation as above.
4567
4568  7.2.1.3 Earthworms
4569  There is a keen interest in earthworm responses to rock dust application, e.g. due to their key role
4570  in ingesting, fragmenting, mixing and transporting inorganic and organic matter in soils, which
4571  may further stimulate weathering.69,597 Studies to date have shown either neutral or positive effects
4572  on survival and activity of the various ecological types of earthworms. A single application of 20
4573  t ha-1 of basaltic rock powder on temperate vineyard soils resulted in an average increase in
4574  earthworm abundance of 71% after one month.475 In contrast, in a mesocosm experiment, no
4575  changes in the survival of endogeic (Aporrectodea caliginosa) or anecic (Lumbricus terrestris)
4576  earthworms were observed after basalt amendment, while earthworm activity was suggested to be
4577  enhanced.91 Enhanced earthworm activity, as indicated by a twentyfold higher number of
4578  earthworm casts compared to non-amended plots, was observed six-month post-application of
4579  basaltic rock dust in a field experiment.598
4580
4581  As with the microbial community, the change in pH is likely a key driver in these effects131 given
4582  that the optimal pH for earthworms is slightly acidic to neutral,94 but changes in other soil
4583  properties may also be relevant. Variables influencing the structure and drainage potential were
4584  found to dominantly control survival and activity of the endogeic species A. calignosa and
4585  Allolobophora chlorotica in artificial organo-mineral systems, with best conditions achieved when
4586  using straw, basanite rock and coarse-grained rock dust.599 In addition, heavy metals released from
4587  rock powders may be immobilised in earthworm bodies,94 as shown in a vermicomposting
4588  experiment.600 Yet, these authors also found that this immobilisation correlated with higher
4589  earthworm growth and reproduction, in line with results from an earlier experiment which showed
4590  neutral to positive effects on earthworms when using silicate rock powders for
4591  vermicomposting.163
4592
4593  7.2.1.4 Aquatic organisms
4594  In freshwater ecosystems, changes in carbonate chemistry resulting from EW may alleviate
4595  acidification and modify ecological responses to nutrient enrichment resulting from e.g.
4596  agricultural activities, atmospheric deposition, wastewater input and acid mine drainage.131,179 In
4597  such systems, higher pH and alkalinity have been suggested to reduce primary productivity in
4598  some contexts and support more diverse food webs, and more nutrient-rich phyto- and
4599  zooplankton, particularly in nutrient-rich ecosystems downstream of tropical agricultural
4600  areas.131,385 However, photosynthesis by aquatic plants and phytoplankton in rivers is generally
4601  suggested to be stimulated after receiving terrestrial EW products, with multiple possible
4602  mechanisms proposed but not confirmed.67
4603
4604  In marine ecosystems, EW-derived alkalinity could partially alleviate some impacts of ocean
4605  acidification on organisms, although the dominant source of ocean acidification is enhanced
4606  atmospheric CO2.38 Shifts in carbonate chemistry resulting from EW, most notably an increase in
4607
4608

      123
      Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

4609  aragonite saturation state (arg), may benefit calcifying organisms, such as corals, by increasing
4610  carbonate saturation state.131,415 The extent to which coral reefs may still be above the critical arg
4611  threshold of 3.5 by 2100 depends on both the scale of EW deployment and the CO2-emission
4612  scenario used in future projections with Earth System Models.279,413 The exact impact on marine
4613  food webs also depends on the type of rock dust that is used. With carbonate feedstocks, growth
4614  of calcifying phytoplankton such as coccolithophores is stimulated at the expense of both diatoms
4615  and cyanobacteria.38 In contrast, when silicate rock dust is used for EW, it can act as a source of
4616  Si and alter the Si:N and Si:P ratios of waters, favouring the growth of diatoms and affecting the
4617  biological pump.17,385 Also cyanobacterial growth may be stimulated as a result of the Fe and/or
4618  Ni released from silicate feedstocks.38 Trace metals released during EW may however have both
4619  positive and negative effects on marine organisms in multiple ways. In a lab experiment using
4620  peridotite and basalt rock powder, Zhang et al. (2024)601 found that the Ni, Fe and Co released
4621  from the rock powder stimulated growth and methanogenesis of a model methanogen, while the
4622  observed levels of Ni are suggested to be harmful to some marine phytoplankton. In contrast, Cong
4623  et al. (2024)180 highlight the negative effects that Ni and Cr have on organisms via interrupting ion
4624  regulation, having toxic effects on the respiratory system, and causing oxidative stress.
4625
4626  Taken together, the evidence reviewed across terrestrial, freshwater, and marine systems is
4627  consistent with Figure 28: terrestrial organism responses are more frequently positive or mixed,
4628  whereas aquatic responses and broader biodiversity outcomes remain more uncertain and strongly
4629  context dependent.
4630
4631  7.2.2 Impacts on water quality and demand

4632  EW can influence freshwater use, water quality, and freshwater ecosystems both during rock
4633  mining, comminution, and transport, and after land application when weathering occurs. Life cycle
4634  assessments have largely focused on the former, while experimental and modelling studies have
4635  emphasized the latter.
4636
4637  7.2.2.1 The role of water in mining, comminution, and transport of rock
4638  Water is used throughout the EW life cycle both directly (e.g., dust suppression and rock-mill
4639  cooling) and indirectly via its involvement with energy production. Due to both direct and indirect
4640  water use, mining and comminution are the most water-intensive activities within the life cycle.
4641  The specific context, such as the energy mix and rock demand resulting from weathering
4642  efficiency, influences which of these two activities dominates water use.426,602 However, the total
4643  water requirement for EW is small compared to existing freshwater withdrawals (<0.25%) and 10­
4644  100x lower than that of other CDR technologies like afforestation, DACCS, and BECCS.426
4645
4646  In LCAs, EW indirectly impacts freshwater quality through its reliance on fossil-fuel energy
4647  sources. The mining and combustion of fossil fuels releases pollutants--including nitrogen,
4648  phosphorus, and heavy metals--that cause eutrophication and ecotoxicity within freshwater
4649  systems. Thus, comminution (an energy-intensive activity) and transport processes that rely on
4650  fossil fuel are responsible for most of the negative effects on freshwater quality.209,426,602 These
4651  impacts scale with operational variables such as grind size and transport distance and diminish as
4652  electrical grids and transport networks transition away from fossil fuels. Overall, these indirect
4653
4654

      124
      Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

4655  impacts on freshwater quality are small. For context, one study found the entire environmental
4656  footprint of mining, grinding, transporting and spreading rock in a coastal EW context was
4657  equivalent to 0.3% of an average European inhabitant's annual environmental load, with freshwater
4658  ecotoxicity and eutrophication contributing only a small fraction of that total.602
4659
4660  7.2.2.2 Effects on water quality and demand during weathering
4661  The weathering process itself requires water, but the amount needed for weathering is substantially
4662  less than that required for crop growth. Thus, when practiced on agricultural fields, EW does not
4663  noticeably affect water demand. Rock application holds the possibility of affecting field hydrology
4664  by altering soil hydraulic conductivity via the formation of clays63 or by altering plant-water use
4665  efficiency via the introduction of plant-available Si.385 But these potential impacts are not well
4666  established, and at this point, are either only theorized or captured in isolated column studies.
4667
4668  Rock weathering can have both positive and negative effects on downstream freshwater quality.
4669  First and foremost, rock weathering adds alkalinity to a system, which eventually reaches
4670  downstream freshwater systems where it can neutralize acids and increase pH. Acid-neutralization
4671  has been detected in leachate of mesocosm experiments179,237 and at the catchment scale at
4672  Hubbard Brook where wollastonite application (~3,800 kg/ha) increased stream pH and acid
4673  neutralizing capacity over at least six years.603
4674
4675  In locations affected by acidification processes (e.g., acid rain), acid neutralization improves water
4676  quality. At Hubbard Brook, for example, the pH increase resulted in a decrease in stream water
4677  aluminium concentrations and reduced toxicity risk to aquatic organisms.72 However, the pH
4678  response to alkalinity additions depends on existing river chemistry, and at higher rock application
4679  rates, modelling studies show it is possible to push river pH above optimal thresholds for
4680  ecosystem health, suggesting that deployment limits might need to be set based on river pH
4681  thresholds.17,65 Further, the ecosystem response to increased alkalinity and pH is unclear and likely
4682  site specific. These co-occurring water chemistry changes could alter food web community
4683  structure, rates of aquatic photosynthesis, and metal bioavailability to both positive and negative
4684  outcomes.15,67,131,385
4685
4686  Weathering also releases Si, which researchers have theorized could help mitigate
4687  eutrophication.15,106,385 Past work has established that increased Si:P and Si:N ratios tend to support
4688  diatom populations over harmful algae blooms. Thus, the reasoning is that the addition of Si via
4689  weathering could positively alter nutrient ratios and reduce toxic algal blooms, particularly in
4690  agricultural landscapes where N and P are in ample supply. However, one of these reviews15 also
4691  notes that changes in Si concentrations and altered nutrient ratios could be problematic for surface
4692  waters, highlighting the uncertainty in the biological ecosystem response to water chemistry
4693  changes.
4694
4695  Trace metal contamination is a persistent concern for EW and is one of the main reasons why the
4696  environment and health theme in Figure 28 shows a negative-leaning profile. Column, mesocosm,
4697  and pot studies indicate that silicate or alkaline mineral amendments can increase aqueous trace
4698  metal concentrations, particularly Ni and, in some cases, Cr, in soil porewaters or
4699  leachates.63,179,184,223 However, reported concentrations vary widely and often remain low or below
4700

      125
      Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

4701  regulatory thresholds, with exceedances or near-threshold values occurring mainly for olivine-rich
4702  or metal-rich waste-derived feedstocks. As such, multiple reviews and policy documents call for
4703  close tracking and further study of metal release from EW into downstream waters.15,69,180,383,387,541
4704  This risk-oriented emphasis contrasts with some experimental studies in which aqueous metal
4705  concentrations remained below levels of concern,131 and with evidence that metal bioavailability
4706  can decrease where EW raises soil pH or promotes secondary mineral formation (cf., Section
4707  7.2.4). Ultimately, the literature indicates that metal risk depends on feedstock choice, especially
4708  the use of olivine-rich or waste-derived materials; particle size, with finer particles potentially
4709  releasing more metals initially; and timescale, because metals may either accumulate through
4710  repeated applications or become immobilised in secondary phases over time.98,222,436,518 These
4711  factors suggest that metal release risks can be reduced through careful feedstock selection, particle-
4712  size optimisation, conservative initial application rates, and long-term monitoring of soil,
4713  porewater, leachate, and biomass metal concentrations.
4714
4715  7.2.3 Impacts on air quality

4716  As highlighted in Section 3.5, airborne particulates represent an important exposure pathway for
4717  EW, particularly during mining, crushing, grinding, transport, storage, spreading, and post-
4718  application erosion of fine rock powder. This is also reflected in Figure 28, where air quality is
4719  one of the more consistently negative-leaning topics within the environment and health theme.
4720  However, direct EW-specific evidence remains limited, and most current understanding is inferred
4721  from industry analogues, including silicate dust erosion, stone quarrying, rock crushing, road
4722  transport of crushed limestone, and agricultural liming.131
4723
4724  Inorganic mineral dust can have severe impacts on human health, depending on particle size,
4725  composition, and exposure level.395 The most severe outcomes are expected when particle size is
4726  fine (<10 µm)131,396 and when exposure is chronic or severe.604 Mineral structure and heavy metal
4727  content may also affect outcomes.387,390,395 Health outcomes can include pneumoconiosis, cancer,
4728  systemic poisoning, hard metal disease, irritation and inflammatory lung injuries, allergic
4729  reactions, and irritation or infection of skin.396,397 Silicate dust in exceedance of air quality
4730  standards has been associated with increased hospital admissions and mortality in Iceland and
4731  Spain.605,606
4732
4733  Crushing and grinding of rock in limestone operations can increase particulate pollution in the
4734  immediate vicinity, but with considerable variability depending on wind direction and operational
4735  layout.607,608 Air pollution rapidly improved with distance from the source,396 falling below
4736  detection against background levels within 100s of meters.607 There is very limited evidence that
4737  granitic quarries produce less dust than limestone ones, but comparison of additional sites with
4738  fully analogous operational requirements is needed.607 Transportation dust is more difficult to
4739  measure609 and is believed to be most significant on the quarry site itself, where it re-entrains dust
4740  into the air.610 Meta-analysis suggests that on-site hauling alone can raise dust concentrations well
4741  past hazardous US air quality thresholds.396 Transport from mine to application site will also
