4742  increase airborne particulate concentrations, especially within a 30m distance from the haul road
4743  or where dirt roads must be used.393,394
4744
4745

      126
      Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

4746  EW may pose inhalation hazards during in-field application, particularly where fine, silica-
4747  containing powders are spread under dry or windy conditions.15,131,383 These risks can be reduced
4748  through established dust-control and occupational-safety measures, including avoiding
4749  excessively fine particle-size distributions where compatible with CDR performance, moisture
4750  conditioning, enclosed handling or dust collection during processing, enclosed tractor cabs,
4751  appropriate personal protective equipment, and avoiding spreading during high-wind or low-
4752  humidity conditions.20,131,387,391,392 These measures are discussed more broadly in Section 3.5.2,
4753  including baseline air-quality assessment, safeguard thresholds, setback distances, monitoring, and
4754  adaptive management.
4755
4756  Enhanced Weathering may also affect air quality indirectly through changes in emissions of
4757  reactive nitrogen species. Increases in soil pH associated with EW have been hypothesized to
4758  reduce soil emissions of nitric oxide and nitrous oxide19 Reduced nitric oxide emissions could in
4759  turn lower the formation of ground-level ozone. However, other analyses suggest that nitrogen
4760  oxide emissions associated with transportation of rock material have the potential to net elevated
4761  ground-level ozone concentrations.385
4762
4763  Mining and crushing of rock can result in hazardous noise pollution where additional rocks are
4764  processed (i.e., where EW does not utilize existing waste streams). Although noise pollution is
4765  classified separately in Figure 28, it is closely linked to the same upstream activities that generate
4766  particulate air-quality risks. Elevated noise levels during mining and blasting activities are well
4767  documented, with demonstrated impacts on both mine workers and surrounding communities.392
4768  Stone grinding and crushing operations in particular have been identified as high-intensity noise
4769  sources, with measured sound levels reported in the range of approximately 100 dBA.611
4770
4771  Noise impacts may be mitigated through operational and engineering controls, including limiting
4772  the use of explosions or restricting blasting to designated working hours.392 Structural
4773  interventions, such as the construction of high-inertia foundations, may reduce vibration and
4774  associated noise transmission.611,612 Additional mitigation options include insulating or physically
4775  isolating worker areas to reduce occupational exposure.611 Overall, air-quality and noise impacts
4776  are likely to be most important near points of material extraction, processing, transport, and
4777  application, and should therefore be evaluated through site-specific exposure assessment and the
4778  mitigation and monitoring safeguards outlined in Section 3.5.2.
4779
4780  7.2.4 Impacts on soil heavy metal concentrations and toxicity

4781  Enhanced Weathering will inevitably introduce trace metals contained in feedstocks to soils. Many
4782  metals are important micronutrients but can be toxic at higher concentrations. Metal release is
4783  therefore one of the key environmental concerns associated with EW, and may lead to adverse
4784  effects on plant growth, aqueous ecosystems, and food safety in some conditions (e.g., refs. 15,131;
4785  Figure 28). To assess risks, the key question is to what extent feedstock derived metals increase
4786  bioavailable metal pools, mobilise to waters, or transfer into biomass at agronomically and CDR-
4787  relevant application rates. Potential impacts strongly depend on feedstock metal content, which
4788  can vary widely. Of key concern are feedstocks strongly enriched in metals compared to typical
4789  soil background levels, including ultramafic feedstocks, rich in Cr and Ni, and some industrial by-
4790  products (e.g., refs. 106,109,129,248,567). If such high-metal feedstocks would be unsuitable for
4791

      127
      Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

4792  widespread agricultural deployment of EW, this would substantially constrain feedstock
4793  availability and may limit its global CDR potential.130,415
4794
4795  Experimental results are highly variable but, overall reassuring at the tested rates, with limited
4796  reports of breaching of environmental regulations. A systematic, semi-quantitative assessment
4797  conducted here (Figure 28) indicates that concerns about heavy metal accumulation are more
4798  frequently discussed in a negative context for both soils and biota. However, this signal is driven
4799  largely by reviews and synthesis studies, whereas primary laboratory and field data do not
4800  consistently corroborate these concerns and instead show more balanced or neutral outcomes. With
4801  a few exceptions, available data from field studies do not show a significant accumulation of metals
4802  in soils,444,475 likely reflecting high background soil concentrations, low application rates, and/or
4803  the use of low-metal feedstocks. Many soil core and mesocosm studies do find significant
4804  accumulation of metals in soils, but this rarely translates into proportional increases in bioavailable
4805  or toxic levels.98,171,222,223,404,464 In fact, several studies report no significant effect or even a
4806  decrease in bioavailability as metals are immobilised due to a pH increase and/or secondary
4807  mineral formation.83,453,613 This effect is particularly well established for exchangeable Al in acidic
4808  soils, where the liming effect of feedstock dissolution raises pH and reduces Al availability and
4809  toxicity.47,388,513,614,615
4810
4811  This pH effect on the bioavailability of most metals can also explain the lack of significant metal
4812  accumulation observed in biomass.108,386,404,444,453,458,616 Other studies do report elevated biomass
4813  metal contents linked to silicate rock additions, but these generally do not surpass regulatory
4814  guidelines.171,443,617 To mitigate impacts from high-metal feedstocks, phytoremediation using
4815  hyperaccumulating plants has been proposed.150,179 Increased porewater or effluent metal
4816  concentrations can occur following feedstock applications, particularly for Ni and Cr after olivine
4817  additions, with concentration peaks sometimes occurring shortly after application.179,184,222
4818  Reported Ni concentrations occasionally approach or exceed regulated groundwater limits for
4819  experiments using olivine.98,223
4820
4821  A key limitation of most experimental studies, however, is their relatively short duration and
4822  reliance on single feedstock applications. Projections suggest that repeated annual rock powder
4823  applications can lead to substantial accumulation of metals over time, potentially exceeding
4824  environmental thresholds even for relatively low-metal feedstocks like basalt.129,617 Although
4825  elevated soil metal contents may not pose risks due to immobilisation, discontinuation of liming
4826  or EW practices could lower pH and remobilise previously accumulated metals (e.g., ref. 69).
4827
4828  Additionally, at the scales required for meaningful global CDR, EW is also likely to introduce
4829  additional, more subtle risks. Beyond direct toxicity, metal inputs may alter micronutrient
4830  availability and ecosystem functioning, potentially affecting, for example, soil microbial
4831  communities (cf., Section 7.2.1) or downstream aquatic systems (cf., Section 7.2.2). Multiple
4832  sources therefore emphasize that repeated applications, high application rates, and high feedstock
4833  metal contents, could push metals into more biologically relevant pools and ultimately food
4834  chains,171 urging for monitoring of metals in field deployments (e.g., refs. 131,383) and caution, even
4835  where short-term toxicity is not observed.618
4836

      128
      Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

4837  Overall, until clear data-informed and EW-specific guidelines and regulations are in place, a
4838  pragmatic deployment strategy is to couple site- and feedstock-specific risk assessments with
4839  conservative initial application rates and systematic monitoring of soil, aqueous, and plant tissue
4840  metal concentrations. These measures align with the broader mitigation and safeguard framework
4841  outlined in Section 3.5.2, including feedstock screening, baseline characterization, predefined
4842  environmental thresholds, adaptive management, and contingency planning where thresholds are
4843  exceeded.
4844
4845  7.3 Agronomic effects

4846  Agronomic co-benefits are among the main reasons why EW has attracted interest as a CDR
4847  strategy in managed landscapes. By supplying base cations and other nutrients, increasing soil pH,
4848  and altering soil chemical and physical properties, EW feedstocks can influence crop performance,
4849  nutrient cycling, and overall soil functioning.
4850
4851  The semi-quantitative assessment of agronomic side effects conducted here (Figure 28) indicates
4852  a strongly positive overall impact of EW across multiple domains, including system resilience,
4853  crop productivity (c.f., Section 7.3.1), and soil quality. This signal is particularly pronounced in
4854  experimental studies, where positive outcomes consistently outweigh neutral and negative
4855  responses, suggesting that these co-benefits are not merely conceptual but are supported by
4856  empirical evidence. For soil quality specifically (incorporating parameters discussed in more detail
4857  in Sections 7.3.3­7.3.5), the compiled dataset shows a clear dominance of positive effects. Across
4858  EW studies, 48 out of 81 report exclusively positive outcomes, compared to only 10 exclusively
4859  negative and 23 neutral cases. This pattern is also evident within the experimental literature: in
4860  field studies, 6 out of 8 report exclusively positive effects with no exclusively negative outcomes;
4861  in laboratory studies, 9 out of 20 are exclusively positive compared to 3 exclusively negative; and
4862  in model studies, 13 out of 20 are exclusively positive compared to 3 exclusively negative. A
4863  similar, and in some cases stronger, signal is observed in the conventional fertilisation literature,
4864  where 19 out of 20 field studies and 14 out of 20 laboratory studies report exclusively positive
4865  effects, with very few exclusively negative outcomes. For agronomic impacts more directly
4866  (Figure 28), the positive signal is even stronger, as discussed below.
4867
4868  Taken together, these results indicate that improvements in soil chemical properties--such as pH
4869  buffering, replenishment of weathering-derived nutrients, and enhanced cation availability--are
4870  consistently observed across study types, while adverse effects remain comparatively rare and
4871  context-dependent. Overall, there is strong empirical evidence that EW can improve soil quality
4872  and deliver tangible agronomic co-benefits alongside CDR. At the same time, the magnitude and
4873  direction of these responses remain strongly dependent on soil conditions, climate, and crop type,
4874  as well as on feedstock properties and application strategies. The following sections therefore
4875  assess the current evidence for the agronomic effects of EW in more detail, including impacts on
4876  crop yields, NUE, soil pH, and broader soil chemical and physical properties.
4877
4878  7.3.1 Impacts on crop yields
4879
      The idea to boost agricultural productivity with ground rocks dates back to the 19th century 40, and
4880  was studied extensively throughout the 20th century with a focus on fertilizing intensively
4881
4882

      129
      Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

4883  weathered or depleted soils, especially in the tropics.619,620 Van Straaten's foundational reviews
4884  comprehensively addresses research on rock fertilisation in agriculture prior to the integration of
4885  EW.620,621 Consistent with this historical body of work, the semi-quantitative assessment
4886  conducted here (Figure 28) shows strong overall support for agronomic benefits associated with
4887  EW, with 129 positive impacts reported compared to 11 negative and 26 neutral outcomes.
4888  Importantly, this pattern is also evident within the experimental literature: across field and
4889  laboratory studies, positive-only responses dominate (e.g., EW: 13/13 field and 11/17; rock flour
4890  as fertiliser: 25/31 field and 32/41 laboratory studies), while exclusively negative findings remain
4891  rare. Together, this indicates that agronomic co-benefits of EW are not merely conceptual but are
4892  consistently supported by empirical evidence across study types and conditions. This is supported
4893  by recent modelling studies that have proposed that EW feedstocks may function as a form of rock
4894  fertilisation, increasing global crop yields by supplying essential macro (P, K, Ca, Mg) and
4895  micronutrients.22,64,106 Pot, greenhouse and field trials have further evidenced these potentials and
4896  the pitfalls of supplying rocks to crops since the latest agronomic review.47
4897
4898  The agricultural effects of EW are influenced by the target crop, background soil composition and
4899  nutrient status, as well as by rock type, particle size, application rate, climate, and potential co-
4900  applications. In the data compile here, treatments targeting total biomass or crop yield most
4901  commonly show modest positive responses, but responses vary substantially across studies and do
4902  not scale simply with application rate or feedstock type (Figure 29). This should not be interpreted
4903  as evidence that dose effects are absent where application rate is varied while other factors are held
4904  constant. Rather, within this dataset, many higher-application-rate studies were conducted on
4905  agricultural soils that may already have undergone pH correction or nutrient management,
4906  potentially limiting additional yield responses. Conversely, where background soil composition or
4907  nutrient status is limiting, rock powders can strongly increase total biomass or crop yields even at
4908  relatively low application amounts. Many feedstock types remain understudied, particularly across
4909  gradients in application rate, soil type, crop type, and background nutrient status.
4910
4911  Beyond the semi-quantitative synthesis in Figure 28 and some of the response-ratio data shown in
4912  Figure 29, individual studies illustrate that rock powders can substantially increase crop yields or
4913  total biomass where they alleviate specific soil constraints. For maize (Zea mays), dacite additions
4914  in mesocosm studies produced 1.4-fold higher yields relative to controls,622 while gneiss powder
4915  improved growth in greenhouse trials.600 Field trials report yield increases ranging from 1.1- to
4916  1.7-fold.388,444,556,623 In wheat (Triticum sp.) greenhouse studies, carbonatite increased shoot and
4917  root biomass 1.4-fold compared to lime,624 while finely milled feldspars and other silicates can
4918  increase potassium uptake and biomass.169,625 However, responses are not universally positive
4919  (Figure 29), producing no yield increase or even slight decreases compared to untreated
4920  controls.613,626 Outside agriculture, positive long-term woody biomass increments have also been
4921  reported in forestry, with yield response ratios of 1.2 to 1.7.70,514,627,628
4922

      130
          Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)
4923

4924  Figure 29: Total biomass and crop yield Enhanced Weathering (EW) responses. (a) Total biomass yield and (b) crop
4925  yield, both across studies and plotted as log10 response ratios as a function of EW feedstock application amount (t ha-
4926  1). Feedstocks (glacial rock flour, mafic (inc. basalt), other, ultramafic (inc. olivine), and wollastonite are
4927  differentiated by shape. Log10 distributions are shown at right for both panels, with feedstock indicated by colour and
4928  line type.
4929
      Overall, the available evidence indicates that EW can provide clear agronomic benefits,
4930  particularly where rock powder additions alleviate existing soil constraints such as low pH or
4931  nutrient limitation. In some settings, EW could partially substitute for liming and mineral
4932  fertilisation while also supporting crop yields and biomass production due to nutrient release as
4933  well as improvements of NUE (see below). However, these benefits depend critically on whether
4934  the minerals present dissolve at agronomically relevant rates. To address this, candidate EW
4935  feedstocks should be routinely characterised by XRD and XRF and assessed for short-term acid-
4936  neutralising capacity and release of agronomically relevant nutrients, particularly Ca, Mg, K, and
4937  P.132,629 Such characterisation would improve prediction of dose-response relationships with soil
4938  nutrient status630 and crop productivity,631 and could ultimately support the integration of EW
4939  amendments into national crop fertilisation advisory services.
4940
4941
4942
4943

      131
      Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

4944  7.3.2 Impacts on nitrogen use efficiency

4945  Nitrogen availability, nitrogen retention, and NUE are all key variables in agriculture, as they have
4946  direct impacts on plant growth and yield, farming costs, and the downstream ecosystem. Increasing
4947  global NUE rates to their maximum potential has been suggested to be an important step in the
4948  path towards achieving global food security.632 Crop yields generally increase with increased N
4949  inputs and optimal N application rates have been increasing over time, even when taking fertiliser
4950  and environmental costs into consideration.633 Nitrogen losses are directly tied to N inputs, and
4951  can be lost in the form of gases, which contribute to GHG emissions or decreased air quality;305 or
4952  lost as leached N into waterways, causing drinking water contamination and eutrophication.634 It
4953  is therefore important to pursue other solutions to directly and indirectly mitigate N losses such as
4954  by increasing N retention and increasing NUE. While it is unlikely that EW would supply N to
4955  soil directly, as silicate rocks contain N in very low concentrations,15,635 changes to soil chemistry
4956  or structure could have indirect effects on N cycling, availability, and NUE.
4957
4958  Only two studies appeared to measure NUE's response to EW specifically and found that NUE
4959  was significantly higher in soils treated with basalt compared to control soils.353,444 Additionally,
4960  Conceicao et al., (2022)623 found that EW treated soils resulted in higher accumulation of N in
4961  shoots of maize. Enhanced Weathering also has been shown to alter root gene expression to
4962  facilitate increased transport of soil N into the plants.444 Another indicator that EW may increase
4963  NUE is its effects on plant growth; in 90% of instances where yield/biomass was measured in
4964  response to EW, EW significantly increased plant growth.
4965
4966  A few studies measured soil N content: one found that soil N increased in response to EW with a
4967  potassium feldspar feedstock,636 whereas another study found that ammonium-nitrate decreased in
4968  EW treated mesocosms in the short term.83 EW has been found to increase soil pH (see also next
4969  section), which may increase adsorption and subsequent N retention in soils, reducing losses via
4970  leaching and causing N to cycle more tightly. Only one study reported leaching losses but found
4971  that EW significantly reduced leaching losses of applied NH4.637 Similarly, EW was found to
4972  increase water use efficiency, which can also contribute to reductions in N leaching.69,617,638
4973
4974  Outside literature provides more context on nitrogen cycling with respect to soil pH. Nitrogen
4975  mineralisation is positively correlated with soil pH,639 and soil pH has been shown to be positively
4976  correlated with rates of nitrification.640,641 Higher soil pH generally results in higher rates of, and
4977  more complete, denitrification; however, the existence of a pH optimum is contentious.642
4978
4979  Overall, N was infrequently measured in the EW literature and warrants further attention in future
4980  studies. Nevertheless, the limited available evidence suggests that improvements in NUE may be
4981  one of the mechanisms through which EW could contribute to yield gains, alongside the more
4982  direct release of nutrients such as Ca, Mg, K, and P from dissolving feedstocks. Liming provides
4983  a useful analogue because, like EW, it increases soil pH and has been found to increase yields,
4984  SOC stocks,42,43 and NUE in some studies.643,644 These patterns suggest that EW-induced changes
4985  in soil pH, nutrient retention, and microbial N cycling could influence crop productivity, but this
4986  pathway remains much less directly tested than nutrient release or yield responses themselves.
4987  Future EW studies should therefore measure N pools, N losses, plant N uptake, and NUE alongside
4988  crop yield to determine whether improved N cycling is a consistent agronomic co-benefit of EW.
4989

      132
      Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

4990  7.3.3 Impacts on soil pH
4991
      The potential for EW to replace liming as a method of soil pH management has been widely
4992  credited as a co-benefit of EW (e.g., refs. 16,106,645). These arguments are supported by decades of
4993  agronomically-focused laboratory, greenhouse, and field trials that have demonstrated increases
4994  in soil pH following rock powder applications (e.g., refs. 625,637,646,647). However, the exact effects
4995  on soil pH are wide-ranging across experimental treatments (Figure 30a), generally ranging from
4996  no significant effect (e.g., refs. 231,514,648) to increases up to several pH units (e.g., refs. 112,114,179).
4997  Soil pH responses are often variable across soil types and show a non-linear response to mineral
4998  application rates, with <50 t ha-1 applications often sufficient to achieve increases in soil pH (e.g.,
4999  refs. 103,114).
5000
5001  This variable soil pH response to EW appears strongly related to initial pH conditions (Figure
5002  30b). In particular, EW and other rock powder applications appear most capable of neutralizing
5003  acid soils, with many studies reporting increases from acidic (pH <4-5) to neutral (pH ~7) soil pH
5004  following application (e.g., refs. 103,114,234,384,647). As pH rises, dissolution rates for most minerals
5005  (cf., Section 3.2.2), including major silicates in basalt, decelerate and thus the soil pH response is
5006  likely to be smaller at higher initial pH values. Alongside direct pH controls on reaction rates, soil
5007  buffering capacity can influence the magnitude of soil pH response to EW. This buffering is driven
5008  by processes including ion exchange, which substitutes cations released during EW (e.g., Ca2+) for
5009  exchangeable acidity, the pH dependency of these exchange sites on soil materials like iron
5010  oxyhydroxides and organic matter, and aluminium buffering in low-pH soils (e.g., refs.
5011  68,368,647,649).
5012
5013  Synergies and challenges reconciling the agronomic and geochemical aspects of EW also occur in
5014  the modelling of soil pH. Many models exist that robustly simulate soil properties such as pH in
5015  agricultural environments, but further development is needed to simulate the geochemical
5016  processes driving mineral dissolution and CO2 sequestration in these models.157,403 Conversely,
5017  RTMs that simulate chemical affinity and mineral dissolution and precipitation kinetics under
5018  semi-realistic to realistic hydrologic conditions typically do not simulate soil pH directly, although
5019  robust soil pH simulations in RTMs are emerging.232
5020
5021  From a global perspective, acid soils are estimated to comprise ~30% of the world's current land
5022  area, in some cases constraining agricultural production from arable land.650 Modelling in the U.S.
5023  "corn belt" suggests that EW could reduce the fraction of acidic soils (pH < 6.5) from the current
5024  70% to between 40% and 5% by 2070.22 Silicate applications also show promise beyond
5025  agricultural settings. For example, the application of silicate minerals to forest soils acidified by
5026  "acid rain" deriving from industrial pollution during the 20th century has shown the capability of
5027  restoring ecological health by increasing base cation saturation and reducing soil
5028  acidity.70,368,369,514,651 The widespread deployment of EW needed to achieve sufficient CDR could
5029  thus have significant benefits for soil pH across ecosystems. Overall, soil pH correction is likely
5030  one of the clearest agronomic pathways through which EW can improve crop performance,
5031  particularly in acidic or poorly buffered soils, but the magnitude and persistence of this benefit
5032  depend on feedstock reactivity, application rate, soil buffering capacity, and initial pH.
5033
5034
5035

      133
          Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)
5036

5037  Figure 30: Change in soil pH due to Enhanced Weathering (EW) interventions as a function of the application amount
5038  (a) and as a function of the initial soil pH (b).
5039
      7.3.4 Impacts on cation sorption in soils
5040
5041  Base saturation and CEC are central soil fertility metrics because they influence nutrient
      availability, acidity buffering, and the retention of agronomically important cations such as Ca2+,
5042  Mg2+, K+, and Na+. In the context of EW, these same soil chemical properties are important not
5043  only as agronomic co-benefits, but also because they influence the fate of weathering-derived
5044  cations. Together, BS and CEC largely determine the extent to which soils can retain, at least
5045  temporarily, the cations released during mineral dissolution68--with associated CDR losses (cf.,
5046  Section 3.4.2). Soils with greater exchange capacity and available exchange sites (low BS) may
5047  retain more Ca, Mg, K, and Na in exchangeable pools, delaying their export in solution, whereas
5048  soils with limited retention capacity and/or high BS may allow more rapid leaching and greater
5049  downstream losses. Because of this, changes in BS and CEC are central for understanding both
5050
5051
5052

      134
      Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

5053  agronomic responses to EW and the timing of realised CDR, including the lag times discussed in
5054  Section 3.4.6.
5055
5056  7.3.4.1 Base cation saturation
5057  Base saturation is a measure of the percentage of a soil's CEC occupied by "base" cations
5058  (typically K+, Na+, Ca2+, Mg2+) as opposed to exchangeable acidity (H+, Al3+).215 Base saturation
5059  is a commonly used metric in evaluating soil health because soil biota utilize exchangeable base
5060  cations as nutrients whereas soluble aluminium is harmful to many plants, and higher BS provides
5061  greater buffering capacity to maintain soil pH against acidity inputs.215 Through the release of
5062  major cations such as Ca2+ and Mg2+, rock powder applications including EW can increase BS of
5063  soil (e.g., refs. 91,164,369,514,646,651). Our complied data shows the magnitude
5064
5065
5066

5067  Figure 31: Change in base saturation (BS), expressed as the log ratio of final versus initial BS percentages, as a
5068  function of a) application amount (tons ha-1) and b) initial BS (%).
5069

      135
      Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

5070  of response in BS to mineral applications is strongly dependent on the initial conditions of the soil,
5071  with the large responses observed in typically acidic soils with low initial BS and minimal
5072  responses in soils with high initial BS (Figure 31). This effect is intuitive because soils with a high
5073  initial BS will have very little exchangeable acidity (H+, Al3+) to substitute for the cations released
5074  by mineral dissolution, and thus BS is not likely to substantially change.
5075
5076  The application rate response (Figure 31a) should be interpreted cautiously. In the compiled
5077  dataset, many studies with application rates >50 t ha-1 were conducted on soils with already high
5078  initial BS (>70%), where large additional increases are not possible (as 100% is the maximum
5079  fraction of exchangeable sites that can be occupied by cations). The apparent pattern in Figure 31a
5080  therefore likely reflects, at least in part, the distribution of initial soil conditions across the
5081  compiled studies. Nevertheless, BS responses are unlikely to scale linearly with application rate
5082  alone, because they also depend on initial BS, CEC, soil buffering capacity, and feedstock
5083  dissolution.
5084
5085  7.3.4.2 Cation exchange capacity
5086  Cation exchange capacity is a measure of the total amount of negatively charged sites in a soil that
5087  can reversibly adsorb and exchange cations (e.g., K+, Na+, Ca2+, Mg2+).215 It is a key control on
5088  soil fertility and geochemical buffering because it governs the retention, mobility, and
5089  bioavailability of nutrient and weathering-derived cations, thereby influencing both plant uptake
5090  and leaching losses.215
5091
5092  Mineral applications have often been observed to increase CEC of soil.164,646,647 Across the
5093  compiled data, the exact change in CEC tends to be highly variable, with occasional decreases
5094  observed, and displays a slight tendency to increase with increasing application rates up to ~100 t
5095  ha-1 (Figure 32a). Relative changes in CEC are typically greatest when the initial CEC of the
5096  treated soil is low (Figure 32b), mirroring the trends observed for BS.
5097
5098  These increases may be driven by increases in soil pH impacting the charge distribution of variable
5099  charged soils, the formation of secondary phases such as clay minerals, or greater retention of
5100  organic matter, as clay minerals and organic matter both provide major sources of exchange sites
5101  in soils.215,369,637 Conversely, the occasional decreases in CEC could reflect the loss of SOM
5102  observed in some soil experimental treatments (cf., Section 7.4.2). Alternatively, if not accounted
5103  for, decreases in bulk soil CEC may also arise from the addition of low-CEC mineral feedstocks,
5104  which can dilute the exchange capacity of native soils prior to, or independent of, subsequent
5105  weathering reactions. In applications where CEC increases, a greater availability of negatively-
5106  charged sites can promote greater retention of weathering-derived cations, which benefits soil
5107  fertility but may increase the time lag between mineral applications and realized carbon
5108  sequestration (cf., Section 3.4.6).
5109
5110
5111

      136
      Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

5112  Figure 32: Change in cation exchange capacity (CEC, cmolc kg-1), expressed as the log ratio of final versus initial
5113  CEC values, as a function of a) application amount (tons ha-1) and b) initial CEC.
5114
      7.3.5 Impacts on soil physical properties
5115
5116  Chemical processes during silicate mineral weathering, including the formation of secondary clay
      or carbonate minerals, together with the physical addition and subsequent dissolution of finely
5117  ground minerals, can alter soil physical properties. These changes are often seen as beneficial, with
5118  several studies investigating mineral addition as a means of soil stabilisation, improved water
5119  retention, or altered soil hydraulic conductivity.168,519,652,653 However, altered soil hydrology from
5120  processes including the introduction of large volumes of mineral or subsequent precipitation of
5121  swelling clay minerals could act as a bottleneck on weathering rates and the movement of EW
5122  products in some cases.64,69
5123
5124  Soil stabilisation from EW and similar rock powder additions can be a promising co-benefit,
5125  particularly in poorly formed or coarse-grained soils where the formation of soil aggregates and
5126  other stabilising structures can promote the retention of organic matter, reduce vulnerability to
5127
5128
5129

      137
      Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

5130  erosion, and enable the rehabilitation of functional soils in impacted environments.96,653,654 EW
5131  and other mineral additions may be beneficial to soil aggregate formation in agricultural or
5132  manufactured soils, driven by interactions between the primary and secondary minerals involved
5133  in EW and SOM.96,653 The formation of carbonate minerals as the cations released from mineral
5134  dissolution react with DIC can also produce cementing effects and promote stabilisation as
5135  precipitated carbonates fill pore space and bind soil particles together.652 In addition to impacts on
5136  aggregate stability, if rock additions are coarser or finer textured than native soil, they may alter
5137  the distribution of pore sizes or total porosity.519
5138
5139  The physical effects of EW can be beneficial or harmful to the retention of water in soils, with
5140  these diverging effects often driven by baseline soil texture (e.g., sandy vs. clay-rich soils) and
5141  potentially impactful on the rates of weathering. Soil water retention is shaped in large part by the
5142  hydraulic conductivity of the soil, with coarser grained soils often possessing higher hydraulic
5143  conductivity and thus shorter fluid residence times compared to fine-grained soils. Due to their
5144  high hydraulic conductivity, sandy and other course-grained soils typically drain water rapidly to
5145  the detriment of plant growth and potentially the time available for water-rock interaction during
5146  EW, and the formation of secondary mineral phases during EW can reduce hydraulic conductivity
5147  and improve water retention.63,168 Conversely, further decreases in the permeability and hydraulic
5148  conductivity of fine-grained soils (e.g., clay or silt-rich soil) during EW can impede drainage, and
5149  the fine-grained rock powder applied during EW could itself throttle the hydraulic conductivity of
5150  a soil.63,64,69,168 These physical changes are relevant to the geochemical processes driving
5151  weathering, as the lower hydraulic conductivity and longer fluid residence times of low
5152  permeability soils can reduce the influx of reactants, decelerate rates as weathering reactions
5153  approach equilibrium, or promote the formation of secondary minerals.62 However, the impact of
5154  EW on soil water retention has been considered by relatively few studies, and evidence for the
5155  alteration of soil water retention is mixed.173 Overall, soil physical responses to EW may represent
5156  either agronomic co-benefits or constraints on weathering efficiency, depending strongly on initial
5157  soil texture, hydrology, feedstock particle size, and the extent of secondary mineral formation.
5158
5159  7.4 Climate-relevant side effects

5160  Beyond its agronomic and environmental co-benefits, EW directly interacts with multiple
5161  components of the terrestrial carbon cycle and broader climate system. Carbon Dioxide Removal
5162  is typically framed in terms of alkalinity generation and bicarbonate export, but a range of
5163  additional processes--including SIC formation, changes in SOC dynamics, and alterations in non-
5164  CO2 GHG fluxes--can modify both the magnitude and permanence of net CDR. These processes
5165  operate across different timescales and compartments and may either reinforce or partially offset
5166  the intended CDR effect. The following sections assess how EW influences key carbon pools and
5167  GHG fluxes, and the implications for accurately quantifying its climate mitigation potential.
5168
5169  7.4.1 Impacts on soil inorganic carbon
5170
      Weathering releases base cations and alkalinity and increases soil porewater pH, thereby raising
5171  the saturation state of secondary minerals, including carbonates. As a result, SIC, primarily present
5172  as calcite (CaCO3) and other carbonate minerals in soil horizons, can increase during EW where
5173  aqueous cations such as Ca2+ and Mg2+ combine with DIC. The precipitation of paedogenic
5174
5175

      138
      Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

5176  carbonate can provide a relatively durable solid phase carbon sink (cf., Section 3.3.3). However,
5177  carbonate precipitation also releases one mole of CO2 per mole of carbonate formed, making it
5178  less efficient than storage as dissolved alkalinity and complicating CDR accounting frameworks
5179  that assume weathering-derived carbon remains in solution as bicarbonate.
5180
5181  The semi-quantitative synthesis of the literature (Figure 28) further supports this conceptual
5182  expectation, indicating that increases in SIC stocks (though sometimes also interpreted to include
5183  export of dissolved C) are reported more frequently than decreases, but are far from ubiquitous.
5184  Across more than 150 studies assessing SIC responses (incl. aqueous processes), positive
5185  outcomes dominate (70%), with a smaller fraction reporting negative (8­10%) or mixed
5186  responses (20­25%). This pattern is broadly consistent across both EW-focused studies and work
5187  on rock amendments applied as fertilisers. Importantly, these findings complement the dominant
5188  role of alkalinity export as the primary and more consistently observed inorganic carbon storage
5189  pathway in EW systems, with paedogenic carbonate formation representing a secondary, more
5190  variable sink that depends on local geochemical and hydrological conditions.
5191
5192  Mirroring this semi-quantitative assessment, experimental data on SIC concentrations range from
5193  clear decreases108,352,444 to substantial increases.178,246,384,453,485,655 Increases are reported more
5194  frequently than decreases, and their magnitude is generally larger, as reflected by overall larger
5195  positive than negative log response ratios (Figure 33). Nevertheless, SIC formation is not a
5196  universal response to EW. Where it does occur, its formation can represent a major carbon flux
5197  and may dominate over dissolved alkalinity export, making accurate quantification of SIC changes
5198  essential for CDR accounting. Soil inorganic carbon formation should primarily be expected where
5199  soil porewaters are driven toward carbonate supersaturation (cf., Section 3.3.3). This depends on
5200  initial soil chemistry, pH, DIC availability, hydrology, and feedstock stoichiometry, particularly
5201  the supply of Ca, rather than on application amount alone. For example, Ca-rich feedstocks such
5202  as wollastonite may be especially conducive to SIC formation where other conditions favour
5203  carbonate supersaturation.103,112,170,178,246,384,451 In such settings, SIC accumulation may provide a
5204  useful MRV target for quantifying solid phase carbon storage (cf., Section 4.1.2). At the same
5205  time, this spatial specificity means that SIC-based MRV is likely applicable only in some
5206  deployment contexts, rather than as a universal approach across EW systems. This is an important
5207  limitation for MRV, but it also implies that carbonate precipitation losses should not be assumed
5208  to occur uniformly across all EW deployments.
5209
5210

      139
      Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

5211  Figure 33: Soil inorganic carbon (SIC) responses on a log10-scale, as a function of application rate (tons ha-1) for
5212  different feedstocks (mafic (incl. basalt), wollastonite, or other, differentiated by shape and colour). The log10 ratio
5213  distributions are shown at right, with feedstock indicated by colour and line type.
5214
      Changes in SIC are likely temporally variable (cf., Section 3.3.3.1; Figure 14), with several studies
5215  suggesting that timescales longer than typical experiments (e.g., >365 days) may be necessary to
5216  observe SIC formation following EW deployment,108,243 although some long-term studies still do
5217  not observe measurable SIC formation (e.g., ref. 444). Changes in SIC also appear related to the
5218  depth of sampling, with SIC formation particularly relevant in deeper soil layers.103 Most studies
5219  show a minimal influence of biota on SIC formation,91,439 potentially reflecting that precipitation
5220  rates are primarily limited by cation release from mineral dissolution rather than the relatively
5221  abundant DIC produced by soil biota.184 However, the presence of plants accelerated SIC
5222  formation in other studies.656
5223
5224  Paedogenic carbonate formation and thus increases in SIC is likely in part reflective of climate and
5225  particularly the water balance (e.g., precipitation inputs minus outputs via evaporation and
5226  transpiration) of the environment. Multi-site comparisons have found SIC accumulation is greater
5227  in more arid environments where concentrating effects of evapotranspiration and slow leaching of
5228  weathering products may increase the potential for carbonate precipitation.95,234 These
5229  observations mirror overall trends in SIC in soils globally, with greater SIC typically observed in
5230  the soils of more arid environments.286 A hydrologic influence on SIC formation is also particularly
5231  important for laboratory or greenhouse experiments emulating EW in field soils, as a failure to
5232  robustly simulate the leaching or accumulation of weathering products may lead to over- or
5233  underestimates of SIC formation compared to natural environments, respectively.
5234
5235
5236
5237
5238

      140
      Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

5239  7.4.2 Impacts on soil organic carbon
5240
5241  Enhanced Weathering may affect SOC dynamics in multiple ways, through coupled
5242  physicochemical and biological processes that operate on different timescales. Given that SOC
5243  stocks and fluxes often exceed the inorganic carbon flux generated through EW, even modest SOC
5244  responses could materially affect net climate outcomes.94
5245
5246  The semi-quantitative synthesis of the available literature supports this picture of variability
5247  (Figure 28), while suggesting a slight predominance of positive SOC responses overall. Across
5248  field and laboratory studies, positive responses are somewhat more frequent (55­65%) than
5249  negative ones (20­30%), with a substantial fraction of studies (10­25%) reporting mixed or
5250  neutral outcomes. In contrast, model-based studies and review syntheses more consistently report
5251  net SOC gains or expectations thereof (70­80% positive outcomes), reflecting an expectation
5252  that longer-term increases in plant productivity and MAOM formation outweigh initial losses. A
5253  similar, albeit more limited, pattern is observed in studies of rock amendments applied as
5254  fertilisers. These divergent outcomes reflect the interplay of competing processes that operate on
5255  different timescales and respond differently to environmental and management conditions.
5256
5257  This qualitative pattern is broadly consistent with the formal meta-analysis of Xu et al. (2025)93,
5258  who analysed 74 experimental studies of crushed rock amendments, including both silicate
5259  feedstocks and carbonate amendments such as lime. Their synthesis reported modest but
5260  significant increases in SOC and in both mineral-associated and particulate organic carbon pools,
5261  with mean effects of 3.8%, 6.1%, and 7.5%, respectively, whereas dissolved organic carbon and
5262  SIC did not show significant overall responses. Xu et al. (2025)93 further found that SOC responses
5263  were associated with changes in exchangeable Ca, microbial biomass, soil structure, and climate,
5264  and that effects varied with application amount, experimental duration, and region. In their global
5265  cropland simulations, positive SOC responses were concentrated mainly in warm and humid low-
5266  latitude regions, while weaker or negative responses were projected for some colder or drier high-
5267  latitude regions. These results indicate that mineral amendments can enhance SOC accumulation
5268  in some settings, but that effects are highly context-dependent and should not be assumed to occur
5269  uniformly across feedstocks, soils, climates, application rates, or timescales.
5270
5271  Conceptually, EW can be expected to (initially) stimulate SOM decomposition through increases
5272  in soil pH, nutrient availability, and microbial activity. This may lead to increases in soil CO2
5273  efflux following silicate application (e.g., refs. 112,114), but this is not always observed (e.g., refs.
5274  91,224). Moreover, the increase in SOM decomposition may only be short-lived. Indeed, several
5275  experiments have shown that EW can alter SOM turnover and stabilisation pathways over time,
5276  potentially leading to reduced decomposition or enhanced stabilisation rather than sustained SOC
5277  losses.92,101,439 Others have found complex interactions between EW and organic amendments,
5278  including increased total soil C with EW, but less SOM when EW was co-applied with organic
5279  amendments.528 The variability in SOM and SOC responses to EW application is apparent across
5280  feedstock types and application rates, with both positive and negative responses commonly
5281  observed (Figure 34).
5282

      141
      Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

5283  Figure 34: Soil organic matter (SOM) and soil organic carbon (SOC) combined response to Enhanced Weathering
5284  (EW). Log10 combined response ratios of SOM and SOC are shown plotted vs. application amount (t ha-1). Feedstocks
5285  (glacial rock flour, mafic (inc. basalt), other, ultramafic (inc. olivine), and wollastonite are differentiated by shape.
5286  Log10 distributions are shown at right for both panels, with feedstock indicated by colour and line type.
5287
      Over longer timescales, EW may enhance SOC stabilisation via MAOM formation and aggregate-
5288  mediated physical protection. Silicate dissolution releases divalent cations (Ca2+, Mg2+) that
5289  promote cation bridging, clay flocculation, and aggregate formation.527 Mineral weathering also
5290  generates secondary Fe- and Al-(hydr)oxides with high sorptive capacity, enabling strong mineral­
5291  organic associations.526,657 Recent EW studies support these mechanisms, reporting increased
5292  macroaggregate-associated SOC,658 incorporation of weathering-derived cations into oxide- and
5293  organic-bound fractions,439 and enhanced SOC storage in Fe-/Al-rich secondary mineral pools.92
5294  These findings suggest that EW has the potential to shift SOC from labile pools toward more
5295  persistent MAOM fractions. On the other hand, Sokol et al. (2024)113 found reductions in MAOM
5296  in the upper soil layer two years after rock amendment, stressing the need to further investigate
5297  EW impact on MAOM formation.
5298
5299  While empirical evidence on EW effects on SOC remains limited, agricultural liming provides a
5300  useful analogue. Like EW, liming effects on SOC reflect competing processes: stimulation of
5301  microbial activity and organic matter mineralisation versus improved soil structure and mineral
5302  protection, and increased plant productivity and carbon inputs.659 In their meta-analysis, Wang et
5303  al. (2021)42 found an average positive effect of liming for moderately acidic, SOC-poor mineral
5304  soils, but not for other soil categories. However, organic soils and organic surface horizons more
5305  often exhibit SOC losses following liming, reflecting enhanced microbial activity and limited
5306  mineral protection.659,660 Hence, the direction and magnitude of SOC responses depend strongly
5307  on initial soil pH, SOC content, ecosystem type, and soil properties. Such dependencies are likely
5308  to also apply to EW, highlighting the need for careful evaluation of EW impacts on SOC across
5309  diverse soil types and environmental conditions.
5310
5311
5312
5313

      142
      Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

5314  Current EW literature on interactions with SOC has several key limitations. Most studies are short-
5315  term and limited to bulk SOC measurements, which are likely to obscure moderate effects due to
5316  natural soil heterogeneity. Evidence for MAOM formation remains scarce and few studies
5317  explicitly link secondary mineral formation to SOC stabilisation. Moreover, priming effects,
5318  microbial carbon use efficiency, and SOC responses across soil depth profiles remain to be
5319  investigated. Finally, interactions between EW-induced changes in soil hydrology and aggregation
5320  have been rarely quantified, despite their relevance for SOC dynamics.224 Addressing these gaps
5321  will require longer-term field experiments, detailed characterization of SOC dynamics across pools
5322  and depths, and explicit coupling of biogeochemical, mineralogical, and microbial measurements
5323  to robustly assess EW impacts on SOC stocks and stability. Generally, SOC responses should be
5324  reported separately from inorganic EW-derived inorganic CDR, because organic and inorganic
5325  carbon pools and related CDR differ in formation pathways, durability, reversal risk, and MRV
5326  requirements.
5327
5328  7.4.3 Impacts on non-CO2 gasses

5329  Gases other than CO2 emitted from agricultural systems are relevant to climate change and public
5330  health. Nitrous oxide (N2O) and methane (CH4) are potent and well-mixed GHGs and are the
5331  dominant GHGs emitted from agriculture. Agriculture, Forestry, and Other Land Use comprised
5332  22% of global GHG emissions in 2019661 and N2O and CH4 emissions comprise 69% and 41% of
5333  global anthropogenic emissions (in CO2-eq), respectively.662 In addition to GHGs, agricultural
5334  practices also comprise 80% of global ammonia (NH3) emissions663 and 10% of global nitric oxide
5335  and nitrogen dioxide (NOx) emissions,661 which can have implications for harmful air pollutants
5336  such as ozone (O3) concentrations and particulate matter (PM2.5).522 In the literature synthesis
5337  conducted here, N2O was the most frequently measured non-CO2 gas, followed by CH4, NH3, and
5338  NOx. The measured impacts of EW on ground level ozone, dinitrogen (N2), and secondary
5339  inorganic nitrate and ammonium aerosols appeared just once in the literature.
5340
5341  In the majority of papers in our database, EW decreased N2O emissions from soils. The primary
5342  mechanism proposed for this reduction is the rise in pH in response to EW and related changes in
5343  N cycling.83,106 A higher soil pH increases the emissions ratio of N2 to N2O by favouring more
5344  complete denitrification, leaving less N2O to be emitted in the process.22 However, the reduction
5345  in N2O has also been attributed to enhanced NUE and microbial activity changes.664 In a handful
5346  of instances, EW had no effect on N2O emissions. One paper that found no effect on N2O emissions
5347  was a 15-year watershed scale application of wollastonite,70 while another found significant
5348  decreases in N2O emissions in the first round of the study but found no statistically significant
5349  decrease during the second growing season.83 The reason for the lack of observed effect in the
5350  second growing season is unclear; however, it may be the result of a microbial priming effect
5351  following the initial application of rock dust that does not persist over longer time scales.665 This
5352  highlights the need for long term studies of N2O emissions in response to EW.
5353
5354  The majority of papers compiled here reported that EW either decreased CH4 emissions or
5355  increased CH4 uptake by soils. This occurrence has been attributed to the stimulation of iron-
5356  reducing bacteria, by the release of iron in silicate rocks, at the expense of methanogens; or an
5357  increase root biomass and subsequent oxygen transport from the plant to root by enlarging
5358  aerenchyma gas channels, which stimulates methanotrophy and consumes CH4.589 In a few
5359

      143
      Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

5360  instances, EW was found to either increase or have no effect on CH4 emissions. Papers attributed
5361  any increases in CH4 emissions to the release of trace metals and a subsequent increase in
5362  methanogens.601 More research is needed to understand the mechanisms underlying the effect of
5363  EW on CH4, and whether trace metals are driving changes in microbial composition.
5364
5365  Enhanced Weathering increased NH3 emissions in two instances and decreased NH3 emissions in
5366  one instance. Increases in emissions of NH3 were determined to be driven by pH increase, which
5367  suppressed N2O and NO emissions but moderately enhanced NH3volatilization.522 On the other
5368  hand, zeolite amendments were found to decrease NH3 emissions from manure, a major source of
5369  NH3 emissions, by providing more negative binding sites for the NH3 adsorption.666 Some papers
5370  concluded that EW could decrease NO emissions, attributed to pH-driven changes in nitrification
5371  and denitrification. Such reductions could provide air-quality benefits by lowering ground-level
5372  O3 formation and secondary inorganic nitrate and ammonium aerosols. However, Edwards et al.,
5373  (2017)385 caution that additional transportation activities from EW could indirectly cause an
5374  increase in NOx emissions and raise ground level O3 concentrations.
5375
5376  Overall, the available evidence suggests that EW can reduce some non-CO2 GHG emissions,
5377  particularly N2O and CH4, but responses are context-dependent and must be evaluated on a CO2-
5378  eq basis before concluding whether total GHG emissions decrease.

      144
      Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

5379  8 Life Cycle Assessment of EW Implementation

5380  Life cycle assessment provides the framework for evaluating the environmental performance of
5381  EW across its full supply chain and defined system boundary, rather than considering CDR in
5382  isolation.667 For EW, this typically includes burdens associated with feedstock production or
5383  sourcing, comminution, transport, and field application, and in some studies these burdens are
5384  translated directly into net CDR or CDR efficiency. This perspective is critical because EW is
5385  materially and logistically intensive: upstream emissions from rock processing and transport can
5386  substantially erode the climate benefit of weathering, and under some modelled conditions can
5387  even eliminate net removals altogether. Robust LCA is therefore central to assessing whether EW
5388  can scale credibly in practice, as performance depends strongly on feedstock choice, siting,
5389  transport logistics, and energy supply. Where evaluated more broadly, LCA also reveals important
5390  non-climate trade-offs, including health, toxicity, land-use, and other environmental impacts
5391  relevant to responsible deployment.209,250,422,425,426,428,433,668­670
5392
5393  The literature compiled here includes variables related to GHG emissions and energy demand
5394  associated with EW implementation. We identified 38 studies (9.8% of the database literature) that
5395  provide quantitative and/or qualitative information relevant to assessing the current state of LCA
5396  in the EW literature.15,18­20,22,23,75,171,178,184,185,209,234,240,250,408,411,420­423,425­430,433,449,539,540,543,559,668­
5397  672 Of these, 24 explicitly focus on the sustainability and/or carbon efficiency of EW. This section
5398  synthesizes reported estimates of GHG emissions and energy use across the full EW life cycle as
5399  well as across individual implementation stages, including mining, comminution, transport, and
5400  spreading. Figure 35 summarises the reported ranges discussed below. In addition, we review
5401  LCA-relevant methodological choices in the literature, the relative contribution of different life
5402  cycle stages, and logistical considerations associated with different feedstocks and deployment
5403  contexts. Importantly, nearly all of the evidence synthesized here is derived from modelled
5404  scenarios or experimental deployments rather than from large-scale, real-world EW deployments,
5405  and should therefore be interpreted as indicative of current understanding rather than definitive
5406  operational performance.
5407
5408  8.1 Greenhouse Gas Emissions and Energy Consumption

5409  Across the peer-reviewed EW literature within the database, GHG emissions from EW
      implementation (not considering CDR rates) range from 1.1 to 215 kg CO2-eq trock-1 (mean = 51.6,
5410  median = 33.15).18,75,185,209,426,428,539,670 Grinding and transport accounted for the largest
5411  contributions, averaging 41% and 39.4% of total EW life cycle GHG emissions, respectively.
5412  Some studies also report energy consumption, ranging from 646.2 to 1400 MJ trock-1 (mean=987.2,
5413  median=826).423,425,426 Longer transportation distances and smaller particle sizes generally lead to
5414  larger GHG emissions and energy usage.185,428 Additionally, studies with estimates on the lower
5415  end of these ranges assumed more sustainable energy sources (e.g., solar, wind, hydropower).75,411
5416
5417
5418

      145
      Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

5420  Weathering (EW) implementation. (a) Distribution of reported GHG emissions from key EW implementation stages
5421  across the literature, expressed as kgCO2-equivalent per tonne of rock applied (central line = median). (b) Mean
5422  contribution of individual life cycle stages to total reported EW emissions across studies. (c) Distribution of reported
5423  energy demand associated with EW implementation stages, expressed as MJ per tonne of rock.

      8.1.1 Mining
5425
5426  Mining represents the first industrial stage in the EW supply chain and therefore constitutes a
      foundational component of life cycle environmental performance. Quantifying emissions and
5427  energy demand associated with rock extraction is essential for evaluating the net climate benefit
      of EW deployment, as these upstream burdens contribute directly to the overall carbon efficiency
5428  of the pathway. Across the literature compiled here, reported GHG emissions associated with EW
5429
5430
5431
5432

      146
      Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

5433  feedstock mining span more than three orders of magnitude, ranging from 0.2 to 530 kg CO2-eq t
5434  rock1 (mean = 98, median = 9.4).18,20,209,408,421,427,429,559,669,670 Corresponding estimates of mining
5435  energy demand range from 4 to 35 MJ t rock1 (mean = 18.1, median = 18.0).15,18,408,422,425,426,429,669
5436  These values reflect substantial variation in assumptions regarding mining practices, energy
5437  supply, and feedstock characteristics across studies. Studies evaluating basalt extraction generally
5438  report lower mining emissions than analyses focused on olivine or generic "crushed silicate"
5439  materials, reflecting differences in extraction intensity and associated industrial processing.559 At
5440  the lower end of the reported emission range, several assessments explicitly assume renewable
5441  energy sources, which substantially reduces the mining-stage footprint.411,429,517 Overall, these
5442  results highlight that although mining typically contributes a smaller share of life cycle emissions
5443  than comminution or transport, its magnitude remains sensitive to feedstock choice, extraction
5444  intensity, and the carbon intensity of the energy system supplying mining operations.
5445
5446  8.1.2 Comminution

5447  Comminution--encompassing crushing and grinding of rock feedstocks--is widely recognised as
      one of the most energy-intensive stages of EW deployment (Figure 35c) and therefore represents
5448  a critical determinant of overall LCA emissions and CDR efficiency (on average 41% of LCA
5449  emissions). Across the literature compiled here, twenty studies report GHG emissions or energy
5450  demand associated with comminution of EW materials. Reported comminution emissions range
5451  from 0.19 to 220 kg CO2-eq t rock1 (mean = 38.8, median = 12.39).18,209,408,427,429,540,543,669,670,672
5452  Corresponding estimates of energy demand span 7.25 to 2000 MJ t rock1 (mean = 420.8, median
5453  = 237.9).18,23,178,184,209,408,422,425,426,428,671 As illustrated in Figure 36, smaller particle sizes are
5454  generally associated with larger GHG emissions and energy expenditure within this subset of
5455  LCA-relevant datapoints, reflecting the additional mechanical energy required to achieve finer
5456  grain sizes. Although reducing particle size increases mineral surface area and can therefore
5457  enhance weathering rates and theoretical CO2 removal potential, the associated increase in
5458  comminution energy demand can offset these benefits if not carefully optimized. This trade-off
5459  highlights the importance of balancing dissolution kinetics and operational energy demand when
5460  designing EW systems. In contrast to mined rock feedstocks, studies evaluating mine tailings and
5461  other industrial waste materials often assume little or no additional grinding prior to
5462  application.126,410 These materials may therefore offer opportunities to reduce the energy and
5463  emissions associated with comminution while simultaneously contributing to waste remediation
5464  and resource recovery.
5465
5466
5467

      147
      Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

5468  Figure 36: Particle size relationships in Enhanced Weathering life cycle assessments (LCAs). (a) Particle size versus
