5470  comminution (MJ t rock1). Particle size data combine values reported either as p80 or as mean particle.
5471
      8.1.3 Transportation
5472
5473  Transport of rock feedstocks from extraction sites to application areas represents another key component
      of the EW supply chain and can contribute substantially to overall life cycle emissions depending on
5474  transport distance and modality. Across the literature compiled here, thirteen studies report GHG emissions
      or energy demand associated with transporting EW materials to deployment sites. Reported transportation
5475  emissions range from 0.175 to 65.4 kg CO2-eq t rock1 (mean = 19.23, median =
5476  9.56).18,75,185,209,411,427,428,517,669,670,672 When normalized by transport distance, emissions range from 0.0157 to
5477  0.177 kg CO2-eq tkm1 (mean = 0.121, median = 0.0567).18,75,185,209,427,428,433,540,669
5478
5479  Across the studies assessed here, transportation contributes on average 39.4% of total EW life cycle GHG
5480  emissions, although individual assessments indicate that transport can dominate system impacts under
5481  certain conditions. For example, transportation may account for up to 90.4% of total EW life cycle
5482  emissions in scenarios with long transport distances or inefficient logistics.428 Transportation distances
5483  modelled in the collected literature range from 10 km to 1650 km, with most studies assuming diesel truck
5484  use. Multiple studies identify rail and barge shipping as potentially more efficient methods for
5485  transportation.18,19,185,408,421,428,544,668,672 Collectively, these findings indicate that spatial planning and
5486  logistics optimization--particularly minimizing transport distances and selecting lower-emission transport
5487  modalities--represent critical factors in reducing the life cycle emissions associated with EW deployment.
5488
5489  8.1.4 Spreading
5490
5491  The final operational stage of EW deployment involves spreading finely ground rock materials
5492  across agricultural soils. Compared to upstream processes such as comminution or transport,
5493  relatively few studies explicitly quantify the emissions or energy demand associated with this

5494

5495
5496
5497

      148
      Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

5498  stage.209,411,428,517 Available assessments consistently indicate that spreading contributes only a
5499  minor fraction of total EW life cycle impacts relative to other stages (Figure 35b).
5500
5501  Most studies assume that EW materials can be applied using existing agricultural infrastructure
5502  similar to fertiliser or agricultural lime application. Typical implementation scenarios therefore
5503  involve standard farm machinery such as tractors equipped with broadcast or conventional lime
5504  spreaders.23,178,426,428,429 Because these technologies are already widely used in agricultural
5505  systems, spreading operations are generally assumed to require limited additional infrastructure
5506  and relatively modest energy inputs. As a result, spreading is typically treated as a minor
5507  contributor to life cycle emissions in EW assessments, although additional empirical data from
5508  field-scale deployment would help further constrain these estimates.
5509
5510  8.2 Additional Considerations for EW LCA

5511  Despite the growing body of EW research, explicit treatment of life cycle emissions remains
      limited. Across all literature compiled for this study, approximately 90% of studies do not quantify
5512  embodied GHG emissions or energy demand associated with EW implementation. This gap
5513  represents a major limitation for assessing the net CDR performance of the pathway, as upstream
5514  energy and material inputs can substantially influence overall climate outcomes. Systematically
5515  incorporating embodied emissions and energy use into EW assessments is therefore essential for
5516  producing robust estimates of net CDR and for improving the transparency and credibility of EW
5517  deployment claims. Building on the LCA-relevant literature reviewed in this section, the following
5518  sections highlight several methodological and conceptual considerations that warrant greater
5519  attention in future EW LCAs.
5520
5521  8.2.1 LCA Methodology
5522
5523  Transparent and standardised methodological reporting is essential for ensuring that LCA are
      comparable, reproducible, and interpretable across studies. Only a small subset of EW studies
5524  provide detailed documentation of their methods in compliance with standardised LCA
      procedures. For example, only five studies explicitly define a clear system boundary for their
5525  quantifications, including cradle-to-grave,209,425 cradle-to-gate,422,428 and cradle-to-storage
5526  approaches.668 A few studies report inventory databases, including ecoinvent,209,250,422,425,426 and
5527  some of these studies also document LCA software used for analysis--OpcenLCA,426,428
5528  Simapro,209,250 GaBi (now Sphera).425 Six studies report their choices of LCA methods, including
5529  TRACI,250,429 ReCiPe,425,426,430 and IPCC.428
5530
5531  The limited transparency in methodological reporting across the EW LCA literature represents a
5532  significant constraint for both scientific interpretation and policy relevance. Without clearly
5533  defined system boundaries, inventory sources, and impact assessment methods, it becomes
5534  difficult to compare results across studies, reproduce analyses, or evaluate the robustness of
5535  reported net CDR estimates. Greater adherence to established LCA standards, such as the ISO
5536  14040 series,673,674 will therefore be critical for improving methodological consistency and
5537  comparability within the field. In addition, future EW LCAs should more systematically
5538
5539
5540
5541

      149
      Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

5542  incorporate end-of-life carbon fate within their system boundaries to ensure alignment with
5543  emerging MRV frameworks for CDR.667 Strengthening methodological transparency in this way
5544  will be essential for enabling credible cross-study synthesis and for informing robust evaluation of
5545  EW deployment at scale.
5546
5547  8.2.2 Other Environmental Impacts and Co-Benefit Assessment

5548  The existing EW literature only sparsely reports impacts across environmental categories beyond
      climate change (GHG emissions). Where such assessments are included, they consider additional
5549  impact categories such as human health impacts associated with industrial processes209,422,425,426
5550  and potential effects from rock dust dispersal during application.19 Other reported impacts include
5551  resource depletion--such as land transformation and water use209,422,426--as well as broader
5552  ecosystem effects including ecotoxicity and acidification.209,422,425,426 Ecotoxicity assessments are
5553  particularly relevant for ultramafic rocks and certain industrial materials that may contain elevated
5554  concentrations of trace metals.15,129,130 More broadly, a recent review of LCA approaches for CDR
5555  highlighted the lack of quantitative evaluation of co-benefits within the CDR LCA literature,
5556  identifying this as a critical gap given the potentially important role of co-benefits in shaping
5557  stakeholder acceptance of CDR technologies.667 A recent study proposes a methodological
5558  framework for integrating the co-benefits of EW, including reduced fertiliser requirements and
5559  N2O emission reductions, into LCA and techno-economic analysis.544 The study revealed
5560  significant, region-dependent impacts of co-benefits on the overall environmental and economic
5561  performance of EW applications. Expanding future EW assessments to include a wider set of
5562  environmental impact categories and systematically evaluating various co-benefits across different
5563  regional contexts will therefore be important for developing a more comprehensive understanding
5564  of the environmental performance of EW deployment.
5565
5566

      150
      Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

5567 9 Synergies and antagonisms with other CDR approaches

5568 and regenerative practices

5569 Enhanced Weathering is rarely deployed in isolation within real-world land systems. Instead, it is

5570 typically implemented within agricultural, forestry, or managed ecosystem contexts where

5571 multiple CDR processes could potentially operate simultaneously. As discussed in Section 3.1.1,

5572 EW occupies a distinctive position among CDR approaches because it combines durable inorganic

5573 carbon storage with land-management co-benefits, including nutrient supply, soil pH buffering,

5574 and improvements in soil fertility. These properties create opportunities for EW to interact with

5575 other CDR pathways that rely on biological carbon cycling, particularly biomass-based and soil-

5576 based organic carbon sequestration strategies. In many cases, these interactions may enable

5577 complementary or synergistic outcomes, allowing multiple forms of carbon storage to occur within

5578 the same landscape.

5579

5580 Building on this comparative profile, EW can be viewed as a durable, low-reversal-risk inorganic

5581 sink that can complement biologically mediated CDR approaches and regenerative practices. In

5582 managed systems, EW may also replenish base cations and other nutrients rather than exporting

5583 them via biomass removal, which is particularly relevant where biomass harvest for BECCS or
5584 other Biomass Carbon Removal and Storage (BiCRS) pathways increases fertilisation needs.675 In

5585 contrast to organic amendments such as compost or manure that can acidify soils via nitrification,

5586 EW can raise soil pH (see also Section 7.3), a potential agronomic co-benefit, particularly in

5587 strongly weathered tropical soils where acidity constraints are common. Because EW can often be

5588 implemented on existing managed lands, its land-use and land-surface impacts are generally

5589 expected to differ from large-scale afforestation or dedicated biomass systems, which are

5590 frequently highlighted for land-use trade-offs and regional hydroclimate effects. Enhanced

5591 Weathering also supplies nutrients such as silicon that are not always effectively returned via
5592 biochar or crop residues.47 Finally, because EW relies on hydrologic export, and soil carbon

5593 saturation is not a concern, this allows for high cumulative rates of carbon storage over time, which

5594 may provide portfolio complementarities with biomass-derived sinks that may experience plateaus
5595 or gradual losses; thereby supporting strategies that stabilise net CDR over longer timescales.676

5596 At the same time, EW's MRV readiness lags behind several established approaches (e.g., PyCCS,

5597  BECCS), and deployment can face system-level constraints including quarry expansion and
5598  comminution impacts,677 logistics and quality assurance needs,19,82,132 and environmental concerns
5599  (also see Sections 3.5 and 7.1).131

5600

5601 The integration of EW with other CDR approaches introduces additional biogeochemical,

5602 ecological, and operational complexities. Interactions between mineral weathering, plant

5603 productivity, microbial activity, and soil chemistry can influence both the magnitude and

5604 permanence of carbon sequestration across different pools. Such interactions may enhance total

5605 carbon removal in some systems but can also introduce trade-offs, including shifts in nutrient

5606 cycling, altered soil carbon dynamics, or challenges for MRV. This section therefore examines

5607 how EW interacts with other CDR strategies and regenerative land-management practices,

5608 focusing on potential synergies, trade-offs, and co-benefits across biomass-based CDR, soil carbon

5609 sequestration, and emerging hybrid approaches that combine biological, mineral, and

5610 technological processes.

5611

                                 151
      Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

5612  9.1 Biomass Carbon Removal and Storage

5613  Biomass Carbon Dioxide Removal and Storage refers to terrestrial-based methods that utilize
5614  photosynthesis for the extraction of CO2 from the atmosphere, storing carbon in trees, crops,
5615  residues, and soils. Most BiCRS methods aim to increase organic carbon stored in vegetation and
5616  soils. To extend the duration for which the carbon remains sequestered biomass can be used in
5617  construction,678 to produce BECCS and long-term sequestration in geological formations675,679 or
5618  it can be converted to pyrogenic carbon (PyC, biochar) via pyrolysis.680 Complimentary to natural
5619  regeneration, BiCRS is a human intervention and can influence global biogeochemical cycles.679
5620  Conventional methods include afforestation, reforestation, agroforestry, conservation agriculture,
5621  and peatland or wetland restoration and contribute more than 99.9% to current CDR (2.2 GtCO2
5622  yr-1). Novel methods such as biochar application and BECCS contribute 1.3 MtCO2 yr-1.2 Biomass
5623  CDR offers a versatile set of options for both temporary and long-term CDR, while different
5624  methods provide distinct sets of co-benefits in the field of ecosystem services, resource use
5625  efficiency, and energy supply.675,679 Combining EW with BiCRS allows "stacking" the CDR
5626  potential per area ­ enabling a higher efficiency of terrestrial CDR methods.
5627
5628  9.1.1 BiCRS synergies
5629
      Current research suggests that the combined application of EW with BiCRS could generate a range
5630  of beneficial effects that surpass the impacts of either technique alone. These potential effects span
5631  improvements in soil fertility, sequestration of both organic and inorganic carbon, microbial
5632  processes, crop performance, and ecosystem resilience, although empirical evidence remains
5633  emergent and sometimes inconsistent.69 Several studies indicate that a combined deployment of
5634  EW with biochar results in greater total CDR than either amendment alone, primarily through
5635  organic carbon stabilisation by biochar and inorganic carbonate precipitation driven by
5636  EW,69,477,681,682 although some studies have only shown additional CDR.443,676 Suggestions of
5637  accelerated inorganic carbon sequestration through enhanced paedogenic carbonate formation and
5638  microbial-driven mineral weathering processes still need to be proven.683­685 Nevertheless,
5639  multidimensional CDR pathways can be realized via inorganic and organic carbon, short- and
5640  long-term realization of CDR, low and high permanence and risk of reversal, and several
5641  ecosystem and hydrological services. This may increase CDR portfolio resilience and value in the
5642  SOM while making soil engineering of degraded land more efficient.76,101,514 Multi-amendment
5643  strategies using the same area may reduce the spatial footprint needed to meet specific CDR
5644  targets, offering advantages in scenarios with land use constraints.681 In drought-affected degraded
5645  or otherwise marginal soils, these combined approaches have demonstrated promising results,
5646  suggesting wide applicability598,686 while providing a chance to mitigate possible CO2 emissions
5647  through a EW-BiCRS negative feedback.477,598,676 Moreover, the higher public acceptance of
5648  BiCRS compared to EW alone could facilitate social and policy support for integrated deployment.
5649
5650  Improvements such as MAOM formation, SOC stabilisation, and biological induced mineral
5651  dissolution have been observed in combined applications of EW and biochar that surpass simple
5652  additive effects108,404,443,617 and thus strengthen the resilience of soils and soil fauna against climate
5653  extremes and pests. Another benefit of biochar is the improvement of soil structure, water
5654  retention, and porosity, allowing prolonged weathering during dry seasons and optimizing water
5655  drainage in fine grained soils.76,676
5656
5657

      152
      Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

5658  Although agroforestry has been recognised for its contributions to global food security687 and its
5659  capacity for sequestering atmospheric CO2 both above and below ground688, the combination with
5660  EW for CDR has not been addressed in long-term laboratory or field studies so far. A 2-year field
5661  study by Medeiros et al. (2024)689 showed the improvement of soil quality, biodiversity, and
5662  microbial activity from spreading powdered gneiss on coffee plants, but the impact on CDR
5663  remains unclear. Studies of EW in matured forest plantations show increased CDR rates by
5664  reduced soil CO2 fluxes and increased tree biomass.686 In general, degraded soils in forests benefit
5665  from elevated soil pH and, supporting sustained tree health and productivity.675,682
5666
5667  9.1.2 BiCRS trade-offs
5668
      Despite the promising potential for synergies, the combination of EW with BiCRS presents a range
5669  of uncertainties and will likely increase complexity, arising mainly due to the high variability and
5670  context dependence of outcomes of each single approach. The effectiveness of biochar heavily
5671  depends on feedstock type, pyrolysis conditions,690 and soil initial conditions;691 agroforestry and
5672  afforestation are subject to climatic and environmental changes; and results from EW are
5673  influenced by rock mineralogy, grain size, and soil texture.69,675,692,693 Further, long-term field trial
5674  data regarding the integration of EW and BiCRS are still missing675 as are explorations into how
5675  EW impacts the risk of reversal due to droughts and wildfires.2 Since weathering is slow, EW
5676  might not counterbalance the removal of nutrients via biomass for BECCS.675 Combining EW with
5677  afforestation, reforestation, BECCS, or wetland restoration will not solve the competition with
5678  food production and other land use.561,580,682 Both, EW and biomass removal for BECCS and
5679  biochar require logistics and may buildup to large volumes for transport expanding costs and
5680  energy expenditure, potentially offsetting some climate mitigation gains.209,694 Also, the
5681  application of both rock powder and biochar holds risks from dust and wind blow out,695 while
5682  spreading rock powder after tree planting might be a logistical challenge.675 In agroforestry, access
5683  to spreading equipment might be limited as agroforestry practitioners are mainly smallholder
5684  farmers.687
5685
5686  Since excessive application of both biochar and EW can cause undesirable shifts in soil pH,
5687  affecting nutrient availability, nutrient solubility, and decomposition of SOM due to increase
5688  microbial activity69,91,112 their combined use introduces further complexities and uncertainties to
5689  soil chemical interactions. An EW-driven increase in soil pH may influence biochar's priming
5690  effects, microbial activity, and nitrogen cycling, potentially leading to reduced mineral dissolution
5691  rates and altered soil chemistry.528,683 The influence of increased alkalinity on organic matter decay
5692  rates can compromise long-term carbon storage ambitions and may accelerate organic matter
5693  decomposition.98,112,343 Potential GHG emissions add another layer of uncertainty.
5694  Counterbalancing CO2 release from organic matter decomposition via EW may fail due to slow
5695  weathering rates and large differences in the CO2 fluxes and differences in the CDR potential per
5696  ton of material.676 Although MAOM formation requires minerals, the application of both
5697  amendments simultaneously may lead to short-term reductions in MAOMC attributed to
5698  microbial mineralisation of labile C inputs528 and some treatments exhibited net negative stocks of
5699  C and N in MAOM over time, suggesting enhanced microbial decomposition.528,696
5700
5701
5702

      153
      Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

5703  When nutrient release from EW leads to enhanced biomass production, additional plant uptake of
5704  cations will decrease alkalinity and lower the inorganic CDR provided by EW. At the same time,
5705  these cations will contribute to CDR via BiCRS but it remains unclear if it can counterbalance the
5706  lost alkalinity from soil pore water.220 Similarly, the release of cations from biochar will interfere
5707  with alkalinity produced by EW and may produce a higher inorganic CDR than from EW alone.
5708  This is problematic for MRV and CDR accounting as the cations from biochar are biogenic (i.e.,
5709  part of the short-term nutrient cycle in the soil) and do not provide an additional carbon sink.676
5710  Monitoring the combined effects and verifying additionality and long-term durability of different
5711  forms of carbon storage at once remains complex, necessitating robust long-term, multidisciplinary
5712  research and extensive field studies that are carefully designed to address either inorganic and
5713  organic CDR on different timescales.104
5714
5715  9.1.3 BiCRS co-benefits

5716  Enhanced Weathering can improve soil nutrient availability and ecosystem functioning, leading to
5717  increased plant productivity, soil health, and overall BiCRS potential. The release of Ca, Mg, K,
5718  P, Si, Fe, and Zn to the rhizosphere by EW improves the nutrient supply and availability. The
5719  improved plant growth and health can lead to greater ecosystem resilience and
5720  multifunctionality.106,487,561,580,675 This can be further realized in forests where increased soil
5721  moisture supports the access of water to interact with minerals69,697 and certain trees like bamboo
5722  may immobilise heavy metals by accumulating them in their roots.698 In an agronomic context,
5723  EW shows positive effects including reduced fertiliser needs, improved soil structure, higher pH,
5724  and elevated microbial activity, which collectively can improve or restore soil health and
5725  productivity resulting in higher BiCRS.47,76,675,682,683
5726
5727  While EW-BiCRS combinations aim for an increase of biomass, the use of biochar plays a special
5728  role in storing both organic and inorganic carbon in the soil. Pyrogenic carbon capture and storage
5729  (PyCCS) is the approach that uses pyrolysis in an oxygen limited environment to convert biomass
5730  into biochar with high concentrations of pyrogenic carbon (PyC).680 Biochar can be used in
5731  multiple ways including in water filtration, build materials, as reducing agent in the metallurgical
5732  industry, in anaerobic digestion, and in animal feeding.685,699,700 The most common practice is in
5733  agriculture where biochar is charged with nutrients via co-composting with manure or urine and
5734  returned to the soil as a soil amendment.701 With this, biochar provides multiple benefits with
5735  agronomic relevance such as yield increase, and improvement of plant physiology, soil biology,
5736  and soil remediation.693 Due to high CEC and sorption sites,702 biochar provides sustained nutrient
5737  retention acting as an agent to buffer nutrient release and soil pH changes from EW but also may
5738  be able to retain heavy metals released during EW.69,76 Further, biochar enhances soil physical
5739  conditions by improving structure, water-holding capacity, and soil drainage.97,675,676 The
5740  combination of rock powder and biochar may accelerate the development of soil structure and
5741  aggregates, including MAOM.99,101,683,691,703,704 Biochar can also supply Ca2+ and Mg2+, which
5742  support carbonate formation, while microbial activity stimulated by biochar can lead to increased
5743  CO2 production and CO2 partial pressure in the rhizosphere which accelerates weathering.683,705
5744  Practical synergies come from the opportunity to include sequential or simultaneous application,
5745  or co-processing approaches such as composting biochar with rock powders or blending rock
5746  powders into nutrient-poor biochars.706,707
5747
5748

      154
      Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

5749  9.2 EW in combination with regenerative and conservation agriculture

5750  The combination of EW with methods aiming to increase SOC emerges as a promising approach
5751  for the integration of CDR into agricultural practices. Rock power can be applied in systems
5752  already managed according to principles of conservation or regenerative agriculture such as cover
5753  cropping, diversified crop rotations, polyculture, reduced/no tilling, mulching and the addition of
5754  organic amendments allowing both organic and inorganic carbon sequestration.99,101,477,681,708
5755  While these sets of techniques deliver multiple agronomic benefits, such as reduced pest pressure
5756  or enhanced water retention ­ they all can support SOC sequestration. The technical CDR potential
5757  of SOC ranges from 3.3 to 6.8 GtCO2 yr-1.709 However, the sustainable CDR potential is expected
5758  to be lower due to economic, social and soil biogeochemical constraints.709,710 Due to its inherent
5759  molecular heterogeneity and site-specific degradation dynamics, the mean residence time of SOC
5760  ranges from years to decades.711­713 Further, SOC based carbon sinks have a higher risk of reversal
5761  through tillage, drainage, land-use change, and global warming.714,715 Still, SOC build-up offers
5762  multiple co-benefits including improved soil fertility, water management and ecosystem
5763  resilience.709,715,716
5764
5765  9.2.1 Regenerative and conservation agriculture synergies
5766
      Organic amendments such as compost, manure, and crop residues stimulate the soil-microbial
5767  activity which can accelerate the weathering of silicate minerals mediated by the exposure to
5768  organic acids and elevated CO2 partial pressure in the soil.94,439 Further, both organic amendments
5769  and rock powder can ameliorate limitations in nutrient supply. Overcoming such limitations can
5770  prompt ecosystem responses such as enhanced plant growth and SOM formation ­ and
5771  proportional carbon sequestration.69,76,234,528,703,717 Potentially toxic trace elements (PTTEs)
5772  released from EW can be immobilised through sorption and complexation to amendments
5773  (including biochar) which can further benefit ecosystem performance.675
5774
5775  Organic amendments can locally decrease the soil pH due to the release of organic acids and CO2
5776  from mineralisation. Both can accelerate EW and buffer a pH shift.47,96,170,477 A high soil pH
5777  favours the precipitation of carbonates from the soil solution which can increase soil carbon
5778  sequestration as paedogenic carbonates.96,170 Carbonates, in turn, can stabilise SOC.47 Alongside
5779  the release of Ca2+ and Mg2+ rock weathering produces reactive secondary clay minerals and iron
5780  oxides that bind particulate organic matter (POM) and microbial necromass. This leads to the
5781  formation of persistent MAOM which is of higher recalcitrance than free POM and can increase
5782  the carbon storage duration.99,101,106,703,712,718 Additional physical protection of SOM is provided
5783  by the formation of soil aggregates involving minerals and organic matter.99
5784
5785  Regenerative agriculture practices and organic amendments can improve soil physical properties,
5786  including water retention and hydrological functioning. Because mineral weathering requires
5787  sufficient water availability, practices that maintain soil moisture, reduce runoff, or increase water
5788  retention may enhance EW efficiency, particularly in drought-prone regions.76,719 Conversely,
5789  irrigation, managed runoff, or desalination-supported water supply may also increase weathering
5790  potential where water availability limits reaction progress. Mineral amendments themselves may
5791  further improve water-holding capacity in coarse-grained soils, creating a potential feedback in
5792  which improved soil moisture conditions support continued weathering.96,170,676 Deeper rooting
5793
5794

      155
      Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

5795  (due to better soil structure, improved plant performance, or rotations with complementary root
5796  morphology) and increased aeration of the soil may improve the access to CO2 for EW as well.76,675
5797  Generally, greater plant productivity can enhance root respiration, delivering more CO2 towards
5798  the weathering process in the soil while increasing carbon storage in roots and above ground
5799  biomass.69,76,477,675
5800
5801  9.2.2 Regenerative and conservation agriculture trade-offs

5802  Several potential trade-offs were recently addressed by the scientific community ­ however,
5803  uncertainties remain due to the lack of long-term field studies and a strong influence of soil type,
5804  climate, and hydrology on both the responses of EW and SOC.76 The largest concern so far is the
5805  occurrence of a priming effect from the stimulation of microbial activity mediated by nutrient
5806  supply and soil pH optimization accelerating soil respiration via the mineralisation of organic
5807  matter and proportional CO2 release.101,477,696 This may temporarily offset SOC gains, especially
5808  when mineral dissolution or nutrient release is rapid.100,111,112 On the other hand, a pH spike from
5809  EW may harm microbial communities47,177 and temporarily reduce soil respiration.686 Still, high
5810  uncertainties remain in the absolute quantification of positive and negative priming effects as
5811  short-term experiments do not capture the longerterm feedbacks.179
5812
5813  When rock powder is incorporated by tillage, the associated physical disturbance of topsoil and
5814  shallow root systems may counteract some mitigation benefits of no-till or reduced-disturbance
5815  practices.477 Monitoring SOC responses and EW-derived inorganic CDR is already challenging
5816  when each is assessed separately, because both involve high spatial variability, cost constraints,
5817  uncertain storage permanence, and complex biogeochemical feedbacks.104,113,475,676,720 Combining
5818  SOC-focused practices with EW may therefore exacerbate MRV challenges and costs, as organic
5819  and inorganic carbon pools differ in mechanisms, permanence, and accounting requirements. A
5820  combined application further increases system complexity potentially reducing MRV reliability.
5821  Large amounts of cations may be bound in metalorganic ligands via organic acids or incorporated
5822  in SOC.343,721,722 These cations do not charge balance DIC and cannot contribute to CDR any
5823  longer. Although these cations still facilitate CDR via BiCRS and SOC, their contribution per mole
5824  of released cation from EW is difficult to account for precisely.220 From an agronomic perspective,
5825  nutrient release from EW may not be fast enough or balanced with plant or microbial demand to
5826  substitute for fertiliser application.675 The drainage of water in an agricultural soil can have a major
5827  impact on weathering rates and export of alkalinity from soils and can be impacted by soil and
5828  rock power grain size.63,97,676
5829
5830  9.2.3 Regenerative and conservation agriculture co-benefits
5831
      Despite the uncertainties, the combination of EW with SOC enhancing agricultural practices shows
5832  evidence of co-benefits for both inorganic and organic CDR pathways. A key benefit is a greater
5833  total carbon sequestration per land-unit without requiring land-use
5834  changes.47,101,106,113,234,477,528,681,703 The stabilisation of SOC by the formation of soil aggregates and
5835  MOAM can improve the soil structure and hydrological parameters - improving the overall soil
5836  fertility and resilience.100,101,113,686,703 Improved soil moisture management due to organic and
5837  inorganic amendments can maintain not only EW but also biological activity in drier
5838  seasons.96,97,170,676,723 Additional supply of geogenic nutrients from EW can lead to higher crop
5839
5840

      156
      Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

5841  yields and a reduced need for mineral fertilisers.47,76,100,104 The rehabilitation of degraded soils by
5842  the combination of EW with SOC build-up not just supports food production under the pressure
5843  of rising food demand and global warming but also contributes largescale ecosystem
5844  restoration.76,118
5845
5846  9.3 EW in combination with other methods and CDR approaches

5847  9.3.1 Mineralenriched biochar

5848  The co-pyrolysis of biomass and minerals was recently suggested as an avenue to enhance CDR
5849  efficiency by increasing pyrolysis carbon yield and PyCCS recalcitrance.724 Likewise, rock powder
5850  can be added to biomass pre-pyrolysis to produce rock-enhanced (RE) biochar. Rock-enhancement
5851  is a novel approach to combine inorganic and organic CDR pathways in a single composite
5852  material.725 Several studies have suggested that the addition of minerals rich in alkali- and alkaline
5853  earth metals (AAEMs) is effective in enhancing the yield and stability of PyC.726­728 This may also
5854  have impacts on the long-term buildup of SOC.729 Rocks suitable for EW typically contain these
5855  AAEMs in high concentrations ­ however they remain largely un-reactive during pyrolysis.725 RE-
5856  biochars may contribute more to CDR by promoting faster and more effective mineral weathering,
5857  which enhances the formation of stable secondary carbonates.724,726 Recent laboratory experiments
5858  have shown higher weathering rates of co-pyrolysed minerals through an increase of their specific
5859  surface area and a closer mineral-water interaction facilitated by biochar's high water holding
5860  capacity.730 The exposure of serpentine-rich minerals to high temperatures resulted in a thermal
5861  activation and the formation of olivine minerals resulting into significantly higher CDR from
5862  increased Mg2+ release.731 The biochar­mineral interfaces established during co-pyrolysis promote
5863  the formation of organomineral complexes that stabilise SOM and PyC.730 Further, phyllosilicates
5864  such as montmorillonite or vermiculite augment this mineral stabilisation potential.732,733 RE-
5865  enhanced biochar may improve the habitat quality for soil-microbial communities which can
5866  generate synergies with EW, leading to enhanced nutrient availability and mineral transformation,
5867  thereby strengthening both inorganic and organic soil carbon sinks.724,726
5868
5869  Nonetheless, several limitations warrant consideration. Mineral additions may not consistently
5870  increase the fixed carbon yield but some mineral additives can alter pyrolysis dynamics
5871  unfavourably or accelerate post-application oxidation.726,728 Copyrolysis outcomes are not
5872  uniformly synergistic and both reduction and increase of the potential carbon sink have been
5873  reported depending on the mineral feedstock.730,731 Moreover, mineral inputs originating from
5874  industrial byproducts can introduce contaminants, including heavy metals.725,734 The presence of
5875  "pyrogenic coating" (secondary char coatings) on mineral particles following co-pyroylsis was
5876  described by Meyer zu Drewer et al. (2025)725. This process may be exploited to design
5877  functionalized RE-biochars but could also reduce mineral-water interaction.725 Despite these
5878  concerns, the deployment of RE-biochars presents a resilient dual-pathway by combining
5879  recalcitrant PyC with forms of inorganic carbon sequestration.725,728 The combination of two
5880  materials in one homogeneous aggregate of high bulk-density and reduced dust evolution bears
5881  practical advantages in agriculture.676
5882
5883
5884

      157
      Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

5885  9.3.2 Biomineralisation and biotechnological approaches
5886
5887  Biomineralisation offers a complementary mechanism to EW. Hereby, biological processes
5888  accelerate mineral dissolution and carbonate precipitation through microbial or enzymatic
5889  catalysis.682 Biotechnological strategies employing carbonic anhydrase enzymes or engineered
5890  microbial strains can further accelerate silicate dissolution and carbonate mineralisation, thereby
5891  enhancing the CDR potential of EW on an economic viable scale.735­740 Microbial processes
5892  applied to mine tailings or industrial residues can simultaneously sequester CO2 and remediate
5893  hazardous waste streams.739,741­743 Microbial biomass produced in these systems may additionally
5894  serve as a feedstock for fuels or biobased chemicals.744 Still, scalability of these systems is limited
5895  by the sensitivity of microbial activity to environmental conditions such as moisture, temperature,
5896  and CO2 availability.745 Biomineralisation systems require energy and nutrient inputs for
5897  cultivation and process control, potentially reducing net carbon benefits.746 Moreover, the use of
5898  untreated industrial or mining waste material poses the risk of releasing toxic elements,747 and
5899  engineered microorganisms raise biosafety concerns. When integrated with EW, biomineralisation
5900  processes may increase weathering kinetics, accelerate carbonate precipitation, and improve
5901  contaminant stabilisation, offering a combined remediation and CDR strategy. Joint deployment
5902  could be especially effective in industrial settings, mine sites, or engineered soil systems, where
5903  reactive mineral surfaces from EW can be paired with biological catalysts to significantly enhance
5904  carbon sequestration efficiency.682 Beyond these direct effects, yield enhancements associated
5905  with EW in agricultural and forest systems may also increase biomass availability per unit land
5906  area, thereby improving the efficiency of biomass-based CDR pathways such as BECCS, biochar
5907  production, or biomass burial. In this way, EW can indirectly amplify the CDR potential of bio-
5908  based approaches by increasing organic feedstock supply without requiring additional land
5909  expansion.
5910
      9.3.3 Phytomining and soil remediation
5911
5912  Phytomining presents a biologically mediated pathway for metal recovery from soils and can build
5913  onto the geochemical changes induced by EW.179,675 Hyperaccumulator species can extract
5914  substantial quantities of heavy metals such as Ni and Cr from soils concentrating Ni up to 3% in
5915  biomass and up to 20% in biomass ash.524,748 EW of ultramafic and mafic rocks can mobilize Ni
5916  and Cr (cf., Sections 7.2.2 and 7.2.4), which creates opportunities for consecutive phytomining
5917  which simultaneously mitigates metal accumulation in soils.150,749 Though generally more rare
5918  than for Ni, several hyperaccumulator species are capable of Cr uptake,750­752 supporting
5919  multimetal extraction and remediation. Enhanced Weathering also increases the availability of
5920  other essential nutrients, thereby stimulating plant biomass production and CO2 uptake, which in
5921  turn increases the realized yield-potential of hyperaccumulators.75 Highbiomass accumulating
5922  species such as willow or bamboo may serve a dual purpose as being both phytomining crops and
5923  BECCS feedstocks, enabling carbonnegative energy production and metal recovery from
5924  combustion ash.753­755 These interactions are mirrored in emerging concepts such as
5925  carbonnegative Ni production ("NegativeNickel").749 If metals accumulate primarily in roots,
5926  extraction requires disruptive harvesting that may disturb SOM and reduce SOC stocks.755
5927  Nevertheless, 99% of mobilised metals may remain unavailable for plant uptake (ref. 184; cf.,
5928  Section 7.2.4) while increased soil pH following EW may further reduce metal bioavailability and
5929  speciesspecific uptake patterns can leave certain metals unaddressed, such as Co or Cs.756 Despite
5930  these constraints, by removing metals mobilised during weathering, phytomining reduces the risk

      158
      Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

5931  of longterm metal accumulation that could otherwise limit EW deployment.150,756 Nutrient release
5932  induced by EW enhances plant productivity, and coupling with BECCS enables metal recovery
5933  alongside negativeemissions energy creating synergies and connections between resource
5934  recovery, CDR, and sustainable land management.
5935
5936  Similar to phytomining, EW can also be integrated as a soil remediation strategy to address heavy
5937  metal contamination through stabilisation of toxic metals by reducing their solubility and
5938  bioavailability through adsorption and precipitation processes423,681 while enhancing the
5939  stabilisation of both organic and inorganic carbon pools.104 However, in certain soil conditions,
5940  EW may mobilise specific metals, necessitating careful selection of mineral amendments or
5941  vegetation to ensure safe immobilisation.423,681 The increased nutrient availability associated with
5942  EW can alter plant community composition, and the longterm ecological consequences remain
5943  insufficiently studied.423,681
5944
5945  9.4 Analytical and systemic comparison

5946  Beyond the physical combination of EW with other CDR methods, the integration into
5947  modelbased mitigation scenarios provides a broader understanding of its role within CDR
5948  portfolios. In climatemitigation modelling, EW is typically deployed alongside other CDR
5949  approaches as an independent method rather than through physical integration with other methods.
5950  Strefler et al. (2021)78 shows that EW appears within multimethod climate scenarios, contributing
5951  through its own sequestration pathway while operating in parallel with other CDR approaches.
5952  The modelling of CDR approach interactions is still an emerging frontier; climate mitigation and
5953  net-zero scenario frameworks increasingly emphasize CDR approach portfolios rather than
5954  singlemethod strategies, treating CDR approaches as a coordinated suite, supporting
5955  portfoliostyle deployment of EW.413,682,757,758
5956
5957  Assessments of CDR implementation also highlight policy mechanisms such as agricultural
5958  subsidy schemes that could co-incentivize EW with other CDR approaches.413,759­761 Separating
5959  CDR from emissionreduction target mixed portfolios in which EW complements technologies
5960  such as CCS can strengthen mitigation outcomes.413,762 For example, EW can address diffuse
5961  agricultural CO2 emissions while geological storage manages concentrated industrial emissions.104
5962  Still, such combinations may require infrastructure including MRV and technical capacity that are
5963  not uniformly available.423 EW, due to its lower technological readiness and associated
5964  uncertainties, may benefit from combinations with other methods, like BECCS or DACCS, to
5965  bolster effectiveness, scalability, and public acceptance.541
5966

      159
      Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

5967  10 Public perception, climate justice, and stakeholder
      engagement
5968
      Research on EW has largely focused on its geochemical mechanisms, CDR potential, prospective
5969  costs and environmental impacts, and life cycle considerations. However, the feasibility and
5970  legitimacy of large-scale deployment will not ultimately depend on technical performance or
5971  economic viability alone. As with other climate mitigation and CDR strategies, EW necessarily
5972  operates within and across salient social, political, legal and ethical contexts that shape whether,
5973  where, and to what extent it can be effectively implemented. Public and stakeholder perceptions,
5974  governance structures, and questions of justice therefore represent critical dimensions for assessing
5975  its deployment, particularly as EW moves from the stages of conceptual assessments and
5976  experimental trials toward real-world application at scales meaningful for potentially helping to
5977  mitigate climate change.
5978
5979  These considerations are especially relevant because many regions identified as highly suitable for
5980  EW--such as humid tropical agricultural systems--are located in parts of the Global South that
5981  have contributed relatively little to historical GHG emissions but may experience disproportionate
5982  impacts of both climate change and climate interventions.1,5 The following sections review
5983  emerging research on public perceptions of EW, the relevance of ethical and climate-justice
5984  considerations, and the growing need for broad stakeholder engagement in shaping responsible
5985  research, governance, and potential implementation pathways. Also note that a range of related
5986  topics are discussed in Section 7.1 as societal "side effects".
5987
5988  10.1 Public Acceptance and Perceptions
5989
      Public acceptance is generally acknowledged as critical for EW development and
5990  deployment,20,541,552,554,763 with the key role of farmers and local stakeholders also recognised.104,575
5991  Public stakeholders generally have a low awareness and understanding of EW.2,21,423,569,573,764­766
5992  In specific, participants have characterized EW as abstract, confusing, and difficult to grasp,764,767
5993  or even inducing feelings of "dread".768 Most attention in the literature and public discourse, for
5994  instance on social media, focuses less on EW versus more familiar, ecosystems-based methods as
5995  well as those such as DACCS.2
5996
5997  Keeping in mind the nascent understanding of EW, there appears to be relatively more support
5998  than opposition.2,569,764­766,769 Moreover, there seems to be stronger support for EW ­ and for CDR
5999  methods in general--among publics in the Global South.569 Further research on public perceptions
6000  in the Global South is much needed, given the relevance of such regions (cf., Section 5.2.3) for
6001  EW deployment versus how little research has engaged with them.555,569,571,770 In terms of factors
6002  that drive support, both trust in relevant actors and institutions, such as industry, scientists, and
6003  national governments, and prior knowledge of EW are found to matter.765,766,771,772 Furthermore,
6004  those with stronger beliefs that science and technology will help solve climate change were more
6005  likely to support EW,569,764 while those more concerned about tampering with nature were much
6006  less likely to do so.764,766,772. Concern about climate change had a more inconsistent, though often
6007  positive effect on EW support,764,766 with some research finding this factor to matter less once
6008  others are included.765,771 Rather, some studies suggest that it is more affective factors such as hope
6009  and/or worry about climate change which strongly (and positively) predict EW support.572,773
6010
6011

      160
      Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

6012  Lastly, while the relevance of socio-demographic factors is inconsistent, Sovacool et al. (2024a,
6013  2024b)570,774 analyses of data from a 30-country survey exercise identified greater EW support
6014  among younger groups (44 and under), male versus female, and among minority and indigenous
6015  groups, specifically those living in urban areas.
6016
6017  In any case, Cox et al. (2020)573 have described such support as "conditional" given unfamiliarity
6018  of EW among participants and the variety of concerns which they raised--noting, for instance,
6019  that support is stronger for research and field trials than large-scale deployment.2,569 Concerning a
6020  field trial for palm oil agriculture in Malaysia, Cox et al. (2025)555 also found that workshop
6021  participants better understood the consequences and purpose of field trials vis-à-vis if EW were to
6022  be scaled-up. Opportunities for deliberation in groups, to ask questions to experts as well as
6023  information and real-time data about projects that were underway all positively influenced
6024  support.555,574,775
6025
6026  Still, the level of support for EW is usually less than for other CDR methods.569,572,573,776­778 The
6027  concerns which publics referenced the most are: opposition to mining; interfering with nature and
6028  oceans in specific; the impact on rural and agricultural landscapes; and distributional (un)fairness
6029  and justice in the Global South.541,569,571,574,581 Another crucial concern was the perceived failure
6030  of EW to address the root cause of climate change.569,573,776 Indeed, Cox et al. (2020)573 directly
6031  cited this "temporal dilemma" as reason for the greater support of research vis-à-vis large-scale
6032  deployment for many people.
6033
6034  Diverging views on the potential acceptability of EW also emerged when considered from an
6035  objective (usually informed by the natural sciences) vs. subjective perspective, where empirical
6036  methods were used to engage members of the public. As examples of the former, one evaluation
6037  suggested EW could achieve broad public acceptance owing to its low cost and infrastructure
6038  requirements and remediation potential,779 and with social impact scoring used as a proxy for
6039  potential acceptance to suggest that the below-average population density near quarries could
6040  result in fewer social conflicts and greater likelihood of acceptance for further extraction
6041  activities.133 In contrast, the social sciences literature has identified substantial concern about
6042  impacts on rural and agricultural landscapes.574 Drawing on 44 focus groups in 22 countries around
6043  the world (one rural, one urban in each), Sovacool et al. (2025)780 found greater concern about the
6044  need for more extractive activities in urban versus rural areas (at least in a European context); the
6045  latter were more likely to perceive EW as relatively natural, less expensive, and not needing to
6046  confront storage-related hazards.
6047
6048  In general, the European focus groups frequently wrestled with siting concerns, land-use conflicts,
6049  and the prospect of NIMBYism (Not In My BackYard-ism) in their discussions of land-intensive
6050  forms of CDR. Analysing these focus groups on a global level, Low et al. (2024)571 identified a
6051  fault line for EW, where the balance of hopes and concerns broadly depended on whether it was
6052  envisioned primarily as an expansion or refinement of mining operations or rather in terms of
6053  additional benefits through agricultural management. Notably, a small though global North-South
6054  cross-cutting group of participants discussed co-benefits of EW for soil enhancement, also
6055  highlighting the need for further assessment of rock types, mining locations, and efficacy as a soil
6056  amendment (see also ref. 555). While there was general discussion on the siting and logistics of
6057  potential mining operations in many focus groups, participants from countries with large mining

      161
      Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

6058  sectors, such as Australia and South Africa, also considered how EW could be used to recycle
6059  mining waste or clean up existing quarries. In this regard, there is some early evidence of EW
6060  discussions taking on nationally specific flavours--though it is again crucial to reiterate that these
6061  focus groups were much relatively less likely to engage with EW during their discussions and
6062  found sense-making to be much more difficult, that is, unless there was a specific individual or
6063  national familiarity with the extractive or agricultural sector.
6064
6065  10.2 Climate Justice and Ethics

6066  Enhanced Weathering raises various ethical and justice-related considerations, emerging first and
6067  foremost from the greater promise for deployment in hot, humid climates and tropic regions across
6068  the Global South.19,23,24,78,245 Where this creates opportunities for higher yields in food production
6069  or reduced need for costly fertilisers, local farmers and communities could benefit. Particularly if
6070  new mining and extraction were to be required, however, the placement of such activities in
6071  specific regions could create severe distributional injustice issues, even to the point of land-
6072  grabbing, dispossession, or cultivating "sacrifice zones".560,565,583
6073
6074  In a more general sense, EW, like other CDR technologies, raises justice issues regarding the
6075  displacement of mitigation efforts (e.g., ref. 6), especially if poorer or lower-emitting regions
6076  needed to further reduce their emissions as a result.78,581 EW could also promote intergenerational
6077  injustice, if future generations are left to bear most of the adverse consequences.561,581 Looking at
6078  public perceptions of EW, environmental injustice and the issue of unequal burden-sharing
6079  between poorer and richer countries have consistently been underlined.555,569,571,573,574,580,774
6080  Particularly where EW was to be deployed in the Global South, publics raised concerns about
6081  exploitation and "environmental dumping"--even where this was initially framed in terms of job
6082  creation.574,580 Publics across the Global South were, moreover, most negative about the risks that
6083  EW (and other CDR methods) would decrease the motivation to reduce CO2 emissions.569
6084
6085  10.3 Stakeholder Engagement and Collaboration
6086
      The relevance of stakeholder engagement for developing and deploying EW has mostly been an
6087  implicit or abstract consideration, until recently. Some researchers gesture towards outreach and
6088  dialogue with local stakeholders and communities as a general principle and requirement,
6089  specifically noting farmers and mineral extraction as key actors;21,22,131,161 others suggest the value
6090  of more theoretical work to foster a fairer allocation of benefits and costs among stakeholders, via
6091  multi-agent decision-making models.420 Using a large expert-interview exercise, Sovacool
6092  (2023)580 highlighted the concern that scaling-up of EW can lend itself to more consolidated, top-
6093  down governance, rather than a community-based approach.
6094
6095  Lessons from actual engagement with real-world stakeholders are starting to emerge, including
6096  through the greater involvement of social sciences researchers. Haque et al. (2024)556, for instance,
6097  offers insights into the difficulties of engaging and coordinating with stakeholders in the context
6098  of field trials. Santos et al. (2023)578 similarly stressed the usefulness of listening to farmers when
6099  it comes to suitable application rates. Meanwhile, qualitative research with local communities and
6100  stakeholders, in Malaysia and the United Kingdom, respectively, has established how conditions
6101  for EW implementation must be: tailored to local contexts (e.g., via job creation, local
6102
6103

      162
      Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

6104  infrastructure investments, local-scale monitoring), promote fair, open governance, and provide
6105  transparent and unbiased information about impacts.555,775 While those involved in workshops
6106  raised several concerns and questions about EW (e.g., effectiveness removing CO2, potential for
6107  environmental contamination, mitigation deterrence, and absence of remediation plans), the
6108  studies also found that acceptability could be promoted by providing opportunities for deliberation
6109  and to ask questions (to experts), information about projects that are underway, and presenting
6110  real-time project data. O'Sullivan et al. (2025)775 also identified a broad preference for using public
6111  funding versus private financing on the grounds of being fairer, more transparent, and avoiding
6112  risk of mitigation deterrence. Using a "question-led innovation" approach, where public and local
6113  stakeholders could ask actionable questions to inform ongoing research efforts, Cox et al. (2025)555
6114  identified agreement between stakeholder interests (in Malaysia) and the current research on EW.
6115  Notably, questions focused on rock resources and their sources, including the need for new mining
6116  operations. The second-most discussed subject were benefits for local farmers and crop yields--
6117  more common than questions on health and/or environmental impacts, and regulation. Stakeholder
6118  concerns did however indicate knowledge gaps, notably on full life cycle CO2 emissions, including
6119  sequestration, impacts on aquatic life or worker health, and socio-economic factors such as
6120  governance, cost and regulation. Pointing to the fraught history in the local context, Cox et al.
6121  (2025)555 highlighted underlying concerns about resource mining impacts and carbon goals being
6122  over-emphasized vis-à-vis conservation and biodiversity.

      163
      Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

6123  11 EW in Integrated Assessment Models

6124  The scale of deployment of EW will depend, among other factors, on climate mitigation ambition,
6125  the availability of required resources, and its cost competitiveness relative to other CDR options.
6126  Integrated assessment models (IAMs) are forward-looking optimization frameworks used to derive
6127  cost-optimal mitigation pathways while representing interactions between the energy system, land
6128  use, and the broader economy. By linking these sectors within a single modelling framework,
6129  IAMs allow exploration of how technological developments, policy choices, and biophysical
6130  constraints influence future mitigation portfolios. This section first outlines how EW is represented
6131  in IAMs, focusing on assumptions regarding feedstocks, weathering processes, deployment limits
6132  and cost (Section 11.1), and summarises the insights emerging from scenario analyses (Section
6133  11.2).
6134
6135  11.1 Integration in IAMs

6136  The integration of EW in IAMs is still emerging and currently limited to a small number of models
      and studies. The Sixth IPCC Assessment Report (AR6) identified two IAM-based analyses
6137  including EW,661 using the REMIND,78 and the En-ROADS models.781 Since then, EW has also
6138  been implemented in GCAM,782 GET,717 and EPPA,418 and additional IAMs are exploring EW
6139  scenarios in preparation for the 7th IPCC Assessment Report. Additional scenario-modelling
6140  approaches focus either specifically on EW or on broader CDR portfolios.19,22 While these
6141  analyses similarly consider factors such as cost, energy demand, and land constraints, they
6142  typically rely on different modelling frameworks and represent a narrower portion of the coupled
6143  energy­land­economy system.783­786
6144
6145  Table 6 provides an overview of key implementation differences across IAMs. All IAMs represent
6146  basalt as the primary feedstock and assume a maximum sequestration potential of approximately
6147  0.3­0.33 tCO2 per tonne of basalt under complete dissolution. Existing IAM studies do not
6148  explicitly constrain deployment by global basalt resource availability or mining capacity, allowing
6149  expansion beyond current mining levels. Instead, the theoretical deployment limit is primarily
6150  determined by the land area available for application. Implementations based on Beerling et al.
6151  (2020)19 and Strefler et al. (2018)23 restrict deployment to croplands located in sufficiently warm
6152  and humid climate zones. Under constant cropland area assumptions, this framework yields a
6153  weathering potential of around 4.9 GtCO2 yr1.23 In contrast, Chiquier et al. (2025)418 find a
6154  cropland-based removal potential of 7.7 GtCO2 yr1 in 2100 in one EPPA scenario, reflecting
6155  different assumptions regarding cropland availability and climatic suitability. Beyond croplands,
6156  the GET model additionally allows basalt application in forests, albeit at higher costs and energy
6157  requirements for rock spreading. This substantially increases the modelled cost-effective CDR
6158  potential through greater land availability and through simulated increases in biomass growth that
6159  contribute additional biotic CDR.717
6160
6161  The models take different approaches to geographic differentiation as well as the influence of
6162  climate conditions and grain sizes on the weathering. GCAM and EPPA employ an aggregated
6163  approach, taking the transport distance of 300 km covering 80% of climate-suitable cropland from
6164  ref. 23, and applying an upper bound on the annual spreading of rock on fields derived from
6165
6166
6167

      164
      Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)
