6171  underlying weathering assumptions. The weathering process is not explicitly modelled. REMIND
6172  and GET, on the other hand, explicitly model weathering and limit the amount of non-weathered
6173  rocks per ha at any point in time (Table 6). These differences imply different temporal links
6174  between application of rocks and the CO2 removal accounted for in each year. They also result in
6175  differences in the timing of cost incurred for rock spreading and the accounting of associated CO2
6176  removal compensation.
6177
6178  Electricity demand for basalt mining, crushing, and grinding, as well as fuel demand for rock
6179  transport, are typically based on estimates from Strefler et al. (2018)23 or Renforth (2012)18.
6180  Associated costs and CO2 emissions are calculated endogenously within the models and contribute
6181  to regional cost differences (see also Section 6 on costs). Assumptions on transport distances and
6182  investment, operation, and labour costs are generally derived from average or upper-bound
6183  estimates reported in Strefler et al. (2018)23 (Table 6). Cost dynamics associated with increasing
6184  deployment differ across models. In REMIND, for example, transport distances are represented
6185  explicitly, leading to rising costs as deployment expands to more distant croplands. The model
6186  also prioritizes areas with higher weathering rates within each region, resulting in increasing
6187  marginal removal costs as deployment expands into areas with less favourable weathering
6188  conditions. In the more aggregated representation used in GCAM, increasing marginal costs per
6189  tonne of CO2 removed are represented through region-specific EW supply curves derived from
6190  Beerling et al. (2020)19, which approximate the expansion of deployment into areas with
6191  progressively less favourable weathering conditions.787
6192
6193  In summary, despite differences in how weathering dynamics and application constraints are
6194  represented, current IAM implementations of EW remain relatively simple and are largely shaped
6195  by a small set of foundational studies that are commonly used as benchmarks for theoretical CDR
6196  potential.18,19,23 Across the models summarised in Table 6, EW deployment in ambitious mitigation
6197  scenarios with global cooperation reach several gigatonnes of CDR per year. Cropland-only
6198  implementations generally approach their maximum removal limit with roughly ~3­7 GtCO2 yr1,
6199  while models that include additional land areas or biotic feedbacks can yield higher values (Table
6200  6). Differences relative to the technical potential ranges discussed in Section 5.2 primarily reflect
6201  differing assumptions about what is technologically achievable, together with the fact that many
6202  IAM benchmarks predate recent recognition of geochemical losses and feedback processes that
6203  may reduce realised CDR (Section 3.4).
6204
      11.2 Performance of EW in IAM scenarios
6205
      The existing IAM scenario literature that includes EW remains relatively limited. Across the
6206  studies identified in this review, ten IAM scenario analyses include EW in their modelling
6207  frameworks.78,416­419,717,781,787­789 Most of these studies examine EW as part of broader CDR
6208  portfolios, with only one study explicitly focusing on EW as the primary technology of interest.717
6209  Even where EW is represented in IAMs, it is therefore not treated as a standard technology like
6210  BECCS and remains much less extensively covered than DACCS.32 This is also reflected in
6211  scenario design decisions, for example the exclusion of EW alongside DACCS in scenarios framed
6212  as "limited CDR".789
6213
6214  At present, most IAM implementations do not explicitly represent all geochemical limitations or
6215  feedbacks on primary weathering rates and subsequent CDR or only include a subset of the
6216

      166
      Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

6217  processes now recognised as relevant (see also Sections 5.2.1 and 3.4). These include rate-
6218  dependent effects such as increasing CDR losses at higher application rates due to secondary phase
6219  precipitation, declining weathering rates associated with increasing porewater saturation, limits on
6220  the availability of rock powder (Section 5.2.1). In addition, several EW-related impacts discussed
6221  in earlier sections--such as side effects from large-scale mining, water requirements for slurry
6222  preparation, or MRV challenges--are typically not represented explicitly beyond indirect
6223  assumptions such as delayed technology availability.418 Consequently, some of the higher CDR
6224  rates modelled in IAM scenarios may depend on assumptions about weathering efficiencies that
6225  remain uncertain under real-world geochemical constraints.161,172 Potential agronomic co-
6226  benefits47 (cf., Section 7.3) are likewise generally not represented, apart from an experimental
6227  implementation in GCAM treating basalt as a fertiliser782 and the inclusion of biotic CDR for forest
6228  application in GET. Nevertheless, these frameworks remain highly valuable for exploring the role
6229  of EW within broader CDR portfolios, and their representations are expected to improve as
6230  empirical understanding of how EW unfolds in soils continues to advance and updated projections
6231  of technological potentials these models rely on become available.
6232
6233  In comparison to other CDR options, in IAMs, EW has intermediate cost and potential (Table
6234  6),78,788 making its deployment sensitive to the mitigation ambition717 and, at least regionally, EW
6235  cost assumptions.419 The availability of EW increases total CDR deployment.78,717,787 The
6236  deployment can be sufficiently high to reduce mitigation cost through substitution of more
6237  expensive mitigation options and/or allowing delay of mitigation through the additional removal
6238  capacity, which depends on the scenario design.78,717 Deployment tends to increase with less
6239  favourable conditions for other CDR technologies, for example when geologic CO2 storage or
6240  bioenergy supply are limited, even in regions where the theoretical potential is small.417,419
6241
6242  In high mitigation ambition scenarios, EW can approach the models' theoretical deployment limit
6243  (Table 6) and scales rapidly. For example, it approaches the maximum potential by 2040 in global
6244  and regional GCAM studies.419,787 A notable exception is Apeaning et al. (2025)789 with a global
6245  deployment of only 0.75 GtCO2 yr-1 for a 1.5°C scenario. Although not discussed in the paper, the
6246  reason may be their scenario's regionally differentiated mitigation ambition which increases with
6247  gross domestic product (GDP). The climate conditions cause the tropical regions of Latin America
6248  and Asia to have a higher deployment potential, which includes countries with lower GDP and
6249  historic emission responsibility.78,787 Gigaton-level deployment may thus depend on global
6250  cooperation and could require large international finance transfers, raising equity concerns.78
6251
6252  The studies with gigaton deployment also critically reflect on the upscaling in relation to logistical
6253  challenges, reflecting tensions relating to requirements for technical potentials as discussed in
6254  Section 5.2.1. Although EW can use infrastructure and equipment that already exist today,22,788
6255  large-scale deployment would imply significant modification of material supply chains and
6256  expansion of basalt mining efforts beyond the current size of the coal industry.78,418,419 Potentially
6257  increasing costs from the upscaling of mining are not reflected in the cost assumptions, nor are
6258  other social and legal barriers represented.22,788
6259
6260  Finally, IAMs allow a systems perspective on EW's electricity and fuel demand. Beerling et al.
6261  (2020)19 restrict deployment to 3% of countries' final energy production, which indirectly limits
6262  EW's impact on the energy system in GCAM.787 Nonetheless, the sub-regional distribution can

      167
      Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

6263  lead to much higher local shares.419 Beyond the individual technology perspective, EW energy
6264  requirements also need to be considered in the context of the entire CDR portfolios deployment.
6265  For example, Afrane et al. (2025)416 show scenarios in which the total CDR electricity demand is
6266  up to 20% in some regions, with DACCS as the main driver. The feasibility and impacts of such
6267  demand in the context of regional socio-economic conditions and renewable energy potentials has
6268  so far not been covered extensively in IAM research.

      168
      Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

6269  12 Technology readiness level

6270  Technology readiness level (TRL) is a stage-based framework used to describe how far a
6271  technology has progressed from basic concept to operational deployment.790 In practice, TRL
6272  assessments ask whether a method has only been theorized, validated in the laboratory, tested in
6273  relevant environments, demonstrated at pilot scale, or proven under real operating conditions. For
6274  CDR, TRL matters because lower-readiness methods generally carry greater uncertainty in cost,
6275  performance, scalability, environmental effects, and governance needs;3,790 this is especially
6276  important where policy, procurement, or carbon-crediting systems may move faster than the
6277  underlying evidence base. Technology readiness should not be interpreted in isolation: methods at
6278  similar nominal TRLs can differ strongly in supply-chain maturity, MRV feasibility, and
6279  regulatory readiness.
6280
6281  Enhanced Weathering is generally characterized as an early- to mid-stage CDR method overall,
6282  most often around TRL 3­4 (out of 9, including by the IPCC), with one synthesis placing it at TRL
6283  4­5.3,541,583,790 Technology readiness of EW is typically judged to be below
6284  afforestation/reforestation, BECCS, DACCS, and biochar in overall deployment maturity, while
6285  still ahead of some ocean-based approaches.3,541,790 This overall ranking is consistent with the fact
6286  that EW has been demonstrated in laboratory studies and small field trials, but not yet through
6287  broadly replicated, transparently monitored, large-scale deployment. The same attached
6288  assessments also suggest that EW compares favourably to some other novel CDR options in terms
6289  of conceptual simplicity and use of familiar industrial processes, but less favourably in terms of
6290  proof of durable, quantified net removals under routine operating conditions.
6291
6292  A useful distinction when evaluating the readiness of EW lies between the maturity of its physical
6293  supply chain and that of its carbon accounting and monitoring systems. The infrastructure required
6294  to implement EW is largely derived from well-established industrial and agricultural practices.
6295  Core operational steps--including quarrying, crushing, mineral processing, transport, and field
6296  spreading--closely resemble activities already performed at large scale in the mining, aggregate,
6297  and agricultural liming sectors rather than requiring entirely new technological
6298  systems.19,106,118,538,582 In many regions, existing liming equipment, established logistics networks,
6299  and proximity between rock resources and agricultural land could enable relatively rapid
6300  operational scaling where favourable geographic conditions and rock sources exist. At the same
6301  time, regional constraints such as transportation capacity, supply-chain coordination, and
6302  infrastructure availability may still limit deployment in some contexts (see also Section 8).17,428
6303  Overall, these observations suggest that the physical processes involved in producing, transporting,
6304  and applying rock materials are not the principal factors limiting the overall TRL of EW.
6305
6306  Instead, important readiness challenges currently arise in the domain of MRV as well as the limited
6307  availability of verified field data. Recent assessments highlight that robust methods for quantifying
6308  CDR from EW remain under development, and that standardised accounting approaches suitable
6309  for large-scale implementation are still emerging (see also Section 4).325,383 More broadly, recent
6310  assessments emphasize that MRV approaches for EW are still being developed and tested,
6311  reflecting the complexity of linking mineral dissolution, carbon uptake, and long-term carbon
6312  storage across heterogeneous environmental settings.345,505,541 While robust MRV systems are
6313  widely recognised as essential for establishing credibility and enabling the responsible deployment
6314  of CDR approaches,541 MRV methodologies remain at different stages of development across

      169
      Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

6315  pathways. For EW, the main challenge lies in reliably quantifying net CO2 removal under field
6316  conditions, where carbon uptake depends on coupled geochemical and hydrological processes that
6317  operate across heterogeneous soils, climates, and management regimes, limiting both conceptual
6318  clarity and availability of large-scale, verified EW projects.
6319
6320  Taken together, these considerations indicate that the TRL of EW is uneven across the deployment
6321  chain. While many supply-chain elements already exist and could in principle be mobilised at
6322  scale, key uncertainties remain in demonstrating durable, verifiable CDR under real-world
6323  conditions. Progress toward higher TRL will therefore depend less on further demonstration of
6324  basic engineering feasibility and more on large-scale field deployments, improved monitoring
6325  frameworks, and transparent, independent evaluation of net CDR outcomes. This distinction is
6326  important when comparing EW to other CDR approaches. Relative to methods such DACCS or
6327  BECCS, EW may rely less on entirely new capture hardware, but it currently faces greater
6328  challenges in directly quantifying and verifying removal outcomes in situ.

      170
      Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

6329  13 Policy frameworks for scaling EW

6330  There is a general consensus that CDR as a climate solution requires policy support to scale791­793
6331  and that each CDR solution, including EW, requires additional context-specific assessment and
6332  support.794,795 A holistic approach to EW policy will likely include i) improved estimates for global
6333  and local removal potential, ii) targets for EW embedded in larger climate policy measures, iii)
6334  appropriate and scalable quantification tools built on robust data sets, iv) updated environmental
6335  regulations to prevent harm (e.g. release and bioaccumulation of toxic trace metals) and v) large-
6336  scale subsidies for deployment where on-farm benefits (e.g. crop yield improvements) do not fully
6337  cover the cost of feedstock, spreading, and measurement.
6338
6339  Accurate estimates of CDR via EW must underpin policy decision-making. Existing literature
6340  provides global estimates of current and future EW potential under a range of climate scenarios,
6341  with varying degrees of geographic specificity.19,75,245 More recent efforts have attempted to
6342  provide granular estimates of removal potential for specific pathways in specific jurisdictions,796
6343  though these efforts rely on simple assumptions about feedstock availability and have been
6344  hampered by limited field trial data on EW outcomes, from which one could extrapolate potentials
6345  for larger scale deployment. Efforts have also been made to improve integration of CDR into IAMs
6346  (cf., Section 11). Improved estimates are particularly important as policy discussions move from
6347  high-level multilateral institutions (e.g. IPCC assessment reports) to national, sub-national, and
6348  municipal governments focused on implementation.
6349
6350  Adoption of CDR technologies into policy is often driven by pre-existing climate laws and goals.
6351  The first step is often setting a net-zero target. According to the International Energy Agency, 105
6352  national governments have made net-zero pledges, 30 of which are codified in law. An additional
6353  61 countries have included net-zero pledges in non-binding policy documents such as national
6354  climate plans.797 The second step in adopting CDR policy is defining residual emissions at the time
6355  of net-zero, which would be offset by removals. Recent reviews of long-term climate strategies
6356  suggest that residual emissions are poorly defined or entirely excluded from national
6357  strategies.798,799 Where residual emissions are included in national strategies, they are often
6358  balanced by large amounts of land-sector removals, with limited use of novel CDR methods like
6359  EW.800 Integration of novel removals into national strategies, using refined estimates of removal
6360  potential, will likely precede broad EW policy development and implementation. Recent work has
6361  highlighted the utility of separate targets for CDR, alongside targets for emissions reductions and
6362  conventional removals, to unlock public innovation support, reduce the risk of mitigation
6363  deterrence, and enable tracking of progress against multi-decadal goals.541,788,801
6364
6365  To accurately assess progress towards removal targets, including jurisdiction-specific EW targets,
6366  appropriate tools and standards are needed. Significant progress has been made in the
6367  quantification of EW in the last decade, as demonstrated by the rapid increase in academic
6368  publications and codified in carbon registry methodologies (cf., Section 4). Significant challenges
6369  remain given the inherent variability of soils, making it difficult and expensive to achieve the
6370  tonne-level precision often required for carbon crediting. It has been proposed that monitoring of
6371  EW in the context of large-scale agricultural policies, which would in turn be reported in national
6372  GHG inventories, need not require this level of precision. In this context, the ongoing IPCC 2027
6373  Methodology Report on Carbon Dioxide Removal Technologies, Carbon Capture, Utilization, and
6374  Storage for National GHG Inventories is particularly relevant. The report is intended to provide

      171
      Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

6375  additional methodological guidance for estimating and reporting CDR and CCUS in national GHG
6376  inventories, including where existing IPCC Guidelines contain gaps or outdated guidance for
6377  emerging removal approaches. If EW is included, this process could provide an important basis
6378  for how countries account for EW removals in national inventories, although the final guidance
6379  has not yet been adopted.
6380
6381  To support such national-level GHG accounting, landscape-level estimates using soil and
6382  watershed data may be sufficient, in alignment with reporting requirements for other activities in
6383  the agriculture and land-use sector and with emerging proposals for aggregated monitoring.802,803
6384  These efforts can leverage existing soil data collection efforts, such as national soil health cards,
6385  and riverine water-quality monitoring systems, such as the US Geological Survey stream-gaging
6386  network. Regardless, in the near term, public support for research and development is needed to
6387  develop sufficiently robust and transparent datasets to assess CDR via EW across a range of soil
6388  types, climates, hydrological conditions, feedstocks, and crops to enable both carbon-market-based
6389  projects and non-market-based policies. The US, EU, UK, Germany, and Australia have publicly
6390  funded research programs on EW in 2026, but additional public support is needed.21
6391
6392  Recent efforts have highlighted the range of potential environmental impacts of EW on agricultural
6393  lands, both in soils and in downstream waters.113,129­131,475 Particular emphasis has been placed on
6394  the potential for heavy metal accumulation in soil, with possible health impacts on animals and
6395  humans.129 Alongside continued research on potential environmental impacts, updated
6396  environmental regulations are needed to ensure that large-scale EW deployments do not contribute
6397  to environmental harms and, ultimately, environmental injustice. In addition to regulatory limits,
6398  third-party certification and pre-deployment feedstock screening may help mitigate toxicity
6399  concerns by requiring material characterization, traceability, and assessment of potentially toxic
6400  elements before agricultural application. This is particularly important as an increasingly wide
6401  range of industrial waste materials have been proposed as potential feedstocks for agricultural
6402  EW.410,415,478,582,804­807
6403
6404  Near-term policy development for CDR, including EW, largely relies on the logic and language of
6405  carbon markets, drawing on both existing compliance market infrastructure (e.g. the European
6406  Union Emissions Trading Scheme) and activity in the emerging SOM for novel types of permanent
6407  CDR. For example, Canada and the US have proposed directly purchasing CDR credits with public
6408  funds to spur growth in the CDR market and the EU is in the process of developing a voluntary
6409  CDR credit certification scheme and registry.21 A recent expert survey suggests that EW
6410  companies expect to rely on compliance markets for revenue through 2050.808 However, recent
6411  meta-reviews of climate policies suggest that compliance markets are important but insufficient to
6412  achieve climate targets--a mix of policy mechanisms is needed.793,809. Enhanced Weathering, in
6413  particular, could benefit from integration into large-scale agricultural subsidies. Agricultural
6414  subsidies are popular and politically resilient around the world, totalling over 400B USD
6415  annually.810 Carbonate-based EW (liming) is already subsidized in many countries and sub-
6416  national jurisdictions802 and efforts have been made to incorporate silicate weathering into the
6417  same policies.811
6418
6419  Enhanced Weathering, perhaps uniquely among novel CDR solutions, also presents opportunities
6420  to tap into a range of promising policy mechanisms, including but not limited to landscape-level

      172
          Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)
6421 monitoring infrastructure280 (e.g. water quality programs) and agricultural subsidy regimes (e.g.
6422 liming subsidies).

                                                                   173
      Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

6423  14 Conclusion and summary

6424  There is now substantial and converging evidence that EW can deliver meaningful CDR while
6425  simultaneously generating a range of co-benefits, particularly in agricultural and agroforestry
6426  systems. Across laboratory experiments, field trials, and modelling studies, EW has been shown
6427  to increase alkalinity generation, enhance carbon storage, and, in many cases, improve soil fertility
6428  and crop performance. Yet, despite this growing body of evidence, key quantitative metrics remain
6429  highly uncertain. Reported area-normalized CDR fluxes, regional and global potentials, and cost
6430  estimates frequently span orders of magnitude. This spread does not solely reflect unresolved
6431  scientific uncertainty or methodological limitations. Rather, it also captures the inherently context-
6432  dependent nature of EW: its performance, costs, and co-benefits vary systematically across
6433  climatic conditions, soil properties, feedstock characteristics, application strategies, and
6434  monitoring approaches. As a result, EW should not be understood as a single, uniform CDR
6435  pathway, but as a spectrum of system-specific interventions whose outcomes depend on the
6436  interplay of environmental, geochemical, economical, and social factors. These context
6437  dependencies have direct implications for policy design, target setting, and implementation
6438  strategies, which are explored in more detail in Section 13.
6439
6440  14.1 Research landscape and harmonisation challenges

6441  The current evidence base for EW is both rapidly expanding and highly heterogeneous, comprising
6442  241 peer-reviewed studies published between 1995 and April 2025, with particularly rapid growth
6443  since 2020. This literature spans a wide range of research approaches, including experimental
6444  laboratory and mesocosm studies (23%), modelling studies (21%), field experiments (10%), and a
6445  substantial share of reviews and perspective papers, reflecting both active knowledge generation
6446  and ongoing synthesis efforts. With respect to content, the majority of studies (65%) focus on
6447  quantifying CDR through weathering rates, alkalinity generation, and carbon budgets, while
6448  environmental impacts, agronomic effects, and techno-economic considerations are investigated
6449  less frequently. Across this diverse body of work, a range of feedstocks are investigated, with a
6450  clear dominance of mafic (n = 86, including basalt) and ultramafic (n = 53 including olivine/dunite)
6451  feedstocks, with smaller contributions from wollastonite, industrial by-products, and other
6452  materials. This diversity reflects both the flexibility of EW as a concept and the wide range of
6453  environmental and operational contexts in which it is being explored.
6454
6455  At the same time, this breadth introduces a set of persistent challenges that currently limit
6456  comparability and quantitative synthesis across studies. A central issue is the lack of consistent
6457  feedstock characterization,132 with many studies reporting insufficient or non-standardised
6458  information on composition, classification, particle size, or surface area, despite these being key
6459  controls on weathering rates. Even where such parameters are reported, they are described in
6460  highly inconsistent ways. For example, particle size distributions are reported using an
6461  exceptionally wide range of metrics and conventions, with more than 80 distinct units, measures,
6462  or reporting approaches identified across the literature. These span fundamentally different
6463  representation types, including mean or percentile diameters, cumulative mass fractions at
6464  arbitrary thresholds, and discrete size-class bins defined using a wide variety of sieve ranges. As a
6465  result, studies that ostensibly investigate similar materials often cannot be directly compared.
6466
6467

      174
      Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

6468  More broadly, key experimental parameters are often reported inconsistently or incompletely,
6469  further limiting comparability across studies. A recurring issue is the reporting of application
6470  amounts and sometimes CDR data: particularly in laboratory and mesocosm experiments,
6471  feedstock additions or CDR findings are frequently expressed only as mass per treatment (e.g., kg
6472  per pot) without sufficient information on experimental footprint, making it difficult to derive area-
6473  normalized application or CDR rates necessary for comparison. In other cases, applied volumes
6474  are reported without corresponding material densities, or repeated applications are described
6475  without clearly specifying frequency, duration, or cumulative totals.
6476
6477  Similar limitations arise in the reporting of hydrological conditions. In many studies, not all
6478  components of the water balance are documented, and water inputs are often described only
6479  partially, for example as watering regimes intended to maintain constant soil moisture, or as
6480  volumes per treatment, without reporting total water fluxes over time or lacking experiment
6481  dimensions necessary for conversion. This makes it difficult to compare hydrological forcing
6482  across experiments or relate results to field-relevant conditions. Where water inputs are reported,
6483  they are rarely expressed in standardised, scale-independent units such as depth-equivalent fluxes
6484  (e.g., mm or mm yr1), which would provide a more consistent basis for cross-study comparison.
6485  Furthermore, the temporal structure of water inputs is seldom resolved: event frequency, intensity,
6486  and intermittency are rarely documented, despite their strong influence on infiltration dynamics,
6487  residence times, and geochemical reactions.252,371
6488
6489  Such reporting gaps apply to initial soil properties, which are essential for interpreting EW
6490  outcomes and explaining variation across studies. Soil texture, pH, CEC, BS, SOC/SIC content,
6491  bulk density, and initial exchangeable cation pools strongly influence mineral dissolution, cation
6492  retention, alkalinity export, agronomic responses, and SOC dynamics. Yet these parameters are
6493  not consistently reported, are often measured at different depths, or are described using non-
6494  standard units and methods. This limits the ability to identify drivers of variability across
6495  experiments and constrains more detailed meta-analyses aimed at disentangling the effects of
6496  feedstock, soil, climate, hydrology, and management. More consistent reporting of baseline soil
6497  properties, including sampling depth and analytical methods, would therefore substantially
6498  improve the interpretability and comparability of EW studies.
6499
6500  A further harmonisation challenge is that some studies report CDR estimates that combine
6501  inorganic EW-derived CDR with changes in SOC stocks. This can make it difficult, and in some
6502  cases impossible, to distinguish the contribution of EW from changes in SOC or biomass. Such
6503  combination is problematic because inorganic and organic carbon pools differ fundamentally in
6504  formation pathways, monitoring approaches, storage timescales, durability, and reversal risks. We
6505  therefore recommend that inorganic CDR from EW and organic carbon responses should always
6506  be reported separately, even where both are included in a broader net climate-impact assessment.
6507
6508  Together, these issues mirror the inconsistencies observed for grain size and feedstock
6509  characterization and point to a broader lack of standardisation in how key process variables and
6510  CDR accounting boundaries are documented. Addressing these gaps through clearer and more
6511  consistent reporting standards will be essential for enabling robust cross-study comparisons,
6512  improving mechanistic understanding, and constraining the CDR potential of EW.
6513

      175
      Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

6514  14.2 Quantifying CDR potential

6515  A rapidly growing body of MRV approaches has emerged to quantify CDR from EW, spanning
6516  solid, aqueous, and gaseous phases, as well as isotopic tracers.325,383 These approaches differ
6517  substantially in their system boundaries and the loss processes they account for, which makes
6518  direct comparison across studies or approaches challenging. At the same time, this diversity
6519  reflects the fundamentally diverse nature of field conditions EW operates in and provides a
6520  valuable opportunity to interrogate different parts of the CDR and loss pathway with targeted
6521  methods. Rather than converging on a single "best" MRV approach, the field is likely to evolve
6522  toward a suite of complementary methods, each optimized for specific components of the initial
6523  CDR and subsequent loss and transport cascade as well as deployment contexts.
6524
6525  Across the empirical and modelling literature, area-normalized CDR fluxes from EW span several
6526  orders of magnitude, reflecting both true system variability and differences in study design. Across
6527  511 compiled flux estimates from 61 studies, the median flux is 0.84 tCO2 ha1 yr1 but estimates
6528  vary over multiple orders of magnitude. Across experimental studies ~84% of fluxes are positive,
6529  ~34% are <0.1 tCO2 ha1 yr1, 25% are 0.1-1 tCO2 ha1 yr1, and 33% are between 1-10 tCO2 ha1
6530  yr1. However, many of the larger estimates do not fully account for loss processes that reduce net
6531  CDR after initial weathering occurs and/or reflect conditions that would be unrealistic to achieve
6532  in the field. Importantly, these fluxes overlap with--but are often lower than--values assumed in
6533  large-scale projections of CDR potential19,24,245 even though not all estimates fully account for loss
6534  processes or transient early dissolution pulses. Nevertheless, emerging evidence increasingly
6535  shows that EW can deliver measurable CDR and clear co-benefits in some settings, while
6536  producing limited or negligible CDR and agricultural benefits in others. Enhanced Weathering
6537  should therefore no longer be framed simply as a question of whether it can work in the field, but
6538  rather where, when, and how it can be made to work most effectively. The field should be--and
6539  increasingly is 19,24,93,245--pivoting toward identifying the environmental, mineralogical,
6540  agronomic, and management conditions that govern EW outcomes, and toward optimizing
6541  deployments to maximize durable CDR alongside agronomic and SOC/SOM co-benefits.
6542  Importantly, agronomic benefits provide a major opportunity to align climate mitigation with
6543  farmer incentives, offering a pathway for scaling EW that is not dependent on CDR value alone.
6544
6545  When scaled globally, the literature is beginning to converge on a maximum technical CDR (c.f.,
6546  Section 5.2) potential in the range of ~0.2­2 GtCO2 yr1. Empirically constrained approaches
6547  suggest lower-bound estimates of ~0.22­0.24 GtCO2 yr1 14,240, while process-based models
6548  project ~0.5­2 GtCO2 yr1 and cumulative potentials of 64-217 GtCO2 over 75 years, depending
6549  on assumptions regarding application rates, land availability, and model implementation.19,24,245
6550  At the same time, emerging evidence on transient and long-term losses of initial CDR, e.g. via
6551  formation of secondary phases, strong acid weathering, or cation exchange,68,172,216,325,439,445 is not
6552  yet consistently incorporated across models. In addition, many assessments assume sustained
6553  large-scale deployment without accounting for resource limitations and cumulative application
6554  limits. This is particularly evident in earlier landmark assessments deriving higher potentials,17,23
6555  which provided important first-order estimates under simplified assumptions but were developed
6556  prior to the current understanding of key feedbacks and loss processes in the EW context. Together,
6557  these factors suggest that even current "best estimates" of maximum technical potentials may
6558  remain optimistic relative to fully constrained system behaviour.
6559

      176
      Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

6560  The representation of EW in IAMs remains limited but is expanding, with only a small number of
6561  IAMs currently including EW (e.g., REMIND, GCAM, GET, EPPA). Across these models, EW
6562  is typically implemented using simplified and harmonised assumptions, often drawing on a small
6563  set of foundational studies.18,19,23 Deployment is generally constrained by land availability,
6564  particularly cropland, while other factors such as feedstock availability, supply chain capacity, and
6565  some geochemical feedbacks are represented in a more aggregated or implicit manner. As a result,
6566  IAM-derived deployment levels--commonly reaching several gigatonnes of CO2 removal per year
6567  in high-ambition scenarios that sometimes extend beyond agricultural lands--tend to reflect upper-
6568  bound estimates under idealized assumptions, rather than fully constrained real-world outcomes.
6569  At the same time, IAMs consistently identify EW as an intermediate-cost, intermediate-potential
6570  CDR option whose deployment increases when other CDR pathways are limited, highlighting its
6571  potential role within diversified mitigation portfolios. Continued integration of emerging empirical
6572  insights on weathering dynamics, resource constraints, and system-level interactions will further
6573  improve the representation of EW in IAM frameworks and help refine projections of its long-term
6574  contribution, and this represents critically important future work.
6575
6576  Activity in the VCM provides an early indication of how EW is transitioning from research to
6577  deployment, albeit at modest scale. As of early 2026, cumulative EW credit sales amount to ~0.8
6578  MtCO2, representing approximately 1­2% of total CDR credits sold since 2022 (based on CDR.fyi
6579  data). With its rapid scaling, the VCM it is already functioning as an important testbed for EW
6580  implementation, providing early insights into operational feasibility and MRV development, and
6581  can be an important tool for learning where EW does and does not work--provided data is shared
6582  transparently.530
6583
6584  14.3 Technology readiness, costs, and LCA
6585
      Enhanced Weathering is generally classified as an early- to mid-stage CDR approach, with overall
6586  readiness around TRL 3­5.3,541,583,790 While core supply-chain components such as mining,
6587  comminution, transport, and spreading are based on mature and widely deployed technologies, a
6588  primary bottleneck lies in demonstrating durable, quantifiable CDR under real-world conditions.
6589  In particular, MRV frameworks and large-scale field validation remain underdeveloped, and few
6590  deployments have yet achieved robust, transparent verification of net removal. As a result,
6591  advancing EW readiness will depend less on engineering innovation and more on scaling field
6592  deployments, improving monitoring systems, and generating reproducible, independently
6593  verifiable evidence.
6594
6595  Across 25 studies (166 datapoints), EW CDR costs span more than an order of magnitude, with
6596  harmonised central estimates ranging from approximately USD 34 to more than USD 1,000 tCO21
6597  (inflation adjusted to 2024 USD) and a median around USD 190­200 tCO21. While favourable
6598  cases exist, the literature broadly supports a central tendency of roughly a few hundred dollars per
6599  tonne. These estimates are broadly consistent with current prices on the VCM (CDR.fyi data).529
6600  Cost variability reflects both real differences in deployment configurations and inconsistent
6601  assumptions regarding CDR efficiency and system boundaries. In the academic literature, costs
6602  are found to be primarily driven by grinding, transport, and application logistics, while suppliers
6603  report that MRV costs contribute on the order of USD 15­70 tCO21 in early-stage deployments.577
6604
6605

      177
      Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

6606  Life cycle assessments indicate that EW is a materially and energetically intensive CDR pathway,
6607  with upstream emissions ranging from ~1 to >200 kg CO2-eq per tonne of rock applied and energy
6608  demands of ~600­1,400 MJ t1. Nevertheless, unlike many other novel CDR approaches, EW does
6609  not inherently depend on large dedicated renewable energy inputs, although access to low-carbon
6610  energy can substantially improve its life-cycle emissions. Grinding and transport typically
6611  dominate emissions, together accounting for ~80% of total life cycle impacts on average. These
6612  upstream burdens can substantially reduce--or in some scenarios eliminate--net CDR,
6613  highlighting that EW performance depends critically on logistics, particle size optimization, and
6614  energy sources. At the same time, most studies do not comprehensively account for life cycle
6615  emissions, indicating that net CDR estimates remain incompletely constrained.
6616
6617  At scale, EW deployment based on basaltic or other primary rock feedstocks is unlikely to be
6618  supplied solely from existing waste streams or currently available surplus material. While some
6619  industrial alkaline residues may provide locally important near-term opportunities, large-scale
6620  basalt-based EW would likely require substantial expansion of quarrying, mining, comminution,
6621  and associated transport infrastructure. Consequently, LCA and cost studies that assume abundant
6622  low-cost waste or by-product feedstocks may not provide sufficient constraints for assessing the
6623  costs, emissions, permitting requirements, and deployment timelines of primary-rock EW at scale.
6624  In this context, limited data on suitable feedstock reserves, current and expandable production
6625  capacity, quarry development, permitting processes, and the time required to bring new extraction
6626  capacity online may represent a major bottleneck for EW scalability in the mid-term.
6627
6628  14.4. Co-benefits
6629
      Across the literature, EW is consistently associated with positive agronomic outcomes, particularly
6630  in terms of crop yields, nutrient use efficiency, and resilience. Evidence from both EW-focused
6631  studies and a broader body of agronomic rock-flour applications47 indicates that yield responses
6632  are overwhelmingly positive, with relatively few negative or neutral outcomes reported: out of 166
6633  studies, 129 report positive and 26 neutral responses, with only 11 negative responses. These
6634  benefits are commonly attributed to the supply of base cations (e.g., Ca, Mg, K), improved soil
6635  pH, and enhanced nutrient availability, particularly in acidic or nutrient-limited soils.47 In some
6636  cases, yield increases translate directly into economic benefits for farmers, reinforcing the potential
6637  for co-benefits to offset deployment costs, offering an alternative avenue for scaling.556,812
6638
6639  Responses of SOC to EW are variable and less well constrained, but emerging evidence suggests
6640  that they may be substantial. Reported SOC responses range from negative to positive, with
6641  increases often linked to enhanced plant productivity, greater biomass inputs, and changes in
6642  microbial activity and nutrient cycling.19,47,94,101,439 At the same time, EW can stimulate SOM
6643  decomposition through increased pH, nutrient availability, and microbial activity, leading in some
6644  cases to transient increases in CO2 efflux,112,114 although such responses are not consistently
6645  observed.91,224 Over longer timescales, EW may promote SOC stabilisation through MAOM
6646  formation and aggregation, mediated by weathering-derived cations and secondary
6647  minerals.92,526,527,657,658 While these processes point to the potential for significant organic carbon
6648  gains, losses of SOC could partially or fully offset inorganic carbon sequestration, and in extreme
6649  cases turn EW systems into net carbon sources.113,172,325 Evidence from literature on agricultural
6650
6651

      178
      Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

6652  liming suggests that initial pulses of SOC mineralisation can occur following mineral amendments
6653  but are often counterbalanced by longer-term increases in plant productivity and organic carbon
6654  accrual.42,43,659 Whether similar trajectories hold for EW remains insufficiently constrained and
6655  likely context dependent. This highlights the need for careful, long-term monitoring of SOC
6656  responses to ensure that EW delivers net climate benefits and does not inadvertently undermine
6657  them. In this context, parallel monitoring of inorganic EW CDR and soil SOC dynamics becomes
6658  essential, underscoring the potential value of co-application and co-crediting frameworks on the
6659  same land.
6660
6661  14.5 Synergies with other CDR approaches

6662  Enhanced Weathering is rarely deployed in isolation and instead operates within managed land
6663  systems where multiple CDR processes co-occur, creating opportunities for synergistic
6664  interactions. As a geochemical CDR strategy that stores carbon predominantly in stable inorganic
6665  reservoirs, EW occupies a distinctive position within the broader CDR portfolio by combining
6666  durable carbon storage with natural weathering reactions embedded in managed land systems and
6667  associated agronomic co-benefits.7,15,19 By supplying base cations and nutrients (e.g., Ca, Mg, K,
6668  Si) and buffering soil acidity, EW can enhance plant productivity, rooting depth, and microbial
6669  activity, thereby increasing biomass inputs and soil carbon formation.47,64,561 At the same time,
6670  because it can be implemented on existing agricultural lands without requiring dedicated biomass
6671  production, EW can complement rather than compete with biomass-based CDR approaches,
6672  enabling "stacking" of inorganic and organic carbon storage within the same system.7,813
6673
6674  Beyond additive effects, several mechanisms suggest the potential for true synergies between EW
6675  and regenerative or biomass-based approaches. Organic amendments and increased root
6676  respiration can elevate soil CO2 concentrations and organic acid production, accelerating mineral
6677  dissolution, while weathering-derived cations can promote stabilisation of SOC through MAOM
6678  formation and aggregation.92,439,526,527,657,658 In turn, improved soil structure, water retention, and
6679  nutrient cycling can sustain both biological productivity and weathering reactions over longer
6680  timescales.76,676 These agronomic benefits can also translate into increased biomass production,
6681  potentially reinforcing biomass-based CDR pathways such as BECCS or BiCRS generally.
6682  Together, these interactions can enhance total CDR per unit land while increasing system
6683  resilience. However, such synergies remain highly context dependent and introduce additional
6684  system complexity, particularly with respect to nutrient cycling, carbon partitioning, and
6685  attribution of CDR across pathways. Realizing their full potential will therefore require integrated
6686  system design and long-term evaluation of coupled inorganic and organic carbon dynamics.
6687
6688  14.6 Socio-economic impacts, public perception, and governance
6689  considerations

6690  Socio-economic factors are central to the feasibility of EW deployment. Evidence points to
      potential economic co-benefits, particularly in agriculture, where improved nutrient supply and
6691  soil conditions can increase yields and reduce fertiliser demand,180,444,556 with reported revenue
      gains of approximately USD 300 ha1 in smallholder systems. At larger scales, EW could generate
6692  employment across mining and agricultural sectors (~60,000­80,000 jobs under high deployment
6693
6694
6695
6696

      179
      Enhanced Weathering-ESROC systematic review V1 (submitted to EES) ­ Suhrhoff et al. (2026)

6697  scenarios in the US alone; ref. 22) and contribute to multiple SDGs, although outcomes are highly
6698  context dependent.84,560
6699
6700  These benefits are offset by important distributional and environmental challenges. High-potential
6701  deployment regions are concentrated in the Global South,19,24,245 raising concerns about unequal
6702  burden-sharing, extractive pressures, and "sacrifice zones" linked to mining.560,565,583 Economic
6703  gains may also be unevenly distributed across value chains.422 In addition, environmental and
6704  health risks--particularly dust exposure and potential heavy metal mobilisation--require careful
6705  monitoring and management.129,131,209,426
6706
6707  Public support remains conditional and linked to low awareness,573,765 with greater acceptance for
6708  research than large-scale deployment and key concerns focused on mining impacts, fairness, and
6709  governance. Compared to other CDR approaches, support for EW is generally lower than for more
6710  familiar land-based biological options such as afforestation or soil carbon sequestration, but often
6711  comparable to or higher than support for DACCS, reflecting EW's combination of engineered
6712  deployment, geochemical CDR, and links to managed land systems. Support increases with trust,
6713  knowledge, and opportunities for engagement.555,574,775 Overall, deployment will depend on
6714  aligning economic incentives with transparent governance, stakeholder engagement, and equitable
6715  distribution of risks and benefits.

6806

6807 19 Data availability statement

6808
6809 The data used in this systematic review were compiled as part of a broader database effort on EW.
6810 The full database is still being finalized, and additional data not used in the present review are
6811 undergoing quality control. Because the present review uses only a limited subset of the wider
6812 database, we have not separated this subset from the broader data product, as doing so would
6813 fragment a database intended to be published as a single, coherent resource. The complete database
6814 will be made publicly available alongside a dedicated database publication once final quality
6815 control has been completed. Until then, the data underlying the analyses reported in this review
6816 are available from the corresponding author upon reasonable request.

S1 Literature query

The following search query was used to retrieve candidate EW records from Web of Science and
Scopus as the first step in the literature identification process. The query was designed to capture
soil-based EW studies in the context of CDR, while excluding literature focused primarily on
weathering and carbon cycling in the geological past. Retrieved records were subsequently filtered
through an AI-based pre-selection step, manually screened against predefined inclusion and
exclusion criteria, and supplemented with additional studies identified through expert knowledge
and forward/backward snowballing where appropriate.
((TS= ((geoengineer*) AND (silicate OR olivine OR albite OR CACO3 OR liming)) OR
TS= ((silicate OR olivine OR albite OR CACO3 OR liming OR basalt*) AND ((("soil*" OR
"cropland" ) AND ("CO2" AND (stor* OR sequestr* OR remov*))) OR (mitigat* NEAR/3
("climate change" OR "global warming" OR geoengineer*)))) OR
TS= ((silicate OR olivine OR albite OR CACO3 OR liming OR basalt*OR mineral OR apatite*
OR feldspar* ) AND (agricultur* OR soil) AND (crush* NEAR/5 rock*)) OR
TS= ((("enhance*" OR "artificial*") NEAR/2 "weathering" ) AND ((carbon OR CO2 OR "climate
change" OR "global warming") NEAR/3 (remov* OR sequest* OR storage OR sink OR mitigat*
OR reduc*))) OR
(TS = (("enhance*" OR "artificial*" OR "chemical" OR "accelerate*" OR "mineral") NEAR/2
"weathering" ) AND TS = ((carbon OR CO2 OR "climate change" OR "global warming") NEAR/3
(remov* OR sequest* OR storage OR sink OR mitigat* OR reduc*) )) AND
TS = (mineral* OR rock OR silicate) ) OR
TS=((enhance* OR accelerate*)NEAR/4 ("carbon dioxide mineralization" OR "CO2
mineralization") AND (weathering)))
NOT TS = (glaci* OR ice* OR ordovic* OR Aptian OR Cenozo* OR Paleo* OR Mezoso*)
